<image-desc>The Google logo in its characteristic multi-colored (blue, red, yellow, blue, green, red) serif font, with a small superscript trademark symbol.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>Handwritten notes or sketches made in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>The technology used to convert images of text into editable digital text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement <note>The unauthorized use of works under copyright, which can lead to legal penalties</note> liability can be quite severe.

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. ## Concerning the herb Artemisia <note>Commonly known as Mugwort.</note>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a treatise on the medicinal powers of herbs, specifically the work known as "Macer Floridus." The text transitions from the poetic verse of the original herbal to a scholarly commentary explaining the origins and uses of the plants.</meta>

# On the Powers of Herbs

About to speak of the powers of certain herbs in song,
Artemisia <note>Mugwort (Artemisia vulgaris)</note> was given the name "Mother of Herbs,"
To which I think it right to first apply
The Greek name.
Diana is said to have first discovered its aid;
She is called *Artemis* in Greek, and from her the herb
Takes its name, because she is said to be its discoverer.
This herb heals women’s diseases especially;
A decoction of it, when taken, draws out the menses.
The womb <note>original: "matrix"</note> does the same if it is often bathed in this,
Or if the raw herb is ground and drunk with pure wine,
Or if, when green and crushed, it is bound over the belly at night.

->¶ About to speak of the powers of certain herbs, etc.<-

The poet begins his song with Artemisia, first declaring the origin of its name. Some wish to claim that Artemisia is derived from Artemisia, the wife of Mausolus, King of Caria <note>Artemisia II of Caria (died c. 350 BCE), a botanist and researcher</note>, who first gave her name to this herb. This Artemisia (as literary monuments testify) built a tomb for her husband—which she wished to be called the *Mausoleum*—of such magnificence, beauty, and ornament that it is numbered among the Seven Wonders of the World. Concerning this, Martial <note>Roman poet, 1st century CE</note> wrote: *Nor the Mausoleums hanging in the empty air.* Others say Artemisia is named after Artemis, that is, Diana <note>original: "arthemys ei.g. dyana latine fignat" - Artemis in Greek signifies Diana in Latin</note>, because just as Diana was invoked by those in labor (as can be seen in comedies), so too this herb specifically heals the ailments of women, as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note> testifies. Indeed, Juno is so named from "helping" (*iuvando*) and Lucina because she brings the birth into the light (*lucem*). For Diana, Juno, and Lucina all signify the same thing <note>These goddesses were often conflated in Roman religion as protectors of childbirth</note>. But we must now come to the familiar structure of the text. ¶ The Order: I, Macer...

<summary>This opening page introduces Artemisia (Mugwort) as the "Mother of Herbs," discussing its etymological origins linked to both Queen Artemisia of Caria and the goddess Diana, while emphasizing its primary use in women's reproductive health.</summary>
<keywords>Artemisia, Mugwort, Diana, Artemisia of Caria, Mausoleum, women's health, herbal medicine, Macer Floridus, botany, Seven Wonders of the World</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the grades of medicinal potency and begins a glossary of pharmaceutical terms and measurements, citing classical and medieval authorities.</meta>

...a manifest impediment should arise. ————
However, it reaches the point of corrupting and destroying <note>literally "mortifying" or causing tissue death</note>. ¶ And the fourth degree is that it reaches the point of destroying and corrupting. These are the words of Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (980–1037), a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was the standard textbook in Europe for centuries</note>. Furthermore, whoever desires a more exact determination regarding the degrees of medicines should diligently study the *Dialogues on Difficult Physical Matters* by Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples <note>original: "Jacobi fabri stapulensis," a French humanist and theologian</note> and the booklet of Geraldus de Solo <note>a 14th-century master of medicine at Montpellier</note> on the *New Almansor* <note>a commentary on the work of the Persian physician Al-Razi</note>. ¶ And because every discipline has peculiar terms which frequently occur to those reading it, I wish to clarify several terms of this little work here, so that it is not necessary to repeat the same things many times. For the sake of brevity, I have decided to subjoin these common verses for the explanation of those same terms:

-> Then you make a **poultice** <term>cathaplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbs applied to the body</term> when you apply juice and herbs. <-
-> A pure binding of juices is called an **epithem** <term>epythoma: a liquid medication applied via a cloth</term>. <-
-> ¶ **Sachets** <term>sacellas: small bags filled with dry medicinal ingredients</term> are medicines in a full bag. <-
-> If you receive smoke, it is called **fumigation**. <-
-> Any hard preparation is called a **plaster** <term>emplastrum</term>. <-
-> **Fomentation** <term>fomantatio</term> alone is done with soaked sponges. <-
-> Oil and wax with spices make an **ointment** <term>unguentum</term>. <-

These are the words which physicians particularly use in administering medicines. ¶ And because physicians arrange their medications under a certain dose—that is, by weight and measure—it would not be idle to subjoin here the varieties of weights and measures. And because, as we have said, our author described his work for the most part from Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the 1st-century "Natural History"</note>, therefore Pliny's own words concerning measures and weights must be commemorated from Book 27, Chapter 34. And since in measures and weights Greek names must frequently be used, we shall place the interpretation of them in this place once and for all.

<sig>a iij</sig>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on pharmaceutical measurements and the methodology of botanical illustration, likely following a general introduction to medical practice.</meta>

<unclear>.....</unclear> Attic <note>original: "attica"; referring to the ancient Greek standard of weights used in Athens</note> (for physicians almost always use the Attic observation) has the weight of a silver denarius. This same unit also equals the weight of six obols. An obol equals ten chalchoi <note>original: "calchos"; the smallest copper coin/weight</note>. A cyathus <note>a liquid measure, roughly a small ladle</note> weighs ten drachmas by itself. The mina, which our people call the *mna*, weighs one hundred Attic drachmas. These are the words of Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist and author of Natural History</note>. ¶ However, more recent physicians use these names for measures and weights: A scruple <note>original: "scrupulus"</note>, which is equal in weight to twenty grains of wheat. Three scruples make a drachma. ¶ The *aureus*, the *solidum*, and the *exagium* <note>various names for gold coins used as weight standards</note> differ only in name; each weighs one and a half drachmas. ¶ Nine drachmas constitute an ounce <note>original: "vncias"</note>. A pound <note>original: "libra"</note>, however, is made up of sixteen ounces. A *cotyla* is a weight of nine ounces. An *hemina* is half of a *sextarius*; and a *sextarius* is two and a half pounds. A *cyathus* is the weight of one and a half ounces. A *siliqua* <note>the seed of the carob tree</note> is the weight of four grains. An obol is the weight of twelve grains.

As for why the pictures of the herbs have not been represented here, I shall bring forward Pliny’s reasoning from his twenty-fifth book, second chapter: "Some have painted likenesses of herbs and inscribed their effects beneath them. But a picture is deceptive because of the numerous colors, especially when attempting to rival nature; and the varying skill of copyists leads to great degeneration. Furthermore, it is not enough to paint them at a single stage of life, since they change their appearance with the fourfold variations of the year." Thus says Pliny.

But do not let it deter you from further reading, excellent young men, if by chance something has been tasted beforehand that does not immediately charm your minds. For the remaining things I shall set forth with such a familiar interpretation that even young boys just learning their letters may easily grasp them; and I have followed them in such an order that I have subjected the text to the familiar order of the alphabet.

The author concludes by encouraging students with the promise of a simple, alphabetically organized text.</summary>
<keywords>weights and measures, pharmacy, Pliny the Elder, botanical illustration, medical education, drachma, scruple, Attic standard</keywords>

<meta>This page begins a treatise on the medicinal properties of herbs, starting with Artemisia (Mugwort). It establishes the mythological and historical authorities for the text.</meta>

# Page 16

As I am about to speak—that is, wishing to describe the virtues—meaning the powers and certain properties—of some herbs, I think—that is, I judge—it to be just—meaning consistent with law and reason—to set down—that is, to describe—first, in the first place, the Mother of Herbs. For it is just that a mother be preferred over her children. To this herb, the Greek language gave—that is, bestowed—a name, namely *Artemisia*. Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and childbirth, known as Artemis in Greek.</note> is said—that is, reported—to have been the first to discover the aid—meaning the strengths and properties—of that herb. Since Diana is called *Artemis* in Greek, the herb holds—that is, retains—its name from her for the reason that Artemis herself, namely Diana, is called the discoverer of this herb.

This herb heals—that is, assists—chiefly, by a certain special nature, the diseases of women—that is, of females—as will be clarified hereafter. A decoction <note>A liquid preparation made by boiling the herb in water.</note> of this herb, when taken, draws down—that is, provokes—the menses, which are the bloody superfluities of women. 

¶ The menses are indeed named from "month" <note>Latin: *mensis*</note> because women are accustomed to emit these flows through their private parts every single month, unless they have not yet reached the age of childbearing, or have already passed it, or have become pregnant. 

¶ For the menses occur in women, as Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire (c. 129–216 AD).</note> testifies, by the intention of generative nature. ¶ Therefore, if the menses do not fail to reach a just quantity, or do not exceed it, they are causes of health; but if they either fail or exceed, they are causes of sickness. Whence Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> says: 

¶ When the menses occur in too great a quantity, illnesses happen; and when they do not occur, illnesses happen from the womb <note>Latin: *matrice*</note>. 

But let us return to the literal text. The decoction—that is, the water from the boiling of this herb—draws down the menses if they have not flowed at the time appointed by nature; and this herb does this—that is, draws down the menses—if the womb—that is, that member in women appointed for generation—is fomented—that is, warmed with a medicinal compress—of the same herb.

<summary>This page introduces Artemisia (Mugwort) as the "Mother of Herbs," explaining its mythological origins via the goddess Diana and its primary medical use in regulating menstruation. It cites the classical authorities Galen and Hippocrates to explain the necessity of balanced menstrual health.</summary>

<keywords>Artemisia, Mugwort, Diana, Artemis, Galen, Hippocrates, women's health, menstruation, decoction, womb, herbal medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the magical and medicinal properties of herbs, specifically focusing on Artemisia (Mugwort) and its power against enchantments and venomous creatures.</meta>

They rely solely on experiments and affirm that whoever <gloss>that is, anyone who has tasted that herb</gloss> cannot be harmed by any "medicine," that is, by any magical incantation <note>The author is clarifying that 'medicamen' can mean both a cure and a magical potion or spell</note>, which is why they are called enchanted herbs. **Ovid**, in the seventh book of the *Metamorphoses*:
> Having been trusted, he immediately received the enchanted herbs. These are also called medicines.

**The same author**, a little later, concerning the sorceress Circe:
> So great is the power of medicines.

**Therefore**, medicines are called both "good" and "bad"; here the term is used for magical incantations. Hence **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work was the primary source for herbal medicine in the Middle Ages</note> says: **Artemisia** <term>Artemisia: commonly known as Mugwort</term> puts demons to flight when placed in a house; it even prevents "evil medicines" <note>original: "maledicamenta," referring to curses or harmful spells</note> and turns away the "evil eye." Thus says **Dioscorides**. And some affirm that no wild beast <gloss>that is, ferocious animals such as lions, leopards, and wolves</gloss> will seek to bite him <gloss>that is, will not sink its teeth into one who has tasted that herb</gloss>. The root of that herb, when hung from the neck, is said to resist <gloss>that is, to fight against "rubetae," which are the injuries arising from the bites of bramble-toads</gloss>. (**The bramble-toad** <term>rubeta: a type of venomous toad often mentioned in classical toxicology</term> is a type of toad, the most dangerous of all, full of poisons. **Juvenal** <note>A Roman satirical poet</note> says:
> **She** mixes the bramble-toad while her husband is watching.

**The same author** says: I have never inspected the entrails of a toad. And it is also said to resist <gloss>that is, to heal all harmful frogs or "noctuae" <note>The glossator seems to confuse frogs with owls (noctuae), or perhaps refers to night-creatures</note>: there are many kinds of them; there are land frogs, though they live in fresh water, and there are sea frogs which are called *ololygones* <note>A Greek term for a croaking marsh-frog or a specific sea-creature</note></gloss>). The juice of this herb, drunk with wine, will be beneficial for this purpose—namely, for the bite of toads. **If** the fresh, green herb itself is crushed into must <gloss>that is, newly pressed wine</gloss>, it produces a healing remedy <gloss>that is, it makes an excellent medicine for the aforementioned ailments</gloss>, and both the taste and the smell are most pleasant <gloss>that is, quite delightful</gloss> in that new wine so tempered. The wine taken in this way strengthens a weak stomach and one that digests poorly, and it heals the "precordia" <gloss>that is, diseases of the internal organs</gloss>.

It specifically details the herb's use as an antidote to the venom of the "rubeta" (bramble-toad) and its benefits for digestion when mixed with wine.</summary>

...proceeding from coldness, and they affirm that it is useful for many other causes—that is, for many ailments. <note>The author clarifies that "cause" is often used as a synonym for "disease" in medical texts.</note> A cause is frequently substituted for the disease itself: since a disease cannot be cured while the cause creating it remains, as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; his theories dominated Western medicine for over a thousand years.</note> says.

## ¶ Concerning the herb Southernwood. II.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of the herb "abrotanum" (Southernwood). The plant features a single upright stem with several pairs of pinnately lobed or serrated leaves. At the tips of the main stem and some branches are small, rounded clusters of flowers or seeds. The plant emerges from a thick, curved root system. The entire illustration is enclosed within a simple double-line rectangular frame.</image-desc>

The third degree of heat is assigned to Southernwood <note>original: "abrotano"; in medieval humorism, plants were graded 1–4 based on their perceived "temperature" and "moisture."</note>:
And the seed is more intensely drying than the herb itself:
Thus it aids the nerves and all conditions of the chest,
If a decoction <term>decoction: a concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling a substance</term> of it is taken.
So also it benefits those with difficulty breathing <note>original: "dispnotcis," referring to dyspnea</note>: and it repels a cough.
It is beneficial for the loins: and likewise for complaints of the womb.

> This food, consumed raw with wine, will be beneficial;
> Drunk, it clears the urine. Thus it also cleanses the internal organs,
> Thus it cures sciatica <note>original: "sciasim," referring to hip or sciatic nerve pain</note>. Thus taken, it clears the menstrual flow.
> It puts serpents to flight with its scent, and when drunk,
> It extinguishes their poisons. It also drives off the chills of a fever
> If it is taken mixed with water before the fever arrives,
> Or if you anoint the limbs with the oil in which it is boiled.
> By drinking this often, you will kill worms in the belly.
> By joining to this the crumb of a loaf of bread and quinces <note>original: "mala cytonia"</note>,
> Boiling them together in water: apply the cooked mixture to the pain
> Of the eyes, or to an inflammation; it will cure both.
> It draws out embedded splinters and thorns
> When applied by itself, or if animal fat is joined to it.
> As Pliny writes, this herb stirs up desire <note>original: "venerem," referring to Venus or sexual desire</note>
> If it is merely placed under the pillow at night.
> And drunk, it resists all things harmful to love.

¶ The third [degree] for Southernwood, etc. Pliny calls this herb *Abrotanum* <note>Southernwood, a flowering plant in the wormwood family</note>. He embraces its many powers in a few words. He says Southernwood is useful for the nerves, for a cough, for orthopnea <note>term: orthopnoias — a condition where one can only breathe comfortably while standing or sitting upright</note>, for convulsions, for ruptures, for the loins, and for difficulties of urine; in which [descriptions] the author <note>likely referring to Macer Floridus</note> differs very little.
The third degree of heat and of desiccation (that is, dryness) is assigned to Southernwood; that is, Southernwood is hot and dry in the third degree. But the seed is more burning (that is, hotter) than the herb itself. Since it is hot and dry in the third degree, it aids the nerves; that is, it cures ailments of the nerves, namely paralysis <note>original: "paralisim"</note>, numbness, and bruising of the muscles and nerves. For paralysis is a softening and relaxation of the nerves. And it aids, he says, in all cases (that is, diseases) of the chest (for that is what it means among physicians)...

Now, the "scia" is the joint of the haunch or hip, from which the "sciatic" disease gets its name; it is a type of arthritis which is commonly called "the gout" <note>original: "gutta"</note>. Southernwood taken in this way purges the menses—that is, the bloody superfluities of women. It purges—that is, eliminates—sweat; its smoke <note>original: "fumigo"</note> drives away serpents, and when drunk, it extinguishes—that is, causes to vanish—poisons, meaning the deadly power of those serpents’ venom. It also settles the "chills"—that is, the rigors or shivers—of a fever before the fever comes on, if it is taken mixed with water. Or, if you anoint the limbs with oil in which it has been boiled, you will kill "lumbricos"—that is, worms in the intestines produced by phlegm gathered there. Southernwood itself extracts—that is, it draws out—stalks—that is, splinters and thorns—that are stuck in, if it is applied—that is, placed—by itself—that is, alone—

if fat—that is, grease—is joined to it. This herb moves—that is, arouses—desire <note>original: "venerem," referring to Venus, the goddess of love and sexual desire</note> as Pliny writes, if it has been placed—that is, put—only under a pillow—that is, a cushion; and the herb itself, when drunk, resists—that is, fights against—all harmful—that is, poisonous—injuries to desire—that is, those things which could inhibit the sexual act.
Pliny, Book 21, Chapter 21: They say that if a branch of southernwood is placed under a pillow, desire is stimulated. And that the herb is most effective against all sorceries <note>original: "veneficia," referring here to "impotence spells" or "ligatures"</note> by which intercourse <note>original: "coitus"</note> is inhibited.

¶ **In the first [degree] of heat, etc.** This is a definition of the herb Wormwood, which is a most common <note>original: "vulgarissime"</note> but nonetheless most excellent plant. Concerning which **Pliny** in Book 27, Chapter 7, says it is appropriate to speak of its use: "it is indeed a most accessible herb and among the few most useful," the benefits of which follow. The **heat** of wormwood is said to be in the **first degree**, and its **dry power**—that is, its dryness—is said to be in the **second**; that is, it is hot in the first degree and dry in the second. The **herb**—that is, its leaves—**corroborate**—that is, they comfort and restore—a weak **stomach**, which namely does not digest food well. **Also**—that is, in whatever **manner**—**one**—that is, any person—takes that herb. But it strengthens the stomach, [supply the word] **better**—that is...

<meta>This page continues the detailed medicinal properties of Wormwood (Absinthium). It specifically focuses on preparation methods involving rainwater and the addition of Spikenard to treat digestive and gynecological issues, citing the authorities Dioscorides and Galen.</meta>

the benefit is greater if the herb itself is boiled in the liquor of rain, that is, in **rainwater**. For as the author **Dioscorides** <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> states, 
<margin>concerning rainwater</margin>
rainwater is the best, lightest, and sweetest among all waters, and it is more easily digested. And if the rainwater itself in which the wormwood has been boiled is cooled—that is, reduced to coldness—**under the open sky** <note>original: "sub diuo," meaning beneath the open air</note>. For the ancients used the terms *dium* or *divum* to refer to the light of day beneath the heavens. It will be able to assist—that is, to help—various and diverse diseases of the **stomach**. The herb itself, [prepared] for this ailment—that is, boiled with rainwater—drives out or expels **intestinal worms** <note>original: "lumbricos"</note> from their slippery nature; and it softens—that is, loosens the slipperiness—making the **bowels**, that is the belly, [move]. And taken in this way, it calms excessive—that is, violent—pain of the belly, namely its cramps. When taken, supplementally as a drink, it draws forth—that is, leads far out—and **provokes urine**; it releases—that is, brings out—the **menses**, which are the monthly purgings of women. This occurs if you join—that is, combine—with it **nard**, that is, **spikenard** <note>original: "spicam," an aromatic plant often imported from the East or Gaul</note>, that aromatic spice which Gaul produces; and this is called Celtic or Roman spikenard. For three types of spikenard are found: 
<margin>How many species of Spikenard</margin>
one which is called Indian spikenard <note>original: "spica nardi"</note>, another Celtic or Roman, and another Mountain spikenard. However, the Celtic variety is weaker than the others, except in the provocation of urine, as **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire</note> testifies. If, therefore, you join nard to it—that is, Celtic spikenard—and if you grind—that is, crush—them together (namely the wormwood and the spikenard) and dissolve them once ground—that is, after they have been crushed—in **honey-water** <note>original: "mulsa," a mixture of honey and water or wine</note>, you will especially—that is, singularly—purge the menses with such a drink or potion. Also—that is, even—the herb itself, applied beneath to the genital members of women <note>likely as a pessary</note> with soft—that is, gentle—wool. If **hartwort** <note>original: "siler montanus," also known as laserwort</note>, which is called by another name *siselos*, which is an herb...

This occurs when the stomach is clearly weighed down by too great a heap of foods; it desires to vomit up that gluttonous burden and cannot. You will therefore drive off disgust which is harmful—that is, detrimental—to the body: [you do this] by such a drink—that is, a potion—for nothing is more ruinous to human health than immoderate satiety. ¶ **"If you join the blades of rue"** and so on. If you join to this herb <note>Wormwood</note> the blades—that is, the leaves—of **rue** <note>original: "rute"; a bitter herb often used in ancient medicine for its stimulant and antispasmodic properties</note>, about which a discussion will be held a little later, and a sufficient quantity of salt and pepper, and pounding them—that is, crushing them—you give them with **pure wine** <note>original: "mero"; wine not diluted with water</note>—that is, you offer it to drink—either this way or **boiled**—that is, if it has been boiled with pure wine, salt, and pepper. And if you give a drink of this kind, the harmful and **raw humor**—that is, undigested—of the stomach will be purged. This herb, when joined with **iris root** <note>original: "yri radix"; Orris root, the rhizome of certain iris species, often used as an expectorant</note>, which will be treated later, if swallowed, aids—that is, benefits—the **chest** wonderfully—that is, in a marvelous way—namely, for diseases of the chest such as a cough, asthma, and others of that kind. Anyone who rubs themselves with the herb itself, rubbed—that is, crushed—with **sharp wine**—that is, vinegar—need not fear **gnats** <note>original: "culices"; likely referring to mosquitoes or biting midges</note>—that is, the attacks of that most tiny little beast.

<margin>What is a gnat?</margin>
Now, a **gnat** is a tiny winged worm, about which **Virgil** wrote a small work for **Octavius** <note>Referring to the "Culex," a poem traditionally attributed to a young Virgil and dedicated to the future Emperor Augustus</note>, which is titled *The Gnat*; just as **Ovid** wrote a little book about the **flea** <note>A reference to the "De Pulice," a poem often attributed to Ovid in the Middle Ages, though now considered a later medieval creation</note>. And gnats are put to flight—that is, driven off and exterminated—by the scent of that herb when it is burned. You shall give or administer this herb raw and crushed—that is, its liquid—associated—or mixed—with **celery**—the juice of celery, about which will be spoken below—to those with **jaundice** <note>original: "yctericis"; those suffering from "icterus" or yellowing of the skin and eyes</note>, about which a discussion was held a little while ago.

<margin>What is an obstruction?</margin>
It cures the **liver**—that is, an **obstruction** of the liver. An **obstruction** <note>original: "opilatio"; a common term in medieval medicine for a blockage or "stuffing up" of an organ's passages</note> is a disease in some limb or organ that prevents the passage of good or superfluous humors which usually pass through it naturally. If **Gallic nard**—that is, **Celtic spikenard**—is joined to it, that herb, when drunk, will also aid the **spleen**—that is, the hardness or obstruction of the spleen. I say to do this with vinegar—that is, joined with sharp wine. And when taken in this way, it drives off the deadly poison of wine and of **mushrooms**.

<margin>What is a mushroom?</margin>
**Mushrooms** <note>original: "fungi"</note> are certain tuberous and spongy superfluities which the excessive moisture of the earth or of trees produces. And of these, **Galen** <note>A Greek physician of the 2nd century AD whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium</note> says there are...

¶ The vapor of the decoction, etc. Because: that is, the vapor, smoke, or fumigation of that herb decocted—that is, boiled—liberates, that is, opens obstructed ears. Hence pus in the ear. What quinsy is.

For pus flows from the ears when ulcers form in the ear because of sharp **humors** <note>Fluids in the body believed to regulate health</note> descending from the brain through the winding passages of hearing. That herb joined—that is, combined—with honey and **nitre** <note>original: "nitro"; a mineral salt often called "baurach" (borax/natron) used as a cleansing agent</note>, which is a type of salt, assists **quinsy** <note>original: "angine"; a severe inflammation or abscess of the throat</note>, that is, an abscess arising below the throat. That crushed herb benefits fresh wounds—that is, recently made ones—and cures ulcers—that is, infected wounds of the head—if it is applied on top in the manner of a plaster. Furthermore, its decoction aids **itching**, that is, irritations of the skin caused by bile or salty phlegm that stings the skin, when used as a fomentation—that is, a warm wash—of that area.

<margin>
Hence seasickness.

For **Hippocrates** <note>The famous ancient Greek "Father of Medicine"</note> says and declares that motion disturbs bodies. It is also said—that is, asserted—that the herb itself, if it is worn wrapped in a linen cloth, restrains—that is, controls—swelling—that is, inflation—of the **groin**, that is, the male member. <page-num>Fol: 12</page-num>

...arises from windy matter <note>In medieval medicine, "windy" (ventuosa) referred to gas or "vapors" trapped in the body</note> entering the genital member, or a tumor arising from foul intercourse, or any other cause; or perhaps an **abscess** <note>original: "apostema"; a localized collection of pus</note> which forms in the **emunctory** <note>original: "emunctorio"; an organ or body part that carries off waste, in this case referring to the lymph nodes</note> of the liver, which is commonly called the groin. **Wormwood** <note>original: "Absinthium"</note> smelled—that is, its scent—provides sleep; or if the herb itself is placed under the head of a sick person and the sick person does not sense it—that is, does not perceive it being placed under him—it provides—that is, induces—sleep. The ash of this herb blackens—that is, it turns into blackness—the hair—that is, grey hairs—if it is well mixed with a **cerate** <note>original: "ceroto"; a stiff medical ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal ingredients, firmer than a plaster</note>.
<margin>What a cerate is</margin>
And it is often done by him who desires blackness of hair, having anointed the head (it is understood: often with that cerate). It renders a chest safe—that is, immune from those **moths** <note>original: "tineis"; small worms or larvae that eat clothing</note>, those little worms—in which something is stored—that is, put away. This herb, ground with honey, cures tumors—that is, an abscess formed under the tongue. And also the same medicine will purge—that is, cure—a **bruise** <note>original: "liuorem"; a dark or lead-colored mark</note>—that is, a dark spot made from a blow—which bruising usually appears around the orb—that is, the roundness—of the light—that is, the eye.

**A**way it drives the ringing of the ear, when mixed with ox gall <note>The bile of an ox, used in various historical medicines</note>.
**D**issolving the hardness of the spleen, its **poultice** <note>original: "cathaplasma"</note> works well.
**C**ombined with honey, the herb is placed underneath <note>Referring to a pessary or suppository</note>.
**D**rying it out, it forces the **menses** <note>Menstrual flow</note> to flow.
**E**xcellent for the stomach, if the green herb is cooked in oil.
**N**ot a little it helps, when that area is bathed in this ointment.

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of medicinal herbs, transitioning from the previous page's focus on Calamint to the properties and lore of Absinthium, commonly known as Wormwood. It addresses both practical uses for preservation and medical treatments for the ears, spleen, and digestive system.</meta>

Dissolve your **ink** <note>original: "Incaustum"; a term for writing fluid, often made from iron gall or soot</note> in wine in which this herb has been soaked;
If you write on paper with this, the mouse will no longer gnaw upon it.
**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author who wrote the encyclopedic Naturalis Historia</note> extols this herb with great praise,
And records that the Romans, according to custom on sacred days,
While they were accustomed to compete in the chariot races,
Were used to giving the juice of **Wormwood** <note>original: "Absinthij" / Absinthium</note> to be drunk
By the winner stationed upon the Capitoline seat,
Believing they were offering a reward worthy above all others
To him, so that he might be able to preserve firm health,
Which stands as the most precious prize of all the world.

<margin>On the ringing of the ears.</margin>
¶ The Ear, etc. It drives away sound, that is, the **ringing of the ears** <note>original: "tinnitū auriū"; modernly known as tinnitus</note> (which occurs from vaporous and thick air trapped within the instruments of hearing). Therefore, this herb mixed with ox gall <note>original: "felle bouis"; the bile of an ox, frequently used in medieval medicine to "cut through" blockages</note> does not <note>The text says "non," though in many similar medical recipes, this is a positive recommendation; it may imply "not only" or be a scribal variation</note> drive away the ringing of the ears; a **plaster** <note>original: "cataplasma"; a soft, moist mass of herbs applied to the body</note>: what this is has been explained above. This herb dissolves and cures the **hardness of the spleen** <note>original: "duriciem splenis"</note> (which occurs from a drossy **melancholic humor** <note>In the medieval theory of the Four Humors, melancholy or "black bile" was associated with the spleen; if it became "feculent" or drossy, it was thought to cause physical hardening or tumors</note> which, being unable to pass through the pores assigned to it by nature, remains obstructed).

<margin>Whence the tumor and hardness of the spleen</margin>
If this herb is placed underneath—that is, applied as a suppository—to the **womb** <note>original: "stere" / "matrici"; the matrix or uterus</note>, and I say this with honey joined to it, it will dry it—that is, it will purge the womb of its impurity—and it will compel—that is, force—the **menses** <note>original: "mēstrua"</note>—that is, the menstrual superfluities—to flow. If the green herb itself is cooked in oil—that is, with oil—it will help the **stomach** <note>The author adds a grammatical aside noting that "stomachum" is used here in the neuter gender, though usually masculine</note> not a little, but rather very much; the stomach will be soothed by this ointment. If you write—that is, if you wish to write a document—dilute, dissolve, or temper your **ink** <note>original: "atramentum"</note> with wine in which this herb has been so macerated—that is, soaked or steeped.

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal and practical uses of Wormwood (Absinthium). It concludes the section on Wormwood’s history in Roman ritual and begins a new chapter on the Nettle (Urtica).</meta>

That long-lived worm <gloss>referring to the bookworm or larvae that eat parchment</gloss> will no longer wear down or consume that paper as it once did. **Pliny** <note>original: "Pli."; Pliny the Elder, author of Natural History, who was often cited as a medical authority in the Middle Ages</note>, that most illustrious physician, extols this herb with great praise, and reports that the Romans—when according to the ancient custom of the **Olympiads** <note>original: "olimpiarum"; although a Greek tradition, the author conflates it with Roman festivals</note> they were accustomed to compete (that is, they used to engage in mock battles in honor of the gods) in the racing of **quadrigas** <note>original: "quadrigarum"; a chariot drawn by four horses</note> (that is, of chariots).

¶ Moreover, they called such contests "Olympic" because they were celebrated every fourth year; and thus it came about that an **Olympiad** <term>Olympias: a period of four years used as a unit of time in ancient chronology</term> once designated a space of four years, such as the first Olympiad, the second, and so on for the rest. He reports, therefore, that the Romans were accustomed, as a sign of extraordinary honor, to give the juice of **Wormwood** to be drunk by the victor: that is, to the one who had achieved a distinguished victory in that contest, stationed <gloss>that is, placed</gloss> upon the **Capitoline seat**.

<margin>
What the 
**Capitol** 
is
</margin>

(The **Capitol** <note>original: "Capitolium"</note> among the ancient Romans was a temple consecrated to **Jupiter** in which whatever was held in high value in Rome was kept. It was called the "Capitol" because when its foundations were being dug, a **human head** <note>original: "humanum caput"; according to legend, the head of a man named Tolus was found, giving the hill the name Caput-Tolius</note> was found). Thus they gave the juice of Wormwood to be drunk, believing they were giving a reward worthy above all others to that victor. By this reward—namely, the drinking of the juice of Wormwood—he might be able to preserve firm health: that is, physical health, which is known to be more precious than all else. Whence Pliny says concerning Wormwood: *"Furthermore, it is celebrated particularly in the sacred rites of the Roman people. For indeed, during the Latin Festivals, four-horse chariots compete at the Capitol, and the victor drinks wormwood. I believe health is given as a reward, the ancestors having judged it an honorable prize."*

### ¶ Regarding the Nettle, the fourth herb.

**Wormwood**, **Pliny**, **Capitol**, **Olympiad**, **Chariot**, **Rome**, **Nettle**, **Health**

We call it the **Nettle** <note>original: "vticam"</note>; the Greek calls it ***acalyphe*** <note>original: "acalife"; from the Greek akalēphē</note>.
A quite intense burning power is said to be within it,
Whence it seems to have taken its name not without merit,
Since the **nettle** <note>original: "vtica"; likely derived from the Latin "urere," meaning to burn</note>, when handled, burns the fingers of the one holding it.
This herb is accustomed to help those with **jaundice** <term>yctericos: people suffering from jaundice, a yellowing of the skin and eyes traditionally linked to bile</term> when it is taken with wine.
Its seed, with honey, provides a remedy for those with **colic** <note>original: "colicis"; refers to severe, fluctuating pain in the abdomen</note>.
It also cures an old cough, if it is drunk frequently;
It drives away "coldness" of the lungs and swelling of the belly.
For all these ailments, its powder joined with honey is beneficial,
Or the green juice, swallowed quite often with wine.

A plaster made from its leaves with salt
Is beneficial for ulcers and cleanses foul wounds;
And this plaster is effective against dog bites,
And it cures cancers, **parotid swellings** <note>original: "parotidas"; swellings of the salivary glands, often associated with mumps</note>, and dislocations.
Flesh that has moved away from the bone is restored to its place,
And this is accustomed to dry up harmful **humors** <term>humores: the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) whose balance was thought to determine health</term>.
The crushed root of **Arum** <note>original: "Ari"; likely *Arum maculatum*, known as Cuckoo-pint</note> with wine suppresses
Swelling of the spleen; thus it helps **gout** <note>original: "podagre"; a painful form of arthritis often affecting the big toe</note>,
And thus it helps all diseases of the joints.
For all these, it is beneficial if added to them or joined with them,
If you cook this in oil and bathe the limbs with this ointment.

¶ We have spoken of the **Nettle** <note>original: "vrticam"</note>, etc. Our author now approaches the herb whose name is **Nettle** in Latin, but in Greek is ***acalyphe*** <note>original: "acalife"</note>. Regarding this, **Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> writes in Book 22, Chapter 13: "What can be more just [useful] than the Nettle?" But that herb, besides the oil that we said is made from it in Egypt, overflows with many remedies, which indeed our author describes. Understand that we Latins call it "Nettle," while the Greek says it has a "fervent power" <note>original: "calife vis feruida"</note>; that is, it is not moderately hot, but rather excessively so. For it is hot in the third degree and dry in the second <note>Medieval medicine categorized plants on a scale of 1 to 4 based on their "primary qualities"</note>. For that reason, because it is of intense heat, it does not seem to have taken its name without merit—that is, without a legitimate cause—because it burns <note>original: "vrat"; the Latin name *urtica* is related to *urere*, to burn</note> in the manner of a fire burning the fingers of the person holding it.

There is, moreover, a fish which they name the "nettle," having received its name from the similarity of its sting.
<margin>

name for 
jaundice is
borrowed.
</margin>
For when it feels a hand moved toward it, it contracts, biting and stinging just like a nettle. This herb—that is, its juice or a decoction taken with wine—is accustomed to help those seized by **jaundice** <term>yctericos: those suffering from a yellowing of the skin</term>. That disease is named after a bird which they call the ***icterus*** <note>The Golden Oriole; ancient lore claimed that looking at this yellow bird would draw the jaundice out of a patient</note>. <meta>This page continues the prose commentary on the Nettle (Urtica), specifically explaining the medicinal applications mentioned in the previous verses. <margin>For colic and iliac suffering.</margin>
Taken with honey, it heals—that is, it helps—**colic** and **iliac** [suffering]: that is, the sickness which doctors call "colic," which causes distress (among other infirmities of the intestines, there is one called the **colic passion** <note>original: "colica passio"; severe abdominal pain centered in the colon</note> and another called **iliac**, named after the two intestines called the colon and the ileum). These types of sufferings occur in the human body because of some obstacle that prevents the feces and things enclosed in the intestines from having an exit, from which an almost unbearable pain is inflicted. (And this herb cures a chronic cough—that is, 

<margin>The function of the lung.</margin>
a recurring one—if it is drunk often). That same **Nettle** <note>original: "vrtica"</note> drives out cold from the lung (the function of which is to vent the heart and to expel its soot or vapors). It 

<margin>The cause of belly swelling.</margin>
drives out, as they say, the tumor—that is, the swelling of the belly (which occurs from thick and abundant gas trapped in the hollow of the stomach, stretching its linings). Its powder—that is, its seed reduced to a powder and mixed, or rather joined and blended with honey—is beneficial for all these aforementioned sicknesses. Or its juice—that is, its green or fresh liquid—taken frequently with wine. A **plaster** <term>cataplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbs, often heated, applied to the skin to treat aching or inflamed parts</term> made from its leaves with salt is good for ulcers and cleanses foul wounds—that is, poisonous, corrosive, and deceitful ones—and this plaster is effective against dog bites (that is, from dogs). The herb itself, bound or boiled, cures **cancers** <note>original: "cancros"</note>—that is, malignant and corrosive abscesses which 

<margin>What a cancer is. What parotids are.</margin>
arise from sharp **black bile** <term>melancolia: one of the four vital humors; in excess, it was thought to cause "cold" and "dry" diseases like cancer or depression</term>. They are so named because when any part of the body is contaminated by this sickness, it creeps into neighboring parts in the manner of a crab (the animal), or because it gradually eats away at the flesh. (And it cures **parotid swellings** <note>original: "perotidas"; swellings of the glands near the ear</note>, which are abscesses that usually occur under the ears; hence "parotids" is from *para*, which is "near," and *ous/ota*, the ear). Flesh that has detached—that is, been removed from the bone—is restored: that is, renewed...

# Fol. 43

[...is healed] by this **plaster** <term>cataplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbs, often heated, applied to the skin to treat aching or inflamed parts</term>, and also this plaster is accustomed to dry out: that is, to dry up the humors, namely the harmful moistures of ulcers; for ulcers are not healed, says **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was the most influential physician of antiquity; his theory of the four humors was the standard for over a thousand years.</note>, unless they are perfectly dried out. The root of this herb, crushed with sharp wine: that is, vinegar, 

<margin>The function of the spleen.</margin>
restrains—that is, cures—the tumor—that is, the swelling—of the **spleen**, that organ whose function it is to receive the drossy superfluity of the blood which is called **melancholy** <note>original: "melencolia"; literally "black bile," one of the four bodily humors believed to cause various ailments if it became excessive.</note>. 

<margin>Gout</margin>
And that root, so crushed, helps **gout** <note>original: "podagra"</note>, that suffering of the feet whose cause is a raw humor descending to the lower parts of the body; and so it assists all diseases of the joints—that is, arthritic conditions. For from the variety of the limbs, arthritic diseases (likewise known as **the gout** <note>original: "gutte"; literally "drops," from the medieval belief that disease "dropped" from the humors into the joints</note>) receive various names. For if the harmful humor occupies the hands, it is called **chiragra**; if the feet, **podagra**; if the hip, **sciatica**. This herb or its root, applied to or joined with them, is beneficial for all these arthritic diseases if you cook—that is, boil down—this herb in oil—that is, with oil—and warm—that is, treat—the limbs with this ointment: that is, an unguent.

> Put into the nostrils, the juice or the leaf draws out blood. 
> Such a great force of heat is within it to help. 
> But if you wish to hold back flowing blood, 
> Smear the forehead with the juice of the Nettle; it drives it back. 
> If it is applied as a suppository with myrrh, it drives out the menses. 
> If the womb <term>matrix: the uterus or womb</term>, weighed down by humor, closes the vulva, 
> It will be restored if rubbed with Nettle leaves. 
> Drunk with wine, its seed stirs up **desire** <note>original: "venerem"; referring to Venus, the goddess of love, here meaning sexual appetite.</note>; 
> And it is better if you join honey and pepper to the crushed seeds, 
> And if it is drunk with wine in the aforementioned manner.

...it will be able to soothe <gloss>that is, to pacify</gloss> the swelling <gloss>that is, a large **abscess** <term>ap̄am: a contraction of 'apostema', a medieval term for an inflammatory swelling</term></gloss> of the **uvula** <note>original: "vue"</note>. The uvula is a small membrane, not devoid of flesh, of a smooth, round, and oblong shape. It was created by the providence of nature above the head of the **windpipe** <term>canne pulmonis: literally the 'reed of the lung', referring to the trachea</term>, preventing food or anything harmful from entering the **chamber of the chest** <term>clibanum pectoris: literally 'the oven of the chest', a metaphor for the warmth and enclosure of the thoracic cavity</term>. It is also wisely shaped for forming the modulations of the human voice, acting like a finger at the opening of a flute.

The herb itself, boiled in oil <gloss>that is, with oil</gloss>, induces <gloss>that is, provokes</gloss> sweat in those who are anointed <gloss>that is, those who smear themselves with this ointment</gloss>. If any four-footed female animal <gloss>that is, any brute beast</gloss> refuses <gloss>that is, declines</gloss> to receive the male <gloss>that is, to undergo mating</gloss>, let her **vulva** <gloss>that is, her genital organ</gloss> be rubbed <gloss>that is, agitated</gloss> with the leaves of the **nettle** <note>original: "vrtice"</note>. That sting of the nettle thus excites the natural heat of that animal so that she becomes desirous of **intercourse** <note>original: "coitus"</note>.

If you frequently anoint the head with the juice of the seed of this herb, it represses <gloss>that is, restrains</gloss> the **shedding** <gloss>that is, the falling out of hair</gloss> which they call **alopecia** <term>alopicia: a medical term for baldness, derived from the Greek word for 'fox', likely because foxes were thought to lose patches of fur</term>, as **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; his theories on the "humors" dominated medicine for centuries.</note> says. Alopecia occurs due to putrid humors corrupting the matter from which hair is made—namely, a smoky vapor released from the **body fluids** <term>chimis: refers to the 'chymes' or humors produced during digestion</term>.

The seed of that herb, collected at the time of harvest <gloss>that is, while the crops are being reaped</gloss> and dried, will be useful <gloss>that is, beneficial</gloss> for many causes <gloss>that is, ailments</gloss>. The powder of this seed joined <gloss>that is, mixed</gloss> with honey is profitable for all those conditions which have been narrated above.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a double-line rectangular frame. It depicts botanical elements of a plant, likely garlic (Scordeon). ### Garlic

**Scordeon** <note>original: "Scordeon"</note> is the Greek name for what is called **Garlic** <note>original: "allia"</note> in Latin.
Skilled **physicians** <note>original: "medici"</note> have expertly categorized its power as **hot** <note>original: "calidam"</note> and **dry** <note>original: "siccam"</note>
In the **fourth degree** <term>fourth degree: in Galenic medicine, the highest and most intense level of a quality. A substance in the fourth degree was considered extremely potent, often acting as a caustic or a powerful stimulant</term>; whether it is eaten <note>original: "mācū", likely a contraction of "manducatum"</note> or applied as an ointment,
It cures the strikes that a **serpent** <note>original: "serpens"</note> or a **scorpion** <note>original: "scorpius"</note> has inflicted.
When applied with **honey** <note>original: "melle"</note>, it also heals the bites of dogs.
And harmful **worms** <note>original: "vermes"</note> are driven away by its scent alone.
If it is boiled in **mead** <term>mulsa: a medicinal drink made from honey and water boiled together</term> to which **vinegar** <note>original: "acetum"</note> has been added,
And then drunk, it repels **intestinal worms** <note>original: "vermes ventris"</note> and **parasites** <note>original: "tineas", likely referring to tapeworms or skin-crawling pests</note>.
If you boil it in oil along with its own **braided stalk** <note>original: "reste sua"; garlic was often stored in "ropes" or braids</note>:
You will truly restore the **plague-stricken** <note>original: "pestiferos"</note> to health with this ointment.

<summary>This page introduces Garlic, identifying its Greek and Latin names and its humoral classification as "hot and dry" in the fourth degree. It lists several medicinal uses, including treatments for venomous bites, intestinal parasites, and the plague.</summary>

<keywords>garlic, herbal medicine, Galenic humors, toxicology, serpents, parasites, plague, honey, vinegar</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Garlic (Scordeon), following the introduction of its humoral properties. The text transitions from its classification into specific applications for bites, parasites, and urinary distress, maintaining the authoritative voice of medieval medical tradition.</meta>

## With this ointment, bladder pain and swelling are suppressed

## You shall heal an abraded body with the same treatment.

¶ **Scordeon** <note>original: "Scordeon"</note> is what it is called in Greek; in Latin, it is called **Garlic** <note>original: "alia"</note>. Skilled <gloss>learned</gloss> physicians have established <gloss>asserted</gloss> that it possesses a **hot** and **dry** power in the **fourth degree** <term>fourth degree: the highest level of intensity in the Galenic system, used for substances that cause significant physiological changes like burning or blistering</term>. **Garlic**, when chewed <gloss>eaten</gloss> or applied as an ointment, cures <gloss>heals</gloss> the strikes <gloss>bites or stings</gloss> inflicted <gloss>struck</gloss> by a **serpent** or a **scorpion** (which, above all other serpents, possesses a most lethal sting). 

Garlic applied in the manner of a **plaster** <note>original: "emplastri seu cataplasmatis"</note> with **honey** heals dog bites and cures a **hoarse voice** <note>original: "vocem raucam"</note> (provided that cold was the cause of the hoarseness). Harmful <gloss>poisonous</gloss> **worms** <note>original: "vermes nocui"</note>—so named because they crawl by twisting <note>original: "vertendo"</note> themselves—are driven out <gloss>expelled</gloss> by the mere scent of garlic. 

Garlic boiled <gloss>decocted</gloss> in **mead** <note>original: "mulsa"</note> <gloss>honey-water</gloss> mixed with **vinegar**, when drunk, repels <gloss>casts out</gloss> **intestinal worms**, which are called **roundworms** <note>original: "lumbrici"</note>, **threadworms** <note>original: "ascarides"</note>, and **acrocoridines** <note>likely referring to a type of tapeworm or wart-like parasite</note>. It also repels **moths** <gloss>lice</gloss>, for these "moths" are indeed **lice** <note>original: "pediculos"</note>. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the 1st-century Roman naturalist</note> says these are born inside a person, sometimes reaching thirty feet in length and occurring in such a multitude that they cause a disease which the Greeks call **phthiriasis** <note>original: "ptiriasis"</note> and we call the **louse-disease** <note>original: "pedicularis"</note>. 

If you boil that garlic in oil with its **stalk** <note>original: "reste"</note> <gloss>with its herb, which is stretched out like a rope or cord</gloss>, you will make **pestiferous bites** <gloss>death-bringing bites</gloss> healthy again with this **ointment** <note>original: "unguine"</note>. **Pliny** states that for the bites of serpents, it is most effectively smeared on with its stalks in oil, or drunk. **Bladder pain** <note>original: "dolor vesice"</note>—that is, **strangury** <note>original: "stranguria"</note>, which is a painful difficulty in passing urine—and **swelling** <gloss>inflammation</gloss> of the bladder are suppressed by this ointment.

...with ointment <note>original: "vnguine"</note>; and you shall heal an abraded <gloss>bruised or worn</gloss> body with the same care.

->**The Virtues of Garlic**<-

Hippocrates <note>original: "ypocras"</note> himself reports that the **afterbirth** <term>afterbirth: the placenta and membranes discharged from the uterus after childbirth</term> can be drawn out if the womb is fomented for a long time with the smoke of burnt [garlic]. 

It helps various complaints of the **lung** when boiled with **milk** and drunk, or when frequently eaten raw. 

Diocles <note>ancient Greek physician, 4th century BCE</note> ordered it to be given with **centaury** <note>a bitter herb used to treat fever and digestion</note> to those suffering from **dropsy** <term>dropsy: an old term for edema or swelling caused by excess watery fluid in the body</term>. In this way, it dries out the watery **humors** <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that medieval medicine sought to keep in balance</term>. 

The same physician ordered those with **kidney ailments** <note>original: "nefreticis"</note> to take it boiled. 

**Pythagoras** used it with **coriander** and **wine**, thereby curing the causes of **jaundice** <note>original: "ictericorum"</note>; he says that when drunk in this manner, it softens the bowels. 

When boiled with **beans**, it is said to soothe a **headache** if you smear the temples with the crushed mixture. 

If you add **goose fat** to this and pour it warm into a **pained ear**, it provides excellent relief. 

When boiled, it helps a **cough** and calms **labored breathing** <note>original: "suspiria"</note>. Raw or cooked, it also clears a **hoarse voice**, though it helps more when eaten frequently boiled. 

Eaten raw with **porridge** <note>original: "pulti"</note>, it aids **tenesmus** <term>tenesmus: a distressing and painful urge to evacuate the bowels, even when they are empty</term>. 

If you crush it and mix it with **pig fat** <gloss>lard</gloss>, applying it often will suppress no small **tumors** <gloss>swellings</gloss>. 

The drinking of **unknown waters** will not harm, nor will the **change of different locations** <note>a reference to "traveler's sickness" caused by changing climates or water sources</note> affect those who have taken [garlic] in their mouth in the morning on an **empty stomach**.

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on the properties of garlic, specifically citing the "Father of Medicine," Hippocrates, and other classical figures to explain its efficacy in gynecology, respiratory health, and internal obstructions. It follows the previous page's discussion of garlic's general healing powers.</meta>

Hippocrates himself reports—and Hippocrates, that father of physicians, reports—on the **afterbirth** <term>afterbirth: the placenta and membranes discharged from the uterus after childbirth</term>, which physicians call *secundinas*, though orators use the term *secundas* <note>original: "secundas"</note>. This name lacks a singular form. These "seconds" are those membranes, that is, the thin skins in which the fetus is wrapped in the mother’s womb; they are so called because <gloss>that</gloss> they follow the infant at birth. Unless these follow the fetus, the mothers are most severely tormented and sometimes <gloss>and</gloss> perish.

Therefore, Hippocrates reports that the afterbirth—that is, those membranes—can be drawn out (if they remain after the birth) if the vulva—that is, the gateway of the seminal field—is fomented, meaning it is steamed with the smoke of **burnt garlic**.

**Boiled garlic**—that is, a decoction—with **milk** helps various complaints, meaning various sicknesses; it helps many ailments of the lung. Also, drinking it—that is, reduced to a drinkable form—or eating it raw is beneficial.

**Diocles** <note>Diocles of Carystus, a prominent Greek physician of the 4th century BCE</note> often instructed it to be given with **centaury** <note>a bitter medicinal herb used to treat fever and aid digestion</note> to those suffering from **dropsy** <term>dropsy: an old term for edema, or swelling caused by excess watery fluid in the body</term>; the author is about to discuss this herb shortly after. Dropsy, however, is a swelling of the belly or body produced from watery or windy matter, proceeding from a harmful occurrence in the blood-generating power. There are three species of it: **hyposarca** <note>fluid under the skin</note>, **ascites** <note>fluid in the abdominal cavity</note>, and **tympanites** <note>swelling due to gas or wind</note>. It thus dries out the watery humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that medieval medicine sought to keep in balance</term> which are the material of dropsy, especially the ascites type.

The same author ordered those suffering from **kidney disease**—which is called *nephresis*—to take garlic boiled in a decoction. "I call it the nephritic disposition," says **John of Alexandria** <note>a 6th or 7th-century medical commentator known for his lectures on Galen and Hippocrates</note>, "when a stone is generated in the kidneys."

**Pythagoras**, that great philosopher, used it—namely garlic—with **coriander**—that is, with the seed of prepared coriander. When properly prepared, this is friendly to the stomach and most suitable for **epileptics**. With **wine**, it cures the causes of **jaundice** <note>original: "yetericorum," a variant of ictericorum</note> in this manner.

Garlic, both raw and cooked, clarifies and makes clear a **hoarse voice**; however, boiled garlic helps the voice more than raw, uncooked garlic. Taken in a **pottage** <gloss>that is, and</gloss> with porridge, it assists with **tenesmus** <gloss>that is, it calms the pain that arises from tenesmus</gloss>. 

(Now, **tenesmus** <term>tenesmus: a distressing and continual urge to evacuate the bowels, even when they are empty</term> is a frequent desire to empty the bowels, proceeding from stinging, biting, and sharp humors <term>humors: the fluids of the body believed in medieval medicine to control health and temperament</term> stimulating the end of the intestine). 

If you crush the garlic, joining <gloss>that is, combining</gloss> it with **pig fat** (from that domestic animal) and applying it topically, you will often suppress **tumors** and significant <gloss>that is, large</gloss> swellings of the limbs. He who takes garlic in the morning on an empty mouth <gloss>that is, an empty stomach</gloss> will not be harmed by the drinking <gloss>that is, the draught</gloss> of unknown or untested waters, nor will the change <gloss>that is, migration</gloss> made to different places harm him who has swallowed this drink.

### Plantain

The herb which in our language is called **plantain** <note>original: "plantago"</note>.
The Greek calls this **lamb’s tongue** <note>original: "arnoglossam"; from the Greek 'arnos' (lamb) and 'glossa' (tongue)</note> because the leaves of this plantain are like the tongue of a lamb.
There are two species: the first is called the **greater**; the other, indeed, is the **lesser**, which the common people call **ribwort** <note>original: "lanceolatam"</note> because it rises with sharp leaves like a **lance**.
Its power of **cooling** and **drying** is noted to be of the **third degree** <term>third degree: in medieval Galenic medicine, drugs were ranked from 1 to 4 based on the intensity of their primary qualities; the third degree indicates a very potent medicinal effect</term>; however, the greater kind is said to be of even more significant strength. 
They say the greater plantain dries excessively moist **wounds**. 
If it is applied together with **honey**, it also cleanses foul <gloss>that is, infected or dirty</gloss> sores.

<summary>This page introduces the plantain herb, describing its two varieties (greater and lesser/ribwort) and its classification as a potent cooling and drying agent used for healing and cleansing wounds.</summary>

<keywords>plantain, arnoglossa, ribwort, lanceolata, medicinal degrees, wound healing, honey, medieval botany</keywords>

**When cooked with salt and vinegar, just like a vegetable,**
**If eaten, it greatly restrains a flowing belly.**
**If lentils are cooked with the aforementioned plantain, it will help;**
**This food is better for those suffering from dysentery and chronic diarrhea.**

¶ And of herbs... <note>original: "rc", likely an abbreviation for "etc." or a reference marker</note> Pliny, in the twenty-fifth book of his *Natural History*, chapter eight, provides a very full description of this herb, saying that the physician Themison also celebrated the common herb **plantain** <note>original: "plantago"</note> as its discoverer, having published a volume about it which our author follows closely. The Greek calls this herb **lamb's tongue** <note>original: "arnoglossam"</note>, which in our language—that is, Latin—is called **plantain**. This is because its leaves are like a lamb's tongue <note>The text mentions "lingue anguine" (snake's tongue) but then clarifies "arnon agnū vocant" (lamb's tongue), a common medieval linguistic overlap</note>. For the Greeks call a lamb *arnon* and a tongue *glossos*. 

The species of this plantain are **twin** <gloss>that is, twofold</gloss>. The first is called the **greater**; the other, indeed <gloss>that is, "but"</gloss>, is called the **lesser**. The greater kind men also <insert>the text adds</insert> call **ribwort** <note>original: "lanceolatam"</note> because it rises <gloss>that is, grows</gloss> with sharp leaves <gloss>that is, standing upright</gloss> like a **lance**. 

Its power of **cooling** and **drying** is noted to be <gloss>that is, exists</gloss> in the **third degree** <term>third degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating a very strong medicinal potency, where the quality of the drug is powerful enough to significantly alter the body's state</term>. However, physicians <insert>the text adds</insert> affirm that there is a **greater power** <gloss>that is, more potency</gloss> in the greater variety. It dries <gloss>that is, draws out moisture</gloss> **wounds** that are excessively damp, and if it is applied with **honey**, it purges <gloss>that is, cleanses</gloss> wounds that are foul <gloss>that is, malignant and poisonous</gloss>. 

If plantain is joined with **vinegar** and cooked <gloss>that is, boiled</gloss> just as a vegetable is usually boiled, once chewed <gloss>that is, eaten</gloss> it restrains <gloss>that is, checks</gloss> a belly that is "flowing" too much <gloss>that is, an immoderate flux of the bowels</gloss>. If **lentils**—which is that seed whose cooking-water causes a fluid belly, but whose substance actually binds it—are cooked, this food will help <gloss>that is, alleviate the condition</gloss> better <gloss>that is, more effectively</gloss>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of plantain (plantago), moving from its general botanical classification to its specific medicinal applications for digestive disorders and a variety of other ailments. It builds on the previous page's mention of how the herb, when prepared with salt and vinegar, aids the bowels.</meta>

...more effectively those with dysentery <gloss>that is, those troubled by dysentery</gloss>. 

There is, moreover, a threefold flux of the belly. First is **lientery**, when food leaves the bowels in the same quality and manner as it was taken <note>original: "qualitate misericordie," likely a scribal error or variant for "eadem qualitate," meaning the food is undigested</note>. The second is **diarrhetic** flux, which is an immoderate flow of humor. The third is **dysenteric** flux of the belly, which is bloody. Hence there exists a common little verse: 

*Raw is lientery, simple is diarrhea, with blood is dysentery.* 

And it will better help the **celiacs** <gloss>that is, those oppressed by the celiac disease</gloss>, which is a flux of the belly proceeding from a defect of the stomach. **Celiac passions** arise from the indigestion of the belly itself, because due to that failure it cannot provide nourishment to the body, as **Alexander the Physician** <note>original: "alexāder yatros," referring to Alexander of Tralles, a famous 6th-century medical author</note> says.

Crushed and applied, it binds flowing blood,
And it is said to restore the color of black spots <note>referring to bruises or hematomas</note>.
Or with egg white <note>original: "lachrimo," likely referring to the "tears" or clear fluid of the plant or egg white</note> it miraculously heals those who are burned.
Alone it cures the bite of a dog and calms the swelling.
And it disperses **scrofula** <term>scrofula: a form of tuberculosis causing glandular swellings in the neck</term> when crushed and applied with salt.
To those with **dropsy** <term>dropsy: edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</term>, it is beneficial when boiled and eaten as a vegetable.
Thus it helps the **asthmatic**; and it is said to help the **falling** <term>falling sickness: an archaic name for epilepsy</term>.
It will well cleanse any foul wounds of the mouth,
If its juice is swirled and held in the mouth for a long time.
This juice, taken internally, cures **fistulas** <term>siringia: internal sores or pipe-like ulcers</term>.
And ear pain is calmed by this, as is **holy fire** <term>holy fire: likely erysipelas, a painful skin infection, or "St. Anthony’s Fire"</term>.
It brings aid to those **spitting blood** <term>emptoicis: from hemoptysis, the coughing up of blood from the lungs</term> if it is drunk often.
It is stronger on its own if it is joined with vinegar.
And thus it helps the **consumptive** <term>ptisicos: those suffering from phthisis or tuberculosis</term>; but when injected through the anus,
It restrains the considerable flux of those with **dysentery**.
Swelling and heat are driven from the eyes when smeared with this,
Especially if you chew this herb frequently with your teeth.

These are called "scrofulae" by a metaphor taken from a **scrofa** <gloss>that is, a sow</gloss>. For just as a sow brings forth many offspring in the same birth, likewise one scrofulous swelling, in a very short interval of time, produces many more such swellings. 

Plantain boiled <gloss>that is, cooked like a vegetable</gloss> <gloss>that is, like garden herbs which people are accustomed to eat</gloss> and then eaten benefits those with **dropsy** <term>dropsy: an old term for edema, or swelling caused by excess fluid in the body's tissues</term>; it helps those with **asthma** when taken this way (concerning dropsy and asthma, it has been spoken of previously). And plantain is reported <gloss>that is, it is said</gloss> to help those with the "falling sickness" <gloss>that is, those suffering from the falling sickness, which is so called from "falling"</gloss>; however, this disease is called **epilepsy**, which proceeds from a thick, horrible, and vicious humor blocking the ventricles of the brain. 

Any kind of plantain <gloss>that is, any of its species</gloss> will clean well <gloss>that is, properly</gloss> foul wounds of the mouth <gloss>that is, of the throat</gloss> if its juice is held <gloss>that is, retained</gloss> for a long time in the mouth and swirled about in the manner of a **gargle**. Moreover, a gargle or *gargarismus* is a liquid which is introduced into the mouth as far as the **uvula** <note>original: "gargareos," referring to the back of the throat or the uvula</note>.

<meta>This page continues the medical exploration of plantain (plantago). ...it is agitated within itself several times; then it is returned whole through the mouth; and this is done mostly to draw out and shake off some humors from the brain or from the parts of the head. Thus says James of Forlì <note>original: "iacobus de partibus," referring to Jacques Despars, a 15th-century physician</note>: this juice of plantain cures **stringia**, that is, fistulas arising in the corners of the eyes. And ear pain is calmed by this juice of plantain dripped into the ear, as is **holy fire**, which is also called **Persian fire**. <term>holy fire: likely erysipelas, a painful bacterial skin infection characterized by redness and inflammation</term>. And it is a pustule of small mass, corrosive, burning, and like a red-hot iron, it leaves a callous skin beneath the flesh which they call an **eschar** <note>original: "escaram," a dry, dark scab or crust caused by a burn or infection</note>; therefore, **holy fire** is calmed by the juice of this herb. This juice brings aid to **hemoptysis sufferers** <gloss>that is, those spitting blood</gloss> if it is drunk often by itself; it is even stronger—that is, more effective—at stopping blood coming from the chest if it is combined with vinegar; and it helps **consumptives** <gloss>that is, those affected by phthisis</gloss>. **Phthisis** <term>phthisis: a historical term for pulmonary tuberculosis, a disease that wastes the body</term> is moreover an ulcer of the lung from which follows the extreme wasting away of the body. Indeed, the juice injected with the instrument that doctors call a **clyster** <term>clyster: an early form of an enema used to deliver liquid medicine into the lower bowel</term> through the anus—that is, through the lower gate of the body—binds—that is, constricts—in no small measure—that is, greatly—the flux of those with **dysentery**. What dysentery is has already been explained; and a tumor <note>used here in the general sense of a swelling</note>, if smeared with this juice, is driven away and moved from the eyes. If you chew <gloss>that is, crush</gloss> this same herb with the teeth quite often <gloss>that is, frequently</gloss>, it will repress <gloss>that is, make the swelling go down</gloss> the gums <gloss>that is, the swollen jaws full of blood</gloss>; and it is also accustomed to quiet the pain of a tooth.
