<meta>This page continues the discussion of plantain's medicinal properties, transitioning from the treatment of internal stones and scrofula to specific dosages for recurring fevers and the treatment of facial fistulas.</meta>

As medical authors say, these [stones] are mostly formed in these members from stones or sand gradually gathered there. That is, they assert that those hard **scrofula** <term>scrofula: a swelling of the lymph glands, often in the neck</term> do not arise <note>original: "a pantano nasci," likely a corruption or variant for "plantagine," meaning they are prevented by the plant</note> in those who carry its root—

<margin>For pain of the kidneys and bladder</margin>
namely, the root of the **plantain**, suspended from the neck. That is, there is such power in the neck—that is, such a specific property is innate to it. [Instruction:] Crush—that is, pound—three roots of this herb and join—that is, combine—with those same crushed roots three *cyathi* of wine: a **cyathus** <term>cyathus: a Roman liquid measure, approximately 45ml or 1.5 ounces</term> weighs ten **drachms** <term>drachm: a small unit of weight; ten drachms would be roughly 37 grams</term>. And make it so that the same amount of water—that is, let there be the same measure

<margin>What is a cyathus?</margin>
of water as there is of the expressed juice of the roots and the wine. You shall give these medicines to be drunk by the patient suffering from fever but not yet trembling—that is, a little before the febrile rigor <note>shivering fit</note> that causes trembling comes on. You shall cure in the same way the fever to which the "third light" <note>original: "tertia lux," meaning the third day in inclusive counting</note> gives its name: namely, **tertian fever** <note>malaria-like fever recurring every 48 hours</note>, which afflicts every other day by reason of the **choleric humor** <term>choleric humor: yellow bile, which in medieval medicine was thought to be hot and dry, causing sharp fevers</term> on which it depends.

<margin>Tertian fever</margin>
Twice two roots—that is, four roots—are thought to be beneficial for **quartan fevers** <note>fever recurring every 72 hours</note>—that is, those which afflict

<margin>Quartan fever</margin>
from the fourth day to the fourth day, because of the nature of the **melancholic humor** <term>melancholic humor: black bile, thought to be cold and dry, causing more persistent, lingering illnesses</term> from which they are produced—if they are given to the patient with as many *cyathi* of wine and water: that is, with four measures of each. **Lesser plantain** <note>original: "minor plantago," referring to *Plantago lanceolata* or Ribwort Plantain</note> can help all the aforementioned conditions, even though it may not seem to possess as much strength—that is, as much efficacy—as the **greater plantain** for the cited ailments. The juice of this plantain, namely the lesser one, especially cures—that is, by its specific nature—an **ulcer**—that is, a **fistula**—that grows near the nostrils or the eyes in the tear duct of the eye. An ulcer of this kind occurs because of a long-term downward flow of sharp humors that corrode the flesh by the intensity of their sharpness; for this reason, foul discharge <note>original: "obscena sordices," literally "foul filth"</note> seems to gush out from that spot. This plantain, I say, heals a fistula of the eye if it...

...[parotid swellings], this ointment—that is, the unguent—helps a recent wound—that is, one recently inflicted—and **parotid swellings** <term>parotidas: inflammatory swellings of the glands behind or below the ears</term>—that is, things arising behind the ears. <margin>What is called "sperm" in animals is called "seed" in plants.</margin> 
Its seed—that is, its semen. (For the seeds of herbs are provided to produce others, just as the seed of a man and woman is for procreating humans). Therefore, the seed of this herb, when drunk, expels the **afterbirth** <term>secundas: the placenta and membranes expelled after childbirth</term>—that is, those little membranes (in which infants are wrapped in the mother's womb) when—it is understood—they remain after the birth.

If a long journey—that is, the labor resulting from the length of the road—excites—that is, causes—pain in the feet, as often happens—that is, occurs—the crushed herb itself will help when applied to the painful area with sharp wine—that is, vinegar. The power of the greater plantain is enormous—that is, very great—in drying, to such an extent—supplement—that it can dry a body placed near it—that is, brought close—just like **cauteries** <term>cauteria: medical applications of glowing hot iron, steel, silver, or gold used to sear tissue</term> applied over an oozing place.

[To the] Most Illustrious. <note>original: "risi Illustrissimis" — This appears to be a fragment of a formal dedication, likely "Dedicated to the Most Illustrious [Readers]."</note>

### Chapter 7: On the herb Rue

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a rue plant (Ruta graveolens) set within a double-lined rectangular frame. The plant has a thick central stem that splits into several branches, each bearing multiple small, rounded or ovate leaflets.</image-desc>

By Physicians, **rue** is said to possess a burning power,
And among these qualities, its degree of dryness is reported to be the **third**. <note>In the ancient medical system of "humors," plants were ranked 1 to 4 based on their intensity. A "third degree" herb is very potent and must be used with caution.</note>

It is very useful for the stomach if it is drunk frequently.

Taken as a drink, it **expels the birth** <note>This historical reference indicates the herb was used to induce labor or as an abortifacient.</note> and restrains **lust** <term>venerem: literally "Venus," referring here to sexual desire or libido</term>.
If it is drunk, it calms a **cough**; it also purges the **menstrual flow**.
If you boil this in water to which you have added **sharp wine**,
Such a **decoction** <term>decoctio: a concentrated medicinal liquid created by boiling plant material</term> soothes **griping pains of the belly** <term>tormina ventris: sharp, twisting intestinal cramps or colic</term>.

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 7 on Rue (Ruta graveolens), detailing its classification as a "hot" and "dry" herb. It outlines its primary medical uses, including its effects on the stomach, its use in reproductive health, and its ability to treat coughs and abdominal cramps.</summary>
<keywords>Rue, Ruta graveolens, humoral medicine, digestive health, reproductive medicine, colic, decoctions</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced Chapter 7 on Rue (Ruta graveolens), describing it as a "fiery" herb of the third degree. It began detailing its benefits for the stomach, its use in inducing labor, and its ability to suppress sexual desire and coughs. This page continues the poetic description of its healing properties before transitioning into a prose commentary that cites classical authorities like Pliny the Elder and Macer Floridus.</meta>

**It aids the lungs and the chest and heals diseases**
**Of the ribs, which the Attic tongue** <note>original: "attica lingua" — referring to the Greek language.</note> **calls pleurisy.**
**It aids the arthritic and the weary: and it heals fevers**
**If it is drunk, or if you have cooked it while green in oil.**
**You should bathe the patient with the lukewarm [oil] before the onset of shivering.** <note>Medieval physicians believed applying warm oils could prevent the "rigor" or cold fits associated with malaria and other fevers.</note>
**It is beneficial to the womb and to intestinal swelling**
**Whether this ointment is used, or if [the liquid] is poured inside by a clyster.** <term>clisterio: a clyster or enema, used to deliver medicine directly into the lower gastrointestinal tract.</term>
**And if drunk with wine, it will be able to benefit both [the womb and intestines].**
**Cooked in oil and drunk, it repels intestinal worms**
**Especially if cooked for a long time with dried figs and the liquid of wine.**

---

**From the physicians, etc. The powers and properties of Rue.**

Pliny, <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the 'Natural History'.</note> in Book 20, Chapter 13, touches upon [this] most illustriously, beginning: *"Among the principal medicines, however, is rue,"* which Macer <note>Aemilius Macer, the traditional name associated with the herbals written in verse.</note> imitates most exactly. Its power—that is, its "burning" quality—that is, Rue is said by physicians to be **hot and dry**. And the **third degree** is said to be present in these qualities, namely in heat and dryness; that is, it is hot and dry in the third degree. <note>In the Galenic system of humors, a "third degree" intensity meant the herb had a very strong effect on the body's internal balance and required careful dosage.</note>

Rue is useful—that is, suitable—for a weak and cold stomach if it is drunk often. It itself expels—that is, leads out—the birth (that is, the fetus or embryo) in a drink, if its juice is drunk. And it restrains—that is, holds back—**lust** <term>venerem: literally "Venus," referring to sexual desire or libido</term>—that is, the appetite for sexual matters—if it is drunk. It suppresses—that is, calms—a **cough**; it purges—that is, provokes—the **menstrual flow**.

If you cook—that is, boil down—this herb in water to which you have joined—that is, connected—**sharp wine** (that is, vinegar), such a decoction <term>decoctio: a medicinal extract made by boiling herbs in liquid</term>—that is, the liquid of such a boiling—suppresses (that is, the **griping pains** are pacified, meaning the twistings of the belly or the intestines). A *tormen* <term>tormina: severe, twisting intestinal cramps or colic</term> is a type of disease when the intestines are twisted, the name being derived from the word for "twisting." Pliny says: **Wild mint** <note>original: "Mentastrum" — likely horse mint or a similar wild variety.</note> for griping pains...

<meta>The text on this page continues the detailed medical commentary on the herb Rue (Ruta graveolens). ...most effective for [feverish conditions]: it aids: that is, it cures the lungs and diseases of the lungs. And it aids the chest: that is, the diseases of the chest previously mentioned: and it heals the disease of the ribs: that is, of the side, which disease the Attic tongue <note>original: "lingua attica" — referring to the Greek language.</note>: that is, Greek, calls **pleurisy**. For *pleuron* in Greek is what we call the side. Pleurisy is indeed an acute disease of the side, whose symptoms are fever, cough, pain in the side, and difficulty of breathing: it aids those afflicted with the arthritic disease <note>original: "artetico"</note> which is called "the drop" <note>original: "gutta" — a historical term for gout, based on the belief that the disease was caused by harmful humors "dropping" into the joints.</note> / and it aids those with **sciatica**: that is, the sciatic disease, of which was spoken above. 

This herb, having been consumed: that is, drunk: heals the periodic fever <note>original: "typo"</note> that precedes the febrile heat / which is called rigor and "hair-raising" <note>original: "horripilatio" — the medieval term for shivering and goosebumps during the onset of illness.</note> / and is named rigor: or if you have cooked: that is, boiled that green herb: that is, fresh, in oil and you foment: that is, treat the patient’s body with it as a liniment before the trembling: that is, before the onset of the fever: this ointment: that is, **oil of rue**, is beneficial to the womb: that is, for diseases of the womb proceeding from cold or windiness <note>Medieval medicine attributed many gynecological and digestive issues to "windiness" or excess gas trapped in the body.</note>: and it is beneficial for a tumor: that is, an inflation of the intestine which is called the **colon**. Indeed, this inflation brings on the "colic passion" <note>A general term for severe abdominal pain or cramping.</note>. 

Nothing is more effective for dissolving this windiness than oil of rue, as the author reports regarding griping pains <note>original: "tormina"</note>: or if this ointment—that is, oil—is poured in, that is, safely impelled through the lower region, i.e., through the anus by a **clyster**. <term>clisterio: a device or treatment used to inject liquid into the rectum; an enema.</term> A clyster or *clysterium* is an instrument by which [liquid] is sent through the anus to draw out feces and other harmful humors in the intestines; it is a liquid which they call an enema. That which is inserted as a solid at the orifice to provoke excrement is commonly called a **suppository**. 

And the herb, when consumed, will be effective; that is, it will be able to benefit both ailments, as much of the womb as of the intestines: the herb itself, boiled in oil and drunk—that is, after it has been drunk—it repels, that is, it drives out **intestinal worms**.

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on the properties of Rue (Ruta graveolens) from the previous page, shifting from internal ailments like pleurisy and gout to topical treatments for the eyes, skin, and its legendary use as an antidote by King Mithridates.</meta>

It will benefit <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "ydropisis" — a historical term for edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</note> if it is added at night. 
Eaten raw and fresh, it cures the eyes of dimness, 
and even better if used with the juice of <term>fennel</term> <note>original: "maratri"</note> and the gall of a cock. 
If the juice is joined with an equal portion of honey, 
let the eyes of the patient be frequently anointed with it. 
By mixing the juice of this herb with rose oil and also with vinegar, 
it cures the pain of the head, 
even if it is immoderate, if you anoint it often. 
If its expressed juice is poured into the nostrils, 
it stops flowing blood well by drying the gore. 
If it is warmed in the rind of a pomegranate 
and you pour it into the ears, you will drive away the pain from there. 
By mixing its juice with <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "ceruse" — a lead-based pigment used historically in medicines and cosmetics, now known to be toxic.</note>, rose oil, and vinegar, 
you create a marvelous ointment 
to drive away what we call the <term>sacred fire</term> <note>original: "sacrum ignem" — likely referring to erysipelas (a skin infection) or shingles.</note> 
and the scabbing of the skin which in Greek is called <term>herpeta</term> <note>original: "herpeta" — from the Greek for "creeping," referring to spreading skin rashes.</note>. 
And in Greek you may say <term>achors</term> <note>original: "acoras" — scabby eruptions or ulcers on the scalp</note>, which you purge with this ointment; 
these are ulcers of the head that flow with a thick humor. 
It also cures <term>ozena</term> <note>original: "horozimas" — foul-smelling ulcers in the nose</note> when applied to the nostrils; these 
are ulcers born in the nose that stink heavily. 
Consumed in drink or eaten raw, it stands against many poisons. 
This, **Mithridates**, the King of Pontus, often proved; 
he was accustomed to eating, while fasting upon rising in the morning: 
twenty leaves of rue with a little salt, 
two large walnuts and two dried figs, 
[again] two figs and two kernels of a nut. 
Armed with such food, he did not fear any poison 
whatever, nor any traps that might be laid for him.

27
D.iii.

They teach that weasels are able to resist poisons 
wonderfully; they eat that herb first 
when they are about to fight with black serpents. 
Boiled with butter and a little oil, and applied warm, 
it drives away swelling of the <term>womb</term> <note>original: "matricis"</note> and of the neck; 
and applied in this way, it benefits all internal diseases. 
With this you can also cure swollen testicles, 
if you apply it well-ground with laurel leaves. 
From this herb, an antidote useful for many causes 
is prepared; physicians call it <term>diapyganon</term> <note>original: "diapyganon" — a medicine made from rue</note>, 
because Rue is called <term>pyganon</term> in the Greek tongue. 
Otherwise, the preparation of the antidote is held to be this: 
join an equal weight of <term>nitre</term> <note>original: "nitrum" — a naturally occurring salt used in medicine</note>, pepper, and cumin; 
put as much of these three together as you put of Rue. 
But it is fitting first to soak the cumin in sharp wine, 
and afterward to roast it on a heated iron. 
Then you shall join all these things together, well-ground, with honey. 
Pains of the chest, the muscles of the sides, the liver, and the kidneys 
are often cured by eating this antidote. 
It thins the fat man and softens the belly without <term>griping pain</term> <note>original: "tormine" — refers to intestinal cramping or colic</note>; 
it strengthens the stomach and makes it so that one digests food well.

¶ It will benefit those with <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "ydropicis" — swelling caused by excess fluid</note>, etc. This herb, boiled for a long time 
with <term>dried figs</term> <gloss>that is, thick figs</gloss> and with a liquor of wine, 
will be useful for those suffering from <term>dropsy</term> <gloss>that is, those suffering from the condition of edema</gloss> 
if the dried figs and wine are added and mixed 
into this herb at night <gloss>that is, through the night before the boiling is done</gloss>. 
Furthermore, this herb eaten raw and fresh <gloss>that is, green</gloss> 
cures the eyes of dimness <gloss>that is, it makes the vision sharper</gloss>.

Herpes, moreover, is a disease consisting of small pustules contaminating the surface of the skin, affecting the patient with the greatest itching, and creeping from one part to another. And this ointment also clears away the ailments called <term>achores</term> <note>original: "acoras" — scabby ulcers or crusty eruptions, typically on the scalp</note> in Greek.

**Mithridates**, the king of Pontus, that land <note>original: "insule" — though Pontus was a coastal region of Asia Minor, the text uses "island" in a general sense for a territory</note> from whom the medicine called <term>mithridatum</term> <note>a famous ancient "universal antidote" meant to protect against all poisons</note> took its name among other most memorable things, often proved—that is, approved—this medicine. **Pipontinus**, however, asserts that "inglans" does not signify that at all, saying that people have until now through great error thought the <term>inglans</term> to be a hazelnut. But we, along with many others, have corrected this error. An *inglans* <note>a corruption of *juglans*, the Latin term for a walnut</note> is that which we call by the common name of "nut." The same man also, a little earlier, breaks out into these words: In the chests of Mithridates, the great defeated king, **Gnaeus Pompeius** found a recipe for an antidote written in the king's own hand in a private notebook, composed of two dry nuts, as many figs, and twenty leaves of rue ground together with a grain of salt added. Whoever takes this while fasting, no poison shall harm him on that day.

But let us return to the literal text. Hercules <note>original: "Alcides" — a common epithet for Hercules, grandson of Alcaeus</note> is said to have started this: those who followed kept the custom.

¶ **It is Celery [Apium], etc.** Intending to commemorate the powers of celery, he speaks a few introductory words in its praise regarding the origin of its name. Now, the **triumph** was the greatest of all honors which was gi[ven] by the Roman people—

## Drunk down, it opposes all poisonous bites

well cleanses tears of the eyes, wounds, and ulcers,
if it is added frequently in the manner of a plaster.

¶ **Drunk down, etc.** This herb, when swallowed <gloss>that is, eaten</gloss> whole according to its entire substance, opposes <gloss>that is, stands against</gloss> poisonous bites <gloss>that is, the bites of venomous animals</gloss>; but the seed or a decoction <term>apozima</term> <note>original: "apozima"; a medicinal tea or broth made by boiling plant materials in water</note> <gloss>that is, its boiled extract</gloss> will be more beneficial. If its liquid <note>original: "liquoz"</note> is drunk, it will soothe a cough. In the same manner, this herb binds the belly <gloss>that is, the flux of the belly</gloss>. The herb itself, when drunk, restrains <gloss>that is, calms</gloss> vomiting. ...muscles and sinews <gloss>original: musculos & lacertos</gloss>). This herb, therefore, when drunk in this way, aids a fever troubling the body every single day—that is, daily—meaning a daily fever <term>febrem quotidianam</term> <note>A fever that recurs every day, often associated with malaria in pre-modern medicine</note>. The juice <note>original: "saccus," likely a variant of "succus"</note> of that herb mixed with fine flour <term>pollen</term>—that is, with the coarser small powder of flour, from which the "pollinarium" <note>a sifting instrument</note> is named, by which the flour is separated from the bran <note>original: "rarrris"</note>—that is, of grain—and with "tear-drop" <note>original: "lachrimo"</note>—that is, egg white <gloss>original: albumine oui</gloss>—it cleanses well both a wound <term>vulnus</term> and an ulcer <term>ulcus</term> if it is added on top in the manner of a plaster. 

For "wound" and "ulcer" do not have the same distinction among orators and physicians. For orators call a "wound" that which is inflicted by force, but an "ulcer" that which arises on its own; however, physicians understand a "wound" to be when there is only a break in continuity <note>a physical separation of tissue</note> with no loss of the limb's substance, while an "ulcer" is when some of the substance has been removed <note>In other words, a wound is a cut, while an ulcer involves tissue being eaten away or lost</note>. These things are clearly explained by Galen <term>Galieno</term> <note>The famous 2nd-century Greek physician</note> and his commentator Haly <term>Haly</term> <note>Likely Haly Abbas, a 10th-century Persian physician whose works were standard medical textbooks in Europe</note> in the third book of the *Tegni* <note>From the Greek 'Techne', referring to Galen's 'Ars Medica', the 'Art of Medicine'</note>.

¶ Another species of mallow. The division of the mallow reveals its powers and shows the variety of names by which it is called and their derivations. He acknowledges its humoral nature <term>complexionem</term> <note>In medieval medicine, the "complexion" was the specific balance of hot, cold, moist, or dry qualities in a substance</note>, though physicians have affirmed it to be warm with "exaltation"—that is, in its temperament. Among the wild varieties, the one with large leaves and white roots is called Althea." This is according to Pliny. No one denies that Althea is a species of mallow, and physicians call it Althea because it grows—that is, it raises itself on high <note>original: "se tollat in altum." A medieval folk etymology linking the Greek *althaia* to the Latin *altus*, meaning tall or high</note>. They also call this same plant *evisca* because the root of that herb, when crushed, seems to be moist—that is, it has a certain sticky <term>viscosam</term> moisture like birdlime <term>viscum</term>, which is a type of glue. For *viscum* <note>Referring here to mistletoe or the sticky substance made from it</note> is of the neuter gender, used for fowling—that is, for catching birds—as Servius <note>A 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note> asserts, though Niccolò Perotti <note>A 15th-century Italian humanist and grammarian</note> disagrees. However, the more skilled grammarians call whatever is inside the skin or hide of an animal *viscus* <note>The internal organs or flesh</note>. Nevertheless, in the "second declension" <note>A grammatical category of Latin nouns</note>, the word is used by physicians for a certain mucilaginous growth that develops on trees; among these, they especially value oak mistletoe <note>original: "viscum quercuum"</note>. And the herb itself is often called "wild mallow" by many. Its flower, when cooked—that is, boiled down <term>decoctus</term>—in honey-water <term>mulsa</term> <note>A mixture of water and honey</note>, cleanses and purifies wounds. That is, he asserts it disperses—meaning it dissolves scrofulas <term>scrophulas</term> <note>A swelling of the lymph glands, usually in the neck, which was a common medieval ailment</note>—and it is said to help—that is, to mitigate—the pain of the anus <note>original: "dolorem ani"</note> (as Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina, the 11th-century Persian "Prince of Physicians" whose Canon of Medicine was a standard textbook</note> and Serapio <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century physician known for his work on medicinal "simples"</note> state) and it heals—that is, it assists—in this way. For the bruising—that is, the crushing <term>conquassationi</term>—of the nerves and muscles, its root being boiled—that is, first decocted—and added—that is, joined—to fat—that is, to pork lard—and turpentine, which is a resin <term>gummi</term> dripping from the tree called *albotin* <note>The terebinth or turpentine tree, often used as a source of medicinal resin</note>; among other resins, it is obtained after purified mastic <note>A resin from the Pistacia lentiscus tree</note> by grinding—that is, crushing and mixing with the aforesaid—it cures tumors of the womb <term>matricis</term> and it is said to help other pains of that same womb. The root itself, thus mixed, relaxes. It is beneficial for the tension of the nerves and for the contraction which is called a spasm <term>spasmus</term>. The root itself breaks or scatters—that is, disperses—hard abscesses <term>apostemata</term> <note>Inflammatory swellings or boils</note> consisting of hard and stony humors, and those which are mature but do not respond to treatment <note>original: "iouri [iuri] non obedientia"; literally "not obeying the law" of normal healing or maturation</note>. A decoction <term>elixatura</term> of this root will help in these cases—that is, illnesses—if the locations of the diseases are bathed—that is, as physicians say, fomented—with that decoction while it is lukewarm—that is, tepid—often—that is, frequently.

For those with dysentery, a decoction of the root taken
With wine, binds and checks their flux.
This decoction, when taken, expels the delayed afterbirth,
And it benefits those with hemorrhoids and repels stones;
It is also accustomed to help the bladder in various cases.
Join the seed, crushed with sharp wine and olive oil:
With this ointment, you shall drive away unsightly skin spots.
When drunk with vinegar-water <term>posca</term> <note>A common ancient and medieval drink made of vinegar, water, and sometimes herbs</note>, it can withstand poisons.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Dill .10.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a dill plant (Anetum). The plant is shown with a central thick stem branching out into several secondary stems, all featuring the delicate, feathery, and thread-like foliage characteristic of the herb. The illustration is enclosed in a square double-line border.</image-desc>

By physicians, dill <term>anetum</term> is reported to be hot and dry:
And they say that it holds the second degree <term>gradus</term> in both qualities. <note>Medieval medicine classified the intensity of a plant's "complexion" on a scale of one to four. Dill being "second degree" hot and dry meant it was moderately stimulating and drying, effective for digestion but not as caustic as a "fourth degree" substance like garlic.</note>

A decoction <term>decoctio</term> of it, when taken, provides milk abundantly
To a nurse, and it is accustomed to drive away diseases of the stomach.
If a sick person takes three cyathi <note>original: *cyatos*; a Roman liquid measure equivalent to about 1.5 ounces or a small cupful.</note> of it warm,
It is by the evidence of a belch <term>ructus</term> that it demonstrates the opening
Of the mouth of the stomach: and that harmful wind has exited through this.
Also, heavy nausea is soothed by such a drink.
It provokes urine, repelling all obstacles [in the bladder],
And it aids the womb <term>matricem</term>: if the same is well-fomented <note>Fomentation involves applying warm, medicated liquids to the body with a cloth.</note> with it.
If the patient drinks crushed dill with warm liquid,
It cures pain of the intestines and of the belly.
By this drink also, slow digestion is assisted,
Whereby the expulsive force <note>original: *vis egestiua*; the bodily power responsible for moving waste through the digestive tract.</note> seems to be less strained.
Drunk constantly, it harms the sight and closes
The genital pathway, drying up the humor of the seed <note>original: *humorem seminis*; referring to the medieval belief that dill could decrease sperm production or libido.</note> within.

¶ **By physicians, etc.** He briefly touches upon the complexion <term>complexionem</term> <note>The "complexion" refers to the specific balance of the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) within a plant.</note> and properties of Dill (whose seed is most highly praised in medicine). This ash, when frequently poured upon them, is said
To heal ulcers, especially of the male member.

# Betony

The Greeks are accustomed to call Betony *cestron* <note>original: "Betonicam soliti sunt cestron dicere graeci"</note>.
**U**rine <note>The OCR reads "Baunas," which is a transcription error for the Latin *Urinas*</note> is stimulated when this is drunk, and it drives out stones <note>referring to the treatment of kidney or bladder stones</note>.
It brings aid to those spitting blood <term>hemoptoics</term> <note>from the Greek *haimoptoikos*</note> when taken with vinegar-water <term>posca</term> <note>original: *pusca*; a common Roman and medieval drink made of vinegar, water, and sometimes herbs</note>.
It dries out those with dropsy <term>hydropics</term> <note>original: *ydropicos*; a historical term for edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</note> when taken with wine and honey.
A plaster <term>cataplasm</term> <note>a warm, moist medicinal mass spread on cloth and applied to the body</note> made from its crushed leaves is of no small benefit to eyes that a blow has injured.
Its juice, mixed with rose oil <note>original: *roseo oliuo*</note> and poured into the ears, puts to flight various pains from there.
The powder of Betony, joined with cooked honey, helps those with lung abscesses <term>empyics</term> <note>original: *empicos*; referring to empyema, a condition where pus collects in a body cavity, typically the lungs</note> and a cough, and it calms gasping <note>likely referring to asthma or shortness of breath</note>.
And taken in this way, it also helps various pains of the stomach.

<summary>This page introduces the herb Betony, detailing its versatility in treating internal conditions like kidney stones and dropsy, as well as external injuries to the eyes and ears.</summary>
<keywords>Betony, kidney stones, hemoptysis, dropsy, edema, poultice, earache, respiratory health, stomach pain</keywords>

If there is a fever, take it with warm water; if the fever is absent, take it with wine. 
This same powder, drunk with honey-water <term>mulsa</term> <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of water and honey</note>, moves the bowels. 
You will cure wounds, even those recently made, with a plaster <term>cataplasm</term> made of leaves crushed with salt. 
Crushed, this herb alone heals a fracture of the head. 
A plaster made from its boiled leaves is of no small benefit for various afflictions of the eyes. 
Chewed or swallowed, it restrains tearing of the eyes. 
By this means, even a fearsome cloudiness <note>original: "caligo"; referring to cataracts or dimmed vision</note> is usually driven away. 
If the herb is combined by weight with Rue <note>an evergreen shrub used in ancient medicine</note>, and taken crushed with water, the blood that harms the eyes is said to be expelled through the finer channels; thus, it restores clear sight.

¶ **The Greeks are accustomed...** and so forth. The herb Betony is distinguished by various virtues, which Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), author of "Natural History"</note> (Book 25, Chapter 8) extols with much praise. He says that it is called *betonica* in Gaul, *serratula* in Italy, and *cestron* by the Greeks, and it is the most highly praised of all. He subsequently lists its powers, from which Macer <note>refers to Macer Floridus, the traditional author of the poem "On the Virtues of Herbs"</note> does not deviate. The Greeks were "accustomed"—that is, they used to—call Betony *cestron*. The herb itself, when drunk, stirs, moves, and provokes urine. Also, when drunk, it "repels"—that is, breaks—stones <note>referring to kidney or bladder stones</note>. The herb taken—that is, drunk—with honey-water <term>mulsa</term> <note>original: "mulsa"</note> brings aid—that is, it helps—those spitting blood <term>hemoptoics</term> <note>original: "emoptoicis"; from the Greek for "blood-spitter"</note>. The herb taken—that is, drunk—with wine and honey dries out those with dropsy <term>hydropics</term> <note>original: "ydropicos"; a historical term for swelling or edema</note>—that is, it drains the watery fluid of the dropsical. A plaster <term>cataplasm</term>—that is, a soft medicinal application—made from its crushed leaves is beneficial—that is...

Weakness of sight (proceeding from humors or bad vapors or from the visual spirits <term>spirituū visibiliū</term> <note>In medieval physiology, "spirits" were subtle fluids or gases believed to carry sensory information; visual spirits traveled from the brain through the optic nerves to the eyes</note> being blocked in the optic nerves or pressing upon the membranes of the eyes) is to be feared, for eye diseases are as much more dangerous as the eye excels the other external senses. Betony should thus be drunk crushed with warm water, 
Before the chills that signal the onset of fever occur.
An ounce of betony dissolved in a cup of warm liquid
Benefits those with dropsy <note>Edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</note> if it is drunk frequently.

Quickly purges humors through vomiting, just like hellebore. 
Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author of a massive encyclopedia of natural history</note> prescribed four drachms of these roots

Phlegm especially will be purged by this vomiting. 
The powder of the leaves drunk with honey-water benefits the ruptured <note>Those suffering from hernias</note>,

<margin>What an abscess, gangrene, and cancer are; a memorable example.</margin>

When this is taken with warm [water], they are purged, etc. An abscess <term>vomica</term> <note>original: "Vomica"; in medieval medicine, an internal abscess or ulcer that eventually ruptures and discharges pus</note> is a gangrene, which is a cancer beginning to form when the flesh already turns to a sallow or greenish color; but it is not yet truly a cancer, because that is a terrifying and incurable disease. 

Regarding this disease which is called an abscess or gangrene, Pliny recounts a memorable example in the seventh book of his *Natural History*. For he says that a certain man named Pheraeus <note>Referring to Jason of Pherae, a Greek tyrant</note> was seized by this disease and abandoned by his physicians. Because he was weary of life, he sought death in the heat of battle, but instead, he found his cure through a wounded chest; he was healed by his enemy <note>The legend states that an assassin's sword lanced Jason's internal abscess, saving his life instead of killing him</note>. 

Therefore, to return to the subject at hand, this herb purges <gloss>that is, cures</gloss> a "living" <gloss>that is, cruel</gloss> abscess—taken <gloss>that is, by taking a drink of this herb</gloss> with warm [water] <gloss>that is, with tepid water understood</gloss>. When drunk with wine, it resists poisons,
Especially the seed; but if that is missing, the herb itself is given.
Betony alone, taken with warm wine,

Indeed, a bean’s weight of the powder, swallowed with honey

¶ When taken with wine, etc. Betony drunk with wine resists—<gloss>that is, it provides a remedy</gloss>—against poisons taken—<gloss>that is, consumed</gloss>. The herb itself, drunk with <term>mead</term> <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of honey and water</note>—<gloss>that is, with honeyed water</gloss>—releases—<gloss>that is, draws out</gloss>—the menses. Indeed, the powder of dry betony swallowed—<gloss>that is, absorbed</gloss>—after dinner with honey in the weight of one bean helps the stomach so that it digests well—<gloss>that is, suitably</gloss>—the nourishment—<gloss>that is, the food consumed</gloss>. And the authors of medicine say that a leaden color—<gloss>that is, a livid complexion</gloss>—goes away—<gloss>that is, recedes</gloss>—by this herb being drunk quite often with wine, and that a better—<gloss>that is, natural</gloss>—color is recalled—<gloss>that is, restored</gloss>—by its being drained—<gloss>that is, by the drink of this herb betony</gloss>.

# Concerning the herb Savine. 94.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of the Savina plant (Savin Juniper). It depicts a central trunk with several branching stems spreading upwards and outwards, terminating in jagged, needle-like leaves. The illustration is contained within a rectangular double-line border.</image-desc>

*Brateos* <note>original: "Brateos"; the ancient Greek name for the plant</note> is its name in Greek; it is called **Savine** in Latin <note>original: "savina"; the Savin Juniper, *Juniperus sabina*</note>.
Its inherent power is both dry and heating, and it is ranked in the third degree <note>In the Galenic medical system, herbs were classified by four degrees of intensity; the third degree indicates a very potent or "sharp" medicine</note> for both of these qualities.
When applied with honey, it powerfully dries out a **wound** or an **ulcer** and purges away their filth <note>original: "sordes"; referring to pus or morbid discharge from a sore</note>.
In this manner it also assists with **anthrax** <note>original: "antracas"; here referring to a carbuncle or a burning, blackened skin lesion</note>, which the common folk call "good ulcers" <note>likely a euphemistic name used by commoners to ward off the evil of the disease or because the sores were seen as "good" for discharging bad humors</note>.
It draws forth the menses and extracts corrupted conceptions <note>referring to a dead fetus or a miscarriage</note> from the womb <note>original: "aluo"; literally "belly," but here specifically the uterus</note>, if it is taken frequently with wine.
Alternatively, it may be ground first and placed beneath the opening of the womb <note>original: "matrici subditur ori"; likely referring to its use as a pessary</note>.
When mixed into <term>cerates</term> <note>original: "cerotis"; a medical ointment or salve made of wax, oil, and medicinal herbs</note>, it causes the skin to become bright and shining.

<summary>This page describes the Savin Juniper (Savine), emphasizing its "hot and dry" nature and its use in treating skin infections like anthrax and purging the womb of "corrupted" remains.</summary>

<keywords>Savine, Juniperus sabina, wounds, ulcers, anthrax, women's health, menstruation, cerates, Galenic degrees</keywords>

It is beneficial for all diseases caused by cold;
A decoction of it in wine or water cures
Those whom vertigo harms, if the head is washed with it.
Also, the herb, having been cooked, may be bound to the temples and forehead.
The author **Oribasius** <note>original: "orisbasius"; a 4th-century Greek medical writer and personal physician to the Emperor Julian</note> commands that double the amount of **Savine** be put in if **cinnamon** is lacking in medicines.

**¶ Brateos. etc.** He concludes with a few words concerning Savine and its properties. **Pliny** <note>original: "Plinius"; referring to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*, a foundational Roman encyclopedic work</note> in Book 24, Chapter 11, calls this *sabina*; he enumerates its strengths and power in a way that in no way differs from our author. It is called *brateos* in Greek and **savine** in Latin. Its "force"—that is, its quality—is warming <gloss>that is, hot and dry</gloss>. It is in the third degree <note>In the Galenic system, the "third degree" indicated a very potent medicine that could cause significant changes in the body’s temperament</note> in both <gloss>both hot and cold</gloss> <note>The Latin text here says "cold" (frigido), which is likely a scribal error for "dry" (sicco), as Savine was universally classified as hot and dry in medieval herbals</note>. It also purges filth <gloss>that is, sanies, or bloody discharge</gloss> and helps <gloss>that is, cures</gloss> **anthraxes** in this way.

An **anthrax** <note>original: "autrax"; in this context, it refers to a carbuncle or a malignant, burning skin lesion rather than the modern bacterial disease</note> is a small abscess at the beginning, similar to a pustule, formed from malignant and poisonous matter that corrupts the neighboring parts; these anthraxes the good people <gloss>that is, the common folk</gloss> call <gloss>that is, name</gloss> "ulcers." **Savine** draws down <gloss>that is, provokes</gloss> the **menses** and extracts conceptions <gloss>that is, corrupted or dead children</gloss> from the belly <gloss>that is, in the mother's womb</gloss> if it is taken frequently with wine, or if the herb itself is first ground and placed beneath the opening <gloss>that is, in the orifice of the womb</gloss>. The herb itself, when mixed with **cerates** <gloss>that is, plasters brought to a more solid and harder form</gloss>, makes the skin of the body <gloss>that is, the outermost surface</gloss> beautiful <gloss>that is, shining</gloss>. It is also beneficial for all diseases ma[de...] <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it likely refers back to "diseases made from cold" mentioned in the first line</note>

f i

<margin>What dizziness is.</margin>
...diseases caused by cold; a decoction of that herb <note>referring to Savine, described on the previous page</note> in wine or in water cures those whom **vertigo** <note>original: "vertigo"; from the Latin *vertere*, to turn</note>—that is, that suffering of the brain—harms or disturbs. It is called this because those who are oppressed by it judge all things to be moved in a whirling motion. It arises, moreover, from a vapor moved or agitated in the anterior part of the brain; since the **common sense** <note>original: "sensus communis"; in medieval science, this was the faculty located in the front of the brain that integrated information from all five senses</note> is located there, it thinks all things are turning in a circle. In such a case, let the head be washed with the decoction, or let the herb, once cooked, be bound to the forehead and temples of the patient suffering from dizziness. **Oribasius** <note>original: "Oribasius"; a prominent 4th-century Greek physician</note>, an authority on medicine, commands that double—that is, two parts—of savine be put into medicines (that is, in the preparations of medications) if **cinnamon** <note>original: "ciniama"; a highly prized and expensive imported spice</note>—that is, the aromatic spice which we call cinnamon—is lacking or deficient. **Pliny** <note>original: "Plinius"; refers to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*</note> records that in medications, savine used at double the weight has the same effects as cinnamon.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Savine to a new chapter dedicated to the medicinal properties of the Leek. It begins with a verse-based herbal guide attributed to Macer Floridus, followed by a prose commentary that identifies classical authorities like Hippocrates and Pliny the Elder.</meta>

In many cases, the use of the **leek** <note>original: "porri"; the common leek, *Allium porrum*</note> as a medicine 
Is said by **Hippocrates** <note>original: "ypocrates"; the famous Greek physician of antiquity</note>, the greatest author of medicine.
He gave the juice of this [leek] alone to be drunk 
By the sick: those who bring up (or spit out) blood,
Whom the Greeks are accustomed to call **hemoptics** <note>original: "emoptoicos"; from the Greek *haimoptysis*, referring to the coughing up of blood from the respiratory tract</note>.
Others give this with **gall-nut** <note>original: "galla"; a growth on oak trees used for its astringent properties</note> or with the flour of **frankincense** <note>original: "thuris"; a resin used in both incense and medicine</note>; 
Or they take one **drachm** <note>original: "dragmis"; a unit of weight roughly equal to 3.5 grams</note> of leek seed 
Along with two [drachms] of **myrrh** <note>original: "mirre"</note> and dissolve the ground mixture in wine.
They say that, taken this way, it helps more than all other remedies.
You can also restrain blood flowing from the nose
If you smear the inside of the nostrils with such a medicine.
You will heal the **distressed womb** <note>original: "confractas vulvas"; likely referring to injuries or tears resulting from childbirth</note> with the same juice.
Often, the [leek] makes young women **fertile** <note>original: "fecundas"</note> if it is chewed.
Ground with honey and applied, it helps ulcers.
If there should be a "dripping" of **humors** <note>original: "humorum"; in medieval medicine, bodily fluids that must be kept in balance</note> from the head,
Harming the lungs and the chest, and the melodic voice,
From which a cough arises that hurts the **vitals** <note>original: "precordia"; the region of the diaphragm and the heart</note>,
It will be helpful to sip leek juice with **barley-water** <note>original: "ptisana"; a medicinal decoction made from pearl barley</note>. 
And the juice itself is beneficial for all these things even on its own.

¶ "In many cases, etc." The **leek** <term>original: "porrus"; an herb among the vegetables</term> is an herb counted among the vegetables which are suited for eating by the common people; therefore, because its use is so frequent, its specific properties and nature ought to be better known. **Pliny** <note>original: "Plinius"; referring to Pliny the Elder's *Natural History*</note>, in Book 20, Chapter 4, discusses the "cuttable" leek <note>chives or green leeks</note> and the "headed" leek <note>bulbous leeks</note> copiously and elegantly, and our author follows in his footsteps. Macer says, therefore: Hippocrates, the greatest authority on medicine, says the "use"—that is, that he used the medicine of the lee- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "porri" at the end of the page</note>

<summary>This page outlines the medicinal uses of the leek, particularly its efficacy in stopping internal and external bleeding and treating respiratory issues. It cites Hippocrates as a primary source for using leek juice to treat those coughing up blood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal applications of the leek (*porrum*), focusing on its ability to stop bleeding, treat uterine injuries, increase fertility, and soothe respiratory ailments caused by the descent of humors from the head.</meta>

...ry in many cases, that is, in various illnesses, he [Hippocrates] gave the juice of the **leek** <note>original: "porri"</note> to be drunk alone—that is, without the admixture of any other medicine—to the sick who "throw back" or spit out (that is, emit through spitting) gore, which is to say blood. This is the condition that Greek authors are accustomed to call **hemoptysis** <note>original: "emoptoicos"; the coughing up of blood from the respiratory tract</note>. 

Other doctors give (that is, administer) this juice with **gall-nut** <note>original: "galla"; an oak growth used as a powerful astringent</note>—which is very styptic, that is, constrictive—or with the flour of **frankincense** <note>original: "thuris" or "olibani"</note>. Or some apply one **drachm** <note>a unit of weight, about 3.5 grams</note> of myrrh to twin (that is, two) drachms of leeks, and they dissolve these, once ground, in wine. Doctors say that these things, when taken (that is, consumed), help those spitting blood more than all other medicines.

Furthermore, you can restrain (that is, stop) blood flowing from the nose if you smear the inside of the nostrils with such a medicine.

(Blood flows from the nostrils because of the opening of some vein adjacent to the nostrils; this opening happens—unless caused by an external force—either by an excessive surplus of blood or by its immoderate "sharpness" or acidity). 

You will cure (that is, heal) damaged wombs suffering a **loss of continuity** <term>solutionem continui</term> <note>a technical medical term for a physical tear, wound, or rupture in the tissue</note> with this same juice of the leek.

of the

(The rupture of the womb happens either by the impact of some harmful thing, or from a difficult labor, or from the flow of sharp **humors** <note>the bodily fluids that medieval physicians believed governed health</note>).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal properties of the leek, moving from its use in treating internal bleeding and uterine issues to its role in treating "catarrh" (the descent of humors from the head) and various other ailments including poison, earaches, and lung issues. (And **Hippocrates** <note>original: "hypocrate"; the ancient Greek "father of medicine"</note> is extolled with countless praises for this.) And the **leek** <note>original: "porrum"</note> itself, by itself—that is, without the mixture of any other thing—is beneficial for all these illnesses. Such a descent of humors from the head is called **catarrh** <note>original: "catarrus"</note> by doctors as a general name. It happens either from a "resolving" heat, or a "constricting" cold, or from a burdensome abundance of fluid. However, according to the variety of the organs to which the descent of humors occurs, it takes on various names, as is sufficiently declared in these verses:

-> If it flows to the chest: let it be called **rheumatic catarrh**. <-
-> To the throat, **branchus** <note>original: "brancus"; hoarseness or a sore throat</note>: to the nostrils, I call it **coryza** <note>original: "corizam"; a head cold or runny nose</note>. <-

If you cook the white parts of the leek in wine in such a way that you change the first water and add a second, a potion of that water is useful for a **constipated stomach** <note>original: "duro... ventri"; literally "hard belly"</note>. This same preparation is said to bind the bowels when taken with wine. **Leek** is given here with wine to those whom a snake has harmed, or any animal pouring forth lethal poison. It is no less beneficial for these if you apply it over the wounds.

If [applied] like a **plaster** <term>emplastrum</term> <note>a medicated paste spread on cloth and applied to the skin</note>, leek kneaded with honey [is effective]. Leek juice mixed with **breast milk** <note>original: "lacti muliebri"</note> and drunk is said to heal a chronic cough, and the same remedy aids various ailments of the lungs. It settles pain of the ear when mixed with **goat's gall** <note>original: "capre felle"</note>. It also works equally well ground with **mead** <term>mulsa</term> <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of honey and water</note> and poured warm into the ear. It withstands **poisonous mushrooms** <note>original: "fungis"</note> if it is eaten. Let a third part of honey be joined to its juice, and thus by pouring it warm through the nostrils or ears...

**A**thenum <note>original: "Athenum"; likely a variation of *Anthemis*, the Greek name for chamomile</note> the author Asclepius <note>original: "Asclepius"; referring to the Greek god of medicine or more likely a medical writer such as Asclepiades of Bithynia</note> commends with great praise; 
we call it chamomile <note>original: "camomillam"</note> or *comomomilla*. 
This is a very fragrant, though short, herb. 
It is so similar to an herb which the common folk 
rightly call *amarista* <note>original: "amaristam"; likely stinking mayweed (*Anthemis cotula*), which resembles chamomile but has an unpleasant odor</note> because it stinks and is bitter, 
that when gathered, they can scarcely be distinguished by their scent. 
Authors say there are three species of this plant, 
which they teach can be distinguished by the color of the flowers alone; 
it is reported that for all of them, the center of the flower is golden.

That is, they say they can be distinguished—that is, the difference between them is assigned—by the color of the flowers alone. The middle of the flower is golden—that is, of a golden color—in all those three species; but that golden flower placed in the middle is surrounded by various—that is, diverse—petals <note>original: "folijs"; literally "leaves," but in medieval botany referring to the floral rays or petals</note>. And if you bathe a feverishness with it cooked in oil.

<margin>The source of head pain in fevers.</margin> In fevers, especially those depending on "hot matter," hotter and sharp, biting, and smoky vapors rise up, distressing the brain and its membranes <note>original: "panniculos"; referring to the meninges, the layers protecting the brain</note> in an extraordinary way. Or, the fever produces ulcers—I say this regarding ulcers that are gathered together; the Greek calls these *exanthemata* <term>exanthemata: skin eruptions or rashes, often seen in eruptive fevers</term>. Green—that is, fresh—boiled chamomile repels—that is, cures—these same eruptions. 

<margin>What eruptions are and from where they arise.</margin> (These are pustules on the head formed from the "boiling over" of the humors <note>humors: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval medicine believed governed health</note>, specifically when choleric humors <term>choleric humors: the "yellow bile" humor, considered hot and dry</term> ascending upwards cannot be imperceptibly released because of the thickness of the skin. Instead, being congested there, they raise the skin with their sharp quality and create pustules on the head). But if green chamomile is lacking, let the dry plant itself be macerated—that is, ground up—with vinegar, and let one smear—that is, anoint—the head with that liniment; no ointment helps head pains more.

### ¶ 15. On the herb catmint <note>original: "De nepta herba"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a plant labeled as catmint. It features a thick, horizontal, gnarled root from which several long, smooth, upright spear-shaped leaves emerge in a fan-like arrangement, contained within a simple rectangular border.</image-desc>

> **T**he herb which we call *catmint* <note>original: "neptam"</note> in the common fashion,
> the physicians call by the Greek name *calamentum* <term>calamentum: calamint, a medicinal herb in the mint family often confused with catmint in early texts</term>.
> Its inherent power is both drying and heating; and it is ranked
> in the third degree <term>third degree: a classification in medieval medicine indicating a high level of potency in a plant's qualities of heat or dryness</term> of both qualities. 
> Often taken with lukewarm honey-water <term>mulsa: a mixture of water and honey used to deliver medicine</term>,
> it is accustomed to release the entire body in sweat.
> Anoint by rubbing with the oil (in which the herb has been boiled)
> that man who fears the chills of a coming fever;
> it often repels not only the chill, but the fever itself.
> For him who suffers from sciatica <term>scyasim: a Greek-derived term for pain in the hip or sciatic nerve</term>, apply it upon the hip.

> Crushed green, it is said to burn the skin
> And thus, by drying the humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was thought to determine health</term>, to chase away pains.
> Applied as a plaster or in its drink, it quickly purges the menses <term>menstrua: menstrual flow</term>.
> And that type of leprosy which is called elephantiasis <term>elephantiasis: a severe skin condition in medieval medicine, named for its resemblance to an elephant's thick, wrinkled hide</term>,
> Which seems to be greater than all other diseases,
> Just as the elephant wanders larger than all animals.
> In its early stages, a drink of calamint
> Taken with wine cures this disease by thinning the humors.
> Crushed and applied, snake bites are no longer
> Harmful; and taken with wine, it expels poisons <gloss>and</gloss>.

¶ The herb which, etc. The poet begins to describe the powers and nature of calamint. Doctors say—that is, they name—this herb by the Greek name *calamentum* <note>original: "calamentum"</note>, which the Latins call by the common name <gloss>that is, the common [vulgar] name</gloss> *catmint* <note>original: "neptam"</note>. Its power is dry <gloss>and</gloss> heating—that is, hot and dry. It is of the third degree <term>third degree: a classification in the Galenic system indicating a plant has very strong, potentially irritating effects</term>. Therefore, the herb itself will consist more of heat than cold. When taken—that is, often drunk with lukewarm honey-water <gloss>that is, with honey-water</gloss>—it is accustomed to resolve—that is, to transform—the whole body—that is, the humors of the body beneath the skin—into sweat <gloss>into sweat</gloss>. Rubbing him who fears the chills of a coming fever—that is, gently rubbing—with oil in which catmint has been boiled; this oil does not only repel the cold—that is, alone—but also the fever itself. Apply green crushed catmint to the hip of one who suffers from sciatica <gloss>that is, "the drop" [gout] or arthritis</gloss>. This herb, thus crushed, is reported—that is, said—to burn the skin—that is, the hide—by the great intensity of its heat, and to chase away—that is, cure—sciatic pains by drying—that is, desiccating—the humors from which the sciatic pain is born. (Furthermore, this kind of pain arises from a downward flow of humors in the joint of the hip.) The catmint written above.

<margin>What a pessary is.</margin>
...placed in the manner of a pessary <term>pessary: a small, often oblong-shaped medical plug inserted into the vagina to deliver medication or provide support</term>, which should be in a smooth, oblong form; and it is the same for the female genital organs as a suppository is for the posterior parts. And **catmint** <note>original: "nepta"</note>, I say, in its drink—that is, by drinking it—purges, that is, brings forth the menses. There is a certain species of leprosy, and it is called by physicians **elephantiasis** <note>original: "elephantiasis"</note>, which is seen to be greater—that is, more serious—than all other diseases. Concerning

<margin>What leprosy is.</margin>
leprosy, indeed, Avicenna <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina) was a foundational Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> says: since cancer, which is the leprosy of a single limb, is among those things for which there is no cure, what shall we say of leprosy, which is a cancer of the entire body? Just as the elephant is seen to be larger than all animals. Leprosy, says Avicenna, is an evil infirmity arising from the spreading of **black bile** <term>black bile: one of the four bodily humors; in excess, it was thought to cause melancholy or, if "burnt," severe diseases like leprosy</term> throughout the entire body, whereby the complexion of the limbs, their form, and their shape are corrupted, and finally, their continuity itself. There are several species of it, among which one is called *elephantia* or *elephantiasis* by more recent physicians. (However, Avicenna says that *elephantia* is an enlargement in the feet, according to the manner that happens with varicose veins; Pliny also seems to feel the same. And they assert it is called *elephantia* from its similarity to the elephant, to which a swelling of the feet is peculiar.)

<margin>What elephantia is.</margin>
Indeed, the elephant is the largest of land animals (says Pliny), so much so that they carry towers of armed men on their backs. But let us return to our interrupted path. A drink of **calamint** <note>original: "calamenti"</note> administered—add: in the beginning of this disease, for if it is established, it does not admit a cure—taken with wine, cures this disease, elephantiasis, by thinning—that is, refining—the humors, and afterwards evacuating them. (For the humors upon which that disease depends are very thick; therefore, it is necessary to refine and thin them.) **Catmint** <note>original: "nepta"</note>, crushed and applied, does not allow—that is, does not permit—the bites of ser[pents]...

...[of the serpent] to harm, and when taken with wine, it drives out—that is, expels—poisons.
Taken as a drink or injected, its juice kills **intestinal worms** <note>original: "lumbricos"</note>;
In all the limbs of the body, the juice itself kills worms.
If honey is joined to this along with salt, it works more effectively.
If a pregnant woman drinks this, or applies it crushed as a **pessary** <note>original: "subdat"; referring to medicinal insertion</note>, she miscarries <note>original: "abortit"</note>.
It purges the jaundiced <note>original: "yctericos"</note> and, with wine, cures those who are short of breath—

And when drunk with wine, it relieves diseases of the liver.

And when crushed and applied on top, it restores black scars

Taken with wine, it checks the pain of the stomach.
Its smoke drives snakes away from houses.
Drunk with wine, it is said to take away hiccups.
And in this way, if drunk frequently, it inhibits **sexual desire** <note>original: "venerem", literally "Venus," the goddess of love, used here to mean carnal appetite</note>.

¶ **Drunk and injected, etc.** The juice of this herb, being drunk—that is, consumed—and injected in the manner of a **clyster** <term>clyster: an enema or liquid injection into the rectum used to clear the bowels or deliver medication</term> through the lower gate, kills—that is, destroys—intestinal worms <note>original: "lumbricos"</note>, which are the worms of the belly. And the juice itself kills worms in all the limbs. (For worms are sometimes generated not only in the belly, stomach, and intestines, but also in other limbs.) If honey is joined to this juice along with salt, it works more—that is, it brings about a more powerful action in drawing out the worms. If a pregnant woman—that is, a woman carrying a child—should drink this herb, or if she applies it—that is, places it beneath <gloss>crushed, that is, ground up</gloss> against the womb—she miscarries...

<meta>This page concludes the medicinal applications of catmint (nepta), detailing its efficacy in treating internal obstructions of the liver and spleen, its use in dermatology for scars, and its effects on the respiratory and reproductive systems. It then transitions to a new section on pennyroyal.</meta>

This herb causes miscarriage <note>original: "aborsum"</note>. Orators, however, say *aborso* and not *aborsio* <note>the author is noting a linguistic preference for the form of the word for miscarriage</note>. This herb also purges—that is, cures—the jaundiced <term>yctericos: those suffering from jaundice, a yellowing of the skin and eyes caused by liver bile</term> when drunk, specifically with wine. It also cures those who are short of breath—that is, those who breathe with difficulty, whom physicians call by the Greek name **asthmatics** <term>asthmatics: from "asmaticos," referring to those suffering from gasping or labored breathing</term>. And the herb itself, when swallowed—that is, drunk—with wine, mitigates—that is, relieves—diseases of the liver—that is, the *hepar*—namely, its obstruction <term>opilationem: a medieval medical term for a blockage in the internal organs or "vessels"</term> and its pains. Taken in this way, namely with wine, it aids—that is, heals—an old—that is, long-standing—pain in the side. (Indeed, if this pain distresses the right side, it arises from a defect of the liver; if on the left, from a defect of the spleen.) Furthermore, when this herb is crushed and applied over scars—that is, placed upon black or bruised scars—and boiled in wine, it restores—that is, gives back—the natural color by purging—that is, clearing away—the unnatural color. A **poultice** <term>cataplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbal material, typically heated, applied to the body</term>—that is, this herb crushed and applied with its juice—works better for these aforementioned treatments.

¶ This herb, taken with wine, checks—that is, mitigates—pain of the stomach caused by coldness. The smoke—that is, the fumigation—of this herb puts to flight—that is, drives away—serpents—that is, snakes—from houses. This herb, swallowed with wine, is said—that is, asserted—to take away—that is, remove—hiccups <note>original: "singultum"</note>—that is, the harmful, expulsive movement of the opening of the stomach. And this herb, if drunk frequently in this manner (with wine), inhibits—that is, removes—**sexual desire** <note>original: "venerem," literally "Venus," the goddess of love, used here to mean carnal appetite</note>—that is, the appetite or action of sexual business—for its excessive <note>the text reads "minima," but likely intends "nimia" (excessive) given the context of drying qualities</note> abundance of heat dries up and consumes the seed <note>original: "sperma"</note>.

### ¶ On the herb pennyroyal. 16.

The power of pennyroyal <note>original: *pullegij*</note> is said to be hot <note>The OCR "H Ervida" likely represents "Fervida," meaning hot or burning</note> and dry.
A third degree <term>gradus: a medieval system of classifying the intensity of a plant's "humoral" qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) on a scale of one to four</term> is assigned to this herb by physicians in both qualities.

Because I believe this name is not fully understood by some,

Just as the chick <note>original: *pullus*; the author is drawing an etymological link between the word for a young animal and the herb's name</note> not yet born is surrounded by the shell of an egg,

By a certain membrane made from the mother’s seed,
Which the newborn breaks as a chick breaks its eggshell;
Physicians have called these membranes the **seconds**, <term>secundas: the placenta and fetal membranes, so called because they are delivered second, after the baby</term>
Because by the law of nature they soon follow those who have been delivered.
If these should linger longer than is right because of some defect,
Either death follows, or whatever pain can be endured.
Pennyroyal <note>original: *pullegium*</note> drives these out in the way mentioned before.
If you join it, ground with salt, to honey,
You can help **contracted limbs** <term>contractis membris: muscles or joints that are stiff, cramped, or paralyzed</term> with this ointment.

¶ **Hot**, etc. Concerning pennyroyal, whose powers and temperament <term>complexio: the specific balance of humors—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—that determines a plant's medicinal effect</term> are closely related to catmint; immediately after catmint he adds this definition, which Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), whose "Natural History" was the foundational encyclopedia for medieval science</note> discusses at length in Book 20, Chapter 14. The power—that is, the temperament—of pennyroyal is said to be burning and dry. A **third degree** <term>gradus: a medieval scale of intensity from one to four; the third degree indicates a very potent herb that must be used with caution</term> is assigned by physicians to this pennyroyal in both, namely in heat and cold <note>The text mentions "heat and cold," though pennyroyal is traditionally "hot and dry"; this may be a scribal error or a reference to its dual action on different humors</note>. If a pregnant woman frequently takes the fumes of this herb, she aborts—that is, it causes a miscarriage. This herb, taken with warm wine, draws down—that is, provokes—the menses, meaning the monthly purgings of women that happen naturally every month. And the herb taken in this way (specifically with warm wine) extracts—that is, brings out—the delayed "seconds," meaning those lagging behind after childbirth. 

Then he explains what the "seconds" are; because I reckon—that is, I judge—that this name, namely "the seconds," is not known to some, I shall explain—that is, I shall declare—what is signified by such a term, meaning through this name "seconds." Just as a **chick** <note>original: *pullus*</note>... However, he should have spoken more correctly by saying *fetus*. For Valla <note>Lorenzo Valla (c. 1407–1457), a famous Renaissance scholar who wrote on the "elegancies" or correct usage of the Latin language</note> says that *fetus* refers to the young of birds, while *pulli* refers to cattle; just as therefore the chick...

<meta>This page continues the medical description of pennyroyal (Pulegium), specifically focusing on its role in childbirth and respiratory health. It uses a biological analogy between a chick and a human infant to explain the fetal membranes, and then details how the herb helps expel the afterbirth and treat muscle spasms.</meta>

fol. 51
g g

...[a chick] <note>original: *lus*, likely a fragment of *pullus*</note>, that is, a young hen newly born—that is, having come out into this world—is surrounded, that is, contained within a **shell** <gloss>in the eggshell</gloss> and the young itself. Thus, in this same way, the infant is clothed, that is, covered in the mother’s womb by a certain little skin, that is, a small membrane made from the seed—that is, from the sperm and the mother’s menstrual blood. The birthing infant breaks, that is, shatters this membrane, just as a chick, that is, a little young hen, breaks or tears the eggshell. Physicians have called these membranes the **afterbirth** <term>secundas: the placenta and fetal membranes, literally "the seconds" because they follow the baby</term>, because they follow by the law—that is, by the rule and instinct of nature—immediately, without any interval of time, the infants brought forth to the light. If this membrane should linger, that is, be delayed after the infant is born more than is right—that is, more than is natural—either death follows, or some great pain is endured by the woman who has just given birth. For if that membrane is retained longer than is fair after the birth, it easily becomes corrupted, as if it were completely abandoned by the governance of nature; therefore, evil vapors rise from it to the stomach, the heart, and the brain, from which the **suffocation of the womb** <term>prefocationem matricis: a historical condition believed to be caused by toxic vapors from the uterus affecting the rest of the body</term> must necessarily follow. 

<margin>What a spasm is.</margin>
**Pennyroyal** <note>original: *pullegium*</note> drives out, that is, leads forth, this afterbirth in the manner mentioned before—that is, when taken with wine. If you join the ground herb with honey and salt, you can help **contracted limbs**, that is, a **spasm** <term>spasmus: involuntary muscle contraction or cramping</term>, by rubbing with such an ointment. Now, a spasm is a contraction of the nerves <note>In medieval medicine, "nerve" often referred to tendons and ligaments as well as actual nerves</note> caused by a pulling back toward their origin, resulting from either "fullness" or "emptiness" <note>This refers to the humoral balance; a spasm could be caused by too much fluid or by the body drying out</note>.

Its powder, taken with honey or as a drink,
Can thin the viscous humors of the chest,
And you can healthily clear them out through phlegm.

Nausea or a gnawing of the stomach will be cured by it
When taken with **posca** <term>posca: a common ancient drink made by mixing vinegar and water</term> or watery vinegar.
Drunk with wine, it drives away **black bile** <term>coleram nigram: one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids; an excess was thought to cause melancholy or digestive distress</term>;
In this way, it resists the poisonous bites of serpents.
If, by the force of a fever or an excessive flow of blood,
Or by some sudden accident, the sick person should faint—
Which is usually called **swooning** <note>original: *malefactio*, a term used by medieval doctors for a sudden loss of strength or consciousness</note> by learned physicians—
Let **pennyroyal** ground and mixed with strong vinegar
Be held to the nostrils of the patient; by the scent
Alone, they are said to quickly revive the one lying there.
The powder of the herb, dried and burnt, strengthens the gums;
Applied fresh and green, they say it soothes **gout** <term>podagram: a painful inflammation of the joints, often the big toe</term>,
And it usually cures any other swellings
If warm **barley-meal** <note>original: *polente*</note> is mixed in and added over it.
Added to whey, it removes pimples from the body.

<margin>
What nausea is and how it arises.
</margin>

¶ **Its powder, etc.** The powder of that pennyroyal, taken or drunk with honey, can thin the **sticky fluids** <note>original: *humores viscosos*: thick bodily fluids believed to cause illness if they became trapped</note> of the chest that hinder the working of the lungs, and it can healthfully draw out those sticky fluids through **spit** <gloss>that is, by coughing up phlegm</gloss> once they have been broken down and thinned. **Nausea**—that is, a nauseating fullness—is caused by a **gluttonous overfilling** <term>crapulosa repletione: sickness or "hangover" caused by excessive eating or drinking</term> of the stomach. Sometimes it is caused by a **phlegmatic** <note>relating to phlegm, the cold and moist humor</note> fluid dulling the stomach's sensation, so that it does not desire food but rather feels sick, wanting to expel that matter; but it cannot do so because the matter clings to the linings and **fibers** <note>original: *villis*: the internal textures or "hairs" of the stomach wall</note> of the stomach itself.

<margin>
Concerning the gnawing of the stomach.
</margin>

**Nausea**, therefore, or the gnawing of the stomach—which **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon, the most influential physician of the Roman era</note> says arises from an imbalance of **red bile**...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of pennyroyal's medical applications, specifically addressing issues of the stomach, blood loss, and fainting (syncope). It references classical authorities like Galen and medieval figures like Johannes Mesue to explain why blood is vital to the body's "nature."</meta>

...evaporated upward, whose bitterness causes a gnawing of the heart <note>original: *cordis*; in this context, referring to the "pit" of the stomach or heartburn</note>—that is, a stinging of the stomach—and a bitter mouth) [the ailment] will be cured by that herb taken with **posca**.
<margin>What **posca** is.</margin>
(Some say **posca** <term>posca: a common ancient and medieval drink made by mixing vinegar with water or other liquids</term> is the washing of grape skins; others say it is honey mixed with vinegar), or taken with vinegar that has been made watery—that is, mixed with water.

This herb, drunk with wine, draws out (that is, expels) **black bile** <term>coleram nigram: also known as melancholy; one of the four humors believed to govern health and temperament</term>; and, drunk with wine, it resists (that is, provides relief for) venomous bites (that is, of serpents). 

If the sick person should faint (that is, if their strength appears entirely failed and prostrated by the force of a fever—that is, through the symptoms or very severe "accidents" of fever which, as **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note> says, sometimes overthrow one's strength) and by a flow (that is, an excessive or superfluous flux of blood), which is the most powerful cause of this kind of prostration—since blood is the "son of nature" and that which nature retains until the very end as its treasure, as **Johannes Mesue** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a Persian-Syriac physician whose works were foundational to medieval European pharmacy</note> says; or by some other case (that is, a sudden event or symptom). 

<margin>Fainting, lethargy, dissolution, and syncope from the flow of blood.</margin>

This state is usually called "failing of the spirit" by learned physicians—or, to add more terms: **lethargy** <term>hyppotomia: likely a variant of lipothymia, a medical term for a sudden loss of strength or fainting</term>, **dissolution**, or **syncope**. Let crushed (that is, ground) **pennyroyal** be joined with potent vinegar (that is, strong and of a sharp bitterness). Let it be applied in this way to the nostrils of the sick person (that is, the one suffering the syncope). Physicians say it relieves (that is, awakens) those lying there (that is, as if dead) by the mere scent of the pennyroyal itself mixed with strong vinegar. 

The powder of pennyroyal, dried and burnt (that is, charred), strengthens (that is, tightens) the gums. (For the gums are made of soft, porous flesh that is easily affected, which suffers a **breach of continuity** <note>original: *solutionem continui*; a medical phrase for any separation of tissue, such as a cut, ulcer, or receding line</note> from even a small internal or external cause, from which a flow of blood follows.) 

Physicians say that the green herb itself, when applied, soothes (that is, cures) **gout of the feet** <term>podagra: a painful inflammation of the joints, historically associated with the feet</term>, which is an ailment in the feet just as **gout of the hands** <term>chiragra: gout specifically affecting the hands</term> is in the hands. Now, gout (as Galen is the authority) occurs when superfluous humors descend to the feet; whence excessive...
