<meta>This page continues the medical properties of pennyroyal, moving from internal digestive and circulatory issues to the treatment of gout and skin conditions. It transitions from prose into a rhythmic list of remedies for various organs and ailments, citing classical authorities to bolster its claims.</meta>

Rest, excessive exercise, immoderate wine, and likewise sexual intercourse are no small hindrance to this passion <note>original: *passioni*; here referring to the "suffering" of gout</note>. For this reason, eunuchs do not suffer from **gout** <term>podagra: a painful form of arthritis, often affecting the feet, historically associated with rich diets and "immoderate" living</term>, nor do boys before **aphrodisia** <gloss>that is, puberty</gloss>. Nor does a woman suffer from it unless her **menses** <note>menstruation</note> have ceased, as **Hippocrates** <note>Hippocrates of Kos (c. 460–370 BC), often called the "Father of Medicine"</note> is a most reliable witness. It is, however, quite difficult to cure; whence **Ovid** <note>Publius Ovidius Naso (43 BC–17 AD), the Roman poet</note> says:

> Medicine knows not how to loosen knotty gout.

And this herb is accustomed to amend <gloss>that is, to cure</gloss> any **tumors** <gloss>that is, swellings</gloss> if the herb itself is mixed with **polenta** <gloss>that is, flour diluted in water</gloss>. For it is called "polenta" from *poline* <gloss>that is, flour</gloss>. If applied warm over swellings—specifically, if this herb is added to **cerates** <gloss>that is, more solid plasters</gloss>—it removes **papules** <gloss>that is, pustules or pimples</gloss>. Whence **Virgil** <note>Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BC), the Roman poet; this quote is from his *Georgics*, describing a plague</note> says:

> Burning papules and foul-smelling sweat 
> followed the limbs.

From this, the **nipples** <gloss>the heads of the breasts</gloss> were named, because they are similar to papules. Therefore, it removes papules from the body.

Applied as a poultice, it is very beneficial for those with **spleen disorders** when mixed with salt.
If anyone is frequently washed in a warm **decoction** <note>a liquid made by boiling the herb</note> of it, 
He shall not suffer from itching.
And in this way, you will be able to cure pain of the **womb**.
Taken with warm wine, it checks a **cough**.
And thus it compels one to pass a large amount of **urine**.
And it is more effective if the decoction is made from wine,
Or if you apply the crushed herb mixed with oil.
If the crushed root is joined with sharp vinegar,
It will grant [relief] to those tumors to which it is applied.
Taken with wine, it resists the **venom of serpents**,
And they claim that when taken in this way, it **stimulates Venus** <note>original: *venerem stimulare*; increases sexual desire or libido</note>.

# ¶ On the herb Fennel. 17.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a fennel plant (Feniculum vulgare) within a double-line rectangular frame. The plant features several stalks with serrated, feathery leaves growing from a stylized mound of earth. At the top of the central stalk is a single rounded flowering head or seed pod.</image-desc>

Physicians say that **fennel** <note>original: *feniculum*</note> is hot and dry in the **second degree** <term>second degree: a classification in the "humoral" system of medicine where plants were rated 1 to 4 based on their perceived intensity</term>; the Greeks call it **marathron** <note>original: *maratrum*</note>.
Taken with wine, this herb resists all poisons, and they declare that, when consumed in this way, it stimulates **Venus** <term>Venus: a poetic reference to the Roman goddess of love, indicating the herb acts as an aphrodisiac to increase sexual desire</term>.
By chewing this herb, the **serpent** <note>Ancient naturalists like Pliny the Elder claimed that snakes ate fennel to sharpen their sight after shedding their skins, a myth that led to fennel’s use in eye treatments</note> clears its eyes of **dimness** <gloss>that is, a clouding or darkening of the vision</gloss>.
From this, it was discovered that fennel could heal human eyes, and it has been proven through experience.
The juice of the roots is likewise beneficial when the eyes are anointed with it mixed with **honey** <note>original: *melle*</note>.

**Vocabulary:** Fennel, marathron, physician, poison, Venus (desire), serpent, dimness, honey.

# ¶ On Fennel. Chapter 17.

They believe the name **Fennel** <note>original: *Feniculum*</note> is derived from **fervor** <note>original: *feruore*; heat</note>, because when its seed is committed to the earth, it returns its fruit with great heat. In this chapter, the author suggests the **complexion** <term>complexion: in medieval medicine, the balance of hot, cold, moist, and dry qualities</term> and properties of the plant. Physicians say that fennel is **hot and dry in the second degree**. The Greeks call it **marathron**. This herb, when taken with wine, resists all poisons. The **serpent** <note>Ancient lore, popularized by Pliny, claimed snakes ate fennel to sharpen their sight after shedding their skin</note> cures its own eyes of **dimness** <gloss>that is, darkness</gloss>; for that reason, it was discovered that this herb could heal <gloss>that is, assist</gloss> the **lights** <gloss>that is, the human eyes</gloss>, and it has been proven through experience. 

Furthermore, I say that the juice of the roots of fennel—when the eyes are thoroughly anointed with honey mixed with the fennel juice—restores them <gloss>namely, the eyes</gloss> to be clear; I say this once all **dimness** <gloss>that is, darkness</gloss> is driven away. The juice of marathrum expressed from the green seed of marathrum and dried in the sun <gloss>that is, in the sun</gloss> is held to be <gloss>that is, it is made into</gloss> a great <gloss>that is, excellent</gloss> medicine for whichever diseases of the eyes you wish to drive out <gloss>that is, expel</gloss>. The juice of this herb poured into the ears kills <gloss>that is, slays</gloss> the worms that are generated in them <gloss>as mentioned above</gloss>. 

The root of this herb boiled in **barley water** <gloss>that is, in water of barley</gloss> heals <gloss>that is, helps</gloss> the kidneys <gloss>that is, those with kidney disease</gloss>. This herb, when drunk, cures **dropsical** <term>dropsical: relating to dropsy (edema), a condition of swelling caused by excess fluid</term> tumors <gloss>by drawing them out through the passages of urine</gloss>; and this herb, when drunk, resists <gloss>that is, counteracts</gloss> venomous bites; and it also <gloss>that is, even</gloss> resists <gloss>that is, treats</gloss> complaints <gloss>that is, diseases</gloss> of the lungs and [chest]... <note>The text ends abruptly with "reco.", likely a truncation of "reconditis" or "recolligit".</note>

# Sorrel

**Oxidmus** is the little **sorrel** <note>original: *acedula*; a name derived from its sour taste</note> which the Greek calls **oxion** <note>original: *oxionē*; from the Greek *oxys*, meaning sharp or acid</note>.
It is believed to be named so because its flavor is like that of **vinegar**.
It grows in sandy meadows and alongside rivers.
People eat this greedily indeed during the springtime,
and it has been proven that it takes away **nausea** <note>original: *fastidiat*; a loss of appetite or loathing for food</note>.
Its property is drying and very cold;
a **third degree** <note>Galenic medicine classified herbal potencies from one to four; the third degree indicates a very potent effect</note> is assigned to this herb by physicians in both qualities.
With this herb applied, **sacred fire** <note>original: *sacer ignis*; a historical term for erysipelas, a painful, red skin infection</note> and **biting herpes** <note>original: *herpeta mordax*; likely referring to spreading skin rashes or shingles</note> flee.
And swelling leaves the eyes when it is crushed and used as a **plaster**.
It restrains **creeping ulcers** and heals **burns**,
and they say it greatly benefits **hot gout** <note>original: *podagre*; specifically inflammatory arthritis of the foot</note>,
if the area is covered with its green leaves,
or if they are applied as a plaster mixed with crushed **barley meal** <note>original: *polenta*; in this context, peeled and toasted barley flour</note>.
Its juice, rubbed on with **rose oil**,
is said to benefit a long-standing **headache**,
which pain is called **cephalalgia** in the Greek tongue <note>original: *cephalarga*; a general term for head pain</note>.
It is known to wondrously restrain every kind of **flow of the belly** <note>referring to diarrhea or dysentery</note>
when it is drunk with wine or chewed frequently.
In this way, it also stops excessive **menstrual flow**,
or if you apply the crushed green herb over the **womb**.
Thus it also expels **roundworms** when drunk;
likewise, it is useful against all **poisons** when consumed.
Its juice clarifies the **eyesight**,
and drunk with wine, it resists various **venoms**.
They affirm that whoever carries this herb upon their person,
a **scorpion** will not seek to strike them with a lethal sting.

### ¶ Concerning Purslane, etc. 

Pliny says: Purslane, which they call *peplion* <note>original: *peplion*; a name used by ancient authors for various sprawling plants, including purslane.</note>, though not very flavorful <note>original: *fatua*; meaning bland or tasteless.</note>, is highly effective. Applied as a plaster, it extracts the poison of arrows, and treats hemorrhoids and the bite of the *prester* <note>original: *prester*; a mythical or real serpent whose bite was said to cause swelling and intense thirst.</note>. The *prester* is indeed a type of serpent.

...moist and cold in the second degree. If this herb, crushed while green, is placed over the stomach, it cures the fever which Greece—that is, the Greek language—calls *causon* <note>original: *causon*; an acute, "burning" fever characterized by intense heat and thirst.</note>. This fever is caused by red choler <note>original: *colera rubea*; one of the four bodily humors, traditionally associated with the element of fire.</note> that is fiery, sharp, and putrefied in the veins of the whole body, or in the veins of the heart, the mouth, the stomach, or the liver. It may also be caused by salty phlegm <note>original: *flegmate salso*; a variant of the phlegm humor believed to be irritating and hot rather than cooling.</note> putrefied in the veins that serve the regions of the heart, as the leading authority Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose *Canon of Medicine* was the standard medical textbook in medieval Europe.</note> states. It is called *causon*—meaning "intense"—because it far exceeds all other types of fever in its burning heat and fire.

The juice of this herb performs the same effect. If this herb is taken chewed—that is, eaten or consumed as a pot-herb, just as vegetables are usually eaten—it is accustomed to soothe (mitigate) the heat of fever. Furthermore, the herb itself, when chewed or drunk, can restrict an excessive flow of blood and check (stop) an immoderate flux of the belly <gloss>that is, diarrhea</gloss>.

Purslane, when crushed (masticated), prevents the teeth from being "set on edge" <note>original: *stupor dentium*; the tingling or sensitive sensation caused by acidic foods or cold.</note> for those who easily suffer from that dental discomfort.

The herb itself, when crushed and applied to swollen eyes, is beneficial. It does not permit the fervor—that is, the heat—of the sun to do harm in the summer time. When the herb is taken with wine and soft salt, it softens the belly. Also, when eaten, it is accustomed to settle pains of the bladder and those arising from "sharp" urine. # Chapter 20. On the herb Lettuce.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a lettuce plant within a double-line rectangular frame. The plant features a cluster of large, broad leaves with serrated, wavy edges at the base. From the center, several stems rise and branch out, bearing smaller, pointed leaves and rounded flower or seed heads.</image-desc>

The medicinal power of lettuce is known to be very cold and moist <note>original: *Frigida... & humida*; in the system of humoral medicine, lettuce was used to balance "hot" and "dry" temperaments or illnesses.</note>.
For this reason, when chewed, it can relieve excessive heat <note>original: *nimios... calores*; referring to fevers or internal "burning" sensations.</note>.
It will provide the same benefit if you apply it externally as a well-crushed poultice.
It is beneficial for the stomach, induces sleep <note>original: *somnum dat*; lettuce contains lactucarium, a milky sap sometimes called "lettuce opium" for its mild sedative effects.</note>, and softens the bowels <gloss>acts as a gentle laxative</gloss>.
For all these purposes, it is even more effective when eaten cooked.
The seed of lettuce curbs "vain dreams" <note>original: *somnia vana*; likely referring to nightmares or nocturnal emissions, as lettuce was traditionally used as an anaphrodisiac to dampen sexual desire.</note>.
When drunk with wine, it also restrains the flow of the bowels <gloss>restrains diarrhea</gloss>.
If taken frequently by a nursing woman, it causes milk to flow abundantly.
As some say, dimness of the eyes <term>caligo<note>A historical medical term for blurred or clouded vision.</note></term> is also cured by it.

**Vocabulary:** lettuce, herb, sleep, stomach, nurse, dimness of vision.

¶ Some authors report that there is a certain kind of spurge <note>original: *tintimali*, referring to *tithymalus*, a genus of plants often containing toxic milky sap.</note> which they call "goat lettuce." If one writes on the body with its milky sap and then sprinkles ash over it, letters will appear; it is said that in former times, adulterous women used this secret method of writing. However, this lettuce is vastly different from the one the author is about to describe. For the first is poisonous, while the other is truly very healthy, as will become clear in the following poem. Its power <gloss>that is, its "complexion" or humoral makeup</gloss> is cold and moist. It is well established <gloss>that is, it is inherent</gloss> in lettuce; therefore, when chewed <gloss>that is, eaten</gloss>, it can relieve <gloss>that is, cool</gloss> excessive heat, namely the "boiling of the blood" <note>original: *ebullitionem sanguinis*; a medieval medical concept where blood became overheated, leading to fever or agitation.</note>. And it provides the same <gloss>that is, the same effect</gloss> if you apply it, well-crushed, to an overheated limb. This herb is very <gloss>that is, greatly</gloss> useful for the stomach; it gives <gloss>that is, induces</gloss> sleep, and softens <gloss>that is, relaxes</gloss> the bowels. It is more beneficial for all the aforementioned things when eaten cooked <gloss>that is, if it is eaten after it has been boiled</gloss> than if it were raw. Lettuce seed <margin>Definition of sleep.</margin> overcomes <gloss>that is, takes away</gloss> dreams <gloss>that is, "phantasms" appearing in sleep that are vain, i.e., frivolous</gloss>. For a dream, as Aristotle says, is a certain phantasm appearing in sleep; that is, an appearance made in sleep from the motion of existing things and from the images <note>original: *simulacrorum*; the mental "species" or images stored in the memory.</note> preserved in the organ of the internal sense. The varieties of dreams, <margin>Whence the variety of dreams arises.</margin> moreover, are explained by this very beautiful and manifest analogy by the easily foremost of philosophers <note>Referring to Aristotle.</note>. Just as, he says, when a small stone is thrown into a river, motions are created, which first form ripples from which larger ones then rise; and thus from one to another a derivation is made over a long stretch. But if any obstacle <term>offendiculum<note>Literally a "stumbling block" or hindrance; here used to describe something that breaks the flow of water or thought.</note></term> is encountered, it is divided into other shapes. So when we dream, we pass immediately from one motion of an image into another, and from that one again into another, until according to the sequence it reaches the final image of that order; but if some obstacle intervenes, it turns into others which...

The rose is rightly seen by us to be called the flower of flowers,
By which hope it surpasses all flowers in beauty and scent.
Yet it is not only by its appearance nor its scent that it helps 
Us, for it also assists us with various remedies.
Its power is dry and cold in the first degree.
It restrains the "sacred fire" <note>original: *sacrum ignem*; this refers to Erysipelas, a skin infection also known as St. Anthony's Fire.</note> if it is applied crushed.
Likewise, if the stomach is hot, it calms both it and the chest.
It binds the flow of the womb and of the belly <note>original: *fluxum matricis... et alui*; referring to menstrual discharge and diarrhea.</note> when taken with wine.
Many eye-salves <term>colliria: liquid or semi-liquid medicinal washes for the eyes</term> require its juice,
And the powder of the dried rose relieves diseases of the mouth well,
Whether applied by itself or mixed with honey.
When crushed and applied on top, it restrains all inflammations.
The fresh flower, or if it is mixed with honey-water <gloss>mead</gloss>,
From this an oil is made and is called "rose oil,"
Useful for diverse diseases and for many causes.

¶ It says, "The flower of flowers," etc. There is no one who does not know that the rose precedes all other flowers by a long interval in both beauty and excellence. Concerning its utility and fragrance, Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), a Roman author and natural philosopher.</note> determines this in Book 21, Chapter 4. Therefore, the author first bestows much praise upon it: namely, that the rose is seen by us to be called the "flower of flowers" by excellence and by right <gloss>that is, not without a legitimate reason</gloss>. Because <gloss>since</gloss> it precedes all flowers in appearance <gloss>beauty</gloss> and odor <gloss>fragrance</gloss>. However, this rose is not only able to help us through its appearance or scent, but rather it assists us with varied remedies <gloss>diverse medicines</gloss>.

Truly, as Mesue <note>Mesue the Younger, a name attributed to an influential 13th-century author of a Latin pharmacy manual.</note> says, the red roses are much better than the white ones. Its path—that is, its complexion <term>complexion: the balance of the four "humors" or qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that determined health in medieval medicine</term>—is cold and dry.

...toothache <gloss>original: dolorem dentis</gloss>. (Which pain is indeed located in the space of the gums along with the root of the tooth because of the delicate nerves entering the tooth; it occurs because of fluid matter flowing there, whether hot or cold. This oil is also said to make the eyelids—that is, the parts covering the eyes—which are hard—that is, hardened and swollen either because of the sting of some venomous animal, the scent of hot things, or some thick humor—soft—that is, to bring them back to a state of softness). And it is accustomed to help in various "hysteric" <gloss>istericis</gloss>—that is, uterine <note>The term *matricialibus* refers to the *matrix* or womb; "hysteric" conditions were historically believed to originate in the uterus.</note>—causes—that is, illnesses. 

Since this oil varies in its preparation—that is, it is prepared in various ways—I shall say what Palladius <note>Palladius Rutilius Taurus Aemilianus, a 4th-century Roman author whose agricultural works included pharmaceutical recipes.</note> himself, skilled in medicine, wrote about the method of making that oil. Take flowers of rose petals that are red; let one ounce <gloss>uncia</gloss> of olive oil <gloss>olei olivarum</gloss> be joined to one pound of rose flowers that have been cleaned of their stems. Placing all these things—namely the roses and the oil—together, hang them in a glass vessel and expose it for seven days only—that is, solely—under Phoebus—in the sun. Thence—that is, afterward—let it be kept for various—that is, multiple and diverse—uses. For as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon, the primary authority on medical theory in the Middle Ages.</note> says: rose oil cools things that are heated and warms things that are cold <note>original: *calefacta frigida & calefacta infrigidat*; the Latin here is slightly repetitive or corrupted in the transcription, but it refers to the Galenic belief that rose oil could balance opposing temperaments.</note>.

# **Lilies**

**Silver roses** <note>original: *rosas argenteas*; likely referring to the white lily, *Lilium candidum*</note> (as I believe them to be called)
The lilies rightly follow, which in neither appearance nor scent
Are believed to yield when compared to the rose’s beauty;
Nor are they less suitable in many remedies for mankind.
Its roots and the bulb that the lily produces,
Cooked under hot coals <gloss>original: *sub prunis*</gloss> and also ground with olive oil joined to it,
Wondrously benefits burns <gloss>original: *vsturis*</gloss> from fire when applied on top.
And it is even better if rose oil is mixed with it.
It softens hardened wombs <term>matrices <note>From the Latin *matrix*; here referring to the uterus. In medieval medicine, "hardening" of the womb referred to various inflammatory or obstructive conditions.</note></term> if applied in the same way,
As well as if a **poultice** <term>cathaplasmate <note>A soft, moist mass of herbs and flour applied to the body to relieve soreness or inflammation.</note></term> is made from its leaves.

They soften the sinews <note>The Latin *nervous* here refers to both nerves and tendons/sinews.</note>, and burned limbs are helped.
Likewise, the bite of a snake will be cured when [the bulb] is applied,

In this way, it also prevents the poisons of mushrooms from doing harm.

¶ The lily is the whitest flower, second in nobility only to the rose, and linked by a certain kinship to the ointment called *lilion* <note>original: *lilion*; an ancient lily-based perfume or oil mentioned by Pliny</note> which is named after it. No flower reaches a greater height. This is stated by Pliny in book 21, chapter 5, with whom our author agrees. And to deviate slightly from the purpose: I would believe by divine providence that these same lilies were sent to the most Christian King of the Franks to serve as his insignia (as the annals report). For just as lilies excel all other flowers in height, fragrance, and beauty, so the King himself stands far more sublime than all other princes in earthly wealth, nobility, and virtues. 

But we must return to the structure of the text. "Silver lilies"—that is, white like silver—should by right (justly) follow "golden roses"—that is, those of a golden color. These lilies, when compared (matched) to the beauty (loveliness) of the rose, are believed not to yield (to not be inferior) in appearance (beauty) or scent (fragrance) to the roses themselves; nor are they less apt (suitable) in many remedies (medicines) for sick men. Regarding the "bulb": this refers to the onion-like root of the lily. For as **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard European textbook.</note> says, a bulb is a small onion that is eaten; and because the lily root has a shape very similar to an onion, the author calls it a bulb "covered under embers"—that is, under glowing ashes.

Embers and charcoal differ.

"Also"—that is, even when ground—I say that when olive oil (the oil of olives) is joined (added) to it, it benefits burns...

<margin>The multiple meanings of "nails" <note>original: *clavi*</note></margin>
The word "nails" <note>original: *clavi*</note> is used by authors in various senses. Sometimes it designates a type of garment distinguished by certain "golden nails" <note>original: *clavis aureis* - referring to the *clavus*, a decorative stripe on Roman tunics</note>; only senators or consuls were dressed in this. "Nail" <note>original: *clavus*</note> is also named from "closing" <note>original: *claudendo*</note>; for it is fashioned for the purpose of closing or containing something. We also call the rudder of a ship a "nail" <note>original: *clavum*</note> because it directs the ship and keeps it on a straight course. Likewise, from the resemblance to this [physical] nail, a certain type of callous lump is called a "nail" <gloss>a corn</gloss> which grows on various parts of the body, but most especially on the feet. And it is in this sense that it is now used by our author. But let us return to the path we left: apply, therefore, this bulb to the "nails" <gloss>corns</gloss> of the feet; and "do not loosen"—that is, do not remove—this root of the lily or bulb crushed with wine until the third light <gloss>the third day</gloss> passes: unless three...

Therefore, let us understand that "it heals cut nerves" means it eases the pain of nerves suffering breaches of continuity; for a puncture of the nerves, as Galen says, is ready to cause a spasm on account of their great sensitivity, and because the nerve is a part that is continuous with its origin <note>The brain or spinal cord</note>. And for this reason, Avicenna says of the lily that it is good for the cutting of nerves; we wrote something about spasms above. The liquid juice of the leaves, squeezed out <gloss>that is, drawn out by the pressure of hands</gloss>, mixed with vinegar and cooked with honey <gloss>that is, with honey</gloss>—let the parts of honey and vinegar be made so that they match the herb <note>original: *gramen*, which literally means "grass" or "herb," but here refers to the weight of the herbal liquid</note> <gloss>that is, in that weight which was equal to the "gramen," meaning the water or juice pressed from the herb</gloss>, and let there be five parts of the aforementioned liquid <gloss>that is, juice</gloss>. Nothing is more useful <gloss>that is, more beneficial</gloss> for old wounds, and it leads <gloss>that is, induces</gloss> a scar.

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on the healing properties of the lily, moving from the restoration of nerves to the formation of scar tissue, the treatment of skin conditions, and the use of lily-flower juice for gynecological and general health.</meta>

For indeed, there is a certain mark that remains from a wound. Abscesses <term>apostemata: inflammatory swellings or boils filled with pus</term> which end by way of maturation are first ripened, then, after an opening is made, they are cleansed; afterwards, the lost flesh is restored, then they are made firm <gloss>consolidated</gloss>, and finally, they are scarred over. Because the skin, which is a spermatic member <note>As noted on the previous page, "spermatic members" like skin and nerves were thought to be non-regenerative because they were formed from the original reproductive "seed."</note>, has suffered a loss, it cannot be truly restored to its original state. Therefore, as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a thousand years.</note> says, something similar to skin—namely, callous flesh—must be produced. It then produces a scar through excessive drying. 

Even if they are long-standing <gloss>that is, stained by long passage of time</gloss>, the bulb <gloss>that is, the root of the lily itself</gloss>, boiled and mixed <gloss>that is, blended well</gloss> with solid ointments <gloss>that is, wax-based salves</gloss>, stretches <gloss>that is, relaxes</gloss> and smooths facial wrinkles and drives away <gloss>that is, removes</gloss> all spots on the body. That bulb cures "lichens" <note>original: "lychenas," likely referring here to scaly skin eruptions or fungal infections</note>, which is a very severe kind of disease that begins on the chin. Wherefore the ancients called it *mentagra* <note>original: "mentagram," a skin disease of the chin, from "mentum" (chin) and "agra" (seizure/prey)</note>. 

This is just like that most foul contagion which previously exercised its cruelty against the French <gloss>Gallos</gloss>, and has not yet ceased its service, and is called "shameful" <note>A reference to the *morbus gallicus* or "French Disease" (Syphilis), which was a major epidemic in Europe at the time of this writing.</note> because it mostly takes its origin from the shameful parts <gloss>pudenda</gloss> of the body. That former disease <note>Mentagra</note> used to infect through the custom of kissing; this one <note>Syphilis</note> contaminates for the most part through venereal contact. The former was first imported from Asia into Italy; this one was spread from Italy into France <gloss>Gallia</gloss>. 

It also <gloss>that is, furthermore</gloss> purges <gloss>that is, cleanses</gloss> the face of scales <gloss>that is, a cloth-like texture of the skin</gloss> and freckles. Juice is squeezed from its crushed flowers <gloss>that is, drawn out by pressing</gloss>. This juice from the crushed flowers of the lily softens <gloss>that is, mollifies</gloss> the wombs <gloss>that is, the matrices</gloss> when placed beneath them <gloss>that is, inserted</gloss> with wool in the manner of a pessary <note>A medical device or medicated plug inserted into the vagina.</note>. That juice, when smeared on <gloss>that is, rubbed in</gloss>, is asserted <gloss>that is, said</gloss> to be able to induce sweat. Finally, that juice used with wool matures swellings <gloss>that is, abscesses</gloss>...

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on healing wounds and skin conditions. While the previous page focused on the lily's ability to treat scars and "mentagra" (a chin infection), this page begins by defining the biological process of "maturation" or ripening in both plants and the human body, before transitioning to a new entry on the herb Savory.</meta>

<margin>
What maturation is.
</margin>

...ripened. Maturation, says Aristotle <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC) was a Greek philosopher whose works on natural history and biology formed the basis of medieval science.</note>, is a digestion <term>digestio: in this context, the "cooking" or processing of substances by the body's internal heat</term> performed regarding the nourishment of fruit and the fruits themselves. A little later he adds that many other things are called "ripe" by a certain metaphor <gloss>original: translatione; the carrying over of a word's meaning from one thing to another</gloss> related to this type of maturation, because suitable names do not occur to us by which we might call them. For example, abscesses <term>apostemata: inflammatory swellings or boils</term> are said to be "ripe" when the pus <note>original: sanies; a thin, often blood-stained discharge from a wound or sore</note> settles; afterwards it "concocts" <gloss>that is, digests and matures</gloss> and purges the pus quickly, that is, with haste.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Savory. 22.

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut illustration of the plant Satureia (Savory). The image shows a single plant with a thick central stem branching out into several secondary stems, all covered with small, narrow, lance-shaped leaves.</image-desc>

<sig>i. j.</sig>

<vocab>Aristotle, maturation, digestion, abscesses, pus (sanies), concocts, savory herb</vocab>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on Savory (Satureia) introduced at the end of the previous page. While the previous page defined the general nature of maturation and the plant's appearance, this page provides specific medicinal recipes and historical lore regarding its name and association with the god Apollo.</meta>

Savory is usually called *timbia* in Greek and *satureia* in Latin.
Its power is less dry, but it is exceedingly hot.
Taken with wine, it provokes urine and the menses;
It expels a premature birth <note>original: "abortiuum"; referring here to the expulsion of a fetus, often in the context of a stillbirth or a necessary medical evacuation</note> and purges the female organs.
The powder of this plant, worked into cooked honey
And chewed, or likewise if it is taken with wine,
Drives out the stubborn humors of the chest through spitting.
Taken with warm wine, it calms the "torments" <term>tormina: severe, griping abdominal pains or cramping</term> of the belly,
Provided it is drunk in a large dose—indeed, I have often proven this.
There is truly a disease called "lethargy" <note>In medieval medicine, lethargy was viewed as a "cold" brain disorder characterized by excessive sleep and forgetfulness.</note>,
Which doctors say is the opposite of "frenzy" <note>original: "frenesi"; a "hot" disorder of the brain causing delirium and insomnia.</note>.
While frenzy forbids sleep, lethargy weighs the sick man down with it;
For one depressed by such a lethal sleep, let him mix Savory
With vinegar and frequently bathe the head with it.
In this way, he can drive away the vice of that sluggish disease.

<margin>
Apollo
Thymbraeus.
</margin>

¶ "Savory is usually..." etc. This herb, which is called both *satureia* and *timbia*, was held in great honor among the pagans <gloss>original: "gentiles"; non-Christians, specifically the ancient Greeks and Romans</gloss> chiefly because it was frequent and abundant in the field where a temple was consecrated to Apollo. For this reason, Apollo himself—the discoverer of the medical art, as Ovid <note>Publius Ovidius Naso (43 BC – 17 AD), the famous Roman poet.</note> says—was called "Thymbraean" <note>original: "tunbreus"; an epithet of Apollo derived from Thymbra, a place near Troy famous for his temple and the growth of this herb.</note>. *Timbia* in Greek—that is, so called by a Greek word—is usually called *satureia* in Latin. Its power—that is, its complexion <term>complexio: the specific balance of the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) in a substance</term>—is less dry, but it is "fervid," which means very hot. However, it is asserted by other authors to be hot and dry in the third degree <term>third degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating a very potent effect, just below "fourth degree" (toxic/caustic)</term>. When taken with wine, it provokes urine and purges the menses. It also expels—that is, leads out—an aborted child, meaning one suffocated in the mother's womb. It purges the viscera—that is, the secret members...

<summary>This page details the medicinal uses of Savory (Satureia) for respiratory, digestive, and gynecological issues, and its specific use in treating "lethargy." It also explains the herb's mythological association with Apollo Thymbraeus as recorded by Ovid.</summary>

<keywords>Savory, Satureia, Timbia, Apollo Thymbraeus, Ovid, lethargy, frenzy, humoral medicine, third degree, women's health</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the medicinal application of Savory (Satureia or Thymbra), specifically focusing on its ability to expel fluids from the body and its use in treating neurological conditions like lethargy by counteracting "cold" humors. It concludes the section on this herb with a linguistic and mythological diversion into the origins of the names for sleep-related illnesses.</meta>

...destined for the female reproductive system <note>original: "generationi... muliebria"; referring to the organs of generation in women</note>, that is, for women. If any surplus remains after birth—such as the afterbirth <term>secundinas: the placenta and membranes expelled after delivery</term>—the powder of that herb, worked—that is, ground—with cooked or boiled honey and chewed—that is, eaten—is effective. Or, if the same powder is taken with wine, it drives out—that is, leads away—the stubborn, viscous humors of the chest through spitting. If the juice itself is drunk in a large, copious dose with warm wine, it calms—that is, settles—the "torments," which are the twistings of the belly. I have proven this many times in the past. 

A certain disease is called by its name, "lethargy," which doctors say is the opposite—that is, the contrary—of "frenzy." This is because while frenzy forbids—that is, prohibits—sleep, this lethargy weighs down the sick man with sleep, that is, with an excessive depth of slumber. The poets tell a story that there is a river in the underworld whose waters, if anyone tastes them, cause him to forget all past things. As the poet Lucan says: *Near which the silent river Lethe flows, drawing deep forgetfulness through the underworld, as rumor has it.* <note>original: "Lucanus. Quā iuxta lethes..."; a reference to the River Lethe in classical mythology, which the author links to the medical term lethargy.</note>

<margin>
What lethargy is
</margin>
From this name is derived "lethargy." It is a disease that induces a very profound sleep and forgetfulness, arising from a thick, cold, and viscous phlegm occupying the "stern" <note>original: "puppim"; the posterior part or occiput</note> of the brain.

<margin>
What frenzy is
</margin>
Regarding "frenzy," some would have it derived from *frena*—that is, from the membranes of the brain that "rein it in" <note>original: "frenibus... frenātibus"; the author is playing on the Latin word for a horse's reins, *frena*, to explain the brain's membranes.</note>. However, this is not approved by the learned, for it is not very consistent for a Greek name to draw its origin from a Latin one <note>The author correctly notes that "phrenesis" is Greek, while "frena" is Latin, making the etymology unlikely.</note>. Therefore, if it is true frenzy, it is an abscess <term>apostema: a localized collection of pus or a swelling</term> of the brain arising from bloody or bilious <note>original: "corica" / "cholerca"; relating to yellow bile</note> matter. But "false" frenzy is merely a collection of fumes above the brain.

But let us return to the text. For a sick man overwhelmed—that is, oppressed—by such a lethal sleep—that is, one so forgetful or death-like—let him mix Savory <note>original: "tybiā"; a variant of *thymbra* or Savory</note> with vinegar and frequently bathe—that is, soothe—the head with it. 

<margin>
<unclear>Note regarding Savory</unclear>
</margin>

From such a liniment, he will be able to drive away, in this manner, the fault and the error of the disease that induces such sluggishness—that is, laziness.

<summary>This page describes Savory's use in postpartum care and its effectiveness against "lethargy," which it defines through classical mythology and medieval humoral theory. It distinguishes between lethargy (a cold disease) and frenzy (a hot disease), providing etymological and anatomical explanations for both.</summary>
<keywords>Savory, Satureia, childbirth, afterbirth, lethargy, frenzy, Lethe, Lucan, brain anatomy, humors, phlegm, vinegar</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the herb Savory (Satureia/Thymbra). The previous page concluded a section on treating lethargy; this page shifts to the herb's effects on pregnancy, digestion, and its famous reputation as an aphrodisiac, including the mythological origin of its name.</meta>

A pregnant woman should beware of touching this herb,
For not only when eaten, but even when applied beneath, it expels the offspring.
Nausea is calmed by this if it is taken well-ground
With a little wine, or if it is taken in an egg.
And in this way it is wonderfully accustomed to stir desire <note>original: "venerem"; literally Venus, but used here to mean sexual appetite</note>
If it is drunk in a large draught, whether fresh or dry;
And if honey and pepper are joined to it with wine,
It is said to kindle no small fire of passion.
Hence it is rightly called *satureia* after the satyrs,
Because these animals are excessively prone to passion.
If thyme is lacking, doctors instruct to use *thymbra* <note>savory</note> in place of thyme:
Because the power of the two is equal.

**"This herb"** etc. **"A pregnant woman,"** that is, heavy with child: let her beware of touching <gloss>contingere</gloss> this herb. For, that is, because it not only when chewed <gloss>mansa</gloss>, but also when applied beneath <gloss>subdita</gloss>—that is, placed under the womb—it expels <gloss>pellit</gloss>, that is, leads out the offspring <gloss>partum</gloss>, that is, the embryo <term>embrionem: the developing fetus</term>.

**"Nausea,"** that is, the desire to vomit, is calmed, that is, quieted by this, if savory is taken well-ground with a little wine, or if its powder is taken on a soft, sippable egg <term>ouo tremulo et forbili: literally a "trembling" egg; a soft-boiled egg that can be slurped</term>.

It is accustomed to stir <gloss>mouere</gloss>, that is, promote desire <gloss>venerem</gloss>, that is, the libidinous appetite, "wonderfully," that is, greatly, in the following way: if the herb itself is green <gloss>viridis</gloss>, that is, fresh, or dry, with a large <gloss>largo</gloss>, that is, abundant drink.

It is said, but if honey and pepper are joined to that savory with wine, it is "said," that is, asserted, to "kindle" <gloss>succendere</gloss>, that is, to rouse a "fire" <gloss>ignem</gloss>, that is, a blaze of no small passion.

**"Hence,"** that is, for that reason, it was called *satureia* after the satyrs "rightly" <gloss>merito</gloss>, that is, legitimately, because these animals are "prone" <gloss>prona</gloss>, that is, inclined, "excessively" <gloss>nimis</gloss>, that is, beyond measure, toward passion. The Satyrs inhabit the deserts of Ethiopia, having nothing of human character besides their shape...

<unclear>Fa</unclear>uns go about naked; they have goat's feet and surpass <gloss>prestant</gloss> all animals in the speed of their running. They are called Satyrs for this reason: because they are always burning <gloss>efferuent</gloss> with desire <note>original: "venere"; the Latin text uses the name of the goddess Venus to represent sexual passion</note>. For the Greeks called the male member <note>original: "satyr"</note> by that name. The ancients worshiped them as woodland gods. Whence Ovid says: "I have demigods, I have rustic deities, and nymphs. And there are mountain-dwelling fauns and satyrs." <note>original: "Sunt michi semidei sunt rustica numina nymphe. Et fauni satyrique monticole"; a quote from Ovid's Metamorphoses, Book I, where the gods discuss the protection of lesser nature spirits.</note>

If thyme is lacking <gloss>desit</gloss>, that is, is missing: doctors instruct us to use <gloss>ponere</gloss>, that is, to take, *thymbra*, which is savory. Its power <gloss>vis</gloss>, that is, its constitution <term>complexio: the specific balance of hot, cold, wet, or dry qualities that determined a plant's medical use</term> and property, is equal <gloss>equa</gloss>, that is, similar in those two, namely savory and thyme.

<meta>This page continues the new chapter on Sage (Salvia) from the previous page, moving from the title and illustration into the instructional verses of Macer Floridus, followed by a detailed scholarly commentary citing Renaissance and Classical authorities like Marsilio Ficino, Avicenna, and Galen.</meta>

Sage, whose name is *eliphagus* <note>original: "eliphagus"; a Latinization of the Greek *elelisphakos*, a name for sage</note> among the Greeks <note>original: "apud argos"; literally "among the Argives" or Greeks</note>,
When drunk with mead <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of honey and water</note>, it benefits complaints of the liver.
It drives out the fetus, promotes urine <gloss>lotium</gloss>, and purges the
menses.
Pounded and applied, it cures venomous bites.
For raw wounds that flow with much blood,
They say the pounded herb, when applied, holds back the blood.
If its juice is taken warm with wine,
It settles the stomach, a cough, and abdominal pain.
It cures itching of the vulva and the male member <gloss>virge virilis</gloss>,
If you bathe them with wine in which sage has been cooked.
They say hair turns black from its juice,
If it is frequently rubbed on in the hot sun.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial letter 'S' featuring a serpentine or floral motif coiled within a square frame.</image-desc>

¶ **Sage, whose name is, etc.** How much sage is to be valued among health-bringing herbs, the name itself suggests quite enough. For Sage is so named because it heals <note>The commentator is playing on the Latin verb *salvare* (to save/heal) and the name *Salvia*</note> diverse diseases. Indeed, Marsilio Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was a famous Italian scholar and physician influential in the Renaissance</note>, an excellent physician and orator, commends it not a little for prolonging life: "We also approve of sage," he says. "For it temperately warms the power of nature, strengthens it, and drives away paralysis." Our author passes over the <term>complexio: the specific balance of hot, cold, wet, or dry qualities that determined a plant's medical use</term> of this herb in silence. For Avicenna and Galen have by no means revealed a certain degree for its constitution. Concerning it, Avicenna says: "Sage varies according to species and according to the constitutions of individuals; its power is entirely maturing <note>original: "maturativa"; in medieval medicine, this refers to ripening an abscess or "cooking" raw humors</note> and resolving." Galen, however, speaking of sage, says: "The constitution of this herb is hot with a manifest and sufficient heat, and slightly astringent <gloss>stiptica</gloss>." But let us set aside other authors for... <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "ad" (to/for)</note>

<meta>Google digitization watermark at bottom of page. "ad" at the bottom right is a catchword for the next page.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the detailed scholarly commentary on the herb Sage (Salvia), following the introductory verses. ...our little interpretation returns to our subject. **Sage**, which among the Greeks <gloss>original: "apud argos"</gloss> is named *eliphagos* <note>original: "eliphagos"; a Latinization of the Greek *elelisphakos*, a common ancient name for sage</note>, when drunk <gloss>consumed</gloss> with mead <gloss>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of honey and water</gloss>, is beneficial <gloss>useful and medicinal</gloss> for complaints <gloss>pains</gloss> of the liver <gloss>original: "iecoris / epatis"</gloss>. Sage expels <gloss>drives out</gloss> from the womb <note>original: "utero"</note> a dead fetus in the mother’s belly, and it purges the <term>lochum<note>The text glosses this as urine, though in medical Latin "lochia" typically refers to the discharge after childbirth</note></term> <gloss>urine</gloss> and the menses of women. Moreover, pounded sage added on top cures venomous bites <gloss>inflicted by poisonous animals</gloss>. 

For some poisons are from animals, like the viper <note>original: "typus"; likely a reference to the *tyrus* or tyrus-snake</note> and the scorpion. Others are from plants, like <term>monkshood<note>original: "napellus"; a highly toxic species of Aconite</note></term> and <term>white hellebore<note>original: "helborus albus"; used in antiquity as a purgative but dangerous in high doses</note></term>; some are from minerals, like <term>realgar<note>An arsenic sulfide mineral</note></term> and quicksilver <gloss>mercury</gloss>. Indeed, poison is entirely contrary to our life by its whole nature. For just as food is converted into the substance of our body and made similar to it, so too poison converts our body—or the part to which it is applied—into its own nature, making it poisonous.

Apply pounded sage to raw <gloss>recent</gloss> wounds; for physicians say above that the herb itself holds back the blood. If its juice is taken warm with wine, it suppresses <gloss>settles</gloss> an old cough <gloss>one troubling a person for a long time</gloss>. This same sage cures <gloss>heals</gloss> itching of the male member and the vulva <gloss>the womb</gloss>. For just as a person is said to "itch" when the skin is troubled by a rash <note>original: "emperigie"; likely a corruption of *impetigine*, a scaly skin eruption</note> and is affected by a desire for scratching, so too someone is said to "itch" when moved toward sexual desire and the seed begins to be moved from the body so that it may be expressed; in the context of the male member, this is named *lentigo* <note>While *lentigo* usually means freckles, the author here uses it to describe a specific irritation or arousal</note>. Sage, therefore, cures itching of this kind if you bathe <gloss>wash by way of a compress</gloss> these private parts in wine in which sage has been cooked. 

Authors say that hair grows black <gloss>becomes darker</gloss> from the juice of that sage, if the hair is frequently <gloss>often</gloss> anointed with this juice under a hot sun that pours down its heat.

# On the herb Lovage. xxv.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a Ligusticum (lovage) plant. The plant features a central stalk with several branches of serrated, lobed leaves. At the upper left, a rounded, umbel-like flower cluster is shown. The entire illustration is contained within a rectangular double-line border.</image-desc>

From its homeland of Liguria <note>original: "Ligur"; a coastal region of northwest Italy known in antiquity for this plant</note>, the "Ligustica" <note>Lovage</note> took its name.
The "greater loppia" <note>original: "loppia maior"; an archaic botanical name for lovage</note> of that herb is born there.
It possesses a third degree <note>In the Galenic system of medicine, herbs were classified by "degrees" of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness on a scale of 1 to 4</note> of dry and hot
->potency.<-

The power <gloss>medicinal efficacy</gloss> of its root and its seed is greater [than the leaves],
and their power is equal in any medicinal preparation.
Taken with wine, it heals a bloated stomach,
and by such a drink, the digestive power <note>original: "vis degustiva"; though it resembles the word for "tasting," the context of stomach ailments implies the power of digestion</note> is aided.
In this way, it is beneficial for all diseases of the internal organs.

<summary>This page introduces Lovage (Ligusticum), explaining its geographical origins in Liguria and its classification as a "hot and dry" herb. It highlights the use of the plant's seeds and roots, specifically for treating bloating and aiding digestion.</summary>

<keywords>Lovage, Ligusticum, Liguria, medieval medicine, digestive health, herbal seeds, roots, Galenic degrees</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Lovage (Ligusticum) from page 69. Having previously described its qualities and digestive benefits, the author now lists further medicinal uses before providing a geographical and historical commentary on the herb's namesake region, Liguria.</meta>

## Taken this way, it promotes urine and cleanses the menses.
## The whole herb heals the bites of venomous creatures,
## If it is drunk, having been well-crushed beforehand and dissolved in wine,
## And if a portion of the crushed herb is placed upon [the wound].
## This mass <note>original: "māsa"; a prepared medicinal paste or mixture</note> is of no small benefit to those with colic, whether swallowed
## Or if a decoction <note>original: "elixatura"; a liquid produced by boiling the plant</note> of the root is drunk.
## Nor will that decoction benefit only those with colic,
## But when swallowed, it will heal all the aforementioned [ailments].

---

<margin>What Liguria is</margin>
¶ These words, whose name is lovage <note>original: "ligusticia"</note>, took their name from Liguria, as Macer <note>Macer Floridus, the traditional name attributed to the author of the 11th-century poem *De viribus herbarum*</note> says. Now, Liguria is a region of Italy situated between the rivers Var and Magra, the capital of which is Genoa. This city, only a few days ago, the most triumphant King of the Franks, Louis <note>Refers to King Louis XII of France, who conquered Genoa in 1507; this helps date the commentary</note>, brought under his own rule by the force of war. For indeed, in times long past, the Ligurians were carried away into the greatest pride of spirit, a fact to which our Maro <note>The Roman poet Virgil, whose full name was Publius Vergilius Maro</note> is a most excellent witness; for he censures their madness in his *Aeneid* in this way: *O vain Ligurian, puffed up to no purpose by your proud spirit.* <note>original: "Vane ligus frustraque aīs elate superbis"; a quote from Book 11 of Virgil's Aeneid</note> Yet this vain loftiness of mind, which had prevailed for so long among mortals, has been turned into gentleness by the most favorable omens of our king. Truly, those bordering the Ligurians (with God's favor) are soon to receive a similar fate by the same arms and the same leader, as is only fair. But perhaps we speak of matters more lofty than befits this very modest wisdom <note>original: "minerve"; literally 'Minerva,' the goddess of wisdom, used here as a metaphor for the author's scholarly scope</note>. Therefore, let us return immediately to the point from which we digressed. The lovage herbs themselves, of which we were speaking above, took—that is, claimed for themselves—their name from the homeland of the Ligurians because a "copia"—that is, an abundance—of that herb is born—that is, produced—there.

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal profile of Lovage (Ligusticum). The previous page concluded a general list of its uses; this page specifies its elemental qualities (the "degrees" of heat and dryness), provides a grammatical note on its name, and details its efficacy against digestive issues, venomous bites, and colic.</meta>

There in Liguria, it is greater than in other regions. In the third degree of hot vigor—that is, heat—and dry—that is, dryness—this herb's power—that is, its virtue—is greater—that is, more effective. Of the root and the seed of this herb (lest you stumble at all in your grammar, <term>ligustricum</term> <note>Lovage</note> is of the neuter gender and does not vary in the singular; however, in the plural we say *ligustica*). And the power—that is, its virtue—is par—that is, equal—to those things, namely the root and the seed. This herb, taken with wine, heals—that is, provides a remedy for—a swollen stomach,

<margin>Against bloating of the stomach</margin>

which bloating indeed proceeds from a weakness of the digestive power. And the digestive power—that is, virtue—is helped by such a drink, and this herb taken in this way benefits all internal diseases, especially those arising from a cold cause. Taken this way—that is, with wine—it moves—that is, draws out—urine and cleanses the menses. This whole herb—that is, from its whole species or taken all together—heals venomous diseases if it is drunk having been well-trushed—that is, diligently—before it is drunk, and dissolved—that is, pressed out—with wine—that is, with wine. And a portion of the crushed herb—that is, that which remains after the juice is pressed out with wine—should be placed upon a venomous bite. This herb, chewed or swallowed, is of no small benefit to those suffering from <term>colic</term> <note>original: "collicis"; a painful condition of the abdomen</note> (which inflicts such bitterness that it is called by some "Lord have mercy on me" <note>original: "miserere mei deus"; referring to the 'Miserere' or iliac passion, an archaic term for severe bowel obstruction</note>; we have said something of this above). This decoction will not only benefit those with colic—that is, those suffering from the disease—but when swallowed, it will heal—that is, give aid to—all the aforementioned ailments, namely digestive weakness and venomous bites.

**Its seed is well-joined to all**
**Digestive antidotes: because it contains a digestive property within.** <note>original: "pectica ytus"; derived from the Greek "peptikos" (digestive) and "entos" (within)</note>
**This herb is harmful to crossed eyes, both in drink and in use.** <note>original: "strabus"; referring to squinting or misalignment of the eyes</note>

<summary>This page defines Lovage as a plant of the 'third degree' of heat and dryness, emphasizing its origin in Liguria and its potent effects on digestion and venomous wounds. It includes a specific warning that despite its benefits, the herb can be harmful to those with eye afflictions.</summary>

<keywords>Lovage, Liguria, digestion, colic, venomous bites, humoral theory, third degree, medieval grammar, Miserere mei, eye health</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Lovage (Ligusticum). The previous page ended by describing its efficacy against colic and the severe pain associated with it. This page shifts to the specific use of its seeds in compounds and the vital medical principle of "digesting" humors before purging them.</meta>

He asserts that only the **seed** of this herb 
should be mixed into antidotes. Whether he said this 
on his own authority or from the books of the learned 
is not known to me. This I do know: that the ancients 
extol this herb with no small praise, nor do I recall 
reading anyone who agrees with the words of **Strabo** <note>likely referring to Walahfrid Strabo, a 9th-century monk and botanical author</note>.

¶ **Regarding digestive remedies**, etc. Its seed is joined well—that is, suitably—to all digestive antidotes—that is, those suitable for "digesting" the offending matter <note>In medieval medicine, "digesting" a disease meant ripening or preparing the harmful "humors" so they could be safely expelled from the body</note>. These include **syrups**, **juleps**, and several **electuaries** <note>medicinal pastes made with honey or sugar</note>. This is because its **peptic power**—that is, its digestive power—is effective for this purpose (Mattheus Silvaticus <note>a famous 14th-century medical lexicographer</note> says that "peptics" are things that produce or aid digestion).

<margin>Why doctors provide digestives before purgatives</margin>
¶ For this reason, digestive remedies are administered by doctors before **pharmacy**—that is, laxative medicine—so that the matters causing the disease (without the expulsion of which a true healing is never achieved, as **Galen** says) may be prepared for easy expulsion. For as **Avicenna** <note>the famous Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> says: this is what renders the body secure from pain during the process. Therefore, that great father of doctors, **Hippocrates**, wishing this sentiment to sit deeper in our minds, said: *Treat and move those things which are digested, not those which are raw* <note>original: "Digesta medicari et movere non cruda." This is a fundamental rule of ancient medicine: never try to purge a "raw" or "unripe" sickness</note>. And shortly after, he brings forth almost the same sentiment: *When one wishes to purge bodies, it is necessary to make them fluid*. However, this Hippocratic rule is violated everywhere by many who should be counted among the most savage executioners rather than students of health-bringing medicine.

<margin>Error of common physicians.</margin>
¶ For as soon as they approach anyone who is sick, with whatever labor or disease, and with whatever **offending humor** <note>the specific bodily fluid, such as bile or phlegm, believed to be causing the illness</note>, they act without any consideration of the patient's strength, age, **temperament** <note>original: "complexionis"; the individual balance of hot, cold, moist, and dry qualities</note>, or other factors...

<summary>This page discusses the use of Lovage seeds in digestive compounds and emphasizes the Hippocratic principle that a disease's "humors" must be ripened or digested before they can be safely purged. The commentator strongly criticizes "common" doctors who ignore this rule, comparing them to executioners.</summary>

<keywords>Lovage, Ligusticum, Seed, Digestion, Antidotes, Hippocrates, Galen, Avicenna, Humoral Theory, Purgatives, Medical Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of unlearned medical practitioners who bypass the necessary step of "digesting" humors before purging. It specifically concludes the discussion on Lovage (Ligusticum) by addressing a controversial claim by Strabo regarding its effects on vision, before transitioning to the herb Masterwort (Ostruthium).</meta>

Once the discussion is held, yet with no signs premeditated and no prior digestion <note>In medieval medicine, "digesting" or "coction" referred to the process of ripening or preparing the body's humors so they could be safely expelled</note> having occurred, they indiscriminately administer poisonous herbs: such as **spurge** <note>original: *tintimalum/tithymalum*</note>, **wolf’s milk** <note>original: *esula*</note>, and **hellebore** in a dose that even a horse could scarcely tolerate. By these means, they do violence to nature and, through both portals <note>meaning through both vomiting and the bowels</note>, they compel the humors of the body—both the good and the bad—to exit. It is the task of physicians to describe how many dangers to nature are imminent from these practices. But let us leave those fools <note>original: *ydiotas*</note> with their sheer ignorance.

That physician **Strabo** <note>likely Walahfrid Strabo, the 9th-century author of the poem *Hortulus*</note> asserts that this herb <note>Lovage, discussed on the previous page</note> is harmful to the eyes in drink and in scent—that is, when it is consumed or smelled. The same Strabo ordered that only the seed of that herb should be mixed into antidotes—that is, into medications—rather than the leaves or the root. It is not known to me whether Strabo said this on his own authority or if he took it from the books of the learned. Nevertheless, I know this much: that the ancients extol <gloss>that is, they declare</gloss> this herb with no small—that is, with exceptional—praise. Nor do I recall having read any medical author who supports <gloss>that is, agrees with</gloss> the words <gloss>that is, the sayings</gloss> of Strabo: namely, that this herb is detrimental to the eyes by drink and scent.

### ¶ Regarding the herb **Masterwort**. xxvi.
<note>original: *ostructio*, likely referring to *Peucedanum ostruthium*, a plant used for digestive issues and as a general panacea in the Middle Ages</note>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on Lovage to the medicinal properties of Masterwort (Strucium/Ostruthium). It continues the herb-by-herb catalog of remedies common in medieval medical compendiums.</meta>

**Masterwort** <note>original: *Strucium*</note> is **Ostruthium** <note>original: *obstrucium*</note>: as it is called in the common fashion.

Its root possesses a hot and dry potency; its use drives away ailments.
If the crushed root is drunk with wine, it treats diseases of the **liver**.
Taken in this manner, it aids those suffering from **jaundice** <note>original: *yctericos*</note> and reduces swelling of the **spleen**.
It repels that disease which the Greeks called **sclerosis** <note>original: *sclirosim*, referring here to a morbid hardening or stiffening of the internal organs</note>.
And in this way, they say it shatters **stones of the bladder**.
It promotes the **menstrual flow**, provokes the **urine** <note>original: *lotium*</note>, and heals a **cough**.
Thus it benefits those with **orthopnoea** <note>original: *orthonoicis*, a condition where a patient can only breathe comfortably when standing or sitting upright</note> if it is drunk frequently.
Placed beneath the **womb** <note>original: *matrici*</note>, it is said to expel a miscarriage <note>original: *abortum*; in medieval medical texts, this often refers to aiding the expulsion of a non-viable fetus or "cleansing" the womb</note>.

<summary>This page outlines the therapeutic uses of Masterwort, emphasizing its "hot and dry" qualities for treating internal obstructions, respiratory distress, and urinary or menstrual issues. It highlights the herb's specific reputation for softening "sclirosim" (hardened tissues) and breaking down bladder stones.</summary>
<keywords>Masterwort, Strucium, Ostruthium, liver disease, jaundice, spleen swelling, sclerosis, bladder stones, orthopnoea, medieval pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Masterwort (Strucium/Ostruthium) from the previous page, moving from its effects on the liver and spleen to its applications for skin conditions, respiratory issues, and the purging of the head.</meta>

And thus they report that it brings out the **menstrual flow** <note>original: *menstrua*</note> when it is too delayed,
If its juice is joined with sharp wine and **barley-meal** <note>original: *polenta*; a common thickener for medicinal poultices</note>.
Its juice heals **leprosy** <note>original: *lepras*; in medieval contexts, this often referred to various scaly skin diseases</note> when it is rubbed on.
With this juice also joined with **fine flour** <note>original: *poline*</note>, which they give 
In **Media** <note>an ancient region in modern-day Iran</note>: any **pustule** <note>original: *pustula*; a boil or blister</note> is cured, as they say,
If you add it over the pain in the manner of a **plaster** <note>original: *emplastri*</note>.
Its juice, combined by rubbing it with **honey**,
Purges the **humors** of the head if it is drawn through the nose.
By itself, its powder provokes **sneezing**
When cast into the nostrils, no less than **white hellebore** <note>original: *veratro albo*; a potent and dangerous medicinal plant used to induce sneezing or purging</note>.
Its juice will treat those with **jaundice** <note>original: *yctericis*</note> in a wondrous way
If they draw it through the nose mixed with **woman's milk** <note>original: *lacti muliebri*; breast milk was a frequent ingredient in ancient and medieval pharmacology</note>.

¶ These words are rarely in use among doctors; therefore, let us quickly run through its powers with our author. **Masterwort** <note>original: *Strucium*</note> is the herb called *Ostruthium* <note>original: *obstrucium*</note> according to the custom of the common people. It holds—that is, obtains—a **hot and dry** potency. Its root drives away diseases of the **liver** if it is drunk crushed with wine; this herb aids those with **jaundice** when taken thus with wine, and it repels swelling of the **spleen** which the Greeks called **sclerosis** <margin>hand spleen...</margin> <note>original: *chiros splene iro*; likely a corrupt Greek gloss referring to the hand feeling the spleen</note>. Furthermore, **sclerosis** is a hard **abscess** <note>original: *apostemam*</note>; the herb dissolves any thin fluid, but the thick remains; they cure abscesses in the spleen that have become hard and sclerotic. He also brings this reason for it: since the blood that arrives at the spleen to nourish it is **thick blood**, when it settles in an abscess it hardens. And doctors say this herb breaks **bladder stones** in this way (taken with wine), and it promotes the **menses** and the **urine** <note>original: *lotium*</note>. It also heals a **cough** and benefits those with **orthopnea** <note>original: *orthnoicis*; a condition where one can only breathe while upright</note>—that is, the **asthmatic** and those short of breath—if the herb itself is drunk frequently.

<summary>This page details diverse applications for Masterwort, including its use as a snuff to purge the head, a plaster for skin ailments, and a treatment for "sclerosis" or hardening of the spleen. It specifically compares the herb's potency to white hellebore and notes its efficacy in treating respiratory distress like asthma.</summary>
<keywords>Masterwort, leprosy, pustules, jaundice, sneezing, white hellebore, spleen, sclerosis, bladder stones, asthma, orthopnea, medieval pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Masterwort (Strucium/Ostruthium), specifically detailing its use as a pessary to aid in difficult births, and its efficacy for skin diseases, respiratory purging, and jaundice.</meta>

Placed beneath the **womb** <note>original: *matrici*; likely referring to a pessary or suppository application</note>: it forces—that is, compels—the mother to cast out—that is, emit—an **aborted fetus** <note>original: *abortiuum*</note>—that is, a fetus suffocated in the uterus. And authors report that this herb itself draws out **menstrual flow** <note>original: *menstrua*</note> that is too delayed—that is, delaying beyond the time destined by nature. The juice of that herb with sharp wine—that is, with **vinegar**—and with *polenta*—that is, with flour diluted in water—cleanses—that is, expurgates—leprosy when rubbed on. These are not true **leprosies** <note>original: *lepras*; in this period, "leprosy" was a broad term for many scaly or disfiguring skin conditions</note>, however, which are for the most part incurable, but certain freckles adhering to the skin 
<margin>What are *morphea* and *albaras*.</margin>
or **white leprosy** <note>original: *albaras*</note> or **morphea** <note>original: *morfeas*; these terms refer to different types of skin discoloration or localized skin diseases</note> which, due to an error in the "assimilative power" <note>the body's ability to turn food into tissue</note>, is a precursor to leprosy; unless there is also an error in the substantial form of the nourishment within the flesh, such as when grainy and corrupt flesh is generated. And any **pustule** <note>original: *pustula*</note>—that is, a small prominence of the flesh—such as a *bothor* <note>original: *bothor*; a term of Arabic origin used by medieval doctors for various boils or swellings</note>, is cured by this juice joined with *poline*, which they give in **Media** <note>an ancient region in modern-day Iran</note>—that is, with **barley flour** as authors say. If you apply this juice mixed with barley flour in the manner—that is, in the form—of a **plaster** to the pain—that is, to the pustulous limb. The juice of that herb joined—that is, mixed—with **honey** by grinding—that is, crushing—it, purges the head if it is drawn through the nose. The powder of this herb by itself—that is, alone—injected—that is, driven—into the nostrils—that is, within the nostrils—provokes **sneezing** <note>original: *sternutamentum*</note> no less sluggishly—that is, no more slowly 
<margin>How sneezing is caused.</margin>
or weakly—than **white hellebore** <note>original: *veratro albo*</note>. Because of its impetuous sharpness, by its smell alone, it bites the membranes <note>original: *mirigas*; likely *miringas*, referring to the meninges or membranes of the brain</note> of the brain and violently disturbs them, from which sneezing is excited. Its juice will treat those with **jaundice** <note>original: *yctericos*</note> in a wondrous way—that is, in a marvelous manner—clearing the staining of the jaundice, if those with jaundice draw—that is, inhale—this juice mixed with **woman's milk** <note>breast milk was a common medicinal carrier in medieval pharmacy</note>.

# ¶ On the herb chervil. XXVII.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a chervil (cerifolium) plant. It features several branching stems with deeply lobed, serrated leaves and clusters of small flowers (umbels) at the tips. The entire illustration is contained within a rectangular double-lined border.</image-desc>

**Chervil** <note>original: *cerifolium*</note> possesses a power that is very sharp and fiery.
Applied to **cancers** <note>original: *cancris*; in medieval medicine, this term was used broadly for eroding sores, ulcers, or tumors</note> when crushed with honey, it heals them.
Drunk with wine, it often tends to soothe **pains in the side** <note>original: *lateris... dolores*; typically referring to pleurisy or sharp internal pains</note>,
and also if you bind the crushed herb over the affected area.
Drunk with **mead** <note>original: *mulsa*; a mixture of honey and water</note>, it dissolves harmful **phlegm** <note>original: *pituita*</note>.
Cooked in oil and rubbed on as an ointment, it drives away **chills** <note>original: *frigus*; likely referring to the shivering fits associated with ague or fever</note>.
If you dissolve the crushed herb in strong vinegar,
You will repel **roundworms** <note>original: *lumbricos* (transcribed as *lubricos*)</note> and **intestinal parasites** <note>original: *tineafqʒ* / *tineasque*</note> with such a drink.

Old **lard** <note>original: *axungia*</note> mixed with virgin wax and this herb... <note>The sentence is incomplete here, suggesting a recipe for a plaster or salve that continues on the next page.</note>

¶ Chervil <note>original: *cerifolium*</note> is a commonly known herb: because we sometimes eat it together with lettuce, spinach <note>original: *spinarchijs*</note>, and other herbs of that kind, using olive oil and vinegar. The power of chervil is "sharp"—that is, acute, vehement, and fiery. This means it has the greatest activity, just as fire does. Chervil itself, crushed with honey and applied, heals—that is, it provides a remedy for—**cancers** <note>original: *cancris*; referring to various malignant or eroding sores</note>, which are those most pernicious diseases. Chervil itself, drunk with wine, often tends to soothe **pain in the side** <margin>where side pain [comes from]</margin> (arising from windiness distending the membranes of the ribs). 

If you bind—that is, apply—the crushed herb upon the side. Chervil drunk with mead—that is, with honey-water <note>original: *mellicrato*</note>—dissolves the harmful effects—that is, the injuries—of **phlegm** <note>original: *pituite*; also known as rheum or catarrh</note>, which is a humor flowing from the head. **Cornelius Celsus** <note>A famous Roman medical encyclopedist from the 1st century AD</note>, most skilled in medicine, writes thus concerning phlegm: "Moreover, a humor drips from the head, sometimes into the nostrils, which is mild; sometimes into the throat, which is worse; sometimes even into the lungs, which is the worst of all. If it drips into the nostrils, a thin phlegm flows out through them." <margin>On the nature of phlegm</margin> Therefore, according to the opinion of Cornelius himself, it is a thin humor flowing from the head to the lower parts...

...seems to be popular. Marsilio Ficino <note>an influential Italian Renaissance philosopher and physician</note>, saying that where the head fluctuates with distillations because of **phlegm** <note>original: *pituita*; also known as rheum or catarrh</note>, the quantity of this name <note>referring to the length of the vowels for poetic meter</note>, as far as the grammarians are concerned, is found to be varied by various authors. 

For Persius <note>a Roman satirist</note> in his second satire makes the first syllable of this word *pituita* long, and shortens the second-to-last, as can be seen: *Those who send out dreams most purified from phlegm.* <note>original: *Somnia pituita qui purgatissima mittunt*</note> But Macer <note>likely referring to Aemilius Macer, a Roman poet who wrote on the virtues of herbs</note> shortens the first syllable and makes the second-to-last long.

<margin>
On the quantity of this name, *pituita*
</margin>

This is diligently noted by Badius Ascensius <note>Josse Bade, a famous Renaissance scholar and printer</note> in his very full "Storehouse of the Latin Language," in his interpretation of Persius's poem; he also brings into the open this very poem which we now have in hand. 

But let us return to the author. **Chervil** <note>original: *cerifolium*</note> cooked in oil—that is, supplement with olive oil—when applied—that is, smeared on—expels cold. If you dissolve—that is, break down—chervil crushed with violent vinegar—that is, mixed with a sharp, stinging sourness—you repel—that is, draw out—**intestinal worms** <note>original: *lumbricos*</note> (about which we have said no small amount). And you should fear [them]—that is, [worms] teeming for the most part in corrupted and filthy wounds, or in the rectum or the lower bowel. 

With such a drink, chervil stimulates—that is, provokes—urine and purges the menses. Old *strangia* <note>possibly referring to a specific medicinal mixture or a typo for *syngia* or *stranguria* treatment</note> mixed—that is, added—to this chervil with virgin wax—that is, white wax—cures not only—that is, not just—**parotid swellings** <note>original: *perotidas*; swellings or abscesses behind the ears</note>—that is, abscesses that arise in the outlets of the ears—but it also cures any tumors, or represses them—that is, it dissolves tumors of this kind if you add this ointment upon the swellings. 

If chervil, dipped—that is, soaked—in strong vinegar—that is, the strongest—is eaten, it is often accustomed to settle vomiting and to keep the belly loose—that is, obedient. If the **comb** <note>original: *pecten*; here meaning the lower pubic region</note>—that is, the lower pubic area—is covered—that is, protected—with crushed chervil...

They say that **orach** <note>original: *atriplicem*; a leafy vegetable often compared to spinach</note> is able to heal **sacred fires** <note>original: *ignibus sacris*; a historical term for inflammatory skin conditions such as erysipelas or shingles</note> when its surface is applied as a plaster in this way. <meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal discussion of coriander, transitioning from the introductory heading on the previous page into specific verses on its properties and a prose commentary on its "complexion" or humoral nature.</meta>

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'F' decorated with intertwining vine and leaf patterns in a square frame.</image-desc>The power of the herb **coriander** <note>original: *coriandri*</note> is said to be **cold**,
And at the same time to possess a certain **harsh** <note>original: *austere*</note> quality.
**Galen** <note>a highly influential 2nd-century Greek physician</note> says that it is accustomed to drive out
**Worms** and **roundworms** <note>original: *lumbricos tineafque*</note> from the belly if it is drunk ground
With wine, or if it is joined with mixed vinegar.

This medicine especially helps **swollen testicles**.
