If it is drunk frequently mixed with water.
Grind together **litharge** <note>original: *argenti spumam*; literally "foam of silver," a lead oxide</note> mixed with **ceruse** <note>original: *ceruse*; white lead</note>:
To these ground ingredients, add the juice of coriander and vinegar.
By mixing these four, you shall add **rose oil** <note>original: *roseum oliuum*</note>;
By grinding these together, you create a precious **ointment** <note>original: *vnguentum*</note>
With which you may drive away **sacred fires** <note>original: *sacros ignes*; inflammatory skin conditions like erysipelas</note> and hot swellings.

<margin>
On the differing opinions regarding the **complexion** <note>the balance of "qualities" (hot, cold, moist, dry) in a substance</note> of coriander.
</margin>

¶ **The power is cold**, etc. Regarding the **complexion** <note>original: *complexione*</note> of coriander, the opinion among medical authors is varied. For **Galen** asserted that coriander was **hot**, saying this: "Since it resolves **scrofulas** <note>original: *scrophulas*; swellings of the lymph nodes, typically in the neck</note>, how then is it cold? For resolution <note>the dispersing of a swelling</note> is an operation of heat." **Dioscorides** <note>a 1st-century Greek physician and author of a famous herbal</note>, however, had asserted that it possesses a cooling power because, he says, a large amount of its juice is fatal due to its **freezing nature**. **Avicenna** <note>an 11th-century Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook</note>, however, intervening as an arbiter or "friendly reconciler," makes these authors agree. Its **virtue** <note>medicinal power</note>, he says, is **composite** <note>original: *composita*; made of mixed qualities</note>. Wishing later to satisfy the arguments of both, he adds: "And it is indeed possible that he [Galen] says it resolves scrofulas because of its own specific property, and because in it..."

...is a subtle substance, deeply penetrating and deep-reaching, yet it is not itself made deep. It is a **cold substance**; but when it is drunk, it quickly dissolves the heat and the cold agent remains. 

But let us approach the author: the power <gloss>that is, the complexion or humoral nature</gloss> of the herb **coriander** is said to be cold. **Galen** <note>a famous Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> says: we know <gloss>that is, we recognize</gloss> this herb to have a certain <gloss>that is, some</gloss> degree of a **harsh** <gloss>that is, bitter</gloss> quality. Because of this bitterness mentioned above, if this herb is drunk ground up, it is accustomed to drive out **intestinal worms** <gloss>that is, long and round worms</gloss> and **pinworms** <gloss>that is, small worms</gloss> breeding in the final part of the intestine <gloss>which is called the *longaon* or rectum</gloss>. 

(For this reason, very bitter things kill those worms, such as **aloe** and **wormwood** <note>original: *absinthium*</note>; as do sharp, vaporous things like **garlic** <note>original: *allia*</note> and **horse-mint** <note>original: *mentastrum*</note>; and some do so by a specific property, like the water of **purslane** <note>original: *portulace*</note> and **hyssop** <note>original: *ysopus*</note>). 

If coriander is joined <gloss>that is, mixed</gloss> and taken with wine or with vinegar mixed with a **raisin** <note>called *passa* because it has "suffered" the sun in the process of drying</note>—these added ingredients having been ground up <gloss>that is, mixed together by grinding</gloss>—they will soothe **various** <gloss>that is, many kinds of</gloss> **swellings**. This medicine helps <gloss>that is, heals</gloss> especially <gloss>that is, above all other swellings</gloss> the **testicles** <gloss>that is, the genital members</gloss>. 

Its seed is reported <gloss>that is, said</gloss> to **bind** <gloss>that is, to constipate</gloss> a **loose belly** <gloss>that is, one dissolved by a flux or diarrhea</gloss>. If coriander itself is joined <gloss>that is, mixed with water</gloss> and drunk frequently, [it is said to] contain **litharge** <note>original: *spumam argenti*; literally "silver foam," a lead-based substance used in ointments</note> mixed with **ceruse** <note>white lead</note>. Join <gloss>that is, combine</gloss> with these—namely, the litharge and ceruse—the juice of coriander; and join to these **vinegar** <gloss>that is, sour wine</gloss>. You shall link to these four **rose vinegar** <note>which is prepared from vinegar and immature roses</note>; by mixing <gloss>that is, blending them together</gloss>, you create [the remedy]. 

If you are able to prepare a precious **ointment** <gloss>that is, a salve</gloss> by grinding <gloss>that is, crushing</gloss> these together, with this ointment you may drive away <gloss>that is, dispel</gloss> **sacred fires** <gloss>that is, "Persian fires," a term for inflammatory skin conditions like erysipelas</gloss> and **hot swellings** <gloss>that is, those originating from a "hot" humoral cause</gloss>. The dross of silver... <note>the text cuts off here at the end of the page</note>

...which is called **dross** <note>original: *scoria*, referring to the slag or refuse left after smelting metal</note> of silver, just as that of gold is called **chrysitis** <note>original: *chrysitis*, a golden-colored litharge or mineral byproduct</note>, or of lead is called **plumbargenti** <note>original: *plumbargenti*, a lead-silver byproduct</note> or **molybditis** <note>original: *molybditis*, litharge of lead</note>. Scum is gathered from the boiling material of silver and lead. Indeed, the "dross" of metals refers to whatever sinks to the bottom and is, as it were, their dregs and refuse. **Ceruse** <note>original: *cerusa*, white lead</note> is made from lead, and a drink made from both—namely, from the scum of silver and from ceruse—is a lethal poison.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of coriander’s medicinal uses, specifically its cooling properties for skin conditions and its reputation as a remedy for intermittent fevers. It concludes the section on coriander with a biographical anecdote about the philosopher Xenocrates and a warning from ancient medical sects before transitioning to the study of nasturtium.</meta>

That is, it pacifies any—that is, every—external and troublesome heat, if bean flour is joined to it. That is, if the flour alone is mixed with this juice and applied over **scrofulas** <note>original: *scrophis*; a swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck, often associated with tuberculosis</note>. That is, they will be treated—that is, helped—and the burning pustule—that is, a small bump <note>original: *tuberculum*</note> arising from a certain inflammation on the surface of the skin—will turn red and yield—that is, give way—to such a plaster, just as the lesser yield to their superiors.

It is read as written by many authors of medicine that if the sick person—that is, the one suffering from fever—devours—that is, swallows—three grains of coriander seed before the shivering—that is, the rigor of the fever—he will escape the fever—that is, be liberated from the fever to which the **third light** <gloss>the third day</gloss> gives its name <note>original: *febre tertiana*; tertian fever, a form of malaria where the fever recurs every third day</note>. Coriander gathered—that is, collected—in the morning before the sun rises—that is, before its rising is revealed to us—provides—that is, gives—the same medicine if it is placed—that is, put—under the bolster—that is, the pillow <note>original: *ceruicali*; a cushion placed under the neck (cervix) while sleeping</note> of the sick person. 

**Xenocrates** <note>Xenocrates of Chalcedon (c. 396–314 BCE), a Greek philosopher and head of the Platonic Academy</note> wrote that menstruation ceases—that is, it will be restrained—for as many days as the woman devours—that is, swallows—grains of coriander. Xenocrates was a philosopher of admirable wisdom who (as **Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman historian known for his collection of memorable deeds and sayings</note> writes), when he was present in total silence during the malicious talk of certain people, and one of them asked why he alone held his tongue, he replied: “Because I have sometimes regretted speaking, but never being silent.” 

Certain **Assidui** <note>original: *Assiduij*; likely referring to a specific ancient medical sect or the "Asklepiadai," followers of the god of healing</note> condemn—that is, find fault with—the use of it. Indeed, these Assidui think—that is, judge—that coriander prepares—that is, brings about—death, or some other pain. The Assidui were very ancient physicians against whom **Hippocrates** <note>the famous Greek "Father of Medicine"</note> and his interpreter **Galen** <note>the most influential physician and philosopher of the Roman Empire</note> inveigh most sharply in the first entrance <note>original: *vestibulo*; literally "the porch" or introduction</note> of the *Prognostics* <note>original: *pronosticoꝝ*; referring to Hippocrates’ work on predicting the outcome of diseases</note>.

**They say that nasturtiums possess burning powers.**
**And for this reason, when combined with rue, it restrains Venus** <note>original: *venerem*; a reference to sexual desire or libido</note>.
It does this by drying out the seed <note>original: *semen*; referring to reproductive fluids</note> if it is consumed frequently.
This herb, when fresh and ground up, then mixed with leaven <note>original: *fermento*; a fermenting agent like yeast or sourdough starter used in medicinal poultices</note>
cures carbuncles <note>original: *antracas*; severe, painful skin infections or boils</note>, which the common folk call "the good ulcers."
If it is applied, this treatment also heals the boil <note>original: *furunculus*; a small, painful bump under the skin</note>;
it forces it to discharge the pus <note>original: *saniem*; a discharge of blood and pus from a wound</note>, after which the pain subsides.
Its juice is also said to prevent hair <note>original: *crines*</note> from falling out
when it is smeared on, and it is claimed to relieve the pain of a toothache.
For this, the juice is poured into the ear on the side where the tooth is aching.

**F**ortier is the herb’s seed; it expels an abortion <note>original: *abortum*; likely referring to the expulsion of a dead fetus or inducing delayed menstruation</note>.
**O**nly when taken with wine does it expel the animals of the belly <note>intestinal parasites or worms</note>.

**N**asturtium (says Pliny) <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist</note> took its name from "nose-torment" <note>original: *nartu tormento*; an etymological play on *nasus* (nose) and *torquere* (to twist or torture) due to its pungency</note>, because it clearly provokes sneezing. From this, a proverb was created to stir someone from sluggishness: "Eat nasturtium" <note>original: *ede nasturciũ*</note>, which is just as if we were to say, "Wake up already!" Therefore, let us now shake off the numbness of the mind <note>original: *ingenii torpore*</note> by means of the nasturtium generously presented by our author, carefully noting his words. Doctors say that nasturtiums have burning powers—that is, they are very hot and dry. For this reason, they restrain Venus <note>original: *venerem*; sexual desire or libido</note> when taken with rue, by drying out—that is, desiccating and dissolving—the seed <note>original: *semẽ*; reproductive fluid</note>, if they are consumed frequently. This herb, when fresh and mixed with leaven <note>original: *fermento*; so called because it "grows by boiling" or fermenting</note>, and ground up, cures carbuncles <note>original: *antracas*; severe skin infections. The text notes they are named after a "cave" (*antro*) because they dig into the flesh</note>, which the common folk call "good ulcers." If applied to a boil <note>original: *furunculus*</note>—a type of growth that signifies great inflammation and pain—it also cures it. The name "furuncle" is related to a type of weasel <note>original: *mustelle*</note> used to catch rabbits in their burrows <note>original: *cuniculis*</note>; both are named from "thief" <note>original: *fur*</note>, because just as that animal steals into burrows, this ailment "steals" away the good fluids of the body. The boil, therefore, discharges the "sanies" <note>original: *saniem*; a thin, blood-stained discharge</note>, which is pus; through this cure, once the pus is emitted, the pain recedes. And the juice of this herb, when smeared on—that is, rubbed in—is said to...

They say **arugula** <note>original: *Eruca*</note> is moderately hot
And dry; thus it is a food and digests meals,
And eaten or drunk, it is able to stimulate 
Urine. 
It is chewed to great benefit and repels a cough.
Joined with honey, they say it clears spots 
From the skin and returns a face to a clean state, free of freckles.
The root, first boiled and then strongly kneaded 
And applied over broken bones, draws out those [fragments].
Its seed, ground and taken with wine, 
Is reported to cure any poisonous bites.
This cleanses black spots when mixed with ox gall <term>ox gall</term> <note>the bile of a cow, used historically as a cleansing agent in both medicine and art</note>:
I shall speak of a wonder: smeared on or drunk quite liberally with wine, 
They say it hardens the senses against the pain of lashes <note>original: *verbera*; the text suggests that arugula could act as a topical or internal anesthetic for those facing corporal punishment</note>.
If a cook mixes this herb into seasonings,
Or its seed, they say it provides a pleasing flavor.
For this reason, the Greeks are accustomed to call it *Euchinum* <note>original: *Euchinum*; likely a corruption of the Greek *euzōmon*, which translates to "well-flavored" or "good soup"</note> 
Because the juice of the arugula is good in its taste.
Both doctors and many poets alike confirm 
That, whether eaten or drunk, it stimulates sexual desire in no small measure.
And this herb is healthy when eaten with lettuce,
For its heat provides a balance when mixed with the cold <note>In the humoral system of medicine, lettuce was considered "cold," while arugula was "hot." Combining them in a salad was thought to create a healthy, "temperate" dish</note>.

¶ **Arugula**, etc. It is a very common principle among all philosophers that opposites, when placed in the greatest proximity to one another, are revealed more clearly. Moved by this, I believe, Macer <note>refers to Macer Floridus, the traditional pseudonym for the author of the *De viribus herbarum*, a famous medieval herbal poem</note> joined **arugula** to **cress** with no gap between them; for while the former [cress] restrains sexual desire, the latter [arugula] greatly provokes it. See below.

For indeed, the heat of the arugula <insert>added to</insert> and mixed with the coldness of the lettuce produces a "temperate balance" <note>In medieval medicine, health was maintained by balancing the four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry. Since arugula was "hot" and lettuce was "cold," they were seen as a perfect pair</note>, which is the best guardian of health.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the poppy (Papaver) initiated at the end of the previous page, shifting from the general introduction to the specific physical qualities, varieties, and the method for harvesting opium.</meta>

## He says the power of the poppy is cold and dry.
They <note>Ancient botanical authorities like Dioscorides or Pliny</note> attribute three species to it: one with a white flower;
Another with a rosy, red, and pale flower, which is smaller;
This flower is what they commonly call the "wild poppy."
Better known to everyone is the one from which the white flower grows;
From the tender "heads" <note>original: "capitellis"; refers to the seed capsules or pods</note> of this variety, they make opium.
When the outer skin is lightly "cut" <note>the process of scarification</note>, they collect the "milk" <note>latex</note> that
flows from it in "spoons" <note>original: "cocleis"; could also refer to small shells used as containers</note> and preserve it dry;
It is suitable for many antidotes and various remedies.
There are those who chop up the heads together with their milk,
from which they dry the liquid "squeezed out" in the sun;
After the first method, this juice has "secondary" <note>that is, lesser or weaker</note> strengths.
Truly, from the species which I said produces "rosy flowers,"
they make oil from the "crushed seed,"
which is not a little pleasant to the taste and "sleep-inducing."
The third type, whose flower is red and seed is "blackish,"
is beneficial if it is drunk in the manner of the others.

¶ **They say the power, etc.** Ovid Naso <note>The Roman poet Ovid</note>, receiving the "laurel" <gloss>that is, the prize of excellence</gloss> among all Latin poets after Maro <note>Virgil</note>, most skillfully "suggests" to us <gloss>that is, makes known</gloss> the sleep-inducing nature of the poppy under a most pleasant "fiction" <note>original: "figmento"; referring to mythological storytelling</note> in his *Metamorphoses*. For in the eleventh book of this work, describing the "house of the God of Sleep," he "broke forth" <gloss>that is, wrote</gloss> in these words: "Before the doors of the cave, fertile poppies bloom." Who is so "lacking in mind" <gloss>that is, foolish</gloss> as to doubt that Naso implies the poppy is an "inducer of sleep"? But setting aside fictions, [the text moves to] the clear [explanation] of our author regarding the po—

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the poppy (Papaver), specifically detailing the physical characteristics of three varieties and the pharmaceutical preparation of opium from its latex.</meta>

According to the insight of the Authors, they say the poppy is "vigelida," that is, very cold. They attribute—that is, they give to it—three species. The flower of one species is white. The flower of another species is rosy, that is, purple. A large flower is red and pale; people commonly call this species the wild poppy, that is, commonly. That species is nobler—meaning better—in which the white flower grows. Pharmacists <note>original: "pharmacopole"; supply: ointment-makers or spice-dealers</note> make—that is, they prepare—opium from the "capitella"—that is, from the young heads <note>the seed capsules or pods</note>—of this species. I say this is done by lightly cutting the topmost skin—that is, the highest surface of the head—that is, not deeply. They collect the "milk" <note>the latex</note> in "shells" <note>original: "coctis" (likely a scribal error for "conchis"); that is, shells or small bowls</note> and they reserve the opium itself, a juice suitable for many antidotes—that is, medicines—and various cures. (However, it is not permitted to use this opium—which is more "narcotic," that is, stupefying, than other medicines—unless all other things have already failed and the situation is placed in a state of desperation. For narcotics—as Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a famous 9th-century Persian physician</note> says—are contrary to the essence of life; they are "congealing," stupefying, and mortifying.) There are some who cut up—that is, slice—the heads along with their milk, and from these heads they dry the squeezed-out liquid in the sun. This poppy juice has "secondary" strengths—that is, following after [pure opium]. But from the species which I said produces rosy flowers, they make an oil from its crushed—that is, ground—seed, which is pleasant—that is, sweet—to the taste and "somniferum"—meaning sleep-inducing. Indeed, the third flower is red and its seed is blackish; it provides a flavor to the taste, following the manner of the other species.

<meta>This page continues the chapter on the onion (Cepa), following the introductory woodcut and title from the previous page. It details the conflicting opinions of ancient medical authorities regarding the vegetable's benefits and harms.</meta>

Physicians do not seem to agree about onions.
Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician and author of a famous 1st-century herbal</note> says they cause gas and make the head feel heavy,
And he says that onions, when eaten, kindle thirst.
Galen <note>Galienus; an influential Roman physician whose theories on "humors" dominated medicine for centuries</note> says they are not good for those full of bile <note>original: "fellitis" — people with a "choleric" temperament, thought to be naturally hot and dry</note>,
But he thinks they are very healthy for phlegmatic people <note>those with an excess of "phlegm," thought to be cold and damp</note>.
Asclepius <note>either the Greek god of healing or a later physician of the same name</note> asserts they are quite healthy:
Claiming they are especially good for the stomach and create a beautiful skin color,
Provided that someone makes a habit of eating them.
And he says that whoever breaks their fast with them
Every single day will live his life without pain.
All authors affirm that when eaten they provide flavor,
And they say they healthily soften the bowels <note>meaning they act as a mild laxative</note>.
When applied topically, they are said to cure dog bites,
If they have first been ground with honey or vinegar.
Others apply them after they have been cooked with wine and honey,
And after three days have passed, they remove the poultice <term>cataplasm: a soft, moist mass of material applied to the body to relieve soreness</term>.
Dioscorides orders salt, rue, and onions to be applied 
To the bites of a snake; he asserts that this brings help.
The juice of onions mixed with woman’s milk <note>breast milk</note>,
When poured into the ear, often drives away heavy pains.
This juice, mixed with water and drunk, will help
Those whom a sudden illness has made mute <note>likely referring to a stroke or a sudden loss of speech</note>.
If this juice alone is drawn into the nostrils,
It forces the harmful humors <note>bodily fluids</note> of the head to be expelled.
Whether eaten or drunk, they purge lingering menstruation.
They also heal the chafing of the feet caused by the soles of shoes or boots...

You will heal stubborn deafness with that same juice,

¶ The onion is a herb; its bulb—<gloss>that is, the root</gloss>—is in such frequent use by everyone, or rather so often eaten, that no other seems more common. Therefore, it should not be a burden to hear of its medicinal powers. Physicians do not seem to agree—<gloss>that is, to be in accord</gloss>—concerning onions. For Dioscorides <note>a Greek physician of the 1st century AD</note> says that onions cause gas and make the head feel heavy. And he says that onions when chewed—<gloss>that is, eaten</gloss>—inflame the chest <note>original: "precordia"; the area of the diaphragm and upper abdomen, then thought to be the seat of various emotions and physical heat</note>. Galen (commentators mention that Galen says onions are not good for choleric people <note>people with a "choleric" temperament were thought to be naturally hot and dry; adding "hot" onions was believed to worsen this imbalance</note>. For cholerics are out of balance due to heat, and onions are likewise [hot]; indeed, as Galen says, they are easily harmed by causes similar to their own temperament <term>temperament: the "complexion" or balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) within a person</term>). But he thinks these same onions are healthy—<gloss>that is, conducive to health</gloss>—for phlegmatic people, who are cold and damp; they help...

<meta>This page continues the medical commentary on onions (Cepa). Therefore, they [onions] are judged from opposite sides, he says. That physician Asclepius <note>a legendary figure in Greek medicine, often deified as the god of healing</note> asserts that they are not a little healthy and salubrious. For one is called "healthy" [sanus] who receives health, but "salubrious" [salubris] is that which confers health. Nevertheless, they are sometimes confused, especially—<gloss>that is, chiefly</gloss>—in the stomach. And the same author affirms that onions create—<gloss>that is, bring about</gloss>—a beautiful complexion whenever someone looks upon <note>original: "conspexerit"; likely implying the frequent use or presence of onions in the diet</note> those onions. All authors affirm that onions when chewed—<gloss>that is, eaten</gloss>—bring—<gloss>that is, give</gloss>—flavor, and they say that they soften the bowels salubriously—<gloss>that is, conveniently</gloss>. 

Authors report that onions, when applied, cure dog bites if they were first ground with honey or with vinegar. Others apply onions cooked with wine and honey and remove the poultice <term>cathaplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbs or flour applied to the body as medicine</term> after three days have passed. Dioscorides <note>a 1st-century Greek physician and author of 'De Materia Medica'</note> orders salt, rue, and onions to be applied to snake bites; he asserts that the said wound is thus aided—<gloss>that is, cured</gloss>. 

The juice of those onions mixed with water and drunk will help those whom a sudden—<gloss>that is, unexpected</gloss>—disease makes mute, namely for suffocation of the womb <note>original: "prefocationi matricis"; a historical medical condition believed to be caused by the movement of the uterus, leading to breathing difficulties or loss of speech</note>, as Avicenna <note>a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note> says, or for quinsy <term>squinantia: a severe inflammation or abscess of the throat, often referring to tonsillitis</term>, as Serapio <note>likely Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century medicinal author</note> thinks. For both diseases are sudden and unexpected. 

We have already discussed something regarding "suffocation." Quinsy is an abscess <term>apostema: a swelling or localized collection of pus</term> of the throat or its internal muscles, suddenly suffocating due to the loss of breath <note>original: "anhelitus"</note>. Of this there are three species, as Galen <note>the prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> diligently notes in his commentary on that rule "the worst quinsy" in the third book of the *Prognostics* and the two following. The juice of these onions, when drawn in only through the nostrils, forces—<gloss>that is, compels</gloss>—the body to discharge harmful—<gloss>that is, noxious</gloss>—humors from the head...

### Ox-tongue herb. 34.

Ox-tongue <gloss>the plant *Anchusa officinalis*</gloss> is called *buglossa* in the Greek tongue <note>original: "Lingua bouis"; the name reflects the rough, tongue-like texture of the plant's leaves</note>.
This herb purges red choler <note>original: "coleram rubeam"; in the humoral system, this is yellow bile, which was considered hot and dry</note> that has been scorched by excessive heat, if it is taken frequently with wine.
It will be able to cure the heart-pain <note>original: "cardiacam"; likely referring here to palpitations or distress caused by digestive issues</note> which an abundance of black bile <note>original: "colere... nigre"; associated with melancholy and cold, dry conditions</note> has caused, when drunk in the same manner.
When swallowed, it draws out harmful humors <note>The "humors" were the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was thought to determine health</note> from the lungs.
If its juice is taken mixed with lukewarm water, it heals in a marvelous way anyone suffering from sciatica <note>original: "sciasim"; chronic pain in the hip or thigh</note>.
Experts say that it preserves the memory power of the brain.

<term>Ox-tongue</term>, <term>bugloss</term>, <term>red choler</term>, <term>heart-ailment</term>, <term>humors</term>, <term>sciatica</term>, <term>brain</term>, <term>herb</term>

->Wine in which bugloss has been steeped,<-
->or its decoction, is said to make<-
->the guests joyful if it is sprinkled among the feast.<-

¶ This herb is called *euphralmum* by the Greeks, that is, the "rejoicing herb." For the Greeks use the word *euphrainein* <note>original: "euphraim"; a Greek verb meaning to cheer or gladden</note> to mean "to rejoice." Indeed, it is the mortal enemy of black bile <note>original: "colere nigre"; associated with melancholy, depression, and lethargy</note> which (as Marsilio Ficino <note>A 15th-century Italian scholar and physician famous for his work on melancholy</note> says) is worse than death itself. Desiring, therefore, to spend our days in joy, let us receive its powers with a joyful spirit. It is called "ox-tongue" <note>original: "lingua bouis"</note> and *buglossa* in the Greek language. 

This herb purges—that is, drives out—red choler <note>yellow bile</note> that is scorched—meaning burned by excessive heat—if it is taken frequently with wine. For as the prince Avicenna <note>The famous Persian physician Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037)</note> says: of black bile—that is, melancholy—one type is natural to some people, and another is non-natural. The natural kind is the dregs or the sediment <note>original: "hyppostatis"; the solid matter that settles in a fluid</note> of the other humors. The non-natural kind, however, is produced from the incineration and burning of the other humors; specifically, when their subtle part is burned away, and what remains is the thick, earthy, and dreg-filled residue, now incinerated. 

Therefore, Macer <note>Referring to Macer Floridus, the pseudonym for the author of a popular medieval herbal</note> says that bugloss drives away the melancholy that arises from the burning of red choler; that particular melancholy, as the authority Avicenna states, is of a more powerful malice. Bugloss, when drunk, will be able to cure "cardiaca"—that is, the cardiac disease or trembling of the heart—which an abundance of black bile (that is, melancholy) has caused or produced. 

The word <term>cardiaca</term> <gloss>heart-distress</gloss> is derived from the heart: it is a trembling and palpitation of the heart proceeding from bad humors adhering in its capsule <note>likely referring to the pericardium, the sac surrounding the heart</note> and small veins by way of derivation or congestion, and inducing a tremulous motion. When swallowed, bugloss draws out harmful humors from the lungs if its juice is taken (that is, consumed) mixed with lukewarm water. It heals in a marvelous—that is, excellent—way those who su[ffer...] <note>The text breaks off at "pa", likely "patiuntur" (suffer), which in the context of the previous page refers to sciatica.</note>

<summary>This page explains the medicinal benefits of Bugloss, specifically its power to combat black bile (melancholy) and heart palpitations by purging "burned" humors. It cites the Greek origins of the plant's name and references major medical figures like Avicenna and Marsilio Ficino.</summary>
<keywords>Bugloss, Ox-tongue, melancholy, black bile, red choler, humors, Avicenna, Marsilio Ficino, Macer Floridus, cardiac health, medieval medicine</keywords>

...[it heals] sciatica <gloss>that is, the sciatic disease</gloss>. Experts say that **bugloss** preserves the mnemonic power of the brain <gloss>that is, the memory</gloss>. Wine that is drunk in which **bugloss** has been steeped <gloss>that is, crushed</gloss> is said to make the guests <gloss>that is, those feasting</gloss> joyful. Likewise, a decoction <note>A concentrated liquid produced by boiling the herb</note> of it is said to make the guests joyful if it is sprinkled <gloss>that is, scattered</gloss> among the company at the feast.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Mustard. 35.

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the medicinal properties of Mustard (Sinapi), following the chapter heading and illustration from the previous page. It details the herb's humoral qualities and its specific applications for head, lung, and digestive ailments.</meta>

Among those herbs which **Pythagoras** <note>The famous Greek philosopher and mathematician, who was often cited in medieval texts as an authority on the medicinal properties of plants</note> had praised, it is asserted that he gave the first place of honor to **mustard**.

Its potency is ranked in the **fourth degree** <note>In medieval medicine, herbs were categorized by degrees; the fourth degree was the most intense and powerful level of a quality</note> of both dryness and heat. It draws out and thins viscous humors <term>humors: the four primary bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that were believed to govern health and temperament</term>, and it even burns the skin, so great is its heating force. Its greatest power is said to reside in its seed.

Eating it sharpens the senses, and as a food, it moves the bowels. It breaks up stones <note>referring to bladder or kidney stones</note> and clears both the urine and the menstrual flow. If you mix the crushed seed with a warm liquid and swirl it against the palate while standing in the sun, it will purge harmful phlegm from the head. It is also healthy to mix this into powders meant to induce sneezing, for by that means the entire structure of the head is cleared of blockages.

When chewed, it comforts the stomach and calms labored breathing <note>literally "sighs," typically referring to shortness of breath or asthma</note>. For the bites of serpents—if you soak the crushed seed in vinegar and apply it as a medicated dressing—you may treat the bite with such medicine. Chewing it also prevents poisonous mushrooms from harming the internal organs.

As has been said, this herb can burn the skin with its excessive heat; nevertheless, this burning <note>referring to a "mustard plaster" or "sinapism" used to draw out illness</note> is useful for many causes. By this application, the harmful fluid of the head that damages the eyes will be dried up, as well as the fluid that drips down and harms the cavities of the lungs, creating stomach ailments, coughs, and consumption <note>original: "ptisimqȝ"; phthisis, a chronic wasting of the lungs</note>.

Through this blistering, chronic pain of the hip is drawn out—this is what they call **sciatica** <note>original: "sciasim"</note> by its Greek name. By this treatment, the hard swelling of the spleen and the liver is subdued; indeed, it brings relief to many long-standing, chronic conditions.

<summary>This page details the extreme heating and drying powers of mustard seed, explaining its use in clearing the head, treating snake bites, and relieving chronic pain such as sciatica and lung disease.</summary>
<keywords>mustard, Sinapi, Pythagoras, humors, phlegm, sciatica, consumption, medicinal seeds, mustard plaster, ancient pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific medical discourse on mustard (Sinapi), providing the authoritative reasoning for its use by citing Pythagoras and Galen, and detailing its application in "sneezing powders" and digestive health.</meta>

¶ If anyone should by chance demand the reason why **Pythagoras** <note>The famous Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BCE), who, in addition to mathematics, was often cited in medieval herbals as a botanical authority</note> preferred mustard to all other herbs, let him hear **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 CE), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire, whose humoral theories dominated medicine for over a millennium</note>, the greatest authority, who satisfies this question abundantly. Such great veneration was paid to Pythagoras by his followers that they considered it a sin to bring what they had received from him into dispute; even when challenged to give a reason, they would only respond: "He himself said so" <note>original: "ipsum dixisse," a translation of the famous Greek phrase *autos epha* or the Latin *ipse dixit*, used to describe an assertion accepted solely on the authority of the speaker</note>. 

Let us, therefore, lend a trusting ear to the Pythagorean dogma. It is asserted that Pythagoras attributed the highest praise to **mustard** among the other herbs which he had commended. Its power is ranked in the **fourth degree** <term>fourth degree: the most intense level of a quality (heat, cold, dryness, or moisture) in medieval medicine; a substance in the fourth degree was considered potentially dangerous or even toxic due to its extreme potency</term> of both dryness and heat. It draws out viscous <gloss>that is, sticky</gloss> humors <term>humors: the four primary bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to control health</term> and thins them out—meaning it cuts through them by reason of the intensity of its heat—and it burns the skin <gloss>meaning it causes a blister to form</gloss>. So great is the force <gloss>that is, the violence of the heat</gloss> within it. The strength <gloss>that is, the virtue</gloss> of mustard is said to be greater—meaning more intense—in its seed. 

Eating that seed sharpens the senses by consuming the humors that dull and blunt them, and it moves <gloss>that is, loosens</gloss> the bowels. It breaks—that is, dilates—stones of the bladder and kidneys and clears the menstrual flow. If you combine this crushed seed with warm water and swirl it—that is, roll it frequently against the palate, within the throat—facing the sun <gloss>that is, while the heat is strong</gloss>, you will purge harmful phlegm from the head. 

It is very useful for health to mix this seed into sneezing-medicines <gloss>that is, powders meant to provoke sneezing</gloss> such as those made of pepper, hellebore, seasoned black cumin <note>original: *nigelle*</note>, stavesacre <note>original: *staphisagrie*, a plant traditionally used to treat parasites and skin conditions</note>, euphorbia <note>original: *euforbii*, a plant known for its highly irritating sap</note>, and others of that kind. For every constriction <gloss>that is, a blockage or "opilatio"</gloss> <term>opilatio: a common medieval medical term for a blockage in the body's vessels or "pores" that prevented the flow of humors</term> of the head—meaning of the passages of the brain—is resolved <gloss>that is, opened</gloss> by that crushed seed when it is applied to the nostrils. 

This seed, when chewed <gloss>that is, eaten</gloss>, strengthens the stomach and calms sighs <gloss>that is, hiccups</gloss> if you submerge <gloss>that is, mix</gloss> it...

...mustard seed ground in vinegar and applied on top cures snake bites with such a medicine. This seed, if chewed <gloss>that is, eaten</gloss>, prevents mushrooms <gloss>that is, pestilential—meaning lethal—tubers of the earth or trees</gloss> from harming <gloss>that is, attacking</gloss> the internal organs. I have said that it burns the skin by its seething power <gloss>that is, an excessively overflowing heat</gloss>. Nevertheless, this burning is useful for many causes <gloss>that is, illnesses</gloss>. 

A flowing <gloss>namely, harmful to the eyes</gloss> humor of the head will be dried by this burning; and that humor of the head which we previously called phlegm <term>phlegm: one of the four bodily humors, traditionally associated with coldness and moisture</term> on the authority of the Philosopher <note>likely referring to Aristotle or a medical authority like Philistion</note>, as it drips <gloss>that is, flows</gloss>, by reason of its thinness harms the cavities of the lungs, predisposing one to **phthisis** <note>original: "ptisim"; a historical term for pulmonary tuberculosis or any chronic wasting of the lungs</note> or **peripneumonia** <note>original: "paripleumoniam"; an acute inflammation of the lungs</note>; and that previously mentioned humor which creates diseases of the stomach, coughs, and pleurisy will also be dried up. For that humor flowing down from the head by way of a catarrh <term>catarrh: in medieval medicine, the "downward flow" of excess fluid from the brain into other parts of the body, thought to be a primary cause of disease</term> is the cause of various illnesses according to the variety of the organs that receive it.

Furthermore, this kind of burning produced by mustard seed is beneficial because it causes an attraction of the head's humors toward the site of the burning: partly by reason of pain, and partly by reason of heat <note>The Latin text reads "coloris" (of color), but this is likely a scribal error for "caloris" (of heat), which fits the medical theory of "attraction" via warmth</note>. From there, humors are drawn away from the various members to which they were previously dripping. Chronic hip pain—a type of disease which doctors call **sciatica** <note>original: "sciasim," a Greek-derived term for pain in the "ischium" or hip</note> by its Greek name—is drawn out <gloss>that is, led out or extracted</gloss> by this burning through blisters <gloss>that is, through swellings or elevations of the skin full of water</gloss>. For this **blistering**, so to speak, draws internal humors that infest sensitive organs to the surface of the skin. A hard swelling of the spleen and swelling of the liver <gloss>that is, the hepar</gloss> are also driven away by this burning. Indeed, this burning provides great relief for many "causes" <gloss>that is, chronic illnesses</gloss>.

Because a mustard **plaster** <note>original: "cathaplasma," a soft, medicated mass spread on cloth and applied to the skin</note> is useful for so many causes,
Its preparation is frequently varied by physicians.

I will tell how **Meneniacus** <note>original: "meniacus"; likely referring to the ancient Greek physician Menecrates of Zeuphyria, known for his pharmacological recipes</note> orders this to be done:
In a small mortar, place well-ground mustard
Seed; let the white crumb of bread be joined to this
So that the bread is one-third part of the mustard.
To these you join dried figs, and honey, and vinegar,
Either for pleasure or as much as the cause of the disease requires.
For the more dried figs or honey it contains,
That **mustard-plaster** <note>original: "sinapismus"</note> is usually so much sharper.
It is weaker the more bread and vinegar you add.
I warn you: do not let such a preparation be worthless to you,
For I have often proven its effect in many cases.
However, I do not order this to be applied for all diseases,
But rarely for great and long-standing causes.
**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist</note> instructs that the roots of this [mustard] be submerged
With much of its seed [in wine]. Thus, after it has steeped <note>original: "lamplerit haram," a likely corruption of "hauserit auram," meaning to draw out the essence</note>
And drawn out all its power, he thinks the wine is very healthy
For the throat and stomach when drunk, and for the eyes;
And he says it benefits many complaints of the head.

¶ Since a **poultice** <term>poultice: or "cataplasm," a soft moist mass of plant material or flour applied to the body to relieve soreness and inflammation</term> of mustard is useful for so many ailments <gloss>that is, illnesses</gloss>, it is often varied by physicians in the making <gloss>that is, in the method of preparation</gloss>. I will say how **Meneniacus** orders <gloss>that is, teaches</gloss> this poultice to be made <gloss>that is, prepared</gloss>. Place in a small mortar <gloss>that is, in a copper or stone vessel</gloss> mustard seed well ground <gloss>that is, strongly ground</gloss>. Let the white crumb of bread <gloss>that is, white</gloss> be joined to this seed, so that a third part of bread is joined to the mustard. You should join to these dried figs and honey and vinegar as much as the cause <gloss>that is, the quality of age or of the disease</gloss> requires. For by how much <gloss>that is, the more</gloss> the poultice contains more dried figs or honey, so much more <unclear>that</unclear>...

**mustard-plaster** <note>original: "sinapismus"</note>. That is, the preparation of mustard is usually sharper, but it will be weaker <gloss>as a supplement</gloss> by how much more <gloss>that is, in what quantity</gloss> you add bread and vinegar. After the wine has drawn out all the power of these—both the roots and the seed—he thinks that wine, when drunk, is very healthy <gloss>that is, useful</gloss> for the throat <gloss>that is, the upper part of the gullet</gloss>, the stomach, and the lights <gloss>that is, the eyes</gloss>. Moreover, some make an oil from the seed:
By rubbing it, you overcome pains of the loins and also numb nerves.
Juice is squeezed from the tender stalk of the mustard plant;
If you chew this, you will drive away the torments of a toothache.
Mustard seed is said to soften the veins of numbness
In the hands, if pounded and mixed with old fat.
It disperses **scrofula** <term>scrofula: a swelling of the lymph nodes, usually in the neck, historically known as the "king's evil"</term> if you apply it on top.
And it is said to benefit greatly those who are **falling** <term>falling: a historical term for epilepsy, or the "falling sickness"</term>,
And it is said to help the causes of **jaundice** <term>jaundice: a yellowing of the skin and eyes, referred to here as "icterus"</term> by its drying effect.
When pounded with a fig and applied to a shaved head,
It is a great remedy for those who suffer from **lethargy** <term>lethargy: in medieval medicine, a state of morbid, coma-like sleepiness</term>.
Or if you frequently rub their feet with an ointment.
Join the crushed seed with honey or grease;
That ointment helps those with **alopecia** <term>alopecia: patchy hair loss or baldness</term> if rubbed on frequently.
Applied with sharp wine, it cleanses **leprosy** <note>original: "lepras"; in this period, the term often covered various scaly skin conditions as well as Hansen’s disease</note>.
A **periodic fever** <term>periodic fever: a fever that returns at regular intervals, often associated with malaria</term> is also cured by chewing this,
Before the onset of the disease’s disturbance arrives.

Moreover, it is called a periodic fever <term>febris periodica</term> <note>a fever that recurs at regular intervals, such as malaria</note> which does not afflict the patient constantly, but with an interval of time and true rest. This happens because the matter causing the disease is outside the veins; however, when it is enclosed within the veins, it then causes a continuous fever <note>original: 'continuam'</note> because of the constant release of putrid vapors toward the heart. ¶ The Greeks call a "period" <note>original: 'Periodum'</note> a "circuit" or a "circumlocution"—as if we were to say "elephant's tooth" instead of "hellebore." In the same way, we call a periodic fever a "circuitous" one <gloss>that is, one having repeated attacks</gloss>. Hence Hippocrates <note>the famous Greek physician, often called the 'Father of Medicine'</note> says of those whose conditions sharpen during these circuits: ¶ Although some people place a distinction between a "circuit" and a "period." ¶ Nevertheless, as Galen <note>an influential Roman-era physician and philosopher</note> says in his commentary on the third book of the *Prognostics*: "When there is agreement on the intended meaning of the matter under inquiry, then a disagreement in names is of no consequence."

### ¶ On the Cabbage Plant. 36.

The Roman cabbage <note>original: 'caulis'</note> is called *brassica* in the Greek tongue <note>the author here attributes the Latin name 'brassica' to the Greeks; in reality, 'brassica' is Latin and 'caulis' refers to the stem or stalk</note>.
It is said here that it grows everywhere in gardens,
Yet its use is health-bringing for many things.
Cato <note>Cato the Elder, a Roman statesman who wrote extensively on agriculture and home remedies</note> testifies that for six hundred years, cabbage was the medicine
of the Romans, before the art of medicine
had been prescribed for use by physicians in Rome.
At that time, the garden provided them with both food and medicines.
The author himself affirms that cabbage cures
not only recent wounds, but even long-standing ones
and even cancerous areas <note>original: 'cancros'; in a medieval context, this refers to spreading ulcers or malignant growths</note>; but he instructs that the area
first be washed with river water
and with warm wine; finally, if [eaten] raw...

...to show that the most excellent cabbage, a vegetable for the commoners and

of five hundred men sought an overseas province, 
content to use the same food and drink as 
the sailors—would he not be considered remarkable?" 
And Cato endured these things most patiently, because a

greatest sweetness. But let me return my steps

those Romans then with food and medicines. 
Whence Saint Severinus, whom they call Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a philosopher and statesman</note>: 
"Exceedingly happy was the former age, content with 
faithful fields." The author himself, namely Cato, 
affirms that cabbage heals not only fresh wounds, but

when, clearly, there is not a total corruption of the flesh <note>original: 'caris', likely a transcription error for 'carnis' (flesh)</note>.

¶ **The urine of that person, etc.** **Cato** <note>Cato the Elder, the Roman statesman who famously championed cabbage as a cure-all</note> judged, <gloss>that is, he said</gloss> that the warmed urine was suitable for "causes" <gloss>that is, for the illnesses of the nerves</gloss> of the one who had made use of cabbages; and he asserts that if one is frequently washed with the urine of a boy—which is very helpful in deep ulcers and those of difficult consolidation <note>original: "consolidationis"; referring to the closing and healing of a wound</note> as **Matthaeus Silvaticus** <note>A 14th-century Latin medical author and botanist</note> says—it contributes much to the health of those who suffer from diseases of the nerves. **Cato** says (adding [the testimony of]) a certain philosopher whom they call **Chrysippus**, who wrote a small book concerning the powers of this herb. All doctors confirm that the "lights" <gloss>that is, the eyes of a person</gloss> who frequently feeds on fresh cabbage are wiped clean <gloss>that is, cleared of darkness</gloss>. Eating this cabbage increases milk in the breasts and purges the **menses** <term>menses: menstruation</term> and helps the stomach to "cook" <gloss>that is, to be able to digest</gloss> the food <gloss>that is, the nourishment taken in</gloss>. If cabbage is cooked for a long time, it is said to bind the **bowels** <note>original: "aluam"</note>. But taken half-raw <gloss>that is, not yet perfectly cooked</gloss> it is accustomed to loosen <gloss>that is, to relax those bowels</gloss>.

Regarding this, **Avicenna** <note>The famous Persian polymath and physician (980–1037 AD) whose 'Canon of Medicine' was a standard textbook in Europe</note> brings the reason into the open. "Some medicines," he says, "are **simples** <term>simples: medicinal plants or substances used on their own, rather than as part of a complex mixture</term> in which boiling can separate contrary powers within them, just like cabbages; for their substance is mixed from an earthy, **styptic** <term>styptic: a substance that constricts tissue; in this context, it refers to an astringent quality that binds the bowels</term> matter and from a subtle, cleansing, **nitrous** <term>nitrous: in early medicine, this refers to "nitrum," a salt-like substance believed to have thinning and cleansing properties</term> matter. Therefore, when it is cooked in water: the cleansing nitrous substance is dissolved from it into the water; and the earthy, styptic substance remains; thus its water becomes laxative while the body of the cabbage becomes styptic." This prince [of physicians, Avicenna] says cabbage taken raw, provided it is dipped <gloss>that is, soaked</gloss> in vinegar, suppresses a swollen **spleen** <gloss>that is, an inflated one</gloss>; its seed drives out...

¶ It is said, etc. This ointment is said to cure the **testicles** <gloss>that is, swollen testicles, meaning those that are inflamed</gloss> and it is said to cure various diseases which the **genitals** <gloss>that is, the male members</gloss> suffer. And this **poultice** <term>cathaplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbs and liquids applied to the skin to treat aching or inflamed parts</term> will be able to **heal** <gloss>that is, assist</gloss> not a little the **afflictions** <gloss>that is, the pains</gloss> of **gout** <term>podagre: gout, specifically affecting the joints of the feet</term>. The ashes of cabbage well ground with **grease** <gloss>that is, with old lard</gloss> are beneficial for old pains of the side and the **heart** <note>original: "core"; likely referring to the chest or the heart region</note>. Although this medicine is cheap, yet it is **healthy** <gloss>that is, very useful for human health</gloss>. 

If cabbage seed is drunk mixed with vinegar, it often drives out the harmful animals of the belly, namely **roundworms** <term>lumbricos: common intestinal roundworms</term>, **pinworms** <term>ascarides: small, white parasitic worms</term>, and **tapeworms** <term>tineas: a general term for parasitic worms or "belly moths"</term>. Ash is made from dried cabbage roots that are **burnt** <gloss>that is, scorched</gloss>; wine is **added** <gloss>that is, joined</gloss> to these. It breaks the stones of the kidneys or the bladder by **drawing them out** <gloss>that is, leading them out</gloss>. 

Authors affirm that swallowing the juice of fresh **ground** <gloss>that is, crushed</gloss> cabbage greatly assists a **hoarse voice** <gloss>that is, a rough voice</gloss>. Cabbage juice infused in the nostrils purges his head. He who **has** <gloss>that is, eats</gloss> cabbages beforehand will **feel** <gloss>that is, incur</gloss> drunkenness only with difficulty. Mellinus, that very ancient physician, says that the root of the same cabbage, afterwards ground and suspended from the neck—which, however, should not **touch** <gloss>that is, come in contact with</gloss> the neck <gloss>add: it drives away...</gloss>

### ¶ On the herb Parsnip. 37.

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut illustration of a Pastinaca (parsnip) plant, enclosed within a rectangular double-line frame. The **virtue** <gloss>that is, the medicinal power</gloss> of the parsnip is in its **seed** alone,
And in its **root**; if you **boil** <gloss>that is, make a decoction</gloss> of its
Roots in **mead** <note>original: "mulsa"; a drink made of honey and water</note>, that decoction will be very beneficial
If it is given as a drink for **complaints** <gloss>that is, ailments</gloss> of the **spleen** and **liver**.
The same drink relieves the pain of the **loins** <note>original: "lumborum"; the lower back or lumbar region</note>.
If you cook this herb in **milk**, the decoction you take will help
Long-suffering **asthmatics** and **flowing bellies** <note>original: "ventres fluentes"; a common term for those suffering from diarrhea</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the parsnip (pastinaca) from the previous page, moving from general decoctions to specific uses of the root as an amulet, a preventative for snakebites, and an aphrodisiac. It also includes a scholarly commentary citing Galen on the herb's "complexion" (its elemental qualities).</meta>

Its root, when suspended from the neck,
As some say, suppresses tumors of the **testicles**.
Whoever carries a parsnip or has eaten them
Is said to be unable to be harmed by any **serpents**.
It kindles **Venus** <note>original: "venerem"; a poetic reference to sexual desire or libido</note> if it is taken in large amounts.
If you scrape around the **teeth** with its roots,
They say that great pain is very often relieved.
From parsnip wine, if it is frequently drunk,
It restrains the strikes that the **scorpion** has inflicted on men.
Say also that it cures a tumor of the **female belly** <note>original: "feminei ventris"; likely referring to the womb or abdominal swelling</note>,
Which they say makes a woman appear as if she were pregnant.
Because it provides **food** <note>original: "pastum"; the author is providing a folk etymology linking the word "pastus" (food/pasture) to "pastinaca"</note>, it is called the *pastinaca*.

¶ Concerning the **complexion** <term>complexion: the specific balance of the four "humors" or qualities (hot, cold, moist, and dry) that determined a medicine's effect</term> of the parsnip, **Macer** <note>Macer Floridus, the traditional name for the author of this herbal poem</note> remains silent. **Galen** <note>a famous Roman physician whose theories dominated medieval medicine</note>, however, declares it clearly. He says the roots of the domestic parsnip are eaten: though they provide less nourishment than previously mentioned foods, they are manifestly **heating** in the middle of the **second degree**, and **moistening** in the **first degree**. 

Moreover, Macer reveals its powers here. The **virtue** <gloss>that is, the medicinal power</gloss> of the parsnip exists only in the **seed** and in its **root**. If you boil its roots in **mead** <gloss>that is, in honey and water</gloss>, and if that decoction is given to be drunk, it will greatly benefit **complaints** <gloss>that is, pains</gloss> of the **spleen** and **liver** <gloss>that is, the hepatic organ</gloss>; and the same decoction, when drunk, relieves <gloss>that is, alleviates</gloss> pain of the **loins** <gloss>that is, the kidneys</gloss>. If you cook this root in **milk**, the decoction taken <gloss>that is, drunk</gloss> will help <gloss>that is, cure</gloss> **asthmatics** <gloss>that is, those gasping for breath</gloss>. Who they are—supply 'the elderly' <gloss>that is, those worn out by age</gloss>—and the decoction will help **flowing bellies** <gloss>that is, a flux of the stomach</gloss>. The root of that parsnip, suspended from the neck, suppresses tumors of the testicles, as some say...

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the parsnip (pastinaca) from the previous page, specifically focusing on its protective qualities against venomous creatures, its dental applications, and its use in treating "uterine moles." It then introduces the herb oregano.</meta>

He who carries a **parsnip**, or who has eaten it, is said to be unable to be harmed—that is, wounded or stung—by any **serpent**. If it is taken in large, copious amounts, it kindles **desire** <note>original: "veneres"; a reference to sexual libido</note>. For all hot and moist things are incitements to the matters of **Venus**. 

If you scrape—that is, clean—the **teeth** with its roots, authors say those teeth are very often relieved—that is, alleviated—of great and intense pain. If parsnip **wine** is frequently drunk, it restrains—that is, heals—strikes, which are the stings or bites that the **scorpion** has inflicted. 

Authors say that the parsnip cures a swelling of the **female belly**, by which swelling a woman appears to be as if she were pregnant. Doctors call this swelling a **uterine mole** <term>mola matricis: a mass of non-viable tissue in the uterus that could mimic the symptoms of pregnancy</term>, which indeed afflicts many women. 

**Avicenna** <note>a famous Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe</note> says: "Sometimes the conditions of pregnant women happen to a woman due to the retention of her **menses**, a change in her color, and a loss of appetite; and sometimes there occurs a swelling of both breasts, and a movement is felt in her belly which is like the movement of an embryo; and sometimes the pain and distress of labor occurs, yet she does not give birth." Thus says Avicenna. 

**Hippocrates** <note>the ancient Greek "Father of Medicine"</note> indeed gives a most certain proof by which a woman can distinguish a true pregnancy from this uterine mole. He says: "If you wish to know if a woman has conceived, give her **honey-water** <term>melicratum: a medicinal drink made of honey and water</term> to drink when she is about to sleep; and if indeed she has cramps <note>original: "torsiones"</note> around the belly, she has conceived; but if not, not at all." This herb is called the *pastinaca* because <note>original: "quia"</note> it provides **food** <note>original: "pastum"; the author provides a folk etymology linking the word for food to the plant's name</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the herb **oregano**. XXXVIII.

The powers of **oregano** are read to be hot and dry,
The **third degree** <term>degree: in medieval medicine, the intensity of a plant's elemental qualities on a scale of one to four</term> is granted to it in both qualities.
A **decoction** of this taken in wine cures
Any poisonous bites if it is frequently drunk.

<summary>This page details the parsnip's use as a defense against scorpions and serpents, its role in treating dental pain, and diagnostic methods for "uterine moles" and pregnancy. It concludes by introducing the medicinal properties of oregano, specifically its heating nature and use against venom.</summary>

<keywords>parsnip, oregano, Avicenna, Hippocrates, uterine mole, pregnancy test, honey-water, medieval pharmacology, venomous bites</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the herbal descriptions following the discussion of the parsnip. While the previous page ended with a test for pregnancy using honey-water, this page details the medicinal virtues of oregano (Origanum), particularly its role as an antidote and its effectiveness in treating skin conditions and respiratory issues.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a botanical specimen within a rectangular double-line border. The plant consists of a central stem branching into several stalks with oppositely arranged lanceolate leaves. At the tips of several branches are small, budding flower clusters.</image-desc>

Taken with **mead** <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of water and honey</note>, it benefits one who has drunk **aconite** <note>a highly toxic plant, also known as monkshood or wolfsbane</note>.
In this way, they say it resists many other **poisons**.
Crushed and chewed, it is helpful; or, if drunk more frequently,
it restrains **dropsy** <term>ydropicos: a condition where excess fluid collects in the body's tissues or cavities, known today as edema</term> and dries up **swellings**.
Drunk with mead, they say it expels **black bile** <term>colera nigra: in medieval medicine, one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids; an imbalance was thought to cause illness or melancholy</term>
if it is taken in the weight of an **obol** <note>a small unit of weight, originally an ancient Greek coin</note>.
It will draw out the **menses** if drunk; or if you apply it
**crushed**, or if a woman bathes herself with the **boiled herb**.
Its **powder** mixed with **honey** drives away a **cough**.
It banishes **itching**, **scabs** <term>acoras: a term for crusty skin eruptions or scalp sores</term>, and all **spots** on the skin.

¶ **The powers of Oregano, etc.** Our author asserts that this herb heals, above all, **venomous bites**; a property which the **weasel** <note>original: "mustela"</note> surely taught to humans. For when the weasel is about to fight with snakes, it secures its safety with oregano, just as **rue** <note>another medicinal herb, *Ruta graveolens*</note> is a medicine for weasels. Hence **Pliny**, the distinguished physician <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of *Natural History*</note>, rushes to this opinion: "It is a wonder how many things have been learned from beasts." The powers of oregano are read to be **hot and dry**. It is granted to be in the **third degree** <term>third degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating the potency of a substance’s qualities on a scale of one to four; the third degree is quite strong</term> of both heat and dryness <note>The OCR reads "frigido" (cold), but the context of "hot and dry" and Galenic theory suggests this was a scribal error for "sicco" (dry)</note>. Authors say that oregano itself **stands against** <gloss>resists</gloss> many other poisons. Oregano **chewed** <gloss>eaten</gloss> or drunk more often helps the **convulsed** <gloss>one suffering a convulsion in some limbs</gloss>. Oregano drunk...

### ¶ Regarding the herb Wild Thyme. XXXIX.

The ancients called it **Wild Thyme** <note>original: "Serpillum"; from the Greek "herpyllos" and Latin "serpere," meaning to creep</note> because it seems to creep,
Remaining close to the earth. It possesses a hot and dry power.
You should crush it with sharp wine, or cooked with that olive oil
To which the **rose** gives its name <note>Rose oil</note>. Smear the forehead 
Of the sick man with this ointment; it is accustomed to soothe this head pain.
The smoke of it when burned drives away all serpents,
As well as any other creature that pours poison from its mouth.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Wild Thyme (Serpillum) begun on the previous page. While the previous page focused on its practical benefits for reapers and its medicinal effects on the internal organs, this page provides a scholarly commentary on the plant's etymology, its linguistic origins in Greek, its Galenic properties (heat and dryness), and its literary associations in classical poetry.</meta>

¶ **Wild Thyme** <note>original: "Serpillum"</note> the ancients, etc. Although the Latin language, like a certain stream, took its primary origin from a Greek source, nevertheless, Greek aspirations <note>referring to the "h" or rough breathing sound</note> are for the most part changed by us into the letter **s** <note>The OCR reads "f", but this is a transcription of the archaic long "s" (ſ). The author is explaining how the Greek *herpillon* becomes the Latin *serpillum*.</note>. For the Greeks call the herb whose powers we are currently investigating *herpillon* <note>original: "herpillon"; from the Greek ἕρπυλλον</note>: but we call it *serpillum*. For among the Greeks, the vowel **u** [as used in Latin] has no place; instead, where we pronounce **u**, they adopt **y** [the Greek *upsilon*] for themselves. Likewise, they use **hepta** and **hex** where we pronounce **seven** and **six**.

But now is not the place for speaking Greek. The ancients called it *serpillum* because it almost **creeps** <term>serpat|to creep or crawl</term> nearby, that is, very close to the earth. For (as **Serapio** <note>Serapio the Younger, a 12th-century physician often cited in medieval herbals</note> bears witness) it is a plant that is inclined, lying upon the face of the earth. Its "fervid" force—that is, its hot and dry quality—reaches the **third degree** for this herb, as **Avicenna** <note>The influential Persian physician Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037)</note> says.

You should crush wild thyme cooked with wine in **olive**, that is, in the oil to which the rose gives its name—in **rose oil**—and smear the forehead of the sick person with this **unguen**, that is, with this ointment. This ointment is accustomed to soothing headaches. Its **nidor**, that is, its odor, is released by the **nitor** <note>literally "brightness," here referring to the heat or glow of a fire</note> of fire. For "nidor" is the smell of any burnt thing. Therefore, the smoke of burnt wild thyme drives away all **serpents**; and it drives away any animal pouring out—that is, discharging—venom from its mouth.

It is the custom for reapers to mix this wild thyme into their food; therefore, for that reason, so that if **sopor**, that is, sleep, should **depress**—that is, overcome—them when they are **weary**, that is, fatigued by labor (for the spirits, being loosened by excessive labor, are repaired by sleep), these reapers might be able to rest **safe**, that is, secure from **harmful worms** <note>original: "vermibus"; in this era, this term included insects and snakes</note> such as the **gnat** <note>original: "culex"</note>. Regarding this gnat, the famous **Maro** <note>The Roman poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro)</note> published a most charming song in a delightful poem to the Emperor Octavius, the beginning of which is: *We have played, Octavius, while a slender Thalia <note>The Muse of pastoral poetry</note> set the rhythm.* These songs seem to allude to Virgil’s **Corydon** <note>A shepherd character from Virgil's Eclogues</note>.

<summary>This page provides an etymological and scientific commentary on Wild Thyme, explaining how its name derives from its creeping growth habit and its linguistic roots in Greek. It also details the herb's Galenic classification as "hot and dry" and cites its use in warding off pests, supported by references to Avicenna and Virgil.</summary>
<keywords>Wild Thyme, Serpillum, etymology, Avicenna, Serapio, Virgil, medicinal ointment, pest repellent, reapers, Galenic medicine</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Serpillum (wild thyme). The previous page established its Greek etymology (herpillon) and its primary quality as "hot and dry" in the third degree. This page transitions from literary allusion to the underlying medical theories of the time, specifically the debate over whether cures work by "opposites" or "likes."</meta>

... [The poet Virgil writes:] "Thestylis <note>A character in Virgil's *Eclogues*</note> for the reapers worn out by the rapid heat, crushes garlic and **wild thyme** <note>original: "serpillum"</note>, fragrant herbs."

Truly, **Servius**, the commentator on Virgil <note>original: "maronianus"; referring to Publius Vergilius Maro</note>, feels quite differently about the eating of wild thyme by reapers than our author does. For he says that this herb is eaten by reapers to drive away heat, and he cites the authority of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*</note> that medicine works either by **opposites** or by **likes**. Wherefore, he says, heat can be driven out by another heat. 

Nevertheless, the opinion of all these authors—unless I am mistaken—is conformed to the truth, provided it receives a correct interpretation. For medicine works by **opposites** <term>contrario|the principle of 'contraria contrariis curantur'</term> to drive out sickness; however, it works by **likes** to preserve health. For as the **Prince** <note>Usually referring to Avicenna or Galen in medieval medical texts</note> says: sickness is healed by its opposite, and health is preserved by its like. 

Heat can also be driven out by another heat: not, however, through its own nature <note>original: "per se"</note>, but by accident <note>original: "per accidens"</note>. Just as **scammony** <note>A plant resin used as a powerful purgative</note> cools the body although it is hot in itself; it does this not because it is hot, but because it draws out hot humor. For that whose very being is to become something, its absence is to become the opposite, as **Mesue** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a 9th-century Persian physician</note> is the authority. 

But perhaps these things wander a little from our intended purpose; let us therefore return to the point from which we digressed. **Wild thyme** helps—that is, cures—**venomous bites** if it is drunk (that is, if one drinks it) or if it is applied (that is, if it is applied to the wound). Wild thyme provokes urine; it relieves suppressed urine—that is, it calms **griping pains** <term>tormina|intestinal cramps or colic</term>, which are twistings of the belly. It helps the **spleen**, that is, the <unclear>obstruction</unclear> of the spleen, if it is drunk often—and I say this is best when mixed with vinegar. 

If honey is joined to the aforesaid ingredients—namely, the juice of wild thyme and vinegar—this drink is accustomed to help wonderfully those "tossing," that is, those **spitting up blood** <term>emoptoicos|hemoptysis</term>. Wild thyme, when swallowed with wine, is said to calm pains of the **liver** <note>original: "iecoris idest epatis"</note>; and this drink is accustomed to "produce"—that is, to lead forth—the **menses**, which is the flow of women.

<summary>This page discusses the pharmacological theory of how wild thyme cures ailments, debating whether it works by opposing or mimicking the body's humors, and lists its practical uses for treating venomous bites, colic, and respiratory issues.</summary>
<keywords>Wild Thyme, Virgil, Servius, Pliny the Elder, Medical Theory, Scammony, Spleen, Liver, Hemoptysis, Menstruation, Pharmacology</keywords>

# ¶ On the herb Violet. 40.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a plant, likely a violet, contained within a double-line rectangular border. The plant consists of several branching stems originating from a single base. The leaves are lanceolate with serrated edges. There are several flowers depicted as six-pointed stars with circular centers.</image-desc>

Neither the rose’s beauty nor the lilies can surpass fragrant **violets** <note>original: "violas"</note>—neither in their appearance, nor their power, nor their scent.

Their nature is said to be moist and cold in the **first degree** <term>primo gradu|In medieval medicine, qualities like "cold" or "heat" were measured on a scale of one to four; the first degree is the mildest and safest level</term>. We read that there are three species of them, which we are able to distinguish only by their different flowers.

For there are purple flowers, white flowers, and black ones. Their powers in **medicine** <note>original: "medicamine"</note> are almost equal across all types. They help inflamed areas of the body if you apply them crushed <note>As a poultice or topical application</note> over the affected site.

**Vocabulary:** **violet** <note>original: "viola"</note>, **herb** <note>original: "herba"</note>, **lilies** <note>original: "lilia"</note>, **medicine/remedy** <note>original: "medicamine"</note>, **purple** <note>original: "purpurei"</note>, **white** <note>original: "albi"</note>, **black** <note>original: "nigri"</note>

<summary>This page introduces the violet, praising its beauty and scent while classifying it as a "cold and moist" herb used to treat inflammation. It identifies three varieties of the flower—purple, white, and black—noting they all share similar medicinal properties.</summary>

<keywords>violet, herbalism, humoral theory, first degree, inflammation, medieval medicine, botany</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the medicinal properties of the violet, following the general description of its "cold and moist" nature and its three primary varieties (purple, white, and black).</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<sig>n iij</sig>

¶ A **hangover** <note>original: "crapula"</note> and a heaviness of the head are dispelled by drinking an infusion of it, or even by its scent alone, or if you crown your head with a wreath of them. They heal the bites of snakes if the flowers are dissolved in water and drunk. They say that the **purple violet** <note>original: "Purpurea viola"</note> cures **the falling sickness** <term>caducos|A historical term for epilepsy, so named because the sufferer "falls" during a seizure</term>, especially in children, if it is taken mixed with water. Pound the roots of the violet with myrrh and saffron; apply this to inflamed eyes at night. By using the crushed leaves of the violet mixed with honey, you will heal stinging ulcers of the head; if you join this with vinegar and make a **poultice** <term>cathaplasma|A soft, moist mass of herbs and flour applied to the body to relieve soreness or inflammation</term>, any swelling will subside. A warm decoction of the plant heals swelling of the **womb** <note>original: "matricis"</note> if it is frequently bathed with it. Fissures of the anus, which they call **rhagades** <term>ragadias|Painful cracks or linear sores in the skin or mucous membranes</term>, are often cured when the herb is added to ointments and applied.

¶ **Neither the rose’s beauty.** The name "Violet" <note>original: "Viola"</note> is derived from "violating" <note>original: "violando"</note> by way of **antiphrasis** <term>antiphrasim|A rhetorical device where a word is used in a sense opposite to its literal meaning</term>, <gloss>because</gloss> it is the least "violated" (harmed). From this, we call any fragrant flowers "violets" in common speech because of the violet's excellence. **Neither**: that is, "and not," and the **rose’s beauty**: that is, the rose or the lilies, those most lovely flowers, cannot surpass the **fragrant** violets—that is, those that smell sweet—neither in **power** <note>original: "virtute"</note>, nor **appearance** <note>original: "specie"</note>, nor scent. Its **power**: that is, its **complexion** <term>complexio|In medieval medicine, the specific balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, black bile, yellow bile) that determines a plant's or person's nature</term> is said to be moist and cold in the first degree. We read that there are three species of them, which we are able to distinguish—that is, tell apart—by the different flowers—that is, the diversity of the flowers' colors. For there are purple, white, and black flowers. Their **powers**: <gloss>properties</gloss> are equal—that is, similar—in almost all medicinal uses. Violets help inflamed areas if you apply the violets themselves **crushed** <note>original: "trusas"</note>—that is, pounded.

**Vocabulary:** **hangover/excessive drinking** <note>original: "crapula"</note>, **heaviness/headache** <note>original: "grauedo"</note>, **epileptics** <note>original: "caducos"</note>, **fissures** <note>original: "ragadias"</note>, **Violet** <note>original: "Viola"</note>, **sweet-smelling** <note>original: "fragrantes"</note>, **temperament/nature** <note>original: "complexio"</note>, **medicine/remedy** <note>original: "medicaminie"</note>, **crushed/pounded** <note>original: "trusas"</note>
