<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on the violet. It elaborates on the treatments mentioned at the end of the previous page—specifically for epilepsy, quinsy, and swellings—while citing the authority of Hippocrates, Galen, and Avicenna regarding the "hot" or "cold" nature of medicinal fragrances.</meta>

Crushed violets dispel a **hangover** <note>original: "crapula"</note>; that is, the effects of drunkenness are taken away. By drinking an infusion of these violets, the heaviness of the head—which proceeds from the fumes and vapors of hot foods or drinks—is dispelled. This occurs by their scent alone or if you crown your head with a wreath of them. For almost all aromatics (that is, fragrant things), except for a few, are "hot" in nature and cause a heaviness of the head, as **Hippocrates** and his commentator **Galen** state. However, the violet, the rose, the **water-lily** <note>original: "nenufar," from the Arabic "nilufar"</note>, sandalwood mixed with camphor, and a few others are exceptions to this rule because they are "cold" in nature. 

Violets dissolved—that is, steeped—in water and drunk treat the diseases of **quinsy** <term>squinantie|A historical term for a severe sore throat or tonsillitis, often resulting in an abscess that makes swallowing or breathing difficult</term>, which we have discussed somewhat. (It is called *anguina* from "increasing" <note>original: "augendo"</note> because it chokes one so violently). Doctors say that the **purple violet** cures the **falling sickness** <term>caducos|A historical term for epilepsy, referring to the way a patient falls during a seizure</term>, especially in children, if it is taken mixed with water <gloss>original: "amne," meaning a stream or water</gloss>. Indeed, this is a disease common to children because of an excess of **phlegm** <note>In ancient medicine, phlegm was one of the four "humors"; an imbalance was thought to cause sluggishness or seizures</note>, which a change of age, seasons, or location often cures directly, as Hippocrates says. 

Pound the roots of the violet with myrrh and saffron; by applying this medicine to inflamed eyes throughout the night <gloss>original: "pernoctem"</gloss> and anointing them, you will heal them. You may also heal ulcers of the head with crushed violets mixed with honey. If you combine vinegar with violets and make a **poultice** <term>cataplasma|A soft, moist mass of herbal materials applied to the body to relieve soreness and swelling</term>, any "collection"—that is, an **abscess** <term>apostema|A gathering of purulent matter or a localized swelling</term>—will subside and be repelled, provided the abscess is in its early stages. 

As **Avicenna** <note>A hugely influential Persian physician and philosopher (c. 980–1037) whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard European textbook</note> says, when an abscess is in its beginning, we should apply something that only repels the fluids; when it has reached its peak, we apply something that only dissolves them. In the middle stage, however, we should mix both treatments. It is Hippocrates’ custom that by the term "collection," he understands every unnatural thickening, according to Galen. A warm decoction of the violet heals swellings <gloss>that is, inflations</gloss> of the **womb** <note>original: "matricis"</note> if it is frequently bathed with that warm decoction of violets...

<summary>This page details the medicinal applications of violets for hangovers, quinsy, and childhood epilepsy, while explaining the "cold" humoral nature of the plant. It cites major classical and Islamic medical authorities like Hippocrates, Galen, and Avicenna to justify specific treatments for abscesses and swellings.</summary>

### Fol. 102

...added to **cerates** <term>cerotis|A stiff, medicinal ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal ingredients, similar to a modern salve or plaster</term>—that is, hard plasters—it often heals **anal fissures** <note>original: "fissuris ani"</note> when applied as an ointment. Physicians call these fissures **rhagades** <term>ragadias|Small, painful tears or cracks in the skin or mucous membranes</term>, or even **condylomata** <term>condilomata|Small, wart-like growths or swellings</term> or **mariscae** <term>mariscas|Large, fleshy hemorrhoids, named for their resemblance to a type of large fig</term>.

(Regarding these, **Juvenal** wrote: *"But with a smooth rump, let the swollen hemorrhoids be cut while the doctor laughs."* <note>original: "Sed podice Levi. Cedantur tumide medico ridente maresce." from Satires 2.12-13</note>. They are also called **figs** <note>original: "ficos"</note>—not "figs" as the common people judge—wherefore **Martial** <note>The Roman epigrammatist known for his wit</note> published this poem against a certain man suffering from the "disease of figs" who had mocked the word "figs": *"We call 'figs' [ficos] those which we know grow on trees; you, Caecilianus, call your own [hemorrhoids] 'figs'."* <note>original: "Dicimus ficos quas scimus in arbore nasci Dicens ficos castelliane tuos." This is a pun on the Latin word 'ficus,' which refers to both the fruit and the medical growth</note>).

> If there is a pustule, add [the violet] tied up with honey.
> Its seed taken with wine clears the **menses** <note>The monthly menstrual flow</note>.
> Its roots, ground with myrtle and vinegar, dry out the **spleen** if drunk, 
> Or if you apply it over the spleen.
> And they say it soon settles **hot gout** <term>podagram|A form of inflammatory arthritis, historically associated with rich diets, typically affecting the base of the big toe</term>.
> When drunk, it repels **red choler** <term>coleram rubeam|One of the four bodily humors in medieval medicine, associated with heat and dryness; an excess was thought to cause fever and inflammation</term> from the stomach.
> The green herb of the violet—whether the flower be dry or fresh—
> Settles diseases caused by red choler or blood
> In the soft ribs or in the lungs themselves,
> If drunk with myrtle in a **stream of fresh water** <gloss>amne recenti</gloss>.
> In this way, it also settles the coughs and labored breathing of infants.
> From violets, they make an **oil** just as they do from the rose-flower,
> Which they say is healthy for many causes.
> Poured into the ears, it drives away ringing and pain;
> It also benefits the head, no matter what pain it suffers,
> By cooling it slightly and relaxing the body with its quality.
> It kills **worms** <note>The text likely refers to intestinal parasites; "lubricus" is a common medieval shorthand for 'lumbricus'</note> whether infused, drunk, or applied as an ointment.
> And **dandruff** <note>original: "furfur," literally meaning 'bran' or 'scurf'</note> of the head is driven away by this oil.
> Even if the bone of the head has by chance been dented by a blow...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the violet’s medicinal properties, specifically focusing on its ability to restore speech and treat respiratory and digestive imbalances. So much so that the patient had lost the use of the tongue.
First, have them take this violet crushed with wine.
Afterwards, if the right side of the head is injured,
Bind this crushed herb to the sole of the left foot;
And do the opposite if the left side is afflicted.
The mouth springs back into place, and the use of the tongue returns on the same day,
As a reliable physician handed down, who wrote such things.
Another power of the violet is said to be wonderful:
If the root of a white violet is held in the mouth
And its juice is frequently swallowed,
They say it soon stops the flow of blood from a moderate wound.
Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman author of the *Natural History*</note> records that this same power belongs to **licorice** <note>original: "liquiritie"</note>.

### ❡ Regarding the herb birthwort [Aristolochia]. 41. <note>While this heading refers to Aristolochia, the text below continues to describe the violet (viola), likely due to a scribal error or a transition in the source manuscript.</note>

❡ If there should be a pustule, etc. If there is a **pustule**—that is, a **bothor** <term>bothorum|A medieval medical term derived from Arabic, referring to various skin eruptions, boils, or pustules</term>—apply violet joined with honey. If the seed of that violet is taken with wine, it clears the **menses** <note>The monthly menstrual flow</note>. Its roots, crushed and drunk mixed with vinegar, dry out the **spleen**—that is, the "splenetic humors"—or you may apply it topically—that is, place it over the spleen, in the region of the spleen. And they say the violet applied in this way calms **"hot" gout** <term>podagram calidam|Inflammation of the joints believed to be caused by an excess of "hot" bodily fluids</term>—that is, gout resulting from hot humors. Violet, when drunk, expels **red bile** <term>coleram rubeam|One of the four vital humors, associated with fire, heat, and bitterness</term> from the stomach.

The green herb—that is, the fresh violet—or the flower, whether dried or fresh, calms diseases arising from red bile or from blood in the "soft ribs" (which are called the **false ribs** <note>The lower ribs that do not attach directly to the sternum</note>) or in the lungs themselves, if it is drunk with **running water** <note>original: "amne repenti," literally a rushing stream or sudden water</note>. Diseases of this kind are called **pleurisy** <term>pleuresis|Inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs</term> and **pneumonia** <term>peripleum|An old medical term for inflammation of the lungs, from the Greek 'peripneumonia'</term>, all of which primarily arise from bile or bilious blood. It also calms a wild cough and likewise calms **shortness of breath** <note>original: "suspiria," literally 'sighs' or 'gasps', referring here to respiratory distress</note>, which are ailments that [afflict] infants...

The following variety is called **round** <note>original: "rotunda"; referring to *Aristolochia rotunda*</note> because it possesses a round root.
The third kind is called **clematis** <note>original: "clematis"; likely *Aristolochia clematitis* or Climbing Birthwort</note> in the Greek tongue.
The medicinal powers of this plant are said to be nearly equal to those of the round variety.
Authorities state that all these plants possess a hot and dry nature.
The drying power is in the first degree, and the heat in the second <note>in medieval Galenic medicine, herbs were classified by "degrees" of intensity from one to four</note>.
Taken with wine, the **round** birthwort cures poisonous bites;

together with pepper and myrrh, it cleanses the body of all putrefaction <gloss>that is, rot or infection</gloss>.

# Fol. 104

Drunk after childbirth for the womb <note>original: "steras"; from the Greek "hystera" for womb</note>, or simply applied as a suppository,
It benefits asthmatics <note>original: "Asmaticis"</note> and drives away chills when taken.
It cures those suffering from pleurisy <note>original: "Pleureticos"; an inflammation of the lungs</note> if taken with myrtle water.
Applied as a crushed poultice, it repels any inflammation;
Mixed with honey and liquid, it cleanses and fills wounds.
But if you combine iris root with the aforementioned, and then
Rub the gums, you cleanse the teeth of rot.

¶ How horrific poison is to all living creatures, even by its very name, has been so proven that nothing could be more certain. Kings who conquer the vastest nations dread a tiny amount of poison more than a massive army of enemies. The bravest soldiers do not fear charging into the thickest of enemies, yet the mere mention of poison strikes the most violent horror into them from head to heel. Lords, commoners, nobles, plebeians, the rich, the poor, the strong, the weak, the wise, and the foolish—all detest poisons. Finally, no class of man can free himself from the fear of poison. To defeat this enemy, so deadly to the human race, Nature—ever caring for human health—produces several herbs for us, among which **birthwort** <note>original: "aristologia"</note> holds no small place. Let us therefore receive this most healthful gift of nature with grateful hearts. We say there are three species of birthwort: the first is called **long** <note>original: "longa"</note>, because its root is long. The next, that is the second, is called **round** <note>original: "rotunda"</note>, because it has a round root. The third is called **clematis** <note>original: "clematis"</note> in the Greek tongue; this is said to be equal to the round variety in its powers. All species are stated <gloss>that is, said</gloss> to have a hot power...

<note>The text concludes mid-sentence, likely continuing to describe the "hot and dry" humoral qualities of the plant on the following page.</note>

**Technical Vocabulary:**
*   **Birthwort** <note>original: "aristologia"</note>: A medicinal plant traditionally used to aid in childbirth and as an antidote.
*   **Poison** <note>original: "venenum"</note>: Any toxic substance, a major source of anxiety in the medieval and Renaissance periods.
*   **Root** <note>original: "radix"</note>: The medicinal part of the birthwort plant described here.
*   **Climbing Birthwort** <note>original: "clematis"</note>: A specific variety of the plant.
*   **After childbirth** <note>original: "post partum"</note>: Referring to the postnatal period.
*   **Asthmatics** <note>original: "asmaticis"</note>: Those suffering from shortness of breath.
*   **Pleurisy sufferers** <note>original: "pleureticos"</note>: Those with pain or inflammation in the chest/lungs.

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded by introducing the "long" species of birthwort. This page continues that botanical survey, transitioning into a detailed description of "round" birthwort (Aristolochia rotunda), its humoral qualities, and its specific applications in obstetrics, respiratory health, and wound care.</meta>

...[roun]d and dry. Its power—that is, its humoral complexion—is dry in the first degree and burning—that is, hot—in the second. **Round birthwort** <note>original: "aristologia rotunda"</note> taken with wine cures—that is, heals—pestilential bites; and when drunk in this way with wine, it is effective against ingested poisons. This same birthwort, taken in this manner (specifically with wine), draws out the **afterbirth** <note>original: "secundas"</note> from women who have just given birth—those membranes in which the infant is wrapped in the womb that are slow or delayed after delivery. As we have said, such a delay is most dangerous for women.

**Round birthwort**, drunk immediately after the child has been brought into the light, taken with pepper (which is very effective at opening the body's channels) and myrrh (which has an innate power against rot), or even simply applied as a suppository without being drunk, purges the **womb** <note>original: "steras"; from the Greek "hystera"</note> of all rot. For, as **Avicenna** <note>A Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians of the Islamic Golden Age (980–1037 AD)</note> says, during the time of pregnancy many "superfluities" <note>excess fluids or waste products</note> collect in the womb, which nature expels during birth as unnecessary. Indeed, as **Hippocrates** <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Medicine"</note> testifies, the mouth of the womb closes around everything contained in the uterus; if any of this remains for even a little while after birth, being entirely deprived of the governance of nature, it falls into decay.

**Round birthwort** benefits asthmatics—that is, those who suffer from shortness of breath—and when taken, it represses chills—that is, the afflictions of cold. For **Dioscorides** <note>An ancient Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years</note> says that **round birthwort**, cooked in oil or pork fat <note>original: "axungia porcina"; lard used as a base for medicinal ointments</note>, warms those scorched by cold. Indeed, immoderate cold burns just as intense heat does; as the poet **Virgil** wrote: "And the piercing cold burns."

**Round birthwort** cures those with **pleurisy** <note>original: "pleureticos"; an inflammation of the lining of the lungs</note> if it is taken in mixed water. When crushed and applied topically, it repels anything stuck in the body, such as a thorn, a wooden splinter, an iron point, and other things of that kind. Mixed with the liquid of honey, it cleanses wounds and fills them with new flesh.

<summary>This page details the medicinal properties of round birthwort, specifically its "hot and dry" nature used to treat poisons, expel the placenta after childbirth, and heal wounds. The author cites classical authorities like Hippocrates, Dioscorides, and Virgil to validate these treatments.</summary>

<keywords>Round Birthwort, childbirth, afterbirth, Avicenna, Hippocrates, Dioscorides, Virgil, pleurisy, wound care, humoral theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of birthwort (Aristolochia), specifically its use in combination with other plants like orris root, before transitioning into a verse-based mnemonic listing its various medicinal virtues, followed by a detailed prose commentary on those verses.</meta>

...it is if you join to the aforementioned things **orris** <note>original: "yrim"; the root of the Iris flower</note>—that is, the root of the iris—and if you anoint the gums and teeth with them, you purge them of rot. For orris is effective against the rot of the **cheeks** <note>original: "gentium", likely a variant of "genarum"</note> and gums, as **Paul the Physician** <note>Paul of Aegina, a 7th-century Byzantine medical encyclopedist</note> testifies.

It dissolves the hardness of the spleen and the pain of the side.
If drunk mixed with water, it is said to curb 
**Malignant fevers** in a wondrous manner.
Thus also, when drunk, it helps above all against **spasms**.
And thus, when frequently drunk, it calms dire **gout**.
And taken thus, it is accustomed to cure the **falling sickness** <note>epilepsy; original: "morbum caducu"</note>.
And thus it mitigates excessive pain of the belly.
And thus, by taking it, the **paralytic** becomes more nimble.
Its smoke is said to drive away **demons**.
They say that infants are cheered by this smoke.
A **fistula** is cured by the roots of this herb,
If you stuff its hollow areas once they are well cleansed.
Taken internally, it is said to calm **hiccups**.

¶ **It dissolves the hardness of the spleen**, etc. **Birthwort** <note>original: "Aristologia"</note> dissolves—that is, it resolves the hardness of the spleen and the pain of the side if it is drunk mixed in water; and drunk in this way, it is said to curb wonderfully—that is, in a wondrous manner—**malignant fevers** (for it is effective when used as a **fumigant** <note>a treatment using smoke or medicinal vapors</note> in the most severe fevers, as **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of "De Materia Medica"</note> says). Also, when it is "drained"—that is, drunk—it helps against **spasms**—that is, the contraction of the limbs (of which something has already been said)—above all things. And birthwort itself, thus frequently drained, calms dire **gout**—that is, a condition cruelly afflicting the patient—and taken then, it is accustomed to cure the **falling sickness**—that is, **epilepsy**—and taken in this way, it mitigates excessive pain...

The text provides a prose commentary on these verses, citing ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Paul of Aegina.</summary>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary on the medicinal virtues of birthwort (Aristolochia), specifically interpreting a series of mnemonic verses. It transitions from physical ailments like paralysis to the spiritual/supernatural properties of the plant's smoke, before returning to surgical applications for fistulas and hiccups.</meta>

(that is, severe [pain] of the belly; and the **paralytic** <note>original: "paraliticus"</note>—that is, one weighed down by the disease of paralysis—may become lighter, that is, more agile, once it is taken—that is, because of the consumption of it. (Now, **paralysis** is a softening of the nerves with the loss of sensation and motion in the whole body, or in half of the body lengthwise, excluding the head. Its cause is the thickening, hardening, and darkening of the substance of the **spinal cord** <note>original: "nuchee"; from the medieval Latin *nucha*, referring to the spinal cord or the nape of the neck</note>, from which the nerves take their origin. This blocks the way for the most radiant **spirits** <note>original: "spiritibus"; in medieval medicine, these were "animal spirits" or vital energies that flowed through the nerves to facilitate movement and thought</note>, preventing them from illuminating the limbs of the body with their brightness.) 

Its smoke is said to drive away **demons**. These demons—or "demonia"—the pagans used to call *Lares* <note>guardian deities of the household</note>, *Genii* <note>individual protective spirits</note>, or *Penates* <note>gods of the pantry or inner house</note>, believing some to be good and others evil. For **Florus** the historian <note>Lucius Annaeus Florus, a Roman historian</note> writes that a certain dark image appeared during the night to **Brutus** <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, one of the assassins of Julius Caesar</note>, who was about to go into battle the following day; when asked what it was, it replied: "I am your evil spirit." And it was written by **Virgil** concerning **Aeneas**, that he went into Italy carrying his "conquered household gods" <note>original: "penates"</note>. 

But our own religious men [Christians] call demons those spirits created in heaven by the Maker of all things, who, having been cast down from the height of heaven, were condemned to perpetual imprisonment—partly in the lowest part of the earth and partly in this air—as a fitting place for them. I could easily believe that these spirits, contaminated as if by the poisonous overflow of sin, flee from **birthwort** <note>original: "aristologiam"</note>, which acts as an expeller of poison. But I reverently omit these matters, which are more properly discussed by theologians <note>original: "divinethologie"</note>. 

Authors report that infants are cheered by this smoke. A **fistula** <note>an abnormal, hollow passage in the body</note> is cured by the roots of this herb if you **stuff**—that is, fill—the hollow areas of that fistula with the root of birthwort; I say this provided the sites themselves have been well cleansed. For the cleansing of the flesh must precede its restoration. When taken, it is said to calm **hiccups** <note>original: "singultus"</note>—that is, the spasmodic movements of the opening of the **stomach**.

<summary>This page explains the medical theory behind paralysis as a blockage of "spirits" in the spinal cord and discusses birthwort's use in driving away demons, contrasting classical Roman spirits with Christian theology. It also details the plant's use in treating fistulas through cleansing and its ability to calm hiccups.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on birthwort (Aristolochia), specifically comparing the long variety (*Aristolochia longa*) to the round variety (*Aristolochia rotunda*) described on previous pages. It concludes the section on birthwort with a reference to Pliny the Elder and the plant's toxicity to fish before transitioning to the virtues of Horehound (*Marrubium*).</meta>

# All things the long [variety] can do, which I said the round can do—
# The long one, however, has those same powers but weaker.

> ¶ Therefore, indeed, if the round variety should ever be lacking,
> ¶ Let a weight and a half of the long be used [instead].
> ¶ A decoction of the long is wonderfully beneficial as a fomentation <note>A warm, medicinal compress or wash</note>
> ¶ For diseases of the womb, by thinning the humors;
> ¶ And it purges the fatty ones if it is taken [internally].
> ¶ Pliny says that this forms male children when [taken] with beef,
> ¶ Applied to the womb after she has conceived, he says.
> ¶ Crushed and mixed with lime, the round variety kills fish;
> ¶ For this reason, some have called it the "poison of the earth."

¶ **Long Birthwort** <note>original: "Aristologia longa"</note> can do all the things I said **Round Birthwort** <note>original: "aristologia rotunda"</note> can do; however, Long Birthwort has weaker powers than the round. Whence—that is, for that reason indeed, certainly—if the round should ever be lacking, a weight and a half—that is, an equal [amount] plus a half—of the long should be used. 

A decoction of the long is wonderfully useful as a **fomentation**—in warm medicinal applications—for diseases of the **womb** <note>original: "matricum"</note>, by thinning—that is, dissolving and clearing out—fatty **humors** <note>original: "humores"; the bodily fluids that medieval medicine believed governed health</note>—that is, thick and putrid ones. And if that same birthwort is taken in a drink (add: "it purges the womb"). 

¶ **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> says that this, applied to the womb with beef in the manner of a **pessary** <note>a small medicinal plug inserted into the vagina</note> after a woman has conceived—that is, has received the male seed into the "field of nature"—forms—that is, shapes—male offspring. 

**Round Birthwort**, crushed and mixed with **lime** <note>original: "calce"; calcium oxide used in mortar</note>—that is, with the lime which, like glue, smears the stones together (so called because it is "trodden" <note>original: "calcetur"</note> in buildings)—for this reason indeed they called it the "poison of the earth" <note>original: "venenum terre"</note>, [because it kills fish].

## Concerning the herb Horehound .42.

Our people called **Horehound** <note>original: "Marrubium"</note> *passio argo* <note>original: "passio argo"; likely a corruption of the Greek *prasium*</note>.
Physicians say this herb is hot and dry in the second [degree].

# The Virtues of Horehound

They say that a decoction of the whole **herb** <note>original: "herbe"</note> [is effective].
Or, a drink made from its seed wonderfully helps **consumptives** <note>original: "ptisicos"; individuals suffering from wasting diseases like tuberculosis</note>.
This **potion** <note>original: "potio"</note> restrains various diseases of the chest,
And it is even more beneficial if **iris** <note>original: "yris"; likely the fragrant orris root</note> is added to it:
Taken in this way, it helps **asthmatics** <note>original: "asmaticos"</note> and drives away a cough.
The same herb hastens **childbirth** <note>original: "pertus" (partus)</note> and expels the **afterbirth** <note>original: "secundas"; the placenta</note>.
If honey is joined with **horehound** <note>original: "marrubio"</note>, it cleanses wounds.
And in this way it cures the **gnawing ulcers** <note>original: "ulcera"</note> of **cancer** <note>original: "cancer"; in a medieval context, this referred to any spreading, corroding, or malignant-looking sore</note>:
This is said, when drunk, to soothe **pain in the side** <note>original: "lateris dolorem"; often referring to pleurisy or internal inflammation</note>.

¶ Our ancestors once attributed it to the most splendid honor to be marked by various names, as the very rich titles of ancient emperors and princes bear witness. From this, we can gather by conjecture that the ancient doctors by no means thought little of this herb, since they decorated it with so many names; for we find it named *prassion*, *synostrophon*, *philopeda*, and *philocares* among the Greeks. By our people, it is indeed called **horehound**. Let us, therefore, turn our minds attentively to the powers of such a renowned herb. Our people—that is <gloss>the Latins</gloss>—called it **horehound**; the Greeks—that is <gloss>the Argives</gloss>—called it *prassion* <note>original: "prassiō"</note>. Doctors teach that this herb is **hot and dry in the second degree** <note>Medieval medicine categorized plants on a scale of 1 to 4 based on their "humoral" effects; the second degree indicates a moderate medicinal potency</note>. A decoction of the whole herb, or even its seed, when drunk, helps **consumptives** <note>original: "ptisicos"; those suffering from wasting diseases like tuberculosis</note> wonderfully—that is <gloss>miraculously</gloss>. This potion restrains—that is <gloss>calms</gloss>—various diseases of the chest; and it benefits those diseases better if **iris** <note>likely orris root, used as an expectorant</note> is joined to it. Taken in this way, it helps **asthmatics** <note>original: "asmaticos"</note> and drives away a cough; in this way—that is <gloss>in this manner</gloss>—it hastens—that is <gloss>causes to accelerate</gloss>—**childbirth** <note>original: "part9" (partus)</note>. It also expels—that is <gloss>draws out</gloss>—the **afterbirth** <note>original: "secundas"</note>. If honey is joined to horehound, it cleanses wounds and in this way cures the internal organs that gnaw away—they consume the flesh, such as **carbuncles** <note>original: "antraces"; infected boils</note>, **cancers** <note>original: "cācros"; in this period, any persistent or eating sore</note>, **creeping sores** <note>original: "herpetes"; various skin rashes</note>, and others of that kind. This horehound, when drunk...

<meta>This page continues Chapter 42 on the Iris plant, which was introduced at the end of the previous page. The text focuses on the botanical origins, preparation, and extensive medicinal applications of the Iris rhizome (root).</meta>

<margin>y</margin>The **Iris** <note>likely *Iris germanica* or *Iris florentina*</note> gives its name to its flowers from their very color, for they are surely similar to the colors of the celestial **rainbow** <note>original: "yris", referring to the Greek goddess of the rainbow. The plant is named for its multi-colored blooms</note>. They call this plant **Illyrian** <note>original: "ylliricam"</note> because it is said to be found most abundantly in **Illyria** <note>a region in the western Balkans, known in antiquity for high-quality iris roots</note>. Its nature is said to be both dry and hot, and physicians assign it the **second degree** <note>referring to the Galenic system of humoral medicine, where drugs were ranked 1–4 based on the intensity of their qualities</note> in both. The greater medicinal power of this plant lies in its roots. These are to be dried after being cut into round pieces. Arrange them on a string <note>original: "filo"</note> and let them be hung in the shade in a dry place in such a way that the pieces do not touch each other; you will hardly be able to dry the whole year's supply in any other way.

Taken with wine, they soothe a **cough** and provide flavor. If hidden **corruption** <note>original: "sanies", referring to pus or infected fluids</note> settles within the **chest** <note>original: "precordia", the area around the heart or upper abdomen</note>, it is expelled by taking the powder of these roots with wine. When drunk with **mead** <note>original: "mulsa", a mixture of water and honey</note>, it drives away harmful **bile** <note>original: "colerã", or "choler", one of the four humors</note>. When drunk mixed with vinegar, it will soothe **griping pains** <note>original: "tormina", referring to intestinal cramps or colic</note>. Taken in this way, they say it is also beneficial against **poisons**. For those with **spleen ailments**, those with **cramped limbs**, and those harmed by **cold**, the powder of the iris drunk with wine will act as a remedy. Taken in this same manner, it clears delayed **menstruation**.

A decoction of the roots softens **uterine hardnesses** <note>original: "duritias stericas", referring to stiffness, tumors, or tension in the womb</note> if the woman is frequently fomented from below with the warm liquid. When applied as a **clyster** <note>original: "clisterium", an enema or internal wash</note> in this manner, they say it brings no small benefit to the pain of **sciatica** <note>original: "sciasis", nerve pain extending from the hip down the leg</note>. From these roots, **medicinal pastes** <note>original: "coliria", usually eye-salves, but here referring to suppositories or pessaries</note> are made by mixing them with honey; when inserted, these expel the **afterbirth** <note>original: "secundas", the placenta</note> from the womb. Also, when placed into hollow sores, they cure **fistulas** <note>original: "siringia", narrow, deep, pipe-like ulcers</note>. The powder mixed with honey dries out **wounds**.

<summary>This page details the preparation of the Iris root (rhizome) and its diverse medical uses, ranging from respiratory relief and digestive aid to gynecological treatments and the healing of fistulas. It emphasizes the importance of drying the root properly in the shade to preserve its "hot and dry" medicinal qualities.</summary>

And it refills naked bones with flesh (if it is added on top). A third part of white **hellebore** <note>original: "ellebori", likely *Helleborus albus*, often used in antiquity for skin conditions despite its toxicity</note> powder is joined with double the amount of fine **iris** root powder. Both powders, bound together with mixed **honey**, when applied on top, clear the face of darkness <note>original: "caligine", which can refer to dim sight or skin discolorations</note> and this **poultice** <note>original: "cathaplasma"</note> drives away its pimples.

¶ "The color gives the name to the Iris," etc. The name **Iris** <note>the text uses "yreos", a Latinization of the Greek, which the author here links to the concept of "heaven"</note> is most sweet to Christian men, for whom celestial delights are the final end and declared highest happiness. Indeed, "Iris" evokes heaven; for the sake of contemplating heaven's admirable beauty, nature gave us a face turned upward and ordered us to "lift our countenances to the stars," as **Ovid** <note>referring to *Metamorphoses* I.85-86: "Adere dedit pronique cum spectent animalia cetera terram / os homini sublime dedit caelumque videre iussit"</note> sings. You recall this to our memory; for the most learned of poets <note>Virgil, in *Aeneid* IV.701</note> writes: "She draws a thousand various colors from the opposing sun." This iris, however, whose powers we are now about to describe, claimed its name for itself because of its similarity in color to the celestial **rainbow** <note>original: "yris"</note>. Regarding this plant, there exist poetic verses <note>original: "phebei versiculis", literally "verses of Apollo"</note>: "The Iris bears a purple flower; the **white iris** <gloss>yreos</gloss> a white one; the **Gladiolus** <note>likely the yellow flag iris, *Iris pseudacorus*</note> a saffron-colored one; but the **stinking iris** <note>original: "spatula fetida", *Iris foetidissima*</note> has none." But let us agree with the poet: the celestial color of its flowers gives the name to the Iris. Truly they are similar to the colors of the rainbow.

Authors call it **Illyrian** <note>original: "illirycam"</note> because it is said—that is, it is reported—to grow most abundantly in **Illyria**. Illyria is a province whose widest borders include Italy on one side, Germany on another, and then Macedonia, Thrace, and Epirus. Its nature is dry and heating—that is, it is called hot—and they assign to this iris the **second degree** <term>degree</term> <note>a system used by ancient and medieval doctors to measure the intensity of a medicine's primary qualities on a scale of 1 to 4</note> in both. The greater power—that is, the medicinal **virtue**—is in the roots of this plant. You, the **pharmacist** <note>original: "pharmacopola", an apothecary or drug-seller</note>, should provide these roots cut—that is, sliced into round pieces to be dried—that is, desiccated. String those round pieces on a thread so that the parts do not <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the previous page indicates the instruction is to ensure the pieces do not touch while drying</note>

Make sure they do not touch each other; have them suspended in the shade, <gloss>that is, in a dry place</gloss>, for you will scarcely be able to dry those iris roots by any other means throughout the whole year. These roots, infused with wine, calm a cough and provide <gloss>that is, induce</gloss> sleep.

And it is a wonder that a warm thing should be sleep-inducing, since sleep (as **Aristotle** says) is a certain powerlessness <note>original: "impotentia", referring to the suspension of the senses</note> of feeling oneself, arising because of the vapor of nourishment cooled in the brain and the internal heat being recalled to the heart; however, it must be conjectured that this happens either from its vaporous smokiness or from a hidden property. For roots are for the most part vaporous, like the turnip, the navew <note>a type of wild cabbage or rape</note>, the onion, the parsnip, and others of that kind.

If corruption—<gloss>that is, diseased matter</gloss> lurking within—settles, <gloss>that is, defiles</gloss> the **precordia**—<gloss>that is, the spiritual members</gloss> <term>precordia</term> <note>the diaphragm or the region around the heart, thought to be the seat of the "vital spirits"</note>—that same corruption is driven out. I say this is done by taking the powder of those iris roots with wine. The powder of those roots drunk with **mead** <note>original: "mulsa", a mixture of honey and water</note> drives out harmful <gloss>that is, malignant</gloss> **choler** <note>one of the four bodily humors, often associated with bile</note>. Iris powder drunk mixed with vinegar will cut through **griping pains**, <term>tormina</term> <note>acute, twisting pains in the intestines</note> <gloss>that is, twistings of the belly</gloss>. 

Authors say this powder taken in this way is beneficial <gloss>that is, it brings a remedy</gloss> for poisons that have been consumed <gloss>that is, taken in</gloss>. And its powder is drunk with wine taken in this way. Likewise, with wine it will heal the **splenetic** <gloss>that is, those troubled by disease of the spleen</gloss>, and those who are contracted <gloss>that is, reduced to a spasm</gloss>, and those harmed by cold penetrating to the innermost parts. 

It also purges delayed **menses** <note>monthly periods</note>. The decoction <note>a liquid made by boiling the plant material</note> of iris roots softens <gloss>that is, it emollies and soothes</gloss> **hysterical hardnesses** <note>original: "duritias hystericas", referring to obstructions or ailments of the womb</note>, <gloss>that is, of the womb</gloss>—if the womb (supply: the area) is frequently bathed with that decoction from below <gloss>that is, over the pubic bone</gloss> and the nearby parts. If this decoction is applied <gloss>that is, injected with a clyster</gloss> <note>an enema</note> through the anus <gloss>that is, through the lower gate</gloss>, physicians say that decoction is of no small benefit for the pain of **sciatica** <term>sciasis</term> <note>pain affecting the back, hip, and outer side of the leg</note> <gloss>that is, the sciatic nerve</gloss>.

### On the herb Elecampane. 43.

**E**lecampane, which the Greek calls *elua* and *helenium*,
Is a form known to all physicians;
Its nature is said to be moist and fiery,
The moisture is said to be in the first degree and the heat in the second <note>In medieval medicine, herbs were graded 1 to 4 by their "qualities" of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note>.
A decoction of it is said to purge the **menses**;
If drunk, it moves the urine and drives out a miscarriage.
This same herb is said to loosen a bound belly <note>meaning it acts as a laxative for constipation</note>.
The crushed root, applied over the hip, puts **sciatica** <term>ciasim</term> to flight.
From its leaves cooked with wine...

It is beneficial to wrap [the root] in myrrh or in twigs.
The powder of its roots, when swallowed with honey,
Checks a cough and heals those with **orthopnea** <note>original: "orthonoicis"; a condition where one can only breathe comfortably while standing or sitting upright</note>.

¶ **Elecampane**, which the Greek... etc. The Greeks (as **Macer** <note>referring to Macer Floridus, the pseudonymous author of a famous 11th-century poem on the virtues of herbs</note> excellently perceives) call this herb *elenium* from *eleon* <note>the Greek word *eleos*, meaning mercy</note>, as I judge; which Greek word signifies **merciful** <note>original: "misericordem"</note> among us. From this, the god **Liber** <note>the Roman god of wine and freedom, often identified with Bacchus</note>, under whose protection poets serve, is called *Eleus*, as if he were a "pious father" because he is free.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Elecampane (Enula campana) from the previous page, specifically focusing on its etymological roots and its diverse applications in treating respiratory, digestive, and renal issues, before transitioning into Chapter 45 on Hyssop.</meta>

...charity <note>original: "tatē," likely *caritatem*; meaning dearness or high value</note> is the highest value for men, as **Simon Terentianus** <note>Terentianus Maurus, a 2nd-century Roman poet and grammarian who wrote on medicinal plants</note> says; likewise, these words provide that it is rightly called *elenium* because its most pious nature, offering itself everywhere to everyone without any price, has mercifully bestowed a health-giving medicine for the many continuous ailments of men. The poet says, therefore, that this herb which the Greek calls *elua*—that is, **elecampane**—is called *enula* by Latin physicians (to provide the name). Its form—that is, its appearance—is known to all. Its power—that is, its **complexion** <term>complexio</term> <note>the specific balance of "humors" (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) that defines a substance's medicinal nature</note>—is said to be moist and fervent—that is, hot.

But a decoction of it purges the menses; if drunk, it moves—that is, provokes—urine, and drives out a stillbirth—that is, a dead fetus in the womb. This same decoction is said to loosen packed—that is, **constipated**—bowels. The root of this herb, ground and applied to the hip (which is also called the *coxendix*, where the thigh is joined and tightened to the upper part), drives away—that is, exterminates—**sciatica** <note>original: "scyasim"; a painful condition affecting the sciatic nerve</note>, that is, the sciatic disease. It is wonderfully beneficial for **nephritics** <note>original: "neufreticis"; those suffering from kidney disease</note> to wrap—that is, cover—the kidneys with the leaves of this herb cooked with nectar wine—which is excellent and sweet. 

And the powder of its roots, swallowed—that is, gulped down—little by little by licking, suppresses a cough and heals those with **orthopnea** <note>original: "orthonoicis"; a condition where one can only breathe comfortably while standing or sitting upright</note>, that is, asthmatics. If the juice of this [plant] is taken with the juice of **rue** <note>a bitter medicinal herb frequently used in ancient pharmacology</note>, authors affirm that such a **potion** <term>potio</term> is beneficial for the **ruptured** <note>original: "ruptis"; referring to those suffering from an abdominal hernia</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Hyssop. 45.

**Hyssop** is dry and likewise hot; the third degree <note>the "third degree" indicates a high level of intensity in medieval pharmacology, which used a scale from 1 to 4</note> 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Is in each property for it. A decoction of it is made
So that honey and also a dried fig are cooked together with it.

A drink of this herb <note>The text here transitions to discussing Hyssop, as illustrated in the woodcut</note> is of no small benefit to those suffering from a cough. It aids a hoarse voice when gargled <note>original: "versata palato," literally "turned in the palate"</note> and is useful for all complaints of the lungs when consumed. This same plant, when drunk, expels "slippery things" <note>original: "lubricos"; a common medieval reference to intestinal worms</note> from the belly.

If a **distillation** <term>distillatio</term> <note>the downward flow of excess "humors" or phlegm from the head to the chest, known today as catarrh or post-nasal drip</note> from the head should occur into the chest—which very often tends to create both a cough and **phthisis** <note>original: "ptisim"; a historical term for a wasting disease or pulmonary consumption, often identified as tuberculosis</note>—this same decoction, taken frequently, is beneficial. For all these ailments, the powder of the herb joined with honey is also helpful.

¶ I believe the poet <note>The "poet" refers to the author of the original verse herbarium, likely Macer Floridus</note> has industriously placed these three herbs or their roots—Elecampane <term>enula</term> and Hyssop <term>ysopum</term> (with no other intervening)—in successive order, because physicians most frequently apply them to illnesses of the respiratory organs <term>spiritualium membrorum</term> <note>literally "spiritual members," referring to the organs of breath or "pneuma," such as the lungs</note>. But lest you perhaps hesitate, good boy <note>a common address to a student in pedagogical texts</note>, as to how "wet" and "dry" hyssop differ from one another: First of all, I would have you know that when you encounter "wet hyssop" <note>original: "ysopushumida"</note> in the books of physicians, you should not understand it as the herb itself whose powers we are now preparing to describe. Rather, it refers to the filth and juices of greasy wool <note>original: "lanarum succidarum"; this refers to lanolin or the sweat and grease found in raw wool. This was often called "oesypum," which medieval writers frequently confused with the herb hyssop</note>, so called because they are as they were when shorn—that is, impure and filthy.

By "dry hyssop," however, understand the herb, whose temperament <term>complexionem</term> and powers you may receive in a few words. But lest you err even at the threshold, you should know that the herb hyssop is used by our author in the neuter gender. We say, namely, that the herb hyssop is both dry and hot; that is, it is even in the **third degree** in both <note>In Galenic medicine, the "degree" measured the intensity of a plant's qualities on a scale of 1 to 4</note>.

And this same decoction, when "turned"—that is, swirled in the manner of a gargle <term>gargarismatis</term>—in the palate—that is, within the throat—assists a hoarse voice, that is, one that is muffled. When drunk, it is beneficial for all complaints of the lungs <term>pulmonum</term>. This same decoction, when drunk, drives out worms <term>lumbricos</term> <note>specifically intestinal roundworms</note> from the belly. <meta>This page continues the medical discussion of Hyssop, following the previous page's distinction between "wet" hyssop (lanolin) and "dry" hyssop (the herb). It focuses on the respiratory and digestive benefits of the herb, citing the authority of Avicenna.</meta>

It stimulates natural expulsion, which, aided by the motive power of the inner muscles of the chest, strives to drive out harmful matter. This is how a cough <term>tussis</term> is produced; if it persists for a long time, the lung will rarely escape being harmed by an ulcer. This condition leads to consumption <term>ptisiis</term> <note>original: "ptisiis"; a historical term for pulmonary tuberculosis or any wasting disease of the lungs</note>. Taking this decoction <term>decoctio</term> <note>a liquid medicine made by boiling herbs in water</note> frequently is helpful for drawing out catarrhal humors <note>thick secretions or mucus</note> from the chest. Powder of dry hyssop mixed with honey is beneficial for all these aforementioned illnesses.

If oxymel <note>a medicinal drink made of honey and vinegar</note> is added to that "green" powder—that is, freshly ground hyssop—and given as a drink, it will soften a bound or constipated belly, driving out viscous phlegm <term>flegma</term> along with harmful wind <note>gas or flatulence</note>. For such gas can cause colic <term>colicam</term>, iliac pain <term>iliacam</term> <note>severe abdominal pain associated with bowel obstruction</note>, or other sharp, stinging pains. You will loosen the bowels more effectively if you add cardamom <note>original: "cordume" or "cordumenti"</note> to these. Indeed, Avicenna <note>a famous 11th-century Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard medical authority in the Middle Ages</note> says, when speaking of dry hyssop, that it dissolves phlegm; and when it is mixed with cardamom, its power to purge is strengthened.

If a drink of hyssop, whether fresh or dry, is often consumed,
It is said to provide an excellent color to the face.
If you cook it with saltpeter <note>original: "nitro"</note> and dried figs,
And apply it as a poultice <term>cathaplasma</term> over a swollen spleen,
It is accustomed to drive away the disease in those suffering from dropsy <term>ydropicis</term> <note>edema or swelling caused by fluid retention</note> by its drying effect.
Drunk with wine, it relaxes a tight chest <note>original: "precordia"; the area around the heart and diaphragm</note>,
And any harmful internal swelling recedes.
If it is boiled in vinegar to make a warm compress,
It is said that one is often quickly relieved of excessive pain.
By the smoke of the herb set on fire, ringing in the ear is stilled.
Applied as a boiled mash, it clears away all bruises <note>original: "liuores"</note>;
Likewise, if it is injected into the nostrils, it helps those with jaundice <term>yctericos</term>.

## Consider first the age and strength
## Of the one you wish to purge, and the season of the year:
## And whether the region be burning or very cold
## Or if it holds a middle temperament between the two
## For it is permitted to give but little to the elderly and children
## Those of middle age and youths require more than these
## More should be given to the stout and strong than to the thin.
## And more to weak men in a cold region
## Should be given than in a hot one; and to those of hard labor
## You shall grant more than to those whom leisure makes soft.

¶ It is Asarabacca <term>assarum</term>, etc. Some (as Sypontinus <note>Niccolò Perotti, known as Sypontinus, was a 15th-century humanist whose grammatical and encyclopedic works were widely cited.</note> says) have called Asarabacca "Nard," which authors claim blooms twice a year; let us therefore pluck its sweetest flowers. *Asarum* is the Greek name; in Latin, it is *vulgago*. This herb is said to be of a hot and dry nature; it belongs to the third degree <term>gradus</term> in both qualities, as physicians say. This herb, when drunk, provokes urine; and when drunk, it cleanses the menstrual flow. This herb, taken in this manner, treats pain of the liver and helps those with dropsy <term>ydropicos</term> <note>a condition of swelling caused by excess fluid, known today as edema</note>. When swallowed frequently, it puts to flight—that is, eliminates—sciatica <term>sciasim</term>, which is to say, the sciatic disease. A decoction <term>decoctio</term> of it aids diseases of the vulva, that is, the womb <term>matricis</term>. When drunk, it [cleanses] the stomach <note>original: "dici dia. id est stomachum"; likely referring to the cardia or opening of the stomach.</note> (for the ancients, as Galen <term>Galienus</term> <note>A Greek physician whose medical theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> says, were accustomed to call this organ the "heart" <term>cor</term> <note>original: "hoc membrū cor appellare consueuerunt"; referring to the "cardia" or upper part of the stomach.</note>) through vomiting—that is, by way of vomiting, in the manner of hellebore <term>ellebori</term> <note>A potent plant used in antiquity as a powerful, often dangerous, purgative and emetic.</note> (which is the most violent of all in provoking vomiting). But the purging <term>purgatio</term> of this plant is not so violent by nature—that is, not inflicting as much violence—as hellebore; indeed, it is certainly so.

This emetic purgation <note>original: "purgatio vomitiua"; a treatment intended to induce vomiting</note> is to be feared <gloss>that is, dreaded</gloss> if it is performed in such a way <gloss>namely, with hellebore or some other violent substance, such as nux vomica or asarabacca</gloss>. ->Art / age / region / strength / complexion / form /<-
->Habit and symptom / fullness / time and air.<-

For it is permitted to give but little <gloss>that is, rarely and when necessity is pressing</gloss> to the elderly <gloss>because their strength is like a lamp ready to be extinguished, as Galen <note>original: "Gale."; the 2nd-century Greek physician whose work formed the basis of medieval medicine</note> testifies</gloss> and to children <gloss>because this age is tender, soft, and easily dissolved, and they are sufficiently purged by regular bodily evacuations</gloss>. 

Middle-aged men and youths require <gloss>that is, seek</gloss> to be purged more than these, namely children and the elderly <gloss>for youths are, as Galen himself says, stronger and more tolerant, and more perfect in nature; and also because of their lack of moderation <note>original: "inordinationem"; referring to excessive eating or lifestyle</note>, they stand in more need of the said purgation</gloss>. And more medicine should be given to the strong <gloss>that is, those with vigorous bodies</gloss> than to the thin and weak. For an excess of thinness...

...however, its rarity prohibits it, as Avicenna <note>the 11th-century Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> testifies, for fear that the vital spirit <term>spiritus: the subtle substance thought to flow through the body carrying life force</term> might be dissipated. Medicine <gloss>pharmacia</gloss> should rather be given in a chilly <gloss>that is, cold</gloss> region, provided the cold is not so piercing that it would overly constrict the pathways, congeal the humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance determined health</term>, or weaken the vital faculty. You should also give a larger dose of medicine to those who are "hard" <gloss>that is, accustomed to labor</gloss> and to those whom idleness makes "soft" <gloss>that is, fragile and loose</gloss>. You will safely give not only this one, but any herbs
Which purge the stomach by vomiting or loosen the bowels.
But since I have spoken generally about all herbs,
I shall now teach how asarabacca <term>vulgago: *Asarum europaeum*, a plant used in medieval medicine to induce vomiting and sneezing</term> specifically is to be given.
Taking thirty fresh leaves of it,
Add as much pure wine as is needed to cover them all.
Let them soak <gloss>macerate</gloss> in that wine for a whole night.
In the morning, grinding them, dissolve them in the wine in which they soaked.
Then, a cooked dish of vegetables with fresh, fatty pork
Should first be given to the patient in sufficient quantity.
Let him drink as much strong white wine as he wishes;
Then, provide the collected juice of the plant to be drunk.
This number of leaves is for the strong and the large;
For the rest (as we have said) it must be reduced
According to what strength, age, and other factors require.

page xlvi.
# Concerning the herb Mint.

¶ Mint carries a hot and dry power in the second degree <term>degrees: the medieval Galenic system of medicine categorized herbs on a scale of 1 to 4 based on their "elemental" qualities of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</term>.
The power of digestion is aided by a drink of it.
It comforts the stomach and also stops vomiting when it is drunk.
It is also said to drive away intestinal worms in the same manner.
It treats various diseases of the testicles,
if they are bathed in water in which the herb mint has been boiled.
Crushed and applied to the breasts, it dissolves curdled milk.
Dropped in with honey, it drives away the pain of the ear.
Roughness of the tongue vanishes if it is rubbed frequently with it.
Drunk with burdock <note>original: *lappa*, likely referring to *Arctium lappa*</note>, it is accustomed to hasten childbirth.
Crushed and applied with salt, it cures the bite of a dog.
Mixed with vinegar, it assists those who spit blood <term>hemoptysis: a condition where one coughs up blood from the respiratory tract</term>.
If its juice is applied to the womb before
intercourse takes place, a woman will not conceive therefrom <note>Mint was frequently cited in ancient and medieval texts as a contraceptive</note>.
Its juice does not allow small cheeses to rot if mixed in,
or if the green herb is placed on top of them.

¶ **Warm power.** It is written in the fables that Pluto <note>original: *Platonem*, a common medieval scribal error or variant for the god Pluto</note> held as a favorite a nymph named Minthe, who was quite remarkable for the beauty of her body. When Proserpina, the wife of Pluto, caught her in adultery and wished to punish her, Pluto transformed her into mint. However, if one wishes to "unravel the bark" <gloss>that is, explain the outer layer</gloss> of the fable, the sweet fragrance of this herb is revealed to us. For Pluto, who was the son of Saturn and the younger born, received the lowest region—that is, the earth—which provides nourishment for all things; after his own wife caught him...

...unchastity embraced Minthe. Likewise, the stomach, which occupies the lowest region of the body and is the servant of the internal organs and from which the other limbs are nourished—as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was a prominent Roman physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note> says—after it is affected by a weariness for its proper spouse (that is, its accustomed nourishment), it turns itself toward mint. Nothing is more friendly to any stomach than mint; it is also called *hedyosmos* <note>original: *hedyosmus*</note>. For *hedy* in Greek means "sweet," and *osmy* means "smell" <note>Greek: ἡδύοσμος, literally "sweet-smelling."</note>. Therefore, let us make use of the fragrance that mint bears.

¶ Mint has a hot and dry power in the second degree <term>degrees: the medieval Galenic system of medicine categorized herbs on a scale of 1 to 4 based on their "elemental" qualities of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</term>. The power of digestion is aided by a drink of this herb; it also comforts the stomach. For indeed, this same mint, when swallowed—that is, drunk—retains—that is, stops—vomiting; and it is said to drive out intestinal worms in the same manner.

The herb itself treats various diseases of the testicles if the testicles themselves are bathed in water in which the herb mint has been boiled. Crushed mint applied—that is, placed upon—the breasts—that is, the nipples—dissolves—that is, breaks down or clears—curdled—that is, clotted—milk. Mint, or its water, instilled—that is, poured in—with honey, chases away the pain of the ear. Roughness of the tongue vanishes if it is frequently rubbed with the herb. 

And mint itself, swallowed with *sapa* <note>original: *sapa*, a syrup of grape juice (must) boiled down until it is thick</note>—that is, with wine boiled until two parts are consumed—tends to hasten childbirth. Crushed and applied on top with salt, it cures the bite of a dog. It assists "hemoptoics"—that is, those spitting blood—if, I say, it is mixed with vinegar. If the juice of that herb is placed within the womb before intercourse takes place, a woman will not conceive therefrom—that is, from that intercourse <note>Mint was frequently cited in ancient and medieval texts as a contraceptive.</note>. Its juice, when mixed in, does not allow—that is, does not permit—little cheeses (so called from curdled milk) to rot; for as Silvaticus <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus (c. 1280–1342), a medieval physician and botanist known for his medical encyclopedia, the *Pandectarum Medicinae*.</note> says, mint keeps cheese safe from rot, or the green herb is placed on top of it.

With cyperus <term>cyperus: likely *Cyperus longus* (sweet cyperus) or *Cyperus rotundus*, known for their aromatic, ginger-like roots</term>, the doctors say it is hot and dry in the second degree <term>degrees: the medieval Galenic system of medicine categorized herbs on a scale of 1 to 4 based on their "elemental" qualities of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</term>. They say that by a drink of it, a stone <note>original: *calculus*</note> of the urine is driven out. It purges and loosens the passages. 

Apollodorus <note>Apollodorus of Citium (c. 1st century BC), a Greek physician whose works on herbs and poisons were frequently cited by Roman and medieval authors.</note> says that it can drive out a miscarriage <note>original: *abortum*; in medieval medical texts, this often refers to clearing the womb of a fetus that had died in utero or aiding in the expulsion of the placenta.</note> if a woman is bathed with the water in which it has been boiled. He asserts that from this the spleen <note>original: *lienem*; an enlarged spleen was a common diagnosis in humor-based medicine, often treated with "drying" herbs like cyperus.</note> can be shrunken. 

If the smoke of this plant is frequently inhaled, or if a healthy person fumigates themselves with that smoke, he says that person becomes stronger and more agile <note>original: *leviorem*, literally "lighter" or "more nimble."</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Peony. 42.

Doctors confess that Peony is hot and dry;
They say it holds the second degree <term>degrees: the Galenic system of grading the intensity of an herb's "elemental" qualities on a scale from 1 to 4.</term> in both qualities.
Taken with honey-water <note>original: *mulsa*; a mixture of water and honey, often fermented.</note>, it will help the spleen, liver, and kidneys,
If bitter almonds are also added and thoroughly crushed.
Thus it purges the menses and restrains the flow of the bowels.
A decoction of it in wine, drunk frequently,
Dries up the hard pains of the bladder when taken so.
And thus it helps scotosis <note>original: *scothosim*; a term for dizziness or the darkening of vision.</note> and pain of the stomach.
The same greatly helps those with kidney disease <note>original: *Neufreticos*</note> and those with jaundice;
The stone <note>original: *calculus*; refers to kidney or bladder stones.</note> of infants is driven out by a drink of it.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new chapter on the Peony, following the conclusion of the section on Cyperus (galingale). The text transitions from the practical uses of the previous plant to the mythological and etymological origins of the Peony, drawing on classical literature and contemporary poetry.</meta>

The seed of the Peony (if it is drunk quite often) wards off the pressings <note>original: *Preffuras*; this refers to the sensation of weight on the chest during sleep paralysis or nightmares, historically attributed to a "night-mare" or "incubus."</note> which dreams are accustomed to bring on at night.

Paeon <note>A figure in Greek mythology known as the physician to the gods; his name became a title for Apollo and a synonym for a healer.</note> was most highly skilled in the art of medicine. Homer says in the fifth book of the *Iliad* that he cured Pluto <note>The Roman name for Hades, god of the underworld. In the *Iliad*, Hades is wounded by an arrow from Hercules and is healed by Paeon’s divine ointments.</note> after he had been gravely wounded by Hercules. It is established that from him, the medicinal arts are called "Paeonian." Whence Faustus <note>Publius Faustus Andrelinus (c. 1462–1518), a celebrated Italian humanist poet who taught in Paris and was known for his elegiac poetry.</note>, the most auspicious crown and glory of the poets of this time, says in his *Livia*:

*I would not be free from the Paeonian art.* <note>original: *Non ego peonia liber ab arte forē*; a poetic way of saying he would not be healthy or cured without the art of medicine.</note>

Paeon was the first to prove, by a most evident experiment, a certain very hidden property of this herb against epilepsy <term>epylesia: historically known as the "falling sickness," often treated with peony seeds or roots in the Middle Ages and Renaissance</term>. Thus it happened that this herb was called Peony after its discoverer, Paeon.

Physicians confess—that is, they assert—that the Peony is hot and dry and <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with the "degrees" of these qualities according to Galenic humoral theory.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal properties of the Peony, specifically its humoral qualities and its effectiveness against internal blockages and neurological conditions. It follows the previous page's introduction of the plant's mythological origins and its use in treating epilepsy.</meta>

They say that this plant holds a middle nature in the second degree <note>In medieval "humoral" medicine, drugs were classified on a scale of 1 to 4 based on how much they heated, cooled, dried, or moistened the body. The "second degree" indicates a moderate medicinal strength.</note> regarding both heat and cold. If Peony is taken with mead <note>original: *mulsa*; a mixture of water and clarified honey</note>—that is, with water and skimmed honey—it will benefit the spleen, liver, and kidneys by opening the blockages <note>original: *opilationes*</note> of those organs, as well as by thinning, cutting through, and wiping away superfluous humors.

If raw and bitter almonds—which open passages more effectively, as Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and botanist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary source for herbal knowledge for 1,500 years.</note> says—are ground violently (that is, reduced to a very fine powder) and joined with it, it cleanses the menses and restrains the flow of the bowels (that is, the belly). 

Peony taken in this way dries—that is, desiccates—the harsh pains of the bladder. It likewise helps *scotosis* (that is, scotomia) <term>scotomia: a condition where a person experiences dizziness or sees "spots" or "flies" in their vision</term>, which is an affliction of the "common sense" <note>The "common sense" was believed to be an internal faculty that processed and unified the data from the five physical senses.</note> where one judges that little flies appear to flutter before the eyes, even though it is actually a windy vapor violently agitated in the path of the visual spirit. 

It also helps pain of the stomach and greatly aids those suffering from kidney disease (that is, those burdened by kidney ailments) and the jaundiced. A drink made of it dispels the "calculus" (that is, the little grains of sand in infants). This disease, as Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire.</note> says, is characteristic of this age [infancy] due to their gluttony, from which many raw and numerous humors gather in the body and thicken with the urine in the bladder, whereby the material for a stone is formed. Furthermore, because the heat of those infants is great, that soft material coagulates and turns into stone; thus says Galen.

Seven seeds of peony, when drunk, ward off (that is, remove) "pressures" <note>original: *pressuras*; this refers to the sensation of weight on the chest during sleep paralysis or nightmares.</note> which dreams are accustomed to bring on at night. This pressure is called an **incubus** in the specific terminology of physicians. Long ago, people called incubi "evil demons," "fauns," and "silvans"; and if one gives credit to superstitious antiquity, it was found that these wicked beings sometimes harassed women and subjected them to their desire and performed intercourse. 

Physicians, however, say an **incubus** is an affliction in which, while dreaming, a heavy compression seems to trouble the body; this takes its origin from an abundance of phlegm <note>One of the four bodily humors, associated with cold and moisture.</note> blocking the back of the head <note>original: *puppim capitis*; literally the "stern" or "poop deck" of the head.</note> when one sleeps on their back.

Its root, hung [around the necks] of boys with the falling sickness <note>original: *caducis*; refers to epilepsy, traditionally treated by wearing peony roots as an amulet.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal properties of the Peony. Having previously established its humoral qualities, the text now focuses on the "sympathetic" or "amuletic" power of the root as described by Galen, as well as its specific applications for women's reproductive health according to Dioscorides.</meta>

Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a thousand years.</note>, as the author, asserts that the plant is of no small benefit. 
He tells of having seen a certain boy suffering from the falling sickness <note>original: *caducum*; a historical term for epilepsy, so named because the sufferer "falls" during a seizure.</note> who was about eight years of age. 
This boy used to wear a peony root hung around his neck; on one occasion, it fell off, and soon the boy fell into a seizure. 
When it was tied back on in the usual way, he immediately recovered. 
Galen, wishing to test the matter fully, took the root away from the boy's neck again: the boy fell once more, and then flourished <note>original: *viguit*; regained his strength or health</note> when it was replaced. 
Thus, the power of this herb was clearly and manifesty recognized. 

Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work *De Materia Medica* was the foundational text for herbalism.</note> himself says it is suitable for all those with the falling sickness, whether it is drunk or hung around the neck. 
Five grains of its red seed, when taken with wine, restrain the "flow of the womb" <note>original: *fluxum matricis*; likely referring to excessive menstrual bleeding or hemorrhage</note>. 
The black seeds are accustomed to drive away various diseases of the womb if an equal number <note>i.e., five seeds</note> are given to be drunk with wine at night. 

There are two species of this plant: one is called the "male," and the other, which is the smaller species, is called the "female." 
The root of the larger [male] is two palms <note>A "palm" was a traditional unit of measurement based on the width of the human hand.</note> long and thick; the root of the smaller [female] is divided into several parts the width of six fingers. 
It is said that this [female] root, when mixed with wine and drunk, effectively cleanses the womb <term>hysteras: the womb or uterus, derived from the Greek *hystera*</term> after childbirth. 
In all other respects, their medicinal power is said to be similar.

<margin>¶ Regarding the root of that herb: the root of that herb, when hung upon boys who are "falling" (that is, those laboring under the disease of epilepsy, who are called "falling" because they fall down), is of no small benefit, as Galen says.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal properties of the Peony. Having previously established its humoral qualities, the text now focuses on the "sympathetic" or "amuletic" power of the root as described by Galen, as well as its specific applications for women's reproductive health according to Dioscorides.</meta>

The author asserts the same: Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (c. 129–216 AD), a Roman physician whose medical theories influenced Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> tells that he saw a certain boy suffering from the falling sickness <term>falling sickness|A historical name for epilepsy, so called because the patient "falls" during a seizure.</term> whose age was approximately eight years—that is, a little more or less. This boy used to wear a peony root hung around his neck; once—that is, on one occasion—it fell off, and soon—that is, immediately after the falling of the said root—the boy fell into a seizure. When the root was tied back—that is, replaced around the neck in the usual manner—he escaped the fit—that is, he rose up immediately—that is, at once. 

But <note>original: *vero* / *sed*</note>—that is, nonetheless—Galen, wishing to prove—that is, to test—the matter—that is, the power <note>original: *virtutem*</note> of that root—fully—that is, exactly <note>original: *eramussim*; an archaic Latin term for "by the rule" or "perfectly."</note>—removed—that is, moved—the root again—that is, once more—from the boy’s neck. The boy fell, and when the root was taken back up, he flourished—that is, he regained his former strength. The power of this herb was clearly recognized in this way—that is, in this manner. 

Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical manual for centuries.</note> himself says it is beneficial for those with the falling sickness, whether it is drunk or if that root is hung around the neck. Thrice five—that is, fifteen—red grains of its seed, taken with wine, restrain the flow of the womb <note>original: *fluxum matricis*; likely referring to excessive menstrual bleeding.</note>. The black grains of that peony are accustomed to drive away various diseases of the womb, namely "suffocation" <note>original: *suffocationem*; referring to "suffocation of the womb," a historical diagnosis where breathing difficulties were attributed to a displaced uterus.</note>, as Dioscorides says, if an equal number—that is, fifteen—black peony grains are given to be drunk with wine at night. 

For as Matthaeus Silvaticus <note>A 14th-century physician and botanist famous for his medical encyclopedia.</note> says, upon the tops of its branches there are structures like little tubes, and in them appear little red granules like blood, small and similar to the seeds of a pomegranate; and among those grains are black seeds. 

There are twin species of this peony—that is, two types are found: one species is called the "male," and the other is a smaller species and called the "female." The root of the greater peony is two palms <note>Roughly 6 to 8 inches.</note> long and six fingers <note>Roughly 4 to 5 inches.</note> thick. The root of the smaller peony is said to be divided into many roots. This root of the smaller peony, mixed with wine and drunk, effectively purges the womb <note>original: *steras*, from the Greek *hysteras*.</note> after childbirth. A similar power is said to exist in them for all other related matters.

The herb which the Greeks called "honey-lover" <note>original: *mellisophilos*; from the Greek *melissophyllon*, meaning "bee-leaf," the plant we now know as Lemon Balm (*Melissa officinalis*).</note>

This is said to be the most pleasing of all herbs to the bees,
And they seem to delight in the flower of no other more.

That plant also brings immediate aid for bee stings,
If the sting is covered at once with the rubbed herb.

Virgil, being not ignorant and instructing farmers by what

¶ The woman is purged <note>In a medical context, this usually refers to the stimulation of menstrual flow or the cleansing of the uterus after childbirth.</note>, and so forth. A woman is purged with the juice—that is, in the juice of this herb boiled with salt, harmful bloating <gloss>flatulence</gloss> is dispersed—that is, it is resolved by such a drink. A decoction of this green herb should be drunk often; it helps those with dysentery—that is, those suffering from a bloody flux of the belly—and those with "celiac" disease—that is, those vexed by the celiac passion <note>Historical term for chronic diarrhea or malabsorption issues.</note>. This same herb benefits asthmatics and orthopnoics <note>People who can only breathe comfortably while standing or sitting upright.</note>, about whom we have said enough. But because, excellent youth, you have read "apium" <term>apium|Commonly means parsley or celery.</term> once and again in this chapter, which Alexander <note>Likely Alexander of Villa Dei, a 13th-century grammarian whose "Doctrinale" was a standard textbook.</note> (to whom you are perhaps too devoted) writes for his students, "When the winged bee [*apis*] gives..." <note>The author is addressing a potential grammatical confusion between the word for parsley (apium) and the genitive plural for bees (apium).</note>, our author has an authority no inferior to Alexander to protect him. This verse is from Juvenal <note>A Roman satirical poet (late 1st/early 2nd century AD).</note>, whose authority among the Latins is well established: "And a long swarm of bees [*apium*] hangs together in one cluster." <note>original: "Examenque apium longa confederat una." This quote from Juvenal's Satires is used here to prove that 'apium' can correctly refer to bees in a grammatical sense.</note>
