<meta>This page continues the discussion of medicinal herbs, transitioning from Groundsel to Chapter 52, which focuses on Celandine (Chelidonium majus). The previous page concluded with a ritual for toothache and a mention of Pliny the Elder's authority.</meta>

The other variety is indeed smaller: both are used to treat the eyes.
The mother swallow <gloss>Latin: yrundo</gloss> restores sight to her blinded chicks with this herb,
(As Pliny writes) even if their eyes have been plucked out.
He says this herb sprouts when those birds arrive,
And withers in the usual way when they depart;
From this, it takes the name *celidonia*: for *chelidon*
Is what this bird is called in the Greek fashion.
Let the juice of the flowering plant be cooked with honey in a bronze vessel
Over a gentle fire, until it has cast off all its foam,
And the decoction takes on the thickness of honey.
They say no medicine is more useful for the eyes than this.
Those whom dimness harms will benefit if they are often anointed with this.
Its root is ground up with dill added to it,
And thus it is taken with white wine for those suffering from jaundice <term>yctericis|Jaundice; a yellowing of the skin and eyes, which medieval medicine often treated with "yellow" plants like Celandine due to the Doctrine of Signatures</term>.
When applied to the teeth, the crushed plant prevents pain.
If a poultice <term>cathaplasma|Poultice; a soft, moist mass of herbal material applied to the body to relieve soreness or inflammation</term>
Is made from its crushed leaves with wine, they say it wipes away spots on the skin.

¶ Common doctors believe this herb was named *calidonia* by the Latins for this reason—as if it were a "gift of heaven" <note>original: "celi donum"</note>—on account of its truly excellent virtues. Although this interpretation does not seem at all ridiculous, it has been discovered that the name has a Greek rather than a Latin origin. For in the Greek tongue, *chelidon* signifies a swallow. For the swallows taught us that Celandine is most healthful for the sight by applying it to the troubled eyes of their chicks.

¶ Furthermore, we ought to pronounce it *chelidonia*, and not *celidonia*. If the word were derived from *celum* <gloss>heaven</gloss>, it would have a long first syllable; however, Macer <note>Referring to Aemilius Macer, the traditional authority for the herbal poem *De viribus herbarum*</note> easily demonstrates in this first verse that it is a short syllable. Hemistich... <note>The text ends mid-word, likely referring to a "hemistich" or half-line of verse used to prove a point of poetic meter.</note>

...two species of celandine are related—that is, said to exist—by doctors. The first is called the greater; however—that is, but—the other is called the lesser: both are medicinal. That is, they help dim eyes <note>original: "oculis caligantibus"</note>. [To continue:] the mother swallow <gloss>Latin: yrundo</gloss> restores sight to her chicks—that is, her little offspring—when they have been blinded, that is, deprived of light. Even if their eyes have been plucked out—that is, dug out—as Pliny says <note>Pliny the Elder, whose 'Natural History' was the primary source for the legend that swallows used Celandine to heal their young's eyes</note>, he asserts that this herb sprouts when these birds arrive (namely, at the beginning of spring) and says this herb withers when they depart in the usual manner. Celandine takes its name from the swallows; for this bird, namely the swallow, is said to be called *chelidon* <note>original: "chelidon"</note> in the manner of the Greeks. Let the juice of flowering celandine be cooked in bronze—that is, a bronze vessel. (Serapio <note>likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arabic medical authority whose works were translated into Latin</note>, however, says a bronze vessel is harmful for preparing foods). Let it be cooked over a light fire—that is, a tempered one—with honey until it has cast off—that is, skimmed off—all its foam, and until the decoction takes on—that is, assumes—as it were, the thickness—that is, the density—of honey. Medical authors say no medicine is more useful for eyes which dimness harms—that is, afflicts—if they are often anointed with this liniment. The root of that celandine is ground up—I mean with the seed of dill (a certain herb for the mitigation of pain) joined to it—and it is taken thus for jaundice <term>yctericis|Jaundice; a yellowing of the skin and eyes. Because Celandine has a bright yellow sap, medieval physicians applied the "Doctrine of Signatures," believing a plant's appearance indicated its cure</term>, that is, by those who suffer from jaundice, with white wine. The crushed root itself prevents pain brought upon the teeth; if a poultice <term>cathaplasma|Poultice; a soft, moist mass of herbal material, usually heated, applied to the body to relieve soreness</term> is made with wine from its crushed leaves, authors say it wipes away—that is, removes and clears away—spots, that is, freckles <gloss>lentigines</gloss>.

But because the greater species is known to very few,
Omitting that one, I shall tell of the powers of the lesser.

Which I believe is well known to all common folk;
It is said to possess a drying power <term>desiccative: a medicinal property believed to dry up excess "humors" or moisture in wounds</term>.
From this, it knits together recent wounds in no small measure,
And, when applied, leads even old wounds toward scarring <gloss>Latin: cicatrices</gloss>.
For those suffering from sciatica <term>sciasim: a historical term for chronic pain in the hips or legs</term>, a decoction <note>A liquid made by boiling the plant in water to extract its essence</note> of this plant
Is wonderfully beneficial if it is administered through the anus <note>This refers to an enema, a common medieval method for delivering medicine directly to the lower body</note>.

After blood has been drawn, it soon calms the pain.
The same plant will treat ailing nerves by means of a warm compress <term>fomento: a hot, medicinal soak or poultice applied to the skin</term>.
Its juice, when consumed, draws forth the menstrual flow.

¶ Chiron—as the stories tell—was a Centaur, the son of Saturn and Philyra (the daughter of Oceanus). He surpassed not only the Centaurs but all men of his age in goodness and justice. ### ¶ Concerning the herb Colubrina or Dragonthea, 54.

The herb which the usage of the Greeks calls *Dragonthea* <note>original: "dragōtheā"; identified as *Dracunculus vulgaris* or Dragon Arum</note>,
This same herb is called "Colubrina" <note>original: "colubrina"; from 'coluber' (snake), commonly known as Snakewort</note> in the tongue of the common people.
Because it rises with a spotted bark <note>referring to the distinctive mottled or speckled stem of the plant</note> similar to a snake.
From which we believe it was proven by the ancients,
That by similarity <note>An early reference to the "Doctrine of Signatures," the belief that plants that look like body parts or animals can cure ailments related to them</note>, "Snakewort" can put to flight snake venoms.
Whoever anoints himself with its crushed root
Is said to be safe from the attack of serpents.
Taken with wine, it heals their bites.
If you mix oil with the juice of its seed,
Pouring it into the ears, you will be able to settle pain.

### ¶ On the Herb Dragontea
Orators <note>meaning scholars or those skilled in formal speech</note> in more polished speech call this herb *dragontium* or *dracunculus* <note>original: "dracunculum"; translated as "Little Dragon," usually identifying the Dragon Arum or Snakewort</note>. # Martial: "I want a long-nosed man, I do not want a polypous one." <note>original: "Martialis. Nasutum volo nolo poliposum"; a reference to Martial's Epigrams 12.37, punning on the word "polypus" which means both a nasal growth and an octopus.</note>

A **polyp** <term>polypus; a fleshy growth in the nose</term> is an overgrowth of flesh occurring in the cartilages of the nostrils, which is very harmful to humans. For it is named after its resemblance to the **polyp** <note>original: "polipi piscis"; referring here to the octopus</note>, a fish of vast size: whose wondrous magnitude **Lucan** <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus, a Roman poet often cited for his descriptions of nature's terrors</note> writes about. Natural researchers <note>original: "rerum naturalium inuestigatores"; naturalists or early scientists</note> assert that no animal is more ferocious for killing a man in the water. Even the juice [of the Dragontea plant], if applied, is accustomed to be a great help for **cancers** <note>original: "cancris"; here referring to spreading, eating sores or malignant growths</note>. **Cloudiness** <note>original: "caligo"</note>, that is, darkness of the eyes, is driven away by the juice of the root of that herb. Also, various diseases which the lights <gloss>that is, the eyes</gloss> suffer are healed, provided they are smeared <gloss>that is, anointed</gloss> with the pure juice itself or mixed with honey.

---

> The root also benefits the eyes if eaten roasted.
> 
> A draught of thirty seeds taken with **vinegar-water** <term>pusca; a common Roman drink of vinegar and water</term>
> 
> Is said to return clear sight, having driven away the darkness.

> If a pregnant woman draws the scent of that wilting flower
> 
> Through her nose, she is said to suffer a miscarriage,
> Or if the crushed root is placed beneath the womb.
> The powder of its root, when joined with honey,
> Quenches a cough when taken, and also **catarrh** <term>catarrum; excessive buildup of mucus</term>,
> And this often brings relief to those **spitting blood** <term>emoptoicis; from 'hemoptysis', the coughing up of blood from the lungs</term>.
> It drains **humors** <note>original: "humores"; in medieval medicine, these were the bodily fluids believed to control health</note> from the chest that will not return.
> For all these things, the root is beneficial if taken roasted;
> A **decoction** <term>decoctio; a concentrated extract made by boiling the plant</term> of it will do the same.
> Its root, when drunk with wine, stirs **desire** <note>original: "venerem"; literally 'Venus', referring to sexual arousal</note>;
> It provokes urine and cleanses **phagedenic** <term>flagedenica; 'eating' or rapidly spreading</term> wounds.
> If crushed **bryony** <note>a flowering vine used in traditional medicine</note> is joined with honey,
> This cleanses the filth of a wound and cures **pharyngia** <note>original: "firingia"; likely referring to inflammatory sores or throat ailments</note>.

<meta>This page continues from the introduction to Woad (Isatis tinctoria), moving from the identification of the plant into its specific medicinal applications and a detailed scholarly commentary on its names and uses in dyeing.</meta>

**Woad** <term>Isatis</term> is called **gaisdo** <note>original: "gaisdo"; a Germanic-influenced vernacular term for woad</note> by the Greeks in common speech.
It is a herb providing no small profit to dyers.

A poultice shall be made from its crushed leaves.
It binds together large wounds and stops their blood;
When crushed, it also reduces swellings.
If you grind this with the juice of honey, it cleanses foul wounds.
It clears away spots and helps with **sacred fire** <term>igni sacro; a painful skin infection, usually erysipelas</term>
If you join egg white to it and apply it on top.

¶ **Woad by the Greeks**, etc. I have not yet found this herb called by these names while searching through the medical authors. However, I am led by conjecture that it is what more recent doctors call **dyers' grains** <note>original: "grana tinctorum"</note>, which is also called **coccus** <term>scarlet-berry</term> and **kermes** <term>an insect used for red dye</term>. <note>The commentator here conflates Woad, which produces blue dye, with Kermes, which produces red/scarlet. This was a common confusion in medieval botanical texts regarding "dyer's plants."</note> For purple garments are dyed from it. Whence **Martial** <note>Roman poet, 1st century AD</note> says: *You give scarlet and violet-dyed clothes to a famous adulteress* <note>original: "Coccina famose donas et iantina meche"</note>. And sacred scripture testifies that Christ, the best and greatest, was clothed in a **scarlet robe**. Furthermore, **Silvaticus** <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus, author of the 'Pandectae Medicinae'</note>, **Dioscorides**, and **Serapion** say almost the same things about the scarlet-berry as **Macer** says about **gaudon** <note>Woad</note>. It is called **isatis** by the Greeks **commonly** <gloss>that is, in the vulgar tongue</gloss> as **gaisdo**. A herb **providing** <gloss>that is, supplying</gloss> **profit** <gloss>that is, gain</gloss> that is **not small** to **dyers** <gloss>that is, those who practice the art of dyeing</gloss>. A **poultice** shall be made of its crushed leaves; it **binds** wounds even if they are **large**, and **stops** <gloss>that is, holds back</gloss> the **blood** <note>original: "cruorem"</note>. If you grind this herb with honey, it **suppresses tumors** <gloss>that is, swellings</gloss>; it **cleanses foul** <gloss>that is, virulent</gloss> **wounds**, **clears away spots**, and **aids sacred fire** <gloss>that is, very dry skin eruptions</gloss> if you **join egg white** or **apply it on top** <gloss>that is, place it over the area</gloss>.

# ¶ On White Hellebore, 55

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular double-line border, depicting a botanical specimen of white hellebore (Elleborus albus). The plant features a gnarled, woody root or base from which a single main stem arises, curving to the left. Along the stem are several large, lanceolate leaves with visible venation. Small clusters of round flower buds or seed pods hang downwards from the upper portions of the stalk.</image-desc>

Hellebore is attested to have two species:
The **white**, which purges upward <note>original: "sursum purgat"; acting as an emetic to induce vomiting</note>, and the **black**, which purges downward <note>original: "nigrumque deorsum"; acting as a laxative</note>.
Both possess a dry and hot power, and in each of them
this quality is of the **third degree** <term>a classification in Galenic medicine where a substance's qualities—hot, cold, moist, or dry—are very intense and require great caution</term>.
White hellebore is said to be more violent than the black;
for this reason, I shall first speak of its powers.
When applied as a suppository in any manner, it expels a fetus <note>original: "depellit abortum"; used here as a warning or instruction regarding its role as an abortifacient</note>.

<summary>This page introduces White Hellebore, distinguishing it from Black Hellebore by its more violent nature and its effect as an emetic. It classifies the plant's potency within the medieval humoral system and notes its use in inducing abortion.</summary>

<keywords>White Hellebore, emetics, humoral theory, third degree, abortifacients, Elleborus albus, medicinal botany</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of White Hellebore (Veratrum album), specifically focusing on its use as a sternutatory (to induce sneezing) and an emetic (to induce vomiting), while providing etymological and literary commentary on the plant's name.</meta>

Inhaled through the nostrils, the powder of this plant will provoke 
sneezing, driving out diseases of the head.
It is mixed into medicinal preparations that clear the eyes, 
and they say it is of great benefit for defects of the vision.
Mixed into porridge, its powder kills mice;
and when given with milk, it is deadly to flies.
They say that when taken, it draws out various humors 
through vomiting and is reported to dispel long-standing diseases.
Chronic **vertigo** <note>dizziness or a spinning sensation</note> is cleared by such vomiting.
It is effective for the **insane**, the **melancholic**, and those with the **falling sickness** <term>epilepsy, so called because the sufferer falls to the ground during a seizure</term>.

¶ **Hellebore** is a Greek name which in Latin is called ***veratrum***, named for the fact that it "turns" (*vertat*) the mind. 
Regarding this, **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> in Book 25 states that it is well known that even the leaves corrupt the mind if more than four are consumed. 
**Persius** <note>A Roman satirist known for his difficult and moralizing poetry</note>—who was not ignorant of medicine, as **Bartholomeus Fontius** <note>A 15th-century Italian humanist and commentator</note> says—agrees with this. 
"This is no *Iliad* of Accius" <note>A quote from Persius's Satires, suggesting the work is serious medicine, not a bombastic poem</note>. 
Persius frequently mentions hellebore "drunk with *veratrum*." 
Elsewhere, you may see those asking for hellebore in vain when the diseased skin is already swelling; 
"Learn to restrain [the disease] with hellebore at the precise moment." 
But let us return to the poet <note>Likely referring to Macer Floridus, the traditional author of this herbal verse</note>. 
He testifies that Hellebore has two species: 
The **white**, which purges upward (that is, through vomiting), 
and the **black**, which purges downward (that is, through the bowels). 
They possess a **hot** and **dry** power, and both are of the **third degree** <term>an intense potency in the Galenic system of medicine, meaning the plant has a very strong effect on the body's balance</term>. 
The white is said to be more violent—that is, more dangerous—than the black. 
Therefore, I shall speak first of the powers of the white variety. 
**Hellebore** (the word *Elleborus* is used in the masculine gender by orators) 
when applied as a suppository (that is, in any manner [internally] placed), it drives away a fetus <note>original: "repellit abortum"; confirming its use as an abortifacient</note>. 
Its powder, drawn into the nostrils, provokes <unclear>ic</unclear> sneezing...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of White Hellebore (Veratrum album), specifically focusing on its use as a sternutatory (to induce sneezing) and an emetic (to induce vomiting), while providing historical and literary context for its use in treating mental illness and epilepsy.</meta>

...sneezing agents <note>original: *sternutamenta*</note>, that is, things that expel diseases of the head (for a sneeze opens the cleansing channels <note>original: *emunctoria*</note> of the brain, especially the collectors of the nostrils, and by its force expels the superfluous **humors** <term>the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—which were believed to determine health and temperament</term> of the head). Hellebore powder is mixed into preparations—that is, medicines—which purge the "lights"—that is, the eyes—and they say—meaning they report—it is of great benefit for ailments of the eyes. Its powder, mixed with porridge—that is, pottage—kills mice; and given with milk, it is pernicious—meaning deadly—to flies. 

Physicians say that when taken, it draws out various humors through vomiting, and they report—that is, they say—it drives out long-standing diseases. **Vertigo** of the head (which we have touched upon briefly) that is old—that is, chronic—is purged by such vomiting, and it is effective for the insane melancholic. This kind of insanity is generated by the **melancholic humor** <note>black bile</note> corrupting the middle ventricle of the brain and the **animal spirits** <term>in medieval medicine, these were refined substances that traveled through the nerves to enable movement and thought</term> contained within it. The poet **Persius** was not ignorant of this, saying: "it is better to swallow pure doses from Anticyra." It is also effective for those with the **falling sickness** <term>epilepsy, so called because the sufferer falls to the ground; in Rome, it was known as the 'comitial disease' because its occurrence could halt public assemblies</term>—that is, those oppressed by the falling disease. 

Hellebore was taken most safely on the island of **Anticyra** <note>a Greek coastal town famous for its hellebore; though geographically a peninsula, it was frequently described as an island in classical literature</note>, to which, as **Pliny** writes, **Livius Drusus** sailed when he suffered from the falling sickness; having taken the hellebore, he was cured.

And often the one possessed <note>original: *lymphaticus*, referring to a state of frenzy or madness</note> is cured by taking this;
In the beginning, it treats **dropsy** <term>edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</term> marvelously.
It cleanses **leprosy**, puts to flight **tetanus** and **gout** <note>original: *podagram*</note>.
It strengthens those who tremble; it cures recurring **griping pains** <note>original: *tormina*, typically intestinal cramps</note>.
Know that it heals various ailments of the stomach and **sciatica**.
And for an old cough and for fevers it is beneficial above all,
Especially for **quartan fevers** <term>a fever that returns every third day, typically associated with malaria</term> that have been chronic for many years.
**Pliny** warns that one who is to take this
Must prepare himself with great care seven days beforehand.

And he should temper the whole body well with moist foods,

¶ **And often it is cured**, etc. A "lymphatic" person is often cured by taking this hellebore. "Lymphatic" <note>original: *lymphaticus*</note>—that is, one who is insane—because it was reported among the ancients that those who had seen a **nymph** (from which the word *lympha* <note>meaning water or a water-divinity</note> is derived) went insane. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*</note> says of hellebore: It thus heals the **comitial diseases** <term>epilepsy, so called because a seizure during a Roman *comitia* or assembly was an omen to dissolve the meeting</term>, vertigo, melancholics, the insane, and the lymphatic. Black hellebore treats those with **dropsy** <term>edema, or swelling caused by excess fluid</term> in a wonderful way at the beginning. It cleanses leprosy, though not yet "confirmed" <note>meaning chronic or advanced</note> leprosy, because that is incurable; it puts to flight **tetanus**, which is a disease that [restricts] the nerves from their movement according to every position... <note>the text breaks off at 'impez', likely *impediens*</note>

...and puts to flight **gout** <term>original: *podagram*; inflammation of the foot, historically associated with a rich diet</term>, strengthens the trembling—that is, those burdened by tremors (which is a disease of the muscles and like an incipient **paralysis** <note>original: *paralisis*</note>). ¶ **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), Roman author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note> advises whoever is about to take this **hellebore** <term>original: *elleborum*; a potent and toxic plant used as a drastic purgative in ancient medicine</term>—that is, whoever wishes to consume it—to prepare himself with great care for seven days prior and "temper"—that is, moisten—the entire body well with moist foods. Nor—that is, and he should not—dine late, he who must take it in the morning. When he takes it, he orders him to seek out—that is, he shall choose—a day that is serene, warm, and clear, and lacking in **Venus**—that is, the act of lovemaking. 

Indeed—that is, certainly—¶ **Pliny** affirms that if one scorns to observe this care, he will suffer torments—that is, excessive or intolerable anguishes. And he orders that hellebore previously cooked in various [liquids]—that is, diverse ones—be given fearlessly; he says it will not be harmful in this way. And he forbids it to be given to the elderly and tender children, and says that hellebore must not be received by all those who are "soft"—that is, having soft flesh—and the timid, and those of a womanly spirit, the inconstant, and those as changeable as the breeze, and the excessively thin. 

Others instruct taking it cooked in the juice—that is, the liquid—of **barley-water** <term>original: *ptisane*; a medicinal decoction of pearl barley</term> or in **honey-water** <term>original: *mulsa*; water sweetened with honey</term> and bread; they say it is useful to take it in this way.

Many have written of this herb’s powers,
And taught the manner and for whom it should be taken;
Yet, turning through many volumes, I have not found

¶ **Many have written**, etc. Many authors have written about the powers of this herb and taught how it should be taken and by whom. However, I, leafing through—that is, unrolling or examining many (do not say "several" but "many" or "very many") volumes—that is, many books—have not found any author who ordered a certain weight to be given. For **Johannes Mesue** <note>A name attributed to influential Arabic medical writers, likely Masawayh al-Mardini</note> [says] white hellebore entirely...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion regarding the dosage of hellebore. The previous page ended with the author critiquing Pliny the Elder’s lack of precision. This page specifically contrasts the "suffocating" nature of white hellebore with the safer applications of black hellebore, citing the physician Themison and establishing specific weights like drachms and scruples before transitioning into a rhyming verse section on the benefits of the black variety.</meta>

...he urges it to be avoided because it is suffocating. For the **black variety**, however, he assigns a dose from one-sixth up to a third of a **drachm** <term>original: *dragme*; a classical unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams</term>. I believe they thought they had said enough about the weight or dosage when they ordered it to be given according to the patient's strength, age, and the season. But truly—that is to say, but—it does not seem safe enough to me to recommend that a thing—that is, a medicine—which brings about danger—that is, sudden or immediate peril—be given without a fixed weight. **Themison** <note>A famous physician of the 1st century BC and founder of the Methodic school of medicine</note>, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist</note> writes, was accustomed to give two drachms of hellebore; he even confirms that those who followed him gave twice two—that is, four drachms. Yet Pliny himself does not seem to have established by what weight it ought to be taken. Since, however, he orders one drachm of black hellebore or four **scruples** <term>original: *scrupulos*; a small unit of weight, about 1.3 grams</term>—namely, one drachm and one scruple—the same Pliny seems to me to suggest that only half the weight of **white hellebore**—that is, a half-drachm—should be given, because he admits that white hellebore is far more violent—that is, more impetuous—by far—that is, greatly more so—than the black.

### ¶ Concerning the herb black hellebore. 57.

Black hellebore is not so violent,
Nor to be feared as much as the white we mentioned.
The ancients also instruct that this be cooked in lentils,
Or in meat scraps <note>original: *viscellis*; literally "little offals" or small pieces of meat, used to make the medicine more palatable</note> to thus treat the insane.
They say when taken this way, it benefits **gout** <term>original: *podagre*; a painful inflammation of the joints</term>
And is said to help those with **dropsy** <term>original: *ydropicis*; an old term for edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</term> once a fever is stirred.
The paralytic feels a wonderful aid when it is taken,
And when drunk, it relieves various diseases of the joints;
It draws out various biles <note>original: *coleras*; referring to the "choleric" humor</note> and phlegm through the bowels.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on black hellebore. By drinking a decoction <term>original: *elixature*; a medicinal liquid produced by boiling</term>, dimness of the eyes is driven away.
A poultice <term>original: *cathaplasma*; a soft, warm, moist mass applied to sores</term> made for suppurating <note>forming or discharging pus</note> hardnesses
Ripens and purges them; it empties fistulas <term>original: *siringia*; narrow, tube-like ulcers</term> in the neck
When a little of the juice of its root is inserted within,
And after two days have passed, let that small plug <term>original: *surculus*; literally a small sprig or shoot used as a medical insert</term> be removed.

¶ In our times, men use this hellebore quite rarely; they avoid the white variety entirely as if it were a deadly poison. For the bodies of men have now been reduced to a certain **weakness** <note>original: *effeminationem*; a common historical medical trope suggesting that modern people were physically softer and less hardy than the "robust" ancients</note>. Moreover, black hellebore is not suitable except for robust and spirited bodies—as **John Mesue** <note>A name attributed to an influential 13th-century author of pharmaceutical manuals, widely studied in the Middle Ages</note> says—and even then, only for a severe illness. This is so that a strong medicine may meet a strong illness, as **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus, the preeminent Greek physician of the 2nd century AD whose work formed the basis of Western medicine</note> teaches.

...is the author. Black hellebore <note>original: *Elleborum nigrum*; a poisonous plant used historically in minute doses as a drastic purgative</note> is not so violent nor to be dreaded <gloss>that is, feared</gloss> as much as that which we have spoken of—namely, the one we called "white." Also <gloss>that is, even</gloss> the ancients command that this hellebore should be cooked in lentils <gloss>that is, in the juice of lentils</gloss>, as **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*</note> says, to restrain its wildness <note>its extreme potency or harsh effects</note>. For the substance of lentils binds the stomach, according to the testimony of **Galen** <note>A Greek physician whose theories of the "four humors" dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years</note>. Or it can be prepared in meat broths, as **Avicenna** <note>The Persian "Prince of Physicians" (Ibn Sina), author of *The Canon of Medicine*</note> says. Physicians say that when taken in this way, it heals the insane <gloss>that is, the melancholic</gloss> <note>Melancholy was believed to be caused by an excess of "black bile," which hellebore was thought to purge</note>. And they say it is useful in this way for gout.

This hellebore is said to help those with dropsy <note>original: *ydropicis*; an old term for edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</note>; I say this when the fever has been moved. The paralytic feels <gloss>that is, perceives</gloss> wonderful <gloss>that is, miraculous</gloss> help, I say, when it is taken. And when drunk <gloss>that is, when it is consumed</gloss>, it relieves <gloss>that is, alleviates</gloss> various diseases of the joints, such as sciatica, gout, and hand-gout <note>original: *cyagra*; specifically gout affecting the hands</note>. For as **Mesue** <note>Referring to Mesuë the Younger, a likely pseudonymous author of highly influential pharmaceutical texts</note> says, hellebore is among those things used to cleanse the brain and the nerves which the aforementioned illnesses infest. Hellebore draws out various biles <term>scilicet</term> <note>Latin for "namely" or "to wit"</note>: red bile, yellow bile, black bile, and bile mixed with phlegm, as Mesue says. 

And it draws out phlegm through the bowels <gloss>that is, through the belly</gloss>. A "fog" <gloss>that is, a dullness of vision</gloss> is put to flight <gloss>that is, the vision is cleared</gloss> by a drink of the decoction <gloss>that is, the water from boiling the hellebore</gloss>. A poultice <note>original: *cathaplasma*; a soft, warm, moist mass applied to the skin</note> made of hellebore ripens <gloss>that is, digests</gloss> suppurated hardnesses—those which clearly remain hardened after the pus has formed in an abscess <gloss>original: *apostematis*</gloss>—and it purges those hardnesses.

A "sprig" <gloss>that is, a small twig, like those used when grafting trees</gloss> made from the root of this hellebore, when inserted a little way into the neck <note>likely referring to the opening or "neck" of the wound</note>, clears <gloss>that is, purges</gloss> the "surgery" <gloss>that is, the tear-duct fistula of the eye</gloss>. And that sprig <gloss>that is, a small piece of that root</gloss> should be removed after two days <gloss>that is, two</gloss>.

Join the pomegranate peel <note>original: *Sidea*; the rind of the fruit</note> which they have given to ground flour;
When applied to the swellings of those with dropsy, they will be dried out by these means.

<meta>This page continues the specific medicinal applications of black hellebore, moving from its effects on internal humors to its use in topical treatments for the skin, ears, and teeth, while citing the dosage guidelines of the Roman naturalist Pliny the Elder.</meta>

As a suppository, it cleanses the menses. It drives out a fetus.
It restores hearing if you place it in deaf ears
And remove it after two or three days.
Applied as a poultice, it removes spots;
It cleanses leprosies: it drives away all types of scabies.
Only [held] in the mouth for a long time: the vinegar in which you cooked it,
They say, soothes a toothache of no small degree.
**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a 1st-century Roman author of the *Naturalis Historia*</note> commanded one drachm <note>a unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</note>
Of black hellebore to be given, if it moves the bowels gently;
He forbids taking more than twice two obols <note>an ancient Greek weight; four obols would be about 3 grams</note>.

¶ *The barley which it gave, etc.* Join the flour which the barley gave, that is, barley flour, with crushed black hellebore; the swelling of dropsy <note>original: *ydropici*; swelling caused by excess fluid</note> will dry up. I say this when these things—namely, hellebore and barley flour—are applied. Hellebore used as a suppository cleanses the menses and expels a fetus <note>original: *abortum*; used here in the sense of a forced miscarriage or expelling a stillbirth</note>. It restores <gloss>that is, brings back</gloss> hearing if you place it in deaf ears, provided that the deafness is not "original" <note>congenital or present from birth</note>, because that remains incurable; and you remove it after two or even three days. 

A poultice of it—namely, of hellebore—cleanses <gloss>that is, purifies</gloss> leprosies, which is to say *albaras* <note>a term for white leprosy or vitiligo</note> and morphea <note>localized skin discoloration</note>, which are traces of leprosy. This is because they arise from a failure of the "assimilative power" <note>the body's ability to transform nutrients into flesh</note>, just as leprosy does; but there is no defect in the form of the flesh in them as there is in leprosy, as **Galen** <note>The influential 2nd-century Greek physician</note> says. It drives away <gloss>that is, cleanses</gloss> all types of scabies <gloss>that is, eruptions, itches, and other things of that kind</gloss>. 

Authors say that vinegar in which you have cooked that hellebore, [held] only <gloss>that is, rolled around</gloss> for a long time in the mouth <gloss>that is, in the throat</gloss>, soothes a not small <gloss>that is, an immoderate</gloss> toothache. **Pliny** commanded one drachm of black hellebore to be given; it promotes <gloss>that is, provokes</gloss> thus gently <gloss>that is, quickly</gloss> the bowels <gloss>that is, the belly</gloss> through evacuation. **Pliny** forbids taking black hellebore more than <gloss>that is, beyond</gloss> twice two obols <gloss>that is, four</gloss>.

# ¶ On the herb **Verbena**. 58.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a Verbena plant, showing several long, pointed leaves arching from the base and three central upright stems topped with small, round seeds or flowers. The image is enclosed in a rectangular border.</image-desc>

The Greeks call **Verbena** <term>gerobotanum</term> <note>original: *gerobotanum*; from the Greek *hierobotane*, meaning "holy herb"</note> 
and <term>peristereon</term> <note>original: *peristereos*; meaning "pigeon plant," as it was believed pigeons were often found near it</note>. 
This herb is said to have 
two species; the effect of both is nearly the same. 
Taken often with wine, it helps those suffering from jaundice <note>original: *ictericos*; a yellowing of the skin and eyes</note>. 
Crushed and applied [as a poultice] with wine, it cures poisonous <note>original: *pestiferos*</note> bites; 
some say this medicine must be renewed for four days. 
It cleanses wounds well if its warm juice 
is swished in the mouth. 
A recently made decoction <note>original: *decoctio*; a liquid produced by boiling the plant in water</note> of the herb also provides this benefit.

**Vocabulary**
**verbena** (vervain), **gerobotanum** (holy herb), **peristereos** (pigeon herb), **ictericos** (those with jaundice), **pestiferos** (poisonous/deadly), **morsus** (bites), **decoctio** (decoction/boiling)

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Verbena (Vervain) from the previous page, transitioning from its general descriptions to specific medicinal recipes in verse, followed by a scholarly prose commentary on its historical, classical, and religious significance.</meta>

With this herb, any rot of the mouth is also buried <note>original: *sepellitur*; meaning the condition is ended or hidden away</note>,
If it is swished for a long time, warmed, in the vocal throat.
The fresh herb, crushed and applied over a wound, binds it together.
Taken with wine, it counteracts all poisons.
That fever which rages by approaching on alternating days <note>This refers to a tertian fever, which recurs every 48 hours</note>
Is driven out by this. With three of its roots taken,
And drunk with just as many leaves dissolved in water,
Provided it is taken before the patient shudders or feels any chill.
And in this way, it heals those fevered with quartan fevers <note>A fever that recurs every 72 hours</note>,
When twice two leaves [four] are swallowed with as many roots.

¶ Among the superstitious pagans of old, **Verbena** was pursued with the greatest honors. For it was called <term>therobotane</term> <note>original: *therobotan*; a corruption of the Greek *hierobotane*, meaning "holy herb"</note>, that is, the "holy herb," because it was employed in sacrifices. **Terence** [writes] in the *Andria*: "Take out the vervains from here." <note>original: *Exara hinc verbenas sume*</note> **Virgil** [writes]: "Gird these altars with a fillet, and burn fatty vervains." <note>original: *cinge hec altaria vitta. Verbenasq; adole pingues*</note> Ambassadors, whenever they were sent to enemies, were accustomed to be crowned with it; they called that same herb <term>sagmina</term> <note>original: *sagmina*; ritual bundles of plants used by Roman officials</note>. It was believed that whoever violated those distinguished by that crown had committed an inexpiable crime. Now also, this herb has long been held sacred to the Divine Forerunner <note>Refers to Saint John the Baptist</note>, and people are crowned with it everywhere on his feast day. Regarding this, **Baptista Mantuanus** <note>A famous Carmelite poet (1447–1516) known for his Christian Latin verse</note>, the most Christian of poets, elegantly sang in his little work on that Divine Forerunner:

¶ "Also a custom, lest we be thought to condemn ancestral ways: we wear all our temples <note>i.e., our heads</note> with your vervains."

The Greeks call **Verbena** <term>gerobotanum</term> <note>another form of *hierobotane*</note> and <term>peristeron</term> <note>meaning "pigeon-plant"</note>. This herb is reported—that is, said—to have two species. The effect is nearly <note>original: *pene / id est fere*</note> the same for both species. This herb helps those with jaundice <note>original: *ictericos*</note> if it is drunk often with wine.

Verbena

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical commentary on Verbena (Vervain), moving from its ancient ritual uses to its specific application in treating poisonous bites, mouth ailments, and various types of fevers (tertian and quartan) according to the humoral theory of Galen.</meta>

Crushed with wine and applied, it certainly cures pestilential bites: that is to say, this medicine must be renewed for four days to achieve its effect in the case of a poisonous bite. If the warm juice of this herb is swished in the mouth, it also cleanses mouth wounds well. A freshly made decoction of the herb also provides the same effect; any rot of the mouth is repelled by this decoction if the warm liquid is swished for a long time in the "vocal throat" <note>original: *fauce sonora*; likely referring to the back of the throat where sound is produced</note>, as is commonly done in gargles. This herb, crushed and applied, "glues"—meaning it consolidates and heals—a fresh wound (one recently inflicted). The herb itself, taken with wine, counteracts all poisons. 

That fever which "rages" by afflicting on alternating days—meaning from the third day to the third day, namely the <term>tertian fever</term> <note>A fever characterized by paroxysms that occur every 48 hours, or every third day by inclusive counting</note> which stems from <term>choler</term> <note>Yellow bile, one of the four bodily humors, traditionally associated with heat, dryness, and aggression</note>, which is fiery and sharp. Here the poet has used the specific word "rages" (*furit*): for some diseases, especially those arising from choler, are called "furious" by an analogy drawn from brute animals. These are the words of **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note>: just as animals "enraged" for mating cannot stand still in one place due to their excessive desire which moves them and makes them frantic, so too do the humors sometimes move themselves and harm the sick person by running from limb to limb. This is according to Galen. 

Therefore, the tertian fever is driven out—that is, expelled—by this herb. This is achieved by taking three of its roots along with just as many of its leaves. This water, infused with the aforementioned roots and leaves, is effective before the patient feels any chill. However, it is not a true cold, but rather <term>horripilation</term> <note>The medical term for shivering or "goosebumps" where the hair stands on end, often a precursor to a fever spike</note>. The herb heals <term>quartan fevers</term> <note>A fever that recurs every 72 hours, or every fourth day</note> in this way: by swallowing "twice two"—that is, four—leaves with an equal number of roots.

**If a decoction of it is made in wine**

<vocab>Galenus, febris tertiana, collera, morsus pestiferos, gargarismi, decoctio</vocab>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Vervain (Verbena), transitioning from its medical applications for bites and fevers to its folkloric uses in banquets and its reputation in classical antiquity, specifically citing Pliny the Elder and the traditions of the Magi.</meta>

It is said that this herb, when scattered among guests during a banquet, makes them cheerful, just as was previously described for **bugloss** <note>original: *buglossa*; a plant in the borage family traditionally believed to drive away sadness and promote joy</note>. 

If you carry this herb in your hand and inquire of a sick person <margin>c</margin>, saying: "Brother, how are you?"—if the patient responds "Well," he will live; but if "Badly," there is no hope for his health. Some have ordered a crown composed of this herb to be placed upon the head of anyone desiring a cure for any cause. Thus, they say it provides a swift remedy.

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author and naturalist whose *Natural History* became a foundational text for medieval science</note> affirms that this herb is healthy for all the internal organs, and specifically treats ailments of the side and liver, complaints of the chest, and the "vices" or defects of the body. He asserts it is especially medicinal for lung conditions and <term>phthisis</term> <note>original: *ptisi*; a traditional term for pulmonary tuberculosis or a progressive wasting disease of the lungs</note>. 

When mixed with old fat, it suppresses <term>parotids</term> <note>painful swellings of the parotid glands, typically associated with mumps or infections</note>. Combine <term>Yarrow</term> <note>original: *Beroam*; the text notes it was named for its "thousand leaves," identifying it as *Achillea millefolium*</note>—which they named for its thousand leaves—and **betony** in an equal weight to the vervain. When these are mixed and drunk with water, they believe there is no better medicine for those whom a <term>calculus</term> <note>a "stone," such as a kidney stone or gallstone</note> distresses. 

Pliny asserts that the **Magi** <note>Ancient Persian priests or "magicians" often cited in classical texts as sources of occult or botanical lore</note> praise this herb significantly; they say it resists all diseases. They claim that whoever is anointed with it can command whatever they seek; thus, they imagine that the friendships of the powerful can be won, fevers driven away, and many similar things. While a powerful nature *could* perhaps grant such things, these claims rightly seem vain and empty to us.

¶ **Its use in wine, etc.** If a decoction of this herb is made in wine and "scattered"—that is, dispersed—among the guests at a banquet, it is said to "render"—that is, make—the guests cheerful, just as bugloss [is said to do].

...as previously written. If you carry this herb in your hand and ask a sick person, "Brother, how are you?" <gloss>original: *quid agis*; literally "what are you doing?" or "how do you fare?"</gloss>—if the patient responds "Well," he will live, meaning he will recover. If, however, he responds "I am doing poorly," there is no hope for his health. This empirical practice <term>smacks of superstitious paganism</term> <note>original: *sapiens gentilitatem*; literally "tasting of the heathens"</note> and, according to Christian teachings, it should be counted among the trifles of women's tools, which they call <term>distaffs</term> <note>original: *colus*; a tool used in spinning wool, here used as a metaphor for "old wives' tales"</note>. This is said without wishing to offend the author <note>likely referring to the source material or Pliny</note> who is merely a reciter of ancient doctrines.

Certain authors have ordered a crown composed of this herb to be placed upon a head that is aching from any cause whatsoever; they say the herb brings a swift remedy when applied in this way.

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of *Natural History*</note> affirms that this herb is healthy—that is, medicinal—for all the <term>viscera</term> <gloss>meaning the internal organs</gloss>, as well as for diseases and complaints of the liver <gloss>original: *iecoris*; that is, the *hepar*</gloss>, and for defects <gloss>meaning illnesses</gloss> of the chest. **Pliny** also asserts that the herb is a cure especially for conditions of the lungs and <term>phthisis</term> <note>a traditional term for a progressive wasting of the lungs, often tuberculosis</note>. When mixed with old fat <gloss>that is, old grease or lard</gloss>, it suppresses <term>parotids</term> <note>painful swellings of the glands near the ear</note> (which we have briefly touched upon elsewhere). 

Combine <gloss>that is, join together</gloss> the herb to which the authors of medicine gave the name "of a thousand leaves"—namely **yarrow** <note>original: *millefolium*</note>—and add **betony** in an equal weight to the vervain. When these are mixed and drunk in water, they believe no medicine is more useful for those whom a <term>calculus</term> <gloss>that is, a stone</gloss> distresses <gloss>meaning afflicts</gloss>.

**Pliny** asserts that the **Magi** <note>ancient Persian priests and practitioners of secret arts, often cited in medieval texts for both their wisdom and their "vanities"</note> praise this herb quite highly. These Magi say the herb resists all diseases and claim that a person touched with this herb obtains whatever he asks for. They even claim that the friendship of the powerful <gloss>meaning princes</gloss> can be won <note>the text adds a snarky grammatical jab: *non captuari ut rudunt asselli bipedes*, "not 'captuari' as those two-legged donkeys bray," mocking those who use incorrect Latin forms</note> and fevers driven away. They devise—meaning they mix in—many such claims which, although a powerful nature might be able to grant some of them, nevertheless seem to us vain <gloss>that is, laughable</gloss> and empty—meaning momentary...

...spontaneously and by right. <note>The fragment "tanea iure" likely completes "spontanea iure" from a previous sentence.</note> **Magus** <note>original: *magus*</note> is a Persian word that designates a **wise man**. The Greeks call them **philosophers**; the Latins call them **wise men** <note>original: *sapientes*</note>; the Gauls, **druids**; the Egyptians, **prophets**; the Jews, **gymnosophists** <note>literally "naked wise men"; while usually referring to Indian ascetics, the author here groups them with Jewish wisdom traditions</note>; and the Assyrians call them **Chaldeans** <note>a term often used in the Renaissance to describe ancient Mesopotamian astrologers and sages</note>. Everyone calls a man endowed with wisdom by such names.

**Pico della Mirandola** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a brilliant Italian Renaissance philosopher who sought to reconcile all schools of thought</note>, a man excelling in incredible learning, says that magic is a path not worthy of blame, but rather of the highest praise when it brings to light the most hidden <gloss>or "abstruse"</gloss> causes of natural things, from which wonderful effects emerge when they are properly <term>applied</term> <note>the practice of "Natural Magic," which was considered an early form of experimental science</note>. However, he asserts that another kind of magic is to be completely abolished: that which consists in the superstition of **astrological vanity**.

# **Concerning the herb Germander. 59.**

*Germandrea* in Greek, which is *Germander* in Latin <note>original: "Gamedreos grece que germandrea latine"</note>.
The author states this herb is both **hot** and **dry**.
It reaches the **third degree** <term>degree</term> <note>In Galenic medicine, the potency of an herb's "complexion" was measured from the first to fourth degree; the third degree is quite powerful</note> in both qualities; it expels a miscarriage.
If drunk mixed with water, it cures a cough.
When crushed, it helps [bruises]; if drunk mixed with vinegar,
It dries the **spleen** <note>In ancient medicine, a "swollen" or "moist" spleen was associated with various ailments and an imbalance of the humors</note>; taken with wine, it purges the menses.
Taken at the onset of **dropsy** <note>original: "ydropicis"; a condition of swelling caused by excess fluid, now known as edema</note>, it provides a cure.
In this way, it also counters poisonous bites.
Crushed and applied on top, it benefits all aforementioned conditions.
It will cleanse a filthy wound, even an old one,
If you grind it with honey and apply it crushed over the top.
**Germander** juice mixed with honey and rubbed on the eyes
Is said to restore clarity, removing all **dimness** <note>original: "caligine"; refers to clouded vision or cataracts</note>.
If you mix the crushed herb with oil and anoint the body,
It drives away the cold and restores heat.

¶ **Germander in Greek, etc.** **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), the Roman author of Natural History</note> calls this herb the **little oak** <note>original: *quercula*</note>. By **Serapio** <note>Likely Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century Arabic medical writer</note>, however, it is named **oak of the earth** <note>original: *quercus terre*</note>. The Greeks call it *gamedreos*, and the Latins call it *germandrea*. Now, however, usage has prevailed such that it is called *gamedreos*. For where we produce the letter **Gamma** <note>original: *gama*</note> (that is, the letter 'g'), the Greeks pronounce it *gamedreo* in Greek, which is called *germandrea* in Latin. Physicians "confess"—that is, they say—that this herb is hot and dry. It is of the **third degree** in both qualities. If it is drunk mixed with water, it "expels"—that is, brings out—a miscarriage and "cures"—that is, treats—a cough. It helps crushed limbs [when applied] above. If one drinks it mixed with vinegar, it "dries"—that is, desiccates—the spleen. Taken with wine, it purges the menses. Taken thus (that is, with wine), it cures...

<meta>This page continues the detailed medical commentary on the herb Germander (Chapter 59), explaining its physiological effects on the liver and its use in treating dropsy, wounds, and eye ailments. It concludes with a grammatical note on the word for "cold" and begins the next entry on Nightshade (Chapter 60).</meta>

...the sufferer of **dropsy** <note>original: "ydropicus"; a condition of swelling caused by excess fluid, now known as edema</note> is helped in the beginning (namely, by evacuating the watery matter through the urinary passages. This watery matter is generated from a weakness in the **liver's** <note>original: "epatis"</note> digestion. When it finds its path blocked from the lobe of the liver to the kidneys, it enters—by nature's ingenuity—the two channels through which a small child, enclosed in the hidden parts of the womb, used to receive nourishment and pass urine. However, when the infant first comes into the light, the midwife diligently closes these channels. Thus, it happens that this sort of wateriness gradually drips between the membranes of the **peritoneum** <note>original: "siphac"</note> and the **abdominal wall** <note>original: "mirach"</note> by way of sweating, which results in a swelling of the belly). Furthermore, it is effective against pestilential—that is, poisonous—diseases; the crushed herb itself, applied on top, benefits all the aforementioned ailments. It will purge—that is, cleanse—a filthy wound, even if it is long-standing, which they call "badly behaved" <note>original: "male morigeratum"; referring to a chronic wound that resists healing</note>, if you grind it with honey and apply it. That is to say, if the **germander** <note>original: "camedreos"</note> and honey are ground together, the eyes are said to be restored to clarity—that is, made sharp-sighted. I say this happens when they are anointed with the juice of germander, and the **dimness** <note>original: "caligine"; cloudiness of vision</note> is removed—that is, taken away. If you mix this crushed herb with oil—that is, with olive oil—and anoint the body, it drives away—that is, expels—the cold and restores natural heat. For heat and cold are contraries, and their nature is to mutually expel one another from the same subject, as the **dialecticians** <note>logicians</note> say. **Macer** <note>Macer Floridus, the traditional name for the author of the herbal poem being discussed</note> frequently uses "cold" (*frigor*) in the masculine gender in this work, which we read that **Alexander the Physician** <note>original: "Alexander yatros"; likely referring to the 6th-century Greek medical writer Alexander of Tralles</note> also practiced. Orators and poets, however, place it in the neuter gender. **Virgil** <note>The Roman poet Publius Vergilius Maro</note> wrote:
> Immediately Aeneas's limbs are loosened with cold. <note>original: "Extemplo enea soluuntur frigore membra"; a quote from the Aeneid, Book 1, line 92</note>

### ## <unclear>On Nightshade</unclear> <note>original: "De strigum"; referring to *Solanum nigrum*, or Black Nightshade</note>

<warning>The text at the top of the page is extremely faded and nearly illegible. There is some ink bleed through.</warning>

![A woodcut illustration of a botanical specimen, likely Black Nightshade (Solanum nigrum). The plant features multiple stems with broad, lobed leaves. Small, star-shaped flowers bloom from the upper branches, while a cluster of small round berries is shown at the base of the stems. The illustration is enclosed within a simple square frame.](image)

The herb which the Greeks called <term>strigum <note>original: "strychnos"; the botanical name for the nightshade family</note></term>, the Latins call **Maurella** <note>known in English as Morelle or Black Nightshade</note>; its power is exceedingly cold. <note>In medieval medicine, herbs were classified by "degrees" of heat, cold, moisture, or dryness. A "cold" herb was used to treat "hot" conditions like inflammation, rashes, or fevers.</note>

It is said to soothe the pain of the ear wonderfully if its juice is poured gently inside. 

They say a <term>poultice <note>original: "cathaplasma"; a soft, moist mass of medicinal material, usually herbal, applied to the skin</note></term> of nightshade heals **egilopis** <note>a sore or fistula at the inner corner of the eye</note>. They say this same remedy is beneficial for a headache.

If salt and bread are ground together with nightshade and added in the manner of a poultice, it suppresses **parotidas** <note>painful swellings of the glands located behind or below the ears, such as those associated with mumps</note>.

Itching is driven away when smeared with the juice of nightshade.

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nightshade (Solanum nigrum) from the previous chapter. The text transitions from simple recipes into a deeper philosophical and medical debate regarding how the "complexion" (the balance of hot/cold/moist/dry qualities) of a plant relates to its "specific property" (its unique medicinal power).</meta>

The juice, when used as a suppository, binds the womanly flow. <note>original: "fluxum muliebrem"; referring to excessive menstrual bleeding or uterine hemorrhage.</note>
Combine pearl barley with its crushed leaves;
By applying these on top, "holy fire" <note>original: "sacer ignis"; likely referring to erysipelas, a painful skin infection characterized by redness.</note> and biting herpes <note>original: "herpeta mordax"; a term used for spreading skin sores or shingles.</note>
Are said to be driven out, especially if you add to them:
**Litharge** <note>original: "Argenti spumam"; literally 'silver foam,' a byproduct of silver refining used in ointments.</note>, **white lead**, and **rose oil**.
The aforementioned medicine becomes stronger and better.

This herb is named **Solatrum** or **Maurella**; its **specific property** <note>original: "proprietas"; an inherent power of a substance that cannot be explained by its primary qualities of heat or cold.</note>, as experienced, differs greatly from its **complexion** <note>original: "complexione"; the humoral makeup or temperament of the plant.</note>. This seems worthy of wonder, especially according to the author <term>Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note></term>, who states that the complexion disposes a substance toward its property. For he says the operating substance is that which works by the "form of its species," <note>The 'form of its species' was a medieval concept meaning the essential nature of a thing that gives it its specific powers.</note> which it acquired after its complexion; because when its simple elements mixed together and a single thing was generated from them, it prepared itself to receive a species and a form added beyond what those simple elements possessed. According to **Avicenna**.

Nevertheless, the property (or specific form) and the complexion in nightshade seem to be far apart. For nightshade is cold in the third degree, and the action of such coldness is to thicken, to bind together, and to numb <note>original: "stupefacere"; to cause a loss of sensation or "stupefaction."</note>—actions which are entirely opposed to **resolution** <note>original: "resolutioni"; the process of dissolving or dispersing a swelling or inflammation.</note>. However, nightshade, by its specific property, dissolves an **abscess** <note>original: "apostema"; a swelling or gathering of "corrupt humors."</note> from the brain, as <term>Francis of Piedmont <note>Franciscus de Pedemontium, a 14th-century physician and professor at Naples known for his additions to the works of Mesue.</note></term> testifies. He, relying on the authority of <term>Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire (129–c. 216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note></term>, now unties this "knot" in the following manner: many things which appear simple to the senses are actually composed according to their power; thus from such a composition another complexion is acquired, followed by a property different from the first complexion on which the "cooking" <note>original: "coctio"; the process of the body processing or ripening a disease so it can be expelled.</note> primarily depends, as can be seen in nightshade itself; for its very substance is a numbing coldness... <unclear>stupefying</unclear>

<summary>This page discusses recipes for skin conditions like shingles and erysipelas using nightshade, before delving into a theoretical debate on how a "cold" plant can paradoxically dissolve swellings.</summary>
<keywords>nightshade, Solanum nigrum, erysipelas, Avicenna, Galen, medical theory, complexion, specific property, abscess, pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical discussion from the previous page regarding the "specific property" of plants versus their "complexion." It concludes the section on Nightshade (Maurella/Strychnos) and begins the chapter on Henbane.</meta>

Indeed, in the second superficial substance, there is a small amount of **heat**, which is followed by a **dissolving heat**; these two always work together in the action of dissolving. These are the words of <term>Francis of Piedmont <note>Franciscus de Piedimonte (c. 1290–1350) was a prominent physician at the court of King Robert of Naples; his "Supplementum" to Mesue was a standard medical textbook.</note></term>. A similar thing is proven in the case of the **scorpion**, which, although it is cold by nature, crushes kidney stones. But perhaps these matters are too deep for a simple mind; let us therefore slide back down to lower things.

The Latins call the herb **Maurella**, which the Greeks called **Strychnos** <note>original: "strigũ"; referring to *Solanum nigrum*, or Black Nightshade.</note>. Its power—that is, its **complexion**—is very cold, specifically in the third degree, as **Galen** <note>The influential 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories of the "four humors" dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> says. This herb is said to wonderfully soothe ear pain if the juice of that herb is poured in <gloss>gradually—that is, little by little—inside</gloss>. Authors say that a poultice <note>original: "cataplasma"; a soft, moist mass of material, typically of plant leaves or flour, applied to the body to relieve soreness and inflammation.</note> of *Strychnos* heals <gloss>that is, cures</gloss> **lachrymal fistulas** <note>original: "egilopas"; an ulcer or swelling in the inner corner of the eye.</note> (of which we have spoken), and they say this same poultice is beneficial for a headache. If <gloss>salt and bread are ground together—that is, at the same time—with the Strychnos</gloss>, and then <gloss>added—that is, placed—in the manner of a poultice</gloss>, they restrain **parotid swellings** <gloss>that is, abscesses behind the ears</gloss>. **Itching** <gloss>that is, a skin eruption, or minor scabies, or the desire to scratch the skin</gloss> is driven out <gloss>that is, cast off</gloss> when <gloss>smeared—that is, rubbed—with the juice of Strychnos</gloss>.

The juice of *Maurella*, when <gloss>applied to the womb</gloss>, <gloss>binds—that is, restrains—the womanly flow—that is, immoderate menstruation</gloss>. Combine <gloss>barley groats—that is, together—with its crushed leaves</gloss>. **"Holy fire"** <note>original: "ignis sacer"; a term often used for erysipelas or other spreading skin infections.</note> <gloss>that is, Persian fire and biting herpes</gloss> <gloss>that is, corroding</gloss> is said to be expelled <gloss>that is, cured</gloss> by these, specifically by the flour which is mixed in and the *Maurella* applied on top. But <gloss>if you join to these</gloss> **litharge** <note>original: "spumã argenti"; literally 'silver foam,' a lead oxide byproduct of silver refining.</note> (which is called *agiritis*), **white lead** <note>original: "cerusam"</note> (of which we have spoken), and **rose oil**, the aforementioned medicine becomes stronger and better <gloss>that is, more effective</gloss>.

Henbane <note>original: "Iusquiamū"; from the Greek *Hyoscyamus*, meaning "hog's bean."</note> is what the Greeks call this herb (which our people call **Caniculata** <note>literally "dog-like" or "pertaining to the dog-star"; a common Latin name for Henbane in medieval texts.</note>).

**I**t is said that the power of this herb is very cold.
This herb has three species: the first produces white seeds,
The second produces reddish seeds, and the third black.
The one that carries white seeds is more noble <note>In a medical context, "noble" refers to the variety that is most effective and safest for human use.</note> than the others.
If this white variety is lacking, the custom is to use the reddish variety in its place;
But the common practice of physicians rejects the black variety <note>Black Henbane was known to be much more toxic and prone to causing delirium compared to the milder white species.</note>.
Add barley meal <term>polenta <note>In this historical context, "polenta" refers to a flour made from toasted barley, used as a base for medicinal poultices.</note></term> to the crushed leaves of Henbane

By applying these <note>Referring to the mixture of Henbane and barley meal mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> you can cure any swelling.
Thus, you can wonderfully cool down "hot" gout <note>original: "podagram"; a form of inflammatory arthritis characterized by recurrent attacks of a red, tender, hot, and swollen joint.</note>.
Its juice, poured into the ears, kills worms.
And it is accustomed to relieve various pains of the ears
If warm vinegar <note>original: "acetum"</note> is held in the mouth for a long time <note>Likely referring to the herb being boiled in vinegar and used as a rinse.</note>.

Let the roots of this herb be boiled in wine.
It is said to soothe excessive toothache.
The expressed juice of its crushed seeds
Can check a viscous and hot rheum <term>rheum <note>an old medical term for a flow of watery discharge or catarrh from the eyes, nose, or mouth</note></term>.
If the eyes of a patient are frequently anointed [with it].
An obol <note>a small unit of weight, originally a Greek coin</note> of its seed mixed with the seed
Of wormwood <note>original: "absinis"</note> is taken with mead <term>mulsa <note>a mixture of water and honey</note></term> by them.

And they say it can heal those spitting blood <note>original: "emptoicis"; likely a Latinization of the Greek *hemoptysis*.</note>
Or in whatever way blood is cast from the mouth.
This drink is said to provide a cure when frequently swallowed.
Its seed, ground with wine and made into a poultice <term>cathaplasma <note>a soft, moist mass of material, such as herbs and flour, applied to the body to relieve inflammation</note></term>,
Is said to cure swollen breasts and testicles.
It is added as a plaster to many antidotes and:
This herb, eaten like a vegetable <note>original: "olus"</note>, promotes [milk in] the breast.
The same juice is excellent when poured into any wound.

¶ Since three species of this herb are numbered by physicians, we ought to abstain entirely from two of them as if from a deadly poison (if faith is to be given to the advice of Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), an influential Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note>). For these are the words of Galen: "It is necessary that one beware of these two species (namely, the black and the reddish); and let no one use them because they are not..."

s iij

...[there] is [no] help in them; namely, they kill. ¶ But it is permitted to use the other species. However, discretion must be applied on account of its exuberant coldness. ¶ For as Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a preeminent physician of the Islamic Golden Age whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe.</note> says, it is a mortifying cold [substance], making one paralytic and preventing all bodily operations.

¶ The Greeks call the herb *hyoscyamus* <note>original: "iusquiamum"</note> which our people—that is, the Latins—call *camiculata*. Henbane <note>original: "iusquiamus"</note> has three species. The prior—that is, the first species—bears, that is, produces white seed; the other produces reddish seed; the third produces black seed.

¶ One species is more noble—that is, more excellent—than the remaining two species: the one which bears—that is, produces—white seed. In place of this white henbane, it is the custom among doctors to apply reddish henbane. However—that is, but—the frequent, that is, accustomed use of doctors rejects—that is, has thrown out—the black [kind].

With *depolenta* <note>original: "depolentam"; a mixture of water and flour, likely barley meal</note>—that is, water mixed with flour—and the crushed leaves of henbane, you can cure any tumors—that is, by applying these medications to any swellings <note>original: "inflationes"</note>. You can cool "hot" gout <term>podagra <note>a form of inflammatory arthritis, often affecting the big toe, traditionally blamed on an imbalance of "hot" humors</note></term> arising from the blood, or from bile, or even from false phlegm.

The juice of this herb, poured into the ears, kills—that is, slays—worms that are produced in the ears. And the same juice is accustomed to relieve—that is, alleviate—various pains inflicted on the ears. If warm vinegar <note>original: "acetum"</note> in which the roots of that herb have been boiled is held in the mouth for a long time, it is said to soothe immoderate—that is, intemperate—pain of a tooth (provided, however, that the matter causing the pain is "hot"). The expressed juice of the crushed seed of this herb can restrain a rheum <term>rheum <note>original: "reuma"; a watery discharge from the mucous membranes, thought to be a "flow" of humors</note></term>—that is, a pressure in the head—which the Greeks call *rheumatismos* <note>original: "reumatiſmos"</note>.

...viscous—that is, sticky—and hot—that is, proceeding from a viscous and hot matter. If the eyes of the patient are frequently anointed with its juice <note>referring to henbane juice</note>. An obol <note>A small unit of weight, about 0.5 to 0.7 grams</note> of that henbane's seed, mixed with the seed of *miconis*—that is, white poppy—is taken equally—that is, at the same time—and beneficially by those women who suffer from an immoderate flux of the womb <note>original: "fluxum matricis"; refers here to excessive uterine bleeding or discharge</note>. This drink, swallowed frequently, is said to heal—that is, to bring a remedy. Doctors say the seed of white henbane, crushed with wine—I say this when made into a poultice <term>cataplasm <note>a soft, moist mass of herbs applied to the body as medicine</note></term>—cures breasts and testicles that are swollen—that is, inflamed. This herb is added to many plasters and antidotes—that is, medicines. The herb itself, if eaten like a vegetable or other cultivated herbs, takes away the gifts of life (for by its intense coldness it extinguishes the natural color and vitality). The juice poured into any—that is, whatever—wound, performs the same—that is, it induces death. Hippocrates <note>original: "hyppo."</note>, in the *Aphorisms*, section five: Cold is biting to ulcers; it hardens the skin; without pain it causes blackness, rigors, spasms, and tetanus. And in another aphorism: Heat is indeed clarifying and friendly. But cold is contrary and killing. These are the words of Hippocrates.

No bee <note>original: "apium"; while "apium" often means celery/parsley, the context here refers to bees ("apum").</note> will harm—that is, infest—you (a liniment which is very easy to prepare and should not be unknown to farmers who handle beehives). By adding—that is, placing upon the head—mallow boiled in wash—that is, in urine—you will thus drive away <term>achoras <note>Scurf or ulcerative sores on the scalp.</note></term>—that is, swellings of the head—and dandruff—that is, filth of the head bearing a resemblance to wheat bran. Now, "wash" <note>original: "lotium"</note> is urine, so named from "washing away" <note>original: "diluendis"</note> impurities from bodies. Regarding this, Pico della Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a Renaissance philosopher who famously argued against astrology.</note>, in his work against the astrologers, says: "Read the books of the *Prognostics* of Hippocrates; you will never find him seeking the patient's future among the stars. For he knew that these things should be sought from the urine rather than from a star, and should be foreknown more certainly from the pulse of the veins than from the motion of the spheres."

If you join oil to boiled mallow leaves, you will extinguish "sacred fire" <term>ignem sacrum <note>Likely erysipelas, a painful skin infection, or shingles.</note></term> with such a <term>cataplasm <note>A soft, moist mass of material, typically of herbal ingredients, applied to the body to relieve soreness and inflammation; a poultice.</note></term>. You also greatly aid those who are burned with that same poultice. A decoction of it softens <term>steric <note>original: "stericas"; derived from the Greek "hystera" for womb.</note></term>—that is, uterine—hardnesses. This same remedy is wonderfully beneficial for <term>tenesmus <note>A distressing sensation of needing to evacuate the bowels even when they are empty.</note></term>—that is, illnesses of the intestines—and for the bowels, if it is applied to them.

### On the herb Dock [Pericella]. 64.

> The herb of the dock <note>original: "lapati"</note> is commonly called *pricella*.
> Of this herb, there are said to be four species.
> Yet the medicinal power in almost all of them is equal.
> In all of them, there is a hot and harsh quality.
> Whence, if taken, it strengthens the stomach and is accustomed
> To expel trapped winds from the stomach through belching.
> And taken like a vegetable, it is said to bind the belly.
> Biting itch and the mange that tears the skin
> Yield to it when it is applied warm as a poultice—
> Its roots being boiled in wine and well-ground.

Applied [as a poultice], they restrain <term>scrofula <note>original: "scrophas"; a tubercular swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck.</note></term> and <term>parotid swellings <note>original: "perotidas"; inflammation of the salivary glands near the ear.</note></term>.
If a decoction of this plant, while warm, is frequently used as a gargle,
It will soothe swollen <term>uvulas <note>original: "uuas"; the fleshy tissue hanging at the back of the throat.</note></term> and the pain of a tooth.
As a drink, this aids those suffering from <term>dysentery <note>original: "dissintericos"; severe infectious diarrhea.</note></term> and those with intestinal distress <note>original: "ciliacos"; refers to the "coeliac passion," a historical term for chronic abdominal ailments.</note>.
Ear pain is also driven away if the ear is bathed <note>original: "foveatur"; to be treated with a warm medicinal liquid or fomentation.</note> with the warm liquid.
Take the roots boiled in strong vinegar,
And apply them, crushed, over a spleen that is often swollen;
It will check the swelling in no small measure by its drying effect.
A decoction of the roots may be made with either wine or water.

¶ The herb of dock, etc. This herb is extremely common in the use of physicians; however, only one of its species—namely, "sharp dock" <note>original: "lapathium acutum"; likely *Rumex acutus*.</note>—is typically prepared for medicines, while its other species are neglected due to lack of use. ¶ The herb of dock is commonly called *picella* (though Silvanus calls it *parella*), and there are said to be four species of it. However, its power (that is, its <gloss>complexion and property</gloss>) is equal—or nearly the same—in medicines for all species of dock. Their potency in all these species is "fervent" (that is, <gloss>inclining toward heat</gloss>) and "austere" (that is, <gloss>pontic or styptic</gloss>). "Whatever things are moderately dry and somewhat austere, physicians call 'styptic,'" says <term>Marsilio Ficino <note>A prominent 15th-century Italian philosopher and physician who integrated Neoplatonism with medical theory.</note></term>. For this reason, certainly, when the herb is taken, it comforts the stomach. For styptics <note>Substances that contract tissues and stop secretions.</note>, by binding the loose parts of the stomach, strengthen it and are accustomed to expel trapped wind in the stomach through belching (that is, <gloss>evacuation</gloss>). And when taken as a vegetable, it is said (that is, <gloss>reported</gloss>) to bind the bowels. Stinging itching (that is, <gloss>humors irritating the skin with their sharpness</gloss>) and <term>scabies <note>A general historical term for various skin eruptions, itching, and scaling.</note></term> which tears the skin (that is, <gloss>which lacerates the flesh</gloss>) yield to the washing (that is, <gloss>a decoction, bathing, or fomentation made in warm water</gloss>). The roots of that herb, boiled in wine and well-crushed, when applied, check <term>scrofula <note>original: "scrophas"</note></term> and <term>parotids <note>original: "parotidas"; swellings or abscesses of the salivary glands near the ear.</note></term>. If its decoction is frequently gargled while lukewarm (that is, <gloss>tepid</gloss>), it will soothe swollen <term>uvulas <note>original: "vuas"; the fleshy tissue hanging at the back of the throat.</note></term> and the pains of a tooth. This herb, in this drink, aids those with <term>dysentery <note>original: "dissitericos"; severe, often bloody, diarrhea.</note></term>...
