### ¶ Concerning the herb Darnel. 14.

The Greek calls the herb "darnel" <note>original: "lolium"; a common weed in grain fields, often *Lolium temulentum*.</note> harmful to the grain crops.
Which our people call *nigella* <note>original: "nigellam"; while modern Nigella is Black Cumin, in medieval texts this often referred to the purple Corncockle (*Agrostemma githago*) or Darnel which darkened the flour.</note> in the common manner.
It cleanses cancers; it cures emerging wounds
When ground and added together with radish and a little salt.
By the same treatment you restrain <term>skin eruptions <note>original: "zernas"; a medieval term for impetigo or similar rashes.</note></term> and leprosies.
Mix darnel with sulfur, wine, and the droppings of a dove,
[And] the seed which lilies provide.
Join these things together, cooked in pure wine, and apply them to scrofulous swellings.
Thus it disperses scrofula; thus it ruptures <term>abscesses <note>original: "apostemata"; swellings filled with purulent matter.</note></term>.
And this <term>poultice <note>original: "cathaplasma".</note></term> will soften other hardnesses [tumors].
With <term>mead <note>original: "mulsia"; a mixture of honey and water, here boiled with wine.</note></term> boiled down with wine, frankincense, and saffron,
Apply it to the hip which wicked <term>sciatica <note>original: "sciasis"; nerve pain in the hip and leg.</note></term> vexes.
If a woman in labor should <term>fumigate <note>The practice of breathing in or exposing the body to medicinal smoke.</note></term> herself with it,
It is asserted that she more quickly lays down the weight of her womb [gives birth].
Mixing this with <term>litharge <note>original: "argenti spume"; lead monoxide, a common ingredient in historical ointments.</note></term> and fat,
Apply it warm as a healthy poultice for <term>gout <note>original: "podagre"; painful inflammation of the joints.</note></term>.

### ¶ Harmful to grain, etc.
**Virgil** <note>original: "Virgilius"; the Roman poet Publius Virgilius Maro (70–19 BCE), whose works on farming were considered authoritative.</note>, the greatest of poets, calls this herb "unhappy." "The **unhappy darnel** and **sterile wild oats** dominate [the fields]," <note>original: "infelix lolium & steriles dominantur auene"; a famous line from Virgil’s *Georgics* (I.154), describing weeds that ruin a harvest.</note> the cause of which "unhappiness" **Cristoforo Landino** <note>original: "Christoforus landinus"; a 15th-century Italian humanist and commentator on Virgil.</note> adds: because, he says, they utterly suffocate the crops, because the thin stalk supports it.

The Greek calls **darnel** <note>original: "lolium"; likely *Lolium temulentum*, a weed that can be toxic if ground into flour.</note> a "harmful herb"—that is, detrimental to the grain, or to the fruits—which herb our people (namely the Latins) call **nigella** <note>original: "nigellā"; while modern *Nigella* is black cumin, in this context it refers to "black-seeded" weeds like corncockle or darnel that darken bread flour.</note> in the common manner (this is also called **zizania** <note>original: "zizania"; the "tares" mentioned in Biblical parables.</note>). 

This herb, ground with **radish** and with a little **salt** added on top, cleanses **cancers** <note>original: "cancros"; referring here to spreading, ulcerous sores or cankers.</note>—but only those that are beginning—because if they have already become established, there is no longer a place for medicines. Wherefore **Ovid** says: "And my **incurable cancer** is accustomed to creep far and wide, and to add uninjured parts to the corrupted ones." <note>original: "Atqz meum late solet immedicabile cancer serpere: & illesas vitiatis addere partes"; a quote from Ovid's *Tristia* (V.2.45), using the metaphor of a spreading ulcer for his misfortunes.</note> 

And it heals **emerging wounds** (that is, fresh ones). It also cures **leprosies**—that is, <term>morphea <note>original: "morpheam"; a skin condition involving localized patches of discolored or hardened skin.</note></term> and <term>albaras <note>original: "albaras"; a term derived from Arabic (*al-baras*), often referring to white vitiligo or leprosy.</note></term> and <term>zeruss <note>original: "zeruss"; likely a transliteration of an Arabic term for an eruptive skin disease.</note></term> (that is, **skin eruptions** <note>original: "empetigines"; a general term for inflammatory skin diseases.</note>). 

In the same treatment, mix darnel with **live sulfur** and <term>pigeon droppings <note>original: "purgamentis columbe" / "fumo columbino"; while the text literally says "pigeon smoke," medical tradition usually implies pigeon dung (*fimus*), which was prized for its "intense heat" to draw out infections.</note></term> which is of intense heat. Join to these the seed that **flax** provides (that is, they give <term>linseed <note>original: "lina"; the seeds of the flax plant.</note></term>). Let these be cooked in **pure wine** and placed on top of <term>scrofula <note>original: "scrophis"; swellings of the lymph nodes, often in the neck.</note></term>. This **poultice** <note>original: "cathaplasma"; a soft, moist mass applied to the body.</note> scatters them. Thus it breaks the scrofulous swellings and <gloss>opens</gloss> them (that is, it opens **abscesses** <note>original: "apostemata"; a localized collection of pus.</note>) and <gloss>softens</gloss> them (that is, it reduces other hardnesses—namely tumors not yielding to ripening—to a soft state). 

Apply darnel boiled in **honey-water** <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of water and honey.</note> with wine, **frankincense**, and **saffron** to the **hip** (that is, to the joint of the appendage) which <term>sciatica <note>original: "iciasis"; a painful condition affecting the sciatic nerve in the hip and leg.</note></term>—an unequal joint disease (that is, a troublesome one)—<gloss>vexes</gloss> (that is, agitates). 

If a woman is about to give birth, let her subject herself to that **nigella** (that is, let her receive its smoke through a hollow, oblong instrument which they call a **funnel** <note>original: "embotum"; a device used here for fumigation of the birth canal.</note>); it is claimed she can lay down [the fetus]...

### ¶ Concerning the herb Hemlock. lxv.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a Hemlock plant (Cicuta), shown with a central stalk branching into several stems. Each stem is lined with serrated, jagged leaves. The illustration is bordered by a double-line rectangular frame.</image-desc>

The nature of deadly **hemlock** <note>original: "cicuta"; likely Conium maculatum, a highly toxic plant.</note> is a cold power,
Wherefore it kills those who drink it after the manner of a freezing poison.
In one who perishes from this herb, the skin becomes spotted;
Wherefore this kind of death can be proven through these signs.
This used to be the public punishment for criminals in **Athens** <note>original: "athenis"; a reference to the execution method used for the philosopher Socrates, who was forced to drink hemlock in 399 BCE.</note>.

Having taken this, the great **Socrates** <note>original: "socrates"; the famous Athenian philosopher (c. 470–399 BCE) sentenced to death by drinking hemlock.</note> was put to death. 
How this comes to pass, I do not think it is our place to say,
Since nothing that harms, but only what helps, should be reported.
If someone is near death because of this herb being taken,
Perhaps they should drink warm, undiluted wine; they will escape the danger.
But to whomsoever this herb is usually harmful when taken in a drink,
It is nonetheless usually helpful when applied magnificently <note>likely referring to external medicinal applications like poultices, as opposed to ingestion.</note>.

¶ **Melitus** <note>one of the three primary accusers of Socrates.</note> accused **Socrates**, who first called down moral philosophy from the heavens, on the grounds that he violated the laws and that he did not believe the gods existed whom the city of **Athens** had accepted by the institution of their ancestors. He also alleged that Socrates introduced other spirits <note>original: "demonia"; referring to Socrates' *daimonion*, an inner divine voice.</note>. Contrary to law and divine right, he was said to corrupt the youth. Finally, condemned by the judges and thrown into chains, he ended his life with a drink of **hemlock** <note>original: "cicute".</note>. Whence **Persius** <note>A Roman satirist.</note> says: *Are you handling the people's business? Believe this, your bearded master says—* <gloss>meaning the master whom the dire draft of hemlock carries off.</gloss> And **Juvenal** <note>A Roman satirical poet.</note> in his thirteenth Satire: *And the old man, neighbor to sweet Mount Hymettus, who, while in chains, would not give his accuser a portion of the hemlock he had received.* For **hemlock** was a public punishment of the **Athenians**, viewed with hatred. It has a harmful seed, and its cooling power <note>Ancient medicine categorized hemlock as "cold" in the fourth degree, meaning it was potent enough to extinguish the body's natural heat.</note> is such that if it kills, the victims begin to grow cold from the extremities of the body. So says **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*.</note>, to whom **Juvenal** subscribes in his seventh Satire: *And you, Athens, saw this poor man; you offered nothing beforehand except cold hemlock.*

But let us hear our poet, who does not disagree with the others. The nature of **hemlock** is cold <gloss>that is, of a deadly power—meaning death-bringing</gloss>; wherefore <gloss>meaning for that reason</gloss> it kills <gloss>that is, it kills those drinking it after the manner of a freezing and cold poison.</gloss> In one who perishes from this herb <gloss>that is, from a potion of this herb</gloss>, the skin becomes <gloss>that is, is made</gloss> spotted <gloss>meaning infected with spots.</gloss> Wherefore this kind of death is valid <gloss>that is, it can be proven</gloss> through these signs. This used to be the public punishment for defendants <gloss>that is, those condemned.</gloss>

It wonderfully helps summer **inflammations** <term>epiphoras: a medical term for a sudden flow of humors, usually resulting in watering or inflammation of the eyes.</term> of the eyes,
If the forehead is covered with crushed green leaves,
Or if the eyes are smeared round about with its juice.

## ¶ On the Pepper plant. LXVI.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring a detailed botanical illustration of a flowering plant with prominent leaves and central flower stalks, framed within a decorative square border.</image-desc>

Having already spoken in verses about some common herbs,
Now I shall attempt to relate those species which, 
often noted by everyone, the practice of healing has made famous.
And first, I think the properties of **pepper** should be discussed—
a thing known more to the kitchen itself than to medicine.
Pepper is asserted to be of a dry and hot virtue.

### Pepper

There are three species of this: **white**, **long**, and **black**.
But because two <gloss>the white and the long varieties</gloss> are known only to physicians,
of the **black species** I will say certain things known to me.
Taken raw or cooked, or joined with honey,
it will aid the **digestive power** of the stomach and the **liver** <note>original: "iecoris"</note>.
It cures **poisonous bites** and removes **nausea** <gloss>fastidia: a loss of appetite or a loathing of food</gloss>.
It often meets <gloss>addresses or remedies</gloss> various complaints of the **chest** <note>original: "thoracis"</note>.
And the cold <gloss>the chills or shivering</gloss> that a **periodic fever** <term>periodic fever: an illness, such as malaria, characterized by symptoms that return at regular intervals</term> is accustomed to provoke,
it will restrain the fever if it is taken before the **shaking** <gloss>the onset of tremors or rigors</gloss>.
With **laurel berries** or with well-crushed leaves,
and drunk with warm **pure wine** <gloss>merum: wine that has not been diluted with water</gloss>, it settles **griping pains** <term>tormina: acute abdominal cramping or "the gripes"</term>.
And it suppresses **scrofula** <term>scrophas: a swelling of the lymph glands, usually in the neck, historically known as the 'King's Evil'</term> when mixed with hard **pitch** <note>a thick, resinous substance used as a base for medicinal plasters</note>.

And when applied as a dressing, it is also correctly joined to those remedies
Which are accustomed to purge the eyes of harsh darkness <gloss>caligine: a dimming or cloudiness of vision, such as cataracts</gloss>.

No one can describe all the powers of pepper,

¶ Thus far, Macer has explained in clear language the powers and natures of herbs that are in more frequent use by physicians. Now he touches upon several species or aromatics, the knowledge of which is very necessary for those desiring good bodily health. First, however, he discusses a few things regarding pepper. Of which there are three species, which **Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman author of Natural History</note> clearly declares: the buds of pepper which, gathered before they gape open and toasted by the sun, make what is called **long pepper**. But gradually opening through maturity, they show **hot pepper**, which then, toasted by the sun’s heat, is changed with wrinkles. Whence by **Persius** <note>Aulus Persius Flaccus, a Roman satirist</note> it is called "wrinkled black pepper" in his fifth satire: *"Wrinkled pepper and grains of pale cumin."* I, Macer, will attempt to report—that is, to recall to memory—those species (i.e., aromatics) which the practice of healing has now made known often to all. I say this with some common herbs (i.e., more commonly mentioned) explained in song (that is, in verse). And I think the powers of pepper should be discussed (i.e., explained) first, because the kitchen (i.e., the cookroom or the use of cooks) makes pepper better known (i.e., more manifest) than medicine does. Physicians have asserted

<meta>This page contains a prose commentary on the medicinal properties of pepper, expanding upon the verses of Macer Floridus. It provides specific instructions for digestive health, fevers, and topical applications for skin and eye conditions, concluding with a list of famous medieval compound medicines that rely on pepper as a base.</meta>

**Pepper** is of a dry and hot nature. There are three species of it: namely, white, long, and black. But since two of these species are known only to physicians, tell me what is known to me regarding the black species <gloss>that is, of black pepper</gloss>.

Pepper—whether raw, cooked, or joined with honey—will aid the digestive power of the stomach and liver when taken. It cures poisonous <gloss>pestiferos</gloss> bites and takes away—that is, removes—distaste <gloss>nauseating fullness</gloss>. It often treats various complaints of the thorax <gloss>the chest</gloss>. It restrains that which a periodic <gloss>intermittent</gloss> fever is accustomed to provoke, if it is taken before the shivering of the fever. 

And pepper drunk with berries <gloss>laurel grains</gloss> or with its leaves well ground <gloss>strongly</gloss> and with pure wine <gloss>warm wine</gloss> settles <gloss>mitigates</gloss> griping <gloss>contortions of the belly</gloss>. Also, pepper mixed with hard pitch and applied topically resolves <gloss>breaks down</gloss> scrofulas <term>scrofula: a swelling of the lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis</term>.

It is also <gloss>even</gloss> correctly joined to those medicines which are accustomed to purge the eyes of a dread darkness <note>original: "caligine dira"; referring to a clouding or dimming of vision, such as cataracts</note>, which is all the more dread because sight is the most excellent of senses. For as **Aristotle** says in his *Metaphysics*, the eye contributes most to our knowledge of things. For it shows us far more differences between things than the other senses do. 

Pepper mixed with **nitre** <term>nitre: a mineral salt used for cleansing; here specifically "the foam of rock salt"</term> clears away <gloss>cleanses</gloss> skin spots when used as a poultice <gloss>in the manner of a plaster</gloss>. Combine pepper—that is, ground together—with the powder of human dung, and apply it thus to cancers <gloss>cancerous wounds</gloss>. Medical authors say that nothing tested <gloss>that is, which has been tried and proven</gloss> is more useful than this powder. 

No one can describe all the powers of pepper; for indeed <gloss>certainly</gloss> almost all medicines require pepper. It is usually added to many precious **antidotes** <term>antidote: a complex medieval compound medicine, often containing dozens of ingredients</term>, such as electuaries of aromatics, of gems, of musk, of **galangal** <term>galangal: a medicinal root related to ginger</term>, of cinnamon, *diatria pipereon* <note>original: "dyatriō pipereō"; a medicinal compound named for containing three types of pepper</note>, **Mithridate** <term>Mithridate: a famous "universal" antidote and panacea</term>, the great **theriac** <term>theriac: one of the most prestigious medieval medicines, used against poisons and plague</term>, and others of this kind. Hence...

For this reason—namely, because **pepper** is well-approved by all medical authors—I think (that is, I judge) that the praises expressed in such few words and simple style [should be] supplemented; because this pepper is worthy of praise in innumerable medicines <gloss>remedies</gloss>.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Pellitory. 77.

**Pellitory** is hot and dry. It is of the fourth degree in these [qualities].
If a cold humor torments the stomach,
The patient should chew this: and hold it in the mouth for a long time.
And it helps if it is gargled with mixed vinegar.
In this way, it suppresses a tongue swollen from phlegm;
Thus it relieves the uvula <note>original: "uuam"; literally "grape," a common historical term for a swollen uvula</note> when it is pressed by the weight of humor.
And it aids many other vices <gloss>ailments</gloss> of the mouth.
Taken with honey, it is of no small benefit to those with the falling sickness <term>falling sickness: epilepsy</term>.
Thus it is accustomed to alleviate the disease that relaxes the limbs <term>palsy or paralysis</term>.
Hung from the neck, it is believed to benefit children with epilepsy;
It is even said to help by its scent alone.
If you anoint the body with the oil in which it is boiled,
It will diminish and take away the shivering <gloss>rigor</gloss> before the onset of a fever.
As an ointment, it often drives away heavy pain of the kidneys.
The paralytic feels wonderful help from being anointed with it,
Especially if he is frequently and vigorously rubbed by hand.
If the whole body is softened with that ointment,
Copious sweat will pour forth through the opened pores.
This is accustomed to cure any numbness of the limbs,
And it can keep the body safe from the cold.
Tetanus <term>thetanus: a condition of muscle rigidity or spasms</term> is dissolved by this medicine.
It is equally beneficial if ground alone with olive oil.
Thus, by rubbing, you will be able to heal any location.

¶ Although **pellitory** should be numbered among the herbs, **Macer** nevertheless wished to place it here among the species or spices <note>original: "species seu aromata"</note>, because of its similarity to spices. For in the manner of spices, it burns and bites the tongue with its sharpness...

who has stupor in his limbs or relaxations <gloss>palsy</gloss>.

...for those who suffer from the falling sickness—that is, for epileptics <note>original: "epylenticis"</note>—and it is said to assist them by its scent alone. It often drives away—that is, eliminates—severe pain in the kidneys. A person suffering from paralysis, when anointed with the same—that is, with such a liniment—feels—that is, receives—wonderful—that is, marvelous—help <gloss>taking away, evidently, the phlegmatic humors that relax the nerves</gloss>, provided he has been rubbed often, and not gently, but rather quite vigorously. <gloss>For frequent rubbing opens the blocked pores of the flesh, through which this liniment easily penetrates right to the nerves.</gloss> If the whole body is softened—that is, anointed with this ointment—that is, salve—a large—that is, a more abundant—sweat will come forth through the opened pores of the flesh. This liniment is accustomed to cure any numbness—that is, the "falling asleep" of the limbs—and can keep the body safe—that is, secure—from the cold. And "tetanus" is a disease in which the extension of the limbs <unclear>membra</unclear> to any different position is prevented. Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD) was a Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note> says: "Tetanus is a very acute disease composed of both contrary movements of spasm," about which Hippocrates <note>Hippocrates (c. 460–370 BC), the "Father of Medicine."</note> says: "Whoever is seized by tetanus dies within four days; but if they survive these, they are saved." And tetanus will be released—that is, cured—by this medicine. **Pellitory** is just as beneficial if you dissolve—that is, break down or dilute—it, once ground, in olive oil—that is, in the oil of olives. And you will be able—that is, you will have the power—to treat—that is, to heal—by rubbing in this way with this liniment any place occupied by the aforementioned ailments. <meta>This page concludes the discussion on Pellitory-of-Spain and begins a new section comparing ginger to pepper, followed by a detailed herbal entry on the properties of cumin. It maintains the structure of the medieval herbal, alternating between poetic verses and scholarly commentary.</meta>

Ginger and pepper are equal in medicine.
For this reason, I am silent about its powers.

¶ Some call this species *ginginer*, others *gingiber*. Since where we say "b," the Greeks pronounce "v" <note>original: "vita." This refers to the Greek letter Beta (β), which in later Greek and Byzantine pronunciation shifted from a 'b' to a 'v' sound.</note>; however, more recent authors pronounce it *zinziber*. As Nicolaus Prepositus <note>Nicolaus Praepositus (12th century) was a famous medical author known for his "Antidotarium," a standard text on preparing medicines.</note> says, this is not the root of pepper as some think, even though it has a similar taste. Ginger is preserved, and the method of its preservation and its benefits are best declared by Mesue <note>Johannes Mesue (the Younger), a common authority in medieval pharmacy, likely representing Arabic medical tradition.</note>. Commonly, such a preserve is called "green ginger," and it is wonderfully suited for the stomach. ¶ Here Macer <note>Macer Floridus, the traditional name given to the author of this herbal poem.</note>, striving for brevity because of the similarity between ginger and pepper in their powers and temperament <term>complexion|The specific balance of the four qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—within a substance or person.</term>, wished only these few things to be written about it. Physicians say ginger and pepper are equal species in medicine; for this cause—that is, for this reason—I am silent about the powers of ginger <note>The author assumes that since the reader already knows the virtues of pepper, there is no need to repeat them for ginger.</note>.

->¶ On the herb Cumin. 69.<-

Physicians believe **cumin** to be hot and dry;
It is in the third degree of these qualities <note>Medieval medicine ranked the intensity of an herb's effects on a scale from 1 to 4. A "third degree" substance was quite potent.</note>.
They hand down that, taken in any way, it dispels
The wind <note>Intestinal gas or flatulence.</note> vexing the bowels and weighing down the stomach.
And they believe it excites the digestive heat
Of the stomach and the liver <note>original: "iectoris," a variant of "iecoris" (liver).</note> and inhibits the fury of Venus <note>That is, it suppresses sexual desire.</note>.
And, when cooked in vinegar, it binds a flow of the belly <note>It acts as a remedy for diarrhea.</note>.
It provides a wonderful remedy for those with orthopnea <note>original: "orthonoicis." A condition where a person can only breathe comfortably when sitting or standing upright.</note>,
And those experienced say that when taken with wine,
It helps against poisonous bites. Often drunk with *posca* <note>A common beverage made of vinegar mixed with water.</note>,
It offers help; and ground and joined with bean meal
And warm honey, it is accustomed to cure swollen testicles.
Applied or taken with *posca*, it stops the menstrual flow;
They say that frequently chewing it produces a pale complexion.

--- Page 294 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of medicinal herbs, specifically Cumin. It follows a brief mention of Ginger and Pepper on the previous page. This section is notable for its defense of classical poetry as a source of scientific knowledge.</meta>

Physicians think [cumin is hot and dry], etc. Cumin, or *ciminum* as others prefer, is a seed most suited for dissolving windiness <term>ventosity|Flatulence or gas trapped in the body.</term> and is very friendly to the stomach. Nevertheless, as Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note> says, its excessive use makes the face "citrine" <note>original: "citrinat," meaning to turn yellowish or pale like a lemon.</note>; that is, it produces paleness. Persius and Horace were not ignorant of this. Persius [writes of] "wrinkled pepper and the seeds of pale cumin" <note>Persius, Satires 5.55.</note>. Horace [writes] "because if they should happen to be pale, they would drink bloodless cumin" <note>Horace, Epistles 1.19.18. In antiquity, cumin was famously used by students to induce a pale, studious appearance.</note>. 

See, noble youth, how unjust those judges are who hallucinate that poets write mere trifles, fables, and ravings. For there is nothing in the good disciplines so difficult or so hidden that it does not lie hidden under the "bark" <note>The outer layer or literal meaning of a text.</note> of a poem. But this is clear only to the gifted, the refined, and the vigilant. Therefore, in passing, I would most earnestly ask you, while you pass through your flowering years, to pluck the poetic flowers—that is, accustom yourself to reading the poets—so that when you reach a more mature age, you will gather from them the sweetest honey. And do not let the idle and empty drones <note>A metaphor for lazy or unlearned critics who discourage the study of literature.</note> drive you away from this, because they fear you will turn out more learned or more cultured than themselves. 

But, leaving this aside, let us resume our path. Physicians think all cumin is hot and dry; it is in the third degree of these qualities <term>degree|In medieval medicine, the potency of an herb's "complexion" (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) was measured on a scale of one to four.</term>. Authorities hand down that cumin taken in any way, in food or drink, drives out the wind that vexes the viscera (that is, the internal organs) and burdens the stomach. Seeking an exit, [the wind] violently distends the viscera, from which follows a "solution of continuity" <term>solution of continuity|A medical term for a break or disruption in the physical integrity of a tissue, often used to describe the cause of pain.</term>, which is the cause of pain, as Avicenna testifies. 

And it excites the digestive heat of the stomach and liver; for the digestive power needs heat more than the other natural powers, according to Avicenna. They also believe it inhibits—that is, restrains—the frenzies of Venus <note>A poetic way of saying it reduces sexual desire or libido.</note>. And they believe cumin cooked in vinegar binds a flux of the belly <note>Diarrhea or loose bowels.</note> based on "proof"—that is, what they have proven by experience. They say this medicine, cumin drunk with *pusca* (that is, with vinegar and water), provides a wonderful—that is, admirable—remedy for Antonius... <note>Likely referring to Antonius Musa, the famous physician to Emperor Augustus.</note>

...[those] suffering from difficulty in breathing; likewise, a draught of it with wine provides help against venomous bites. ## ¶ On Galangal [Chapter] 70.

**Taken Galangal dissolves inflammation of the stomach**
**And if the stomach be phlegmatic, it strengthens it**
**Taken, it drives out trapped internal wind**
**It aids the digestive powers and cures colic**
**Chewed, it greatly improves the scent of the mouth**
**Taken, it increases the heat of Venus and of the kidneys**

¶ Some say that galangal <term>Galangal|A root spice related to ginger, commonly used in medieval medicine and cooking.</term> is a tree, others a fruit. Dioscorides <note>original: "Dya."; Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD) was a Greek physician and author of the most influential herbal of antiquity.</note> or others say it is a root found around the base of a tree in Persia, which appears because it has a certain root within. Let us hear of its powers. Taken galangal dissolves "phlegmone" <gloss>that is, an abscess of the stomach</gloss>, and if the stomach is "phlegmatic" <note>In the theory of the Four Humors, a phlegmatic stomach was considered cold and moist, leading to sluggish digestion.</note> by its natural temperament, it strengthens it. Its digestive power—which as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the preeminent physician of the Roman Empire.</note> says in *On the Integrity [of the Parts]*, a "cold belly" [stomach] is able to desire what is good [to eat] but not to digest it well—galangal drives this out. As physicians say: it puts an end to trapped internal wind. This galangal aids the digestive powers and cures "colics" <gloss>that is, those suffering from colic</gloss>, which for the most part proceeds from "ventosity" <term>Ventosity|Gas or flatulence trapped in the digestive tract.</term>. Chewed galangal "commends" <gloss>that is, increases</gloss> the scent of the mouth considerably, and when taken, it increases the "heat of Venus" <note>A euphemism for libido or sexual desire.</note> and of the kidneys. For heat in the kidneys makes a man desirous of Venus, and it is the most powerful cause of generating "stones" <note>Kidney stones.</note> within them. From this it follows, as Johannes de Tornamira <note>A 14th-century French physician and author of the *Introduction to the Practice of Medicine*.</note> says, that those who suffer from "sand" <note>Gravel or small kidney stones.</note> are for the most part very lustful.

# ¶ On the herb Zedoary. 71.

**First, it is affirmed that taken zedoary resists poisons**
**And a draught relieves the bites of reptiles**
**Taken, it comforts the stomach and stirs healthy**
**Belches: and with frequent use it represses nausea.**
**If the patient chews it with a fasting mouth**
**And thus gradually swallows the flavored saliva.**
**A draught is said to expel belly worms**
**It drives from the mouth the stench that garlic makes.**
**And it drives away the odor of too much wine.**

¶ **First, etc.** It is a most true word of Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (c. 129–216 AD) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; his theories dominated European medicine for over 1,500 years.</note> that the work of nature is the work of intelligence. This can be conjectured from this fact: Wolfsbane <note>original: "Napellus"; also known as monkshood, this is one of the most toxic plants found in Europe.</note> is an herb than which the earth produces nothing more deadly to men. Yet right next to it, the plant of which zedoary <term>Zedoary|A root spice related to ginger and turmeric (Curcuma zedoaria), highly valued in the Middle Ages as a "warm" medicine and antidote.</term> is the root springs forth from the earth spontaneously. Who would be so dull as not to understand that most wise nature is secretly suggesting to men that zedoary is the most excellent remedy for this so pestiferous poison?

Physicians affirm that taken zedoary "resists" <gloss>that is, helps against</gloss> poisons, and especially those of wolfsbane and the viper, as the Prince <note>original: "princeps"; in medieval medical texts, "The Prince" almost always refers to the Persian physician Avicenna (Ibn Sina).</note> testifies. "First" <gloss>that is, before other medications</gloss>, it acts as an antidote <gloss>that is, among theriacal medicines</gloss>. It "relieves" <gloss>that is, heals</gloss> the "bites of reptiles" <gloss>that is, serpents</gloss>, such as the viper, the scorpion, and others. Taken zedoary comforts the stomach and "stirs belches" <gloss>that is, healthy eructations</gloss> <gloss>that is, those conducive to health</gloss>. For by these, the wind harmful to the stomach breaks out. It also "represses nausea" <gloss>that is, a nauseating feeling of fullness</gloss> by "frequent use" <gloss>that is, often</gloss>.

Physicians say that it cures a long-standing pain of the stomach resulting from coldness—provided that the patient chews it with a "fasting mouth" <note>Medieval medicine often prescribed remedies "fasting" (on an empty stomach) to ensure the medicine's "heat" was not dampened by food.</note> and "swallows" <gloss>that is, gulps down</gloss> the "flavored" saliva <gloss>that is, saliva having the taste of zedoary</gloss>. Thus, gradually and little by little, the zedoary draught <meta>The page ends with the catchword "hau," which is the start of the word "haustus," meaning a draught or drink.</meta>

...a draught is said to expel belly worms. It drives from the mouth the heavy stench and foul smell that garlic creates, and it drives away the odor of wine that has been drunk, that is, when consumed in excess <gloss>beyond measure</gloss>. The odor of such wine is most heavy; Persius <note>Aulus Persius Flaccus (34–62 AD) was a Roman satirist known for his sharp critiques of excess and his Stoic philosophy.</note> attacks those who gulp down wine. Stertinius <note>A character appearing in the Satires of Horace, though here linked with the themes of Persius, who offers Stoic advice on curing the soul's maladies.</note> speaks of sleeping long enough to "skim off" <note>original: "despumare"; a metaphor for the body processing or "boiling off" the impurities of the wine, much like skimming foam from a cooking pot.</note> the Falernian wine.

### ¶ On the herb Clove. 72.

**They say the clove is hot and dry in the**
**Second degree; a draught strengthens the liver and the stomach.**
**And it is effective for almost all internal organs.**
**It kindles lust if, ground to the weight of a dram,**
**It is drunk with fresh cow’s milk.**
**A frequent draught comforts the memory of the brain.**

¶ In India <note>The OCR reads "nida," likely a transcription error for "India."</note> (says Sypontinus <note>Niccolò Perotti (1429–1480), also known as Sypontinus, was a humanist scholar whose work *Cornu Copiae* was a massive encyclopedia of the Latin language.</note>), there is a seed almost like a grain of pepper which is called *gariophilon*; it is larger and more brittle. They report that it is born in an Indian grove and imported; it is of a grand scent and is mixed into various seasonings. Authors say the clove <term>Clove|The dried flower bud of the *Syzygium aromaticum* tree, used in the Middle Ages as both a precious spice and a powerful "warming" medicine.</term> is hot in the second degree. A draught of clove strengthens the liver and the stomach—specifically the digestive power of those organs—and it is effective for almost all internal members. If it is ground to the weight of one dram <term>Dram|A traditional unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams or about three-quarters of a teaspoon.</term> and drunk with fresh cow’s milk, it kindles "Venus" <gloss>that is, the appetite for sexual matters</gloss>. Frequent draughts comfort the memory-power of the brain <gloss>that is, the memory</gloss> by drying up the moistures of the brain which weigh down the power of memory. Indeed, Marsilius <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was an Italian physician and philosopher who wrote "Three Books on Life," focusing on the health and psychological challenges of scholars.</note> says it happens sometimes—either because students read and write with their heads constantly bowed, or because they grow sluggish from too much leisure—that a certain viscous phlegm, together with an excessively cold melancholy, occupies the head, making it heavy and rendering the person dull and forgetful.

# ¶ On the herb Cinnamon.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'C' containing a bird (likely a swan or goose) with its neck curved back, nestled within a square frame featuring foliate decorative elements.</image-desc>

**They say there are three kinds of cinnamon,**
**But among these, that which is more delicate is more precious,**
**And that which bites the tongue more, mixed with sweetness.**
**It dries the humors of the stomach and strengthens it,**
**And it causes the food taken in to be digested immediately.**
**When taken, it heals the liver and the lower regions, and purges the menses.**
**A wet cough and catarrh will be soothed by it.**
**For the type of dropsy <note>A condition characterized by an accumulation of fluid in the body; here specifically "tympanites," where the abdomen is stretched tight like a drum.</note> (to which they give the name "drums"),**
**When taken, it greatly suppresses the pain of the kidneys.**
**It cures the bites of reptiles, if it is joined with those remedies**
**Which treat the eyes; it dries watery humors.**
**If it is well ground and mixed with strong vinegar,**
**Applied, it frees the face from foul freckles.**
**And thus it helps the disease which takes its name from an onset <note>original: "impete"; likely referring to impetigo, a contagious skin infection.</note>.**
**The coarser kind [of cinnamon] constricts the flow of hemorrhoids,**
**If it is well ground and drunk with cold water**
**At the time when the patient has not yet broken his fast.**
**This potion requires two drams <term>Dram|A unit of weight used by apothecaries, roughly equivalent to 1/8th of an ounce.</term> of that kind.**

¶ There are many types of cinnamon, says Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and pharmacologist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary botanical text for 1,500 years.</note>, but the best of all is that which is similar to the cassia of Mossylum <note>original: "messolites"; referring to an ancient port on the Horn of Africa famous for its spice trade.</note>. It should be fresh, black in color, and slow to dissolve in wine; it should have twigs that are slender, smooth, and full of nodes. It should be full of a sharp scent, much like rue, and biting to the taste in the manner of cress seed, with a certain richness and heat. Thus says Dioscorides. Lest you be led into a small error, diligent boy, *cinnama* is the nominative plural derived from this singular, *cinnamum*, which Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), a central figure of the Italian Renaissance, known for translating Plato and for his writings on "natural magic" and medicine.</note>—who was both an excellent physician and orator—frequently uses; because if the sto— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing with "stomach" or "Stoics" on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of cinnamon from the previous leaf, specifically citing authorities like Ficino and Galen on its digestive and restorative properties.</meta>

...[if the] stomach is weak, says the same Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), an influential Italian physician and philosopher of the Renaissance.</note>, and cold: add two drams of cinnamon. Cinnamon—which is commonly called cinnamon or *canelle* <note>original: "canelle"; the French-derived term for the quill-like shape of cinnamon bark.</note>—is said to have three species. But among these species, that which is more precious is held to be more fine and that which bites more—that is, more on the tongue—I say this is when it is mixed with sweetness.

Cinnamon dries the superfluous humors of the stomach (which indeed cling to the stomach walls just as a pot gathers grime and dregs after the boiling of meat). It strengthens the stomach by reason of its astringency <term>Astringency|The quality of shrinking or constricting body tissues, often used to stop secretions or bleeding.</term> and causes the stomach itself to immediately digest the food it has taken in. Cinnamon, when taken, heals a liver corrupted by coldness.

Cinnamon aromatizes the stomach, says Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), the prominent Greek physician whose medical systems remained authoritative until the Renaissance.</note>, and benefits its coldness and the coldness of the liver; and it purges the urine—that is, it provokes urination. A wet cough and catarrh will be soothed by that cinnamon (and there is no warm medicine whose drying power is like the drying power of cinnamon on account of the fineness of its substance, says Galen). 

Cinnamon, when taken, suppresses a type of dropsy to which drums give—that is, impose—the name, namely *tympanites* <note>A condition where the abdomen is bloated with gas, sounding like a drum when tapped.</note> (which is so called because the windy matter dispersed in the space of the lower belly—which is its substance as Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard medieval text.</note> says—emits a sound like a drum when struck with the hand).

It also suppresses moderate pain of the kidneys; it cures the bites of reptiles—that is, serpents—and it cures the watery humors of the eyes if it is joined with those remedies that treat the eyes, namely ocular medicines. If cinnamon is very well ground and mixed with strong vinegar, its application frees—that is, cleanses—the face of freckles <note>original: "lentigine"; referring to skin spots shaped like lentils.</note> and spots resembling lentils. It likewise helps the disease which takes its name from an onset <note>original: "impete"</note>, namely impetigo <note>A contagious skin infection; the text derives the name from the Latin "impetus," meaning an attack.</note>. (Sypontina <note>Niccolò Perotti (1429–1480), a humanist scholar who wrote under the name Sypontinus; his work "Cornu Copiae" was a massive encyclopedia of the Latin language.</note> however says that it [affects] the neighboring parts...

### ¶ Concerning the herb Costus. 74.

There are two species of Costus: one is heavy
and reddish.
It is also exceedingly bitter; this is said to be the Indian kind.
The other, however, is light, not bitter, and whitish in color.
<margin>white</margin> This the Arabs send; the former is more useful for medicine.

They cure the spleen and the liver, and are said to put to flight the pain
of the side if the sick person takes it with warm wine.

likewise, they say it calms the pain of the womb
if she applies a <term>pessary|A medicinal plug or suppository inserted into the vagina.</term> made from them to herself.
They drive out intestinal worms; they clear freckles from the face
if you smear them on, crushed with honey; and they are said to stir up
sexual desire if they are taken with warm honey-water.
Anoint anyone with the oil in which you have cooked the <term>costus|The aromatic root of Saussurea costus, used since antiquity in perfumes and medicine.</term>
before the onset of a fever while it is warm; you will render them safe from the cold.
This ointment aids sciatica and swollen limbs.
Their powder quickly cures an old wound.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural history and the geographical variations of plants, referencing Marsilio Ficino and Virgil to explain why certain medicinal herbs like Costus only thrive in specific climates.</meta>

...armed, you might think his spirit had performed a lowly service. He has left us this opinion, which is truly more true (as they say), in written form. Nature has aided locations with specific virtues, so that the properties of things are imprinted upon them according to how much they differ from the primary cause and the craftsman. The brilliant <note>The text uses "lucidus," meaning shining or clear-headed.</note> Ficinus <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), an influential Italian physician and philosopher who sought to reconcile Neoplatonism with medicine.</note> makes this manifest. "Indeed," he says, "the peach fruit demonstrates how great a difference there is in location and nourishment; in Persia it is a poison, but in Egypt, it is a friend to the heart." <note>This was a common belief in antiquity, recorded by Pliny the Elder, suggesting that the same plant changes its nature based on the soil.</note> To this, Virgil, the greatest of poets, agrees: "Indeed, not all lands can bear all things; willows grow by rivers, and alders in thick marshes; ebony is divided by the countries of trees—only India produces the black kind. The incense-bearing branch belongs to the Sabaeans alone." <note>A translation of lines from Virgil's *Georgics* (II, 109–117), a poem about agriculture and the nature of the world.</note>

But why mention these things? Surely so that it is no wonder that our land is not suitable for producing these twice-aromatic things.

There are genuine species of <term>costus|The aromatic root of the Saussurea costus plant, highly valued in ancient medicine for its warming and drying properties.</term>. One is heavy, reddish, and exceedingly bitter <gloss>that is, very</gloss>. This is said to be the Indian variety. But the other is mild, not bitter, and whitish in color <gloss>that is, tending toward whiteness</gloss>. The Arabs send this species of costus to us. The former species is more useful for medicine. Both species purge the urine and drive out <gloss>that is, repel</gloss> obstructions to them—namely, grains of sand, small stones, and blockages, as well as any other obstacles residing in the urinary tracts. They cure the spleen by opening its <term>opillationes|A medieval medical term for obstructions or blockages in the internal organs.</term>, and they cure the liver. Authors also say it puts to flight pain in the side if the sick person takes it with warm wine. They will purge the menses if a woman fumigates herself with them, and they also say they calm the pain of the <term>vulva|Used here in the historical sense of the uterus or womb.</term> <gloss>that is, the womb</gloss>, or if a woman applies <term>pessaries|Medicinal plugs inserted into the body to deliver treatment.</term> made from these to herself. They drive out intestinal worms because of their extreme bitterness. Furthermore, they clear the face of <term>lentigo|Freckles or small brownish spots on the skin.</term> <gloss>that is, unlovely spots</gloss> if you smear the face with them crushed with honey. And they are said to stir up <term>Venus|Used here to mean sexual desire or libido.</term> if they are taken with warm <term>mulsa|A mixture of water and honey, often fermented into mead.</term> before any onset of fever.

### ¶ Concerning the Herb Spica. 75.

**E**xperts testify that Spica—which is called Indian nard—is warm and dry in the first degree <note>In the medieval Galenic system of medicine, drugs were classified by four "qualities" (hot, cold, moist, dry) on a scale of one to four. A "first degree" rating suggests a mild, gentle effect.</note>. When drunk, it strengthens the liver and eases the pain of the stomach. Its decoction <term>aposima|A medicinal tea or liquid extract made by boiling herbs in water.</term> purges the kidneys. It helps the bladder, and promotes urine and menstruation. It benefits those with jaundice <term>Yctericis|Individuals suffering from icterus, or jaundice, a yellowing of the skin and eyes often associated with liver issues.</term> and stands against the harmful humors which the head often transmits to the chest through the uvula <note>original: "per uvam". Doctors at this time believed that excess "phlegm" or fluids in the brain could drip down the back of the throat (the uvula) into the lungs, causing respiratory illness.</note>. It takes away stings and internal bites. When swallowed, it is accustomed to dispel wind <note>In early medicine, "wind" referred to gas or flatulence trapped within the digestive organs.</note> trapped in the stomach.

¶ Because many simple medicines <note>A "simple" is a medicinal herb or a drug made from a single natural ingredient, rather than a complex mixture.</note> have taken their names due to the resemblance of their shape to other things, such as: Ox-tongue <note>original: "lingua bouis"</note>, Hound's-tongue <note>original: "lingua canis"</note>, Calf's-foot <note>original: "pes vituli"</note>, Crow's-foot <note>original: "pes corvi"</note>, Shepherd's-purse <note>original: "bursa pastoris"</note>, Perforated herb <note>original: "herba pforata," likely St. John's Wort.</note>, Hart's-horn <note>original: "cornu cervi"</note>, Lion's-tooth <note>original: "dens leonis," the root of the modern word "dandelion."</note>, Fox-testicles <note>original: "testiculi vulpis"</note>, Milfoil <note>original: "millefoliũ," or Yarrow.</note>, Five-leaf <note>original: "quinquefoliũ," or Cinquefoil.</note>, and Clover <note>original: "trifoliũ," or Trefoil.</note>. And six hundred other types of herbs. Likewise, Spica <note>The Latin word "spica" means an "ear" or "spike" of grain.</note> received its name because it is shaped like an ear of wheat. Indeed, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author whose "Natural History" served as the primary botanical encyclopedia for the Middle Ages.</note> says that Spica was named from its similarity to an ear of grain; it is found in three varieties among medical authors: Spica of Nard, which is called Indian; Celtic or Roman Spica; and Mountain Spica. However, when we encounter "Spica" without any adjective, "Spica of Nard" is to be understood. <meta>This page continues the medical description of Spica (Indian nard), picking up from the previous page's discussion of its benefits for the stomach and digestive system. ...[comforts] the liver <gloss>that is, the liver</gloss>, mitigates the pain of the stomach, and its decoction <term>aposima|A medicinal liquid or syrup made by boiling down herbs.</term> <gloss>that is, its boiling</gloss> purges the kidneys <gloss>that is, the stone of the kidneys</gloss>. It aids the bladder <gloss>that is, the pain of the bladder</gloss>; it drives out urine and menstruation. It is beneficial for jaundice <term>yctericis|People suffering from jaundice, identified by yellowing of the skin.</term> and it stands against <gloss>that is, meets or opposes</gloss> the harmful humors which the head <gloss>that is, the brain</gloss> often transmits to the chest through the uvula (for the uvula is a fleshy skin situated above the head of the windpipe <note>original: "canne pulmonis", literally 'the reed of the lung'</note>, providing an obstacle so that nothing harmful may enter the region of the thorax; however, sometimes being fatigued by the constant downward flow of humor from the brain, it does not perform its office well).

For the brain <gloss>that is, in man, who is called the microcosm <gloss>that is, the minor world</gloss></gloss> is said to be like the middle region of the air that is elevated in this world.

¶ By the sun and other stars, vapors are raised from watery places; when these reach the middle region of the air, they are condensed due to its coldness and, cooled, they break out downward through drops, and rain is made. Likewise, from the heart—which is the source, mine, and origin of natural heat—and from other members influencing heat, watery breaths <gloss>that is, vapors</gloss> are suddenly raised. For a vapor, as Aristotle <note>The author refers to Aristotle's theories on meteorology, which medieval doctors applied to the "weather" inside the human body.</note> says, is a breath separated from water by heat, coming from the watery humidity of nutrients. When these are carried up to the brain—which is an extremely cold member—they are condensed by the cold and slide back down like rain, and catarrhs <note>original: "catarri"; in medieval medicine, this referred to any downward flow of excess fluid or phlegm from the head into the respiratory or digestive systems.</note> are formed.

But let us return to the text: Spica takes away <gloss>that is, heals</gloss> the stings of poisonous animals and heals "bites" <gloss>that is, swellings which, by their excessive sharpness, seem to bite the internal members</gloss>. Spica, when drunk with wine, is accustomed to repel wind <note>Trapped gas or "wind" was considered a major cause of internal distress and "colic" in medieval pathology.</note> trapped in the stomach.

Applied as a suppository, it greatly restrains the flow of the womb;
If given with cold water, it is said to soothe
Trembling of the heart, and nausea is stopped thereby.
It excites Venus <note>original: "venerem", referring to sexual desire or libido.</note> when taken often with liquid.

<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal description of Spica (Nard), specifically focusing on the "Celtic" variety. ...[the herb is called] Celtic because <note>original: "idest quia"</note> it grows only in the region of the Celts. This Celtic spike is said to be capable of all the same things as Indian nard, even though it may appear to have lesser, that is, weaker, powers. 

Furthermore, Gaul is divided into two parts: one is Cisalpine <gloss>on this side of the Alps, meaning Northern Italy</gloss> and the other is Transalpine <gloss>across the Alps, meaning modern France</gloss>. Cisalpine Gaul is further divided into the Transpadane and Cispadane regions <note>Regions "across" and "on this side" of the River Po.</note>. This land our most august King Louis <note>Louis XII of France (reigned 1498–1515).</note>—accompanied by a very large army of the most vigorous warriors—now successfully occupies, intending to punish the stiff-necked treachery of the Venetians <note>A reference to the War of the League of Cambrai; Louis XII defeated the Venetians at the Battle of Agnadello in May 1509.</note>. From their battle line, a few days ago, more than ten thousand armed men perished under the favorable auspices of our king himself. For this happy victory, all of Gaul, congratulating him in a wondrous manner, has given the greatest thanks to God the Best and Greatest with wonderful celebration, and continually pours out the most humble and suppliant prayers for the most favorable successes of our highest prince himself. 

But let us return to the purpose. Transalpine Gaul is divided into three types of people, distinguished mostly by rivers: from the Scheldt <note>original: "Scalde"</note> to the Seine <note>original: "Secana"</note> is Belgica; from the Seine to the Garonne <note>original: "Garumna"</note> is Celtica—from which comes the Celtic spike, which is also called Gallic. This same region is called Lugdunensis, named after the noble town of Lyon <note>original: "Lugduno"</note>. From the Garonne to the Pyrenees mountains is Aquitania.

### ¶ Concerning the herb Frankincense. lxxvi.
<note>Chapter 76. While "Thus" usually refers to the resin frankincense, medieval herbalists often categorized it among medicinal plants.</note>

> They have said that frankincense is hot and dry in the second degree.
> It clears the eyes if it is dissolved in the white <note>original: "lacrimo," referring here to the fluid or "tear" of the egg</note> of an egg.
> Either ground up with warm breast milk,
> Or ground with sharp wine and mixed with pitch and milk,
> They say it especially cures recent wounds.
> Mixed with pork fat, it will provide a remedy for these things.
> Mixed with honey, it dispels whitlows <term>panaritia|A painful, pus-filled infection at the end of a finger or toe, near the nail.</term> of the nail.
> They affirm that frankincense, well-ground and dissolved in warm wine,
> Greatly benefits the ear of one who desires [relief].

Ground with Cimolean earth <note>A medicinal clay from the island of Kimolos, used in antiquity to cool inflammations.</note> and mixed with rose juices, they say it cures swollen breasts. They believe nothing helps those spitting blood <note>original: "emoptoicis", referring to hemoptysis</note> more than if it is taken mixed with wine or vinegar. If you grind Frankincense with breast milk and apply it, it will heal ulcers that often appear in the anus or any other place. Adding to this the white and the "tear" <gloss>the liquid part</gloss> of an egg white so that it is thick, apply it over a cut vein or to those wounds from which too much blood flows. Do not loosen the dressing until you believe the divided parts have joined. If there is a need, apply the same medicine over the previous layer until the solidified scar is able to be strengthened. They assert nothing can stop the flow of blood better. 

It comforts a forgetful brain with its own scent. If someone chews it together with oregano <note>original: "organo", likely referring to wild marjoram</note>, it repels harmful humors of the head through spitting. This purging of the head lightens a "heavy tongue" <note>This may refer to a speech impediment or lethargy caused by an imbalance of humors.</note>. Mixed with the fat of a goose or duck, Frankincense makes a powerful poultice for limbs that fire has harmed.

¶ They believe that Frankincense <note>original: "Thus"</note> is derived from a Greek word <note>original: "potoichio", likely a corruption of "thyein"</note> which means "I sacrifice," because in ancient times frankincense was customarily used in sacrifices to the gods. Whence Cato—who should be counted not among the petty grammarians but among the most refined poets—says: "Appease the god with frankincense." 

¶ And the Magi, those observers of the stars, came to Christ the leader of life, guided by a star, offering the precious treasure of life: gold, frankincense, and myrrh, as Ficinus <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), an influential Italian physician and philosopher.</note> says. He teaches pharmacists <term>pharmacopola</term> <gloss>merchants or compounders of medicines</gloss> to make pills suitable for preserving life from these three precious gifts melted together. This practice is certainly not at all hated in our Christian religion...

<meta>This page continues the prose medical commentary on Frankincense (Thus), specifically focusing on its properties as a "hot and dry" substance and its various applications in treating the eyes, wounds, and internal ailments. It references the conflicting opinions of Renaissance scholar Marsilio Ficino and the ancient authority Galen regarding the effects of its smoke.</meta>

It softens. Authors say <gloss>that is, they have said</gloss> it is hot and dry in the second degree. It clears the eyes if it is dissolved and washed in the "tear" <gloss>that is, the white of an egg</gloss>, ground with warm breast milk. Although Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), an influential Italian priest, physician, and philosopher who led the Platonic Academy in Florence.</note> says it harms the eyes by its own nature, I would not believe that the scent of frankincense <note>original: "thuris"</note> dulls their sight. For as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the most famous physician of the Roman Empire, whose theories on "humors" dominated medicine for 1,500 years.</note> testifies, everything that is dissolved by another thing is similar to that by which it is dissolved—this much can be gathered by man. If frankincense brightens the sight, the smoke released from it does not darken it; this perhaps led the ancients to perform fumigations in temples with frankincense rather than oils.

Authors say that frankincense ground with sharp wine—I mean mixed with pitch and milk—cures recent wounds especially <gloss>that is, above other medicines</gloss>. Frankincense mixed with pig fat will heal these wounds. Frankincense mixed with honey strikes down <gloss>that is, drives away</gloss> "panaricia" of the nail <note>original: "panaricia vnguis", referring to a paronychia or whitlow, a painful infection of the fingertip.</note>. These are small abscesses at the tips of the nails produced from very sharp matter, which, if neglected, often leave behind a most foul mark.

Physicians say that frankincense ground well <gloss>that is, very much</gloss> and dissolved with warm wine is very beneficial for an aching ear. Physicians say that frankincense ground and mixed with chalk <gloss>that is, with white Cimolean earth</gloss> and rose juice cures swollen breasts. (Cimolean earth <note>original: "Chimolea", a medicinal clay from the Greek island of Kimolos, prized for its cooling properties.</note> is a certain kind of very cold earth; in its place, they take the mud found under a grindstone where swords are sharpened with water.)

Authors do not believe anything helps those spitting blood <note>original: "emoptoicis", a transliteration of the Greek term for hemoptysis.</note> more than if frankincense is taken mixed with wine or vinegar. You shall grind frankincense with breast milk; if you apply it to ulcers that often appear in the anus <note>original: "mano", likely a scribal error for "ano".</note> or any other place, it will heal <gloss>that is, bring medicine to</gloss> those wounds. You will join to this aloe and "the tear" <gloss>that is, egg white</gloss> in this way <gloss>that is, in this manner</gloss> so that it is thick, and apply it over a <unclear>facte</unclear> <gloss>that is, an incision</gloss> or to those wounds that flow...

### On the Aloe herb. lxxxvii.

There are two species of aloe, one of which is reddish <margin>and is.</margin>
On the inside it is like a liver when broken; hence "hepatic" <margin>hepatic.</margin>
It is called, and it possesses great powers in medicine.
It is more useful than the one that appears pitch-black in color when broken.
It cleanses the stomach, head, and joints of phlegm <term>Flegmate: one of the four bodily humors, associated with coldness and moisture</term>
By gently purging the harmful humor through the bowels.
It purges the jaundiced <note>original: "yctericos", those suffering from yellowing of the skin and eyes</note>: it aids the inner parts of the liver.
It cures every fresh wound from all rot;
Its powder, when sprinkled on, heals by drying.
They say that especially the ulcers or wounds of the penis <term>veretri: a formal anatomical term for the male organ</term>
And of the testicles are cured by such a powder.
If it is dissolved in water, it helps an abscess <term>apostema: a localized collection of pus or a swelling</term> of the lips,
And applied over the nose as well as the eyes.
It also drives away a bruise if one is formed around the eyes,
If you mix it with sharp wine and rose oil.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of Frankincense to the chapter on Aloe (Chapter 87). It begins with the concluding verses on the applications of Aloe for the head and hair, followed by a prose commentary that draws on authorities like Oribasius, Mesue, and Hippocrates to defend the medicinal value of bitter substances.</meta>

Anoint an aching head and forehead and it will help wonderfully.
By this, the itching of the eyes from blood will be calmed.
When well ground with wine and made into a poultice,
It strengthens and retains hair falling out from anywhere.
It also assists the tongue, the gums, and the defects of the mouth,
If they are rubbed with it when it is ground with wine and honey.
Taken by itself, it does not loosen the belly <note>original: "ventrem non soluit", meaning it does not act as a strong laxative</note> sharply.
However, Oribasius <note>A 4th-century Greek medical compiler and personal physician to the Emperor Julian.</note> thinks it suitable for the stomach
Even when those things which loosen it might cause harm; other things loosen the belly.

¶ Aloe shows us by a most evident argument that the value of things must be weighed by the intellect, not by the sense. For if faith is given to the sense of taste, what is more bitter than aloe, or what more unpleasant to the palate than costus <term>costus: a bitter, aromatic ginger-like root from India, highly prized in ancient and medieval pharmacy</term>? But if reason holds its place, nothing is more conducive or more health-giving to human well-being. Johannes Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a Persian Christian physician whose pharmaceutical writings were essential to medieval medicine.</note> is a most rich witness of this: "Aloe: what is among the better and more sublime medicines, having a prerogative over other medicines."

¶ And whoever continues its administration <note>original: "accensiōnem", likely referring to the regular taking or "kindling" of the medicine's effect</note> for continuous days does not see in his body nor in the instruments of his senses a sickness that is not capable of being healed. Wherefore Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Medicine" (c. 460 – c. 370 BC).</note> in the vestibule <note>original: "vestibulo", meaning the introduction or opening section</note> of the first of his works ordered that the sick—who for the most part judge by their senses—should be compliant to the doctors. 

There are two species of aloe. One is <gloss>superior</gloss> which is somewhat red <gloss>that is, whose color turns toward redness</gloss> and is inside <gloss>that is, in its internal part</gloss> like a liver <note>original: "sicut epar"</note> <gloss>that is, it presents the color of a liver</gloss> when <gloss>that is, when</gloss> it is broken. From this <gloss>that is, for that reason</gloss> <unclear>it is called</unclear> hepatic <note>original: "epatite", referring to Aloe hepatica, named for its liver-like brownish-red hue</note> and it has great powers in medicine. 

¶ That species of aloe is more useful which, when broken <gloss>that is, when it is broken</gloss>, is seen <gloss>that is, appears</gloss> with a pitch-like color <gloss>that is, to have the color of pitch</gloss> <gloss>that is, black</gloss>. Aloe cleanses the stomach, the head, and the joints <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "articu-", likely for "articulos" (joints).</note>

<meta>This page continues the medical discussion of Aloe (Chapter 87), moving from a general defense of bitter medicines to specific clinical applications for various parts of the body, including the stomach, liver, skin, and eyes. It continues to cite John Mesue and Galen as primary authorities for these treatments.</meta>

...all members of the body from phlegm. For as John Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a Persian Christian physician whose pharmaceutical writings were essential to medieval medicine.</note> says: Aloe draws out bile and phlegm <note>original: "coleram et phlezma"</note> and cleanses the head and the stomach of them, and provides great relief for their pains. It therefore purges these members of phlegm by cleansing them gently—that is, without violence—expelling the harmful humor <gloss>that is, the "offending" humor</gloss> through the bowels <gloss>that is, through the lower gate</gloss>. 

Aloe purges—that is, cleanses—those suffering from jaundice <term>yctericos: patients with jaundice, a yellowing of the skin and eyes believed to be caused by an excess of yellow bile</term>; it helps the internal organs—that is, it opens obstructions <note>original: "opilationes", referring to blockages in the vessels or ducts</note> of the liver. Its powder, when applied, cleanses every fresh wound—that is, one recently inflicted—from all decay. Indeed, its power in preventing decay is marvelous; thus, as John Mesue says, it is used even for the corpses of the dead, especially when mixed with myrrh <term>myrrh: a fragrant, antibacterial resin used since antiquity for both medicine and embalming</term>. It also heals a wound by drying it out; for wounds are not healed unless they are dried, as the authority Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity whose theories of the four humors dominated medieval medicine.</note> asserts. 

Physicians say that ulcers or wounds, especially—that is, most particularly—of the penis and testicles <gloss>that is, the private parts of the body</gloss>, are healed by such a powder (the difference between a "wound" and an "ulcer" was mentioned above). If aloe is dissolved in water, it helps an abscess <gloss>that is, a swelling</gloss> of the lips. And aloe applied topically helps—that is, heals—an abscess of the nose and eyes, and drives away—that is, removes—the bruising <gloss>that is, the lead-colored hue</gloss> formed around the "lights" <gloss>that is, the eyes</gloss>. 

By mixing aloe with sharp wine and rose oil, you may anoint an aching head—provided the fear is that it stems from a cold cause <note>In humoral medicine, "cold" ailments were treated with "warm" or "drying" substances like aloe.</note>—and the forehead, and it will help wonderfully <gloss>that is, in a marvelous way</gloss>. The itching <gloss>that is, the irritation</gloss> of the eyes will be soothed—that is, cured—by this aforementioned ointment. Aloe, well ground with wine and made into a poultice <term>cathaplasma: a soft, moist mass of herbs or flour applied to the body to relieve soreness and inflammation</term>, strengthens and retains any falling hair. It also—that is, additionally—assists the gums and defects of the mouth, provided that there are ulcers in these places. If they are rubbed with it—namely, with aloe...

<meta>This page concludes the medicinal entry for Aloe (Chapter 87). It transitions from general descriptions of the substance's properties to specific dosages and preparations, citing the Greek physician Oribasius and the Persian physician John Mesue. It emphasizes that while many purgatives are harsh and damaging to the organs, aloe is uniquely gentle and beneficial for the stomach, head, and eyes.</meta>

When ground with honey, this aloe taken by itself—that is, without other medicine—does not loosen the bowels <gloss>add "acutely"</gloss>—that is, swiftly. Nevertheless, the physician Oribasius <note>Oribasius (c. 320–400 AD) was a Greek medical writer and the personal physician to the Roman Emperor Julian. His works compiled much of the medical knowledge of antiquity.</note> considers aloe to be well-suited to the stomach. This is so even though other laxatives <gloss>that is, other purgative medicines</gloss> such as scammony <term>scamonea: a harsh resinous purgative derived from the root of Convolvulus scammonia</term>, colocynth <term>colloquintida: also known as "bitter apple," a powerful and often violent laxative used to treat dropsy</term>, turbith <term>turbith: the root of a plant in the morning glory family used in medieval pharmacy to purge "thick" humors</term>, agaric <term>agaricus: a medicinal fungus (larch polypore) used to purge phlegm from the system</term>, and others of that kind might cause harm <gloss>that is, bring about injury</gloss>. For it is characteristic of purgative medicine, as John Mesue says, to bring injury to the principal organs.

He commanded taking two drachms <term>dragmas: a Greek unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams or about a teaspoonful</term> of it with honeyed water;
In this way, it purges bile and phlegm <note>original: "coleram sic flegmaque"</note>.
He said to take it every day after dinner:
It is useful to take two or three fresh pills of it,
Formed with cabbage juice, as large as a bean
Or a chickpea; it purges harmful humors with the stool,
Neither corrupting the stomach nor using force <gloss>violence</gloss>.
He who is frequently purged by these pills
Will hardly ever be troubled by the pain of a headache.
And believe me, nothing is healthier for the eyes than these.
But if you wish the treatment to loosen the bowels more generously,
One part of scammony <term>diagridii: a preparation of scammony juice thickened with quince juice, used as a potent laxative</term> should be mixed by grinding
With two parts [of aloe]; thus an effective solution will be made.

->The End.<-

<meta>This page concludes the medicinal entry for Aloe (Chapter 87) and provides the closing statement for the entire herbal manuscript. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on the gentleness of aloe as a purgative, specifically continuing a sentence about its ability to purge humors from the body.</meta>

It teaches that aloe with honeyed water <gloss>that is, with *melicratum* or honey-water</gloss> purges bile and phlegm <note>In the medieval theory of the four humors, bile (choler) and phlegm were substances that needed to be kept in balance to maintain health; an excess was thought to cause disease.</note> in this way. Also, Oribasius <note>Oribasius (c. 320–400 AD) was a Greek physician whose works were widely studied in the Middle Ages.</note> said that one should take every day—that is, daily—after dinner, three pills of this aloe, or two fresh ones <gloss>that is, recently made and shaped</gloss> with cabbage juice. These should be as large as beans or a chickpea <gloss>that is, the seed of a chickpea</gloss>. It purges harmful humors <gloss>that is, injurious fluids</gloss> from the stomach along with the dung <gloss>that is, with fecal matter</gloss>, without corrupting the stomach or using force <gloss>that is, violence</gloss>. He who has been purged by these pills will rarely be troubled by a headache; and believe me, nothing is healthier for the eyes than these pills.

But <gloss>that is, however</gloss>, if you wish to loosen the hard belly <gloss>that is, the constipated bowels</gloss> more largely <gloss>that is, more copiously</gloss>, one part of diagridium <term>diagridis: a preparation of scammony juice (a harsh purgative) baked inside a quince to temper its acidity and violence</term> <gloss>that is, scammony prepared in a quince fruit</gloss> is to be mixed with twin <gloss>that is, two</gloss> parts of aloe by grinding <gloss>that is, crushing the whole mixture together</gloss> so that a suitable fermentation <note>The term "fermentation" here refers to the chemical or medicinal integration of the ingredients through thorough mixing.</note> occurs. In this way <gloss>that is, once this is done</gloss>, a fitting <gloss>that is, suitable</gloss> loosening of the bowels will occur.

¶ But so that we may finally sound the retreat <note>original: "canamus receptui" — a military metaphor used here to signal the conclusion of the book.</note> before we put the finishing touch on this little midnight study <note>original: "lucubratiūcule," referring to a work composed by candlelight or late at night.</note>, we wish all those unfolding this book of Macer to be warned: do not be too bold and inconsiderate in taking or administering laxative medicines <gloss>that is, drugs</gloss> while spurning the advice of doctors; for from their unadvised administration, more dangers loom than can be brought forth here.

¶ Most studious youths, you have the little work of Macer Floridus <note>Macer Floridus was the pseudonym of Odo of Meung, an 11th-century French scholar. His poem on herbs was a standard medical reference for centuries.</note> on the powers of herbs, most highly corrected from every error, along with the very clear little interpretations of <unclear>Thomas</unclear> Meridiana.
